## Introduction “For This My Son Was Dead, and Is Alive Again; He Was Lost; and Is Found.” / 引言 「我这个儿子是死而复活，失而又得的」
===Original===

He was lost by the Norton-Harjes Ambulance Corps.
He was officially dead as a result of official misinformation.
He was entombed by the French Government.
It took the better part of three months to find him and bring him back to life﻿—with the help of powerful and willing friends on both sides of the Atlantic. The following documents tell the story:

===Chinese===

他被诺顿-哈耶斯救护队（Norton-Harjes Ambulance Corps）弄丢了。
由于官方的误报，他被正式宣告死亡。
他被法国政府幽禁于囹圄之中。
在大西洋两岸有权势而又热心的朋友们帮助下，花了将近三个月才把他找到，并将他起死回生。以下文件讲述了这个故事：

===Original===

> Mr. President:
> It seems criminal to ask for a single moment of your time. But I am strongly advised that it would be more criminal to delay any longer calling to your attention a crime against American citizenship in which the French Government has persisted for many weeks﻿—in spite of constant appeals made to the American Minister at Paris; and in spite of subsequent action taken by the State Department at Washington, on the initiative of my friend, Hon. —﻿—﻿—.
> The victims are two American ambulance drivers, Edward Estlin Cummings of Cambridge, Mass., and W﻿—﻿— S﻿—﻿— B﻿—﻿—.﻿ ﻿…
> More than two months ago these young men were arrested, subjected to many indignities, dragged across France like criminals, and closely confined in a Concentration Camp at La Ferté Macé; where, according to latest advices they still remain﻿—awaiting the final action of the Minister of the Interior upon the findings of a Commission which passed upon their cases as long ago as October 17.
> Against Cummings both private and official advices from Paris state that there is no charge whatever. He has been subjected to this outrageous treatment solely because of his intimate friendship with young B﻿—﻿—, whose sole crime is﻿—so far as can be learned﻿—that certain letters to friends in America were misinterpreted by an overzealous French censor.
> It only adds to the indignity and irony of the situation to say that young Cummings is an enthusiastic lover of France and so loyal to the friends he has made among the French soldiers, that even while suffering in health from his unjust confinement, he excuses the ingratitude of the country he has risked his life to serve by calling attention to the atmosphere of intense suspicion and distrust that has naturally resulted from the painful experience which France has had with foreign emissaries.
> Be assured, Mr. President, that I have waited long﻿—it seems like ages﻿—and have exhausted all other available help before venturing to trouble you.
> After such painful and baffling experiences, I turn to you﻿—burdened though I know you to be, in this world crisis, with the weightiest task ever laid upon any man.
> But I have another reason for asking this favor. I do not speak for my son alone; or for him and his friend alone. My son has a mother﻿—as brave and patriotic as any mother who ever dedicated an only son to a great cause.

===Chinese===

> 总统先生：
> 向您索求片刻时间似乎是一种罪过。但有人极力忠告我：若再拖延，不及时提请您注意法国政府数周以来持续进行的一桩侵害美国公民权益的罪行，那才是更大的罪过——尽管我们已不断向驻巴黎的美国公使申诉；尽管在我的朋友、尊敬的———先生的倡议下，华盛顿国务院也已采取了后续行动。
> 受害者是两名美国救护车司机：马萨诸塞州坎布里奇的爱德华·埃斯特林·卡明斯（Edward Estlin Cummings）和W——·S——·B——。……
> 两个多月前，这两位年轻人遭到逮捕，受尽种种凌辱，像罪犯一样被押解横穿法国，严密囚禁在拉费尔泰马塞（La Ferté Macé）的一个集中营里；据最新消息，他们至今仍被关在那里——等待内政部长对委员会裁决作出最终处置，而该委员会早在10月17日就审理了他们的案子。
> 来自巴黎的私人及官方消息均表明，对卡明斯根本没有任何指控。他之所以遭受这种粗暴的待遇，仅仅是因为他与小B——交情深厚；而后者的全部罪行——据目前所能了解的——不过是写给美国友人的某些信件被一位过分热心的法国检查官曲解了。
> 更令人感到屈辱和讽刺的是：小卡明斯是法兰西的狂热热爱者，他对结识的法国士兵朋友如此忠心，以致即使因不公正的监禁而健康受损，他仍在为那个他甘冒生命危险去效力的国家的忘恩负义开脱，说这是法国饱受外国密使之害的惨痛经历之后，自然而然酿成的极度猜疑与不信任的氛围使然。
> 请相信，总统先生，我已经等了很久——仿佛等了几个世纪——在万不得已打扰您之前，我已用尽了一切其他可求助的途径。
> 经历了如此痛苦而令人困惑的遭遇之后，我转向您——我深知，在这场世界危机中，您肩负着有史以来落在任何人肩上最沉重的担子。
> 但我请求您帮忙还有另一个原因。我不只是为我的儿子一个人说话，也不只是为他和他的朋友说话。我的儿子有一位母亲——她与任何把独生子奉献给伟大事业的母亲一样勇敢、一样爱国。

===Original===

The mothers of our boys in France have rights as well as the boys themselves. My boy’s mother had a right to be protected from the weeks of horrible anxiety and suspense caused by the inexplicable arrest and imprisonment of her son. My boy’s mother had a right to be spared the supreme agony caused by a blundering cable from Paris saying that he had been drowned by a submarine. (An error which Mr. Norton subsequently cabled that he had discovered six weeks before.) My boy’s mother and all American mothers have a right to be protected against all needless anxiety and sorrow.
> Pardon me, Mr. President, but if I were President and your son were suffering such prolonged injustice at the hands of France; and your son’s mother had been needlessly kept in Hell as many weeks as my boy’s mother has﻿—I would do something to make American citizenship as sacred in the eyes of Frenchmen as Roman citizenship was in the eyes of the ancient world. Then it was enough to ask the question, “Is it lawful to scourge a man that is a Roman, and uncondemned?” Now, in France, it seems lawful to treat like a condemned criminal a man that is an American, uncondemned and admittedly innocent!
> Very respectfully,
> Edward Cummings

This letter was received at the White House. Whether it was received with sympathy or with silent disapproval is still a mystery. A Washington official, a friend in need and a friend indeed in these trying experiences, took the precaution to have it delivered by messenger. Otherwise, fear that it had been “lost in the mail” would have added another twinge of uncertainty to the prolonged and exquisite tortures inflicted upon parents by alternations of misinformation and official silence. Doubtless the official stethoscope was on the heart of the world just then; and perhaps it was too much to expect that even a postcard would be wasted on private heartaches.
In any event this letter told where to look for the missing boys﻿—something the French government either could not or would not disclose, in spite of constant pressure by the American Embassy at Paris and constant efforts by my friend Richard Norton, who was head of the Norton-Harjes Ambulance organization from which they had been abducted.
Release soon followed, as narrated in the following letter to Major —﻿—﻿— of the staff of the Judge Advocate General in Paris.

===Chinese===

我们在法国的孩子们的母亲，与孩子们本人一样享有权利。我孩子的母亲本有权免受那数周可怕的焦虑与煎熬——那是由她儿子莫名其妙的被捕和监禁引起的。我孩子的母亲本有权免遭那锥心刺骨的痛苦——巴黎发来的一封荒唐电报，说她儿子被潜艇击沉溺亡。（这个错误，诺顿先生后来来电说，他六周前就已发现了。）我孩子的母亲和所有的美国母亲一样，有权得到保护，免受一切不必要的焦虑与悲伤。
> 恕我直言，总统先生，倘若我是总统，而您的儿子在法国人手中遭受如此漫长的冤屈；倘若您儿子的母亲也像我孩子的母亲一样，被无谓地打入地狱达数周之久——我一定会采取行动，让美国公民身份在法国人心目中，像罗马公民身份在古代世界人们心目中那样神圣。那时候，只需问一句「鞭打一个未经定罪的罗马人，难道合法吗？」就足够了。而在今天的法国，把一个未经定罪、且公认无辜的美国人当作已判刑的罪犯来对待，似乎竟是合法的！
> 谨致崇高敬意，
> 爱德华·卡明斯

这封信被送到了白宫。它是得到了同情，还是遭到了无声的非难，至今仍是一个谜。一位华盛顿官员——在这些磨难中的一位患难之交、真正的朋友——特意小心安排，让信使亲自递送。否则，唯恐信件「邮寄遗失」的担忧又会给做父母的平添一分揪心的不安；误报与官方沉默的交替折磨，早已使他们受尽了漫长而彻骨的煎熬。毫无疑问，当时官方的听诊器正贴在世界的心脏上；指望连一张明信片都肯花费在私人伤痛上，或许过于奢求了。
无论如何，这封信点明了该到哪里寻找失踪的孩子们——这一点法国政府始终不肯透露，无论是不能还是不愿；尽管驻巴黎的美国大使馆不断施压，我的朋友理查德·诺顿也不断努力，而诺顿正是诺顿-哈耶斯救护队的负责人，孩子们就是从那里被掳走的。
不久孩子们便获释了，下面这封写给巴黎军法署参谋———少校的信记述了这一经过。

===Original===

> My dear —﻿—﻿—
> Your letter of January 30th, which I have been waiting for with great interest ever since I received your cable, arrived this morning. My son arrived in New York on January 1st. He was in bad shape physically as a result of his imprisonment: very much under weight, suffering from a bad skin infection which he had acquired at the concentration camp. However, in view of the extraordinary facilities which the detention camp offered for acquiring dangerous diseases, he is certainly to be congratulated on having escaped with one of the least harmful. The medical treatment at the camp was quite in keeping with the general standards of sanitation there; with the result that it was not until he began to receive competent surgical treatment after his release and on board ship that there was much chance of improvement. A month of competent medical treatment here seems to have got rid of this painful reminder of official hospitality. He is, at present, visiting friends in New York. If he were here, I am sure he would join with me and with his mother in thanking you for the interest you have taken and the efforts you have made.
> W﻿—﻿— S﻿—﻿— B﻿—﻿— is, I am happy to say, expected in New York this week by the S.S. *Niagara*. News of his release and subsequently of his departure came by cable. What you say about the nervous strain under which he was living, as an explanation of the letters to which the authorities objected, is entirely borne out by firsthand information. The kind of badgering which the youth received was enough to upset a less sensitive temperament. It speaks volumes for the character of his environment that such treatment aroused the resentment of only one of his companions, and that even this manifestation of normal human sympathy was regarded as “suspicious.” If you are right in characterizing B﻿—﻿—’s condition as more or less hysterical, what shall we say of the conditions which made possible the treatment which he and his friend received? I am glad B﻿—﻿— wrote the very sensible and manly letter to the Embassy, which you mention. After I have had an opportunity to converse with him, I shall be in better position to reach a conclusion in regard to certain matters about which I will not now express an opinion.
> I would only add that I do not in the least share your complacency in regard to the treatment which my son received.

===Chinese===

> 亲爱的———：
> 您1月30日的信今晨到了，自从收到您的电报，我就一直怀着极大的期待等候这封信。我的儿子已于1月1日抵达纽约。监禁使他的身体大受损伤：体重锐减，还染上了严重的皮肤感染——那是在集中营里落下的。不过，考虑到那个拘留营为染上种种危险疾病提供了非同寻常的便利，他只带回其中一种危害最小的病，实在值得庆幸。营中的医疗水平与那里的一般卫生水准倒是十分相称；其结果是，直到获释之后并在船上开始接受像样的外科治疗，病情才大有起色。在这里经过一个月得力的医治，这份「官方款待」留下的痛苦纪念品似乎已经除去了。他目前正在纽约探望朋友。他若在此，我相信他定会同我和他母亲一起，感谢您所给予的关心和您付出的努力。
> 我很高兴地告诉您，W——·S——·B——预计本周乘「尼亚加拉」号轮船抵达纽约。他获释以及随后启程的消息都是来电告知的。您说他当时生活在精神紧张之下，以此解释当局反感的那几封信——这一说法完全被第一手信息所证实。这年轻人所遭受的那种刁难，足以把一个不那么敏感的人逼得失态。他受到的待遇只激起同伴中一个人的义愤，而就连这种正常人类同情心的表露也被视为「可疑」——他身处的那种环境的品性如何，由此可窥一斑。如果您把B——的状态形容为多少有些歇斯底里是对的，那么，对于造成他和他的朋友遭受如此对待的环境，我们又该说什么呢？我很高兴B——写了那封致大使馆的十分明智而有男子气概的信，正如您所提到的。等我有机会与他面谈之后，我将在某些问题上更有把握下结论，这些问题我此刻不便发表意见。
> 我只想补充一句：对于我儿子所受的待遇，我丝毫不敢苟同您那种心安理得的态度。

===Original===

The very fact that, as you say, no charges were made and that he was detained on suspicion for many weeks after the Commission passed on his case and reported to the Minister of the Interior that he ought to be released, leads me to a conclusion exactly opposite to that which you express. It seems to me impossible to believe that any well-ordered government would fail to acknowledge such action to have been unreasonable. Moreover, “detention on suspicion” was a small part of what actually took place. To take a single illustration, you will recall that after many weeks’ persistent effort to secure information, the Embassy was still kept so much in the dark about the facts, that it cabled the report that my son had embarked on *The Antilles* and was reported lost. And when convinced of that error, the Embassy cabled that it was renewing efforts to locate my son. Up to that moment, it would appear that the authorities had not even condescended to tell the United States Embassy where this innocent American citizen was confined; so that a mistaken report of his death was regarded as an adequate explanation of his disappearance. If I had accepted this report and taken no further action, it is by no means certain that he would not be dead by this time.
> I am free to say, that in my opinion no self-respecting government could allow one of its own citizens, against whom there has been no accusation brought, to be subjected to such prolonged indignities and injuries by a friendly government without vigorous remonstrance. I regard it as a patriotic duty, as well as a matter of personal self-respect, to do what I can to see that such remonstrance is made. I still think too highly both of my own government and of the government of France to believe that such an untoward incident will fail to receive the serious attention it deserves. If I am wrong, and American citizens must expect to suffer such indignities and injuries at the hands of other governments without any effort at remonstrance and redress by their own government, I believe the public ought to know the humiliating truth. It will make interesting reading. It remains for my son to determine what action he will take.
> I am glad to know your son is returning. I am looking forward with great pleasure to conversing with him.

===Chinese===

正如您所说，对他的指控一项也没有提出，而且在委员会审理他的案子、并向内政部长报告他应当释放之后，他还以「涉嫌」为由被拘押了数周——恰恰是这一事实，使我的结论与您的截然相反。在我看来，任何一个治理有方的政府都不可能不承认这样的行为是不合理的。何况，「涉嫌拘押」只是实际发生的事情中微不足道的一小部分。举一个例子：您会记得，经过数周坚持不懈的努力打探消息，大使馆对事实真相依旧蒙在鼓里，以致发电报说我儿子已登上「安地列斯」号并据报遇难。而在确信那是个错误之后，大使馆又来电说正在重新努力寻找我儿子。直到那一刻，当局似乎还不屑于告诉美国大使馆这位无辜的美国公民被关在哪里；于是，一份错误的死亡报告竟被视为对他失踪的圆满解释。倘若我当初接受了这份报告而不再采取行动，他此刻是否还活着，可就难说了。
> 我可以坦率地说，依我之见，任何一个有自尊的政府，都不可能听任自己的一名公民——一个从未被提出任何指控的公民——遭受友邦政府如此长期的凌辱与伤害而不提出强硬抗议。我认为，尽力促成这样的抗议，既是一份爱国义务，也是个人自尊之所系。我对我国政府和法国政府依然抱有太高的期望，不相信这样一桩不幸事件竟得不到它应得的严肃对待。倘若我错了，倘若美国公民必须甘愿在他国政府手中承受这样的凌辱与伤害，而本国政府不作任何抗议、不图任何补救，那么我认为，公众就应该知道这屈辱的真相。那将是耐人寻味的读物。至于采取何种行动，则由我的儿子自行决定。
> 得知您的儿子即将归来，我很高兴。我殷切期待与他一晤。

===Original===

> I cannot adequately express my gratitude to you and to other friends for the sympathy and assistance I have received. If any expenses have been incurred on my behalf or on behalf of my son, I beg you to give me the pleasure of reimbursing you. At best, I must always remain your debtor.
> With best wishes,
> Sincerely yours,
> Edward Cummings

I yield to no one in enthusiasm for the cause of France. Her cause was our cause and the cause of civilization; and the tragedy is that it took us so long to find it out. I would gladly have risked my life for her, as my son risked his and would have risked it again had not the departure of his regiment overseas been stopped by the armistice.
France was beset with enemies within as well as without. Some of the “suspects” were members of her official household. Her Minister of Interior was thrown into prison. She was distracted with fear. Her existence was at stake. Under such circumstances excesses were sure to be committed. But it is precisely at such times that American citizens most need and are most entitled to the protection of their own government.
Edward Cummings

===Chinese===

> 对您和其他朋友给予我的同情与援助，我的感激之情难以言表。若为我或为我儿子有任何花费，恳请给我这个荣幸，容我偿还。无论如何，我永远是欠着你们情分的。
> 谨致美好祝愿，
> 您真诚的，
> 爱德华·卡明斯

在对法兰西事业的热忱上，我不逊于任何人。她的事业就是我们的事业，也是文明的事业；悲剧只在于，我们竟用了那么久才明白这一点。我甘愿为她冒生命之险，正如我儿子冒过那样；若不是停战协定使他所在的团开赴海外的行程中止，他还会再次赴险。
法兰西内外交困，腹背受敌。一些「嫌疑分子」就出自她的官方内部。她的内政部长被投入了监狱。她被恐惧折磨得方寸大乱。她的生死存亡悬于一线。在这样的境况下，过火之事在所难免。但恰恰是在这种时候，美国公民最需要、也最有权得到本国政府的保护。
爱德华·卡明斯

## I I Begin a Pilgrimage / 一 我踏上一段朝圣之旅
===Original===

In October, 1917, we had succeeded, my friend B. and I, in dispensing with almost three of our six months’ engagement as Voluntary Drivers, Sanitary Section 21, Ambulance Norton Harjes, American Red Cross, and at the moment which subsequent experience served to capitalize, had just finished the unlovely job of cleaning and greasing (*nettoyer* is the proper word) the own private flivver of the chief of section, a gentleman by the convenient name of Mr. A. To borrow a characteristic-cadence from Our Great President: the lively satisfaction which we might be suspected of having derived from the accomplishment of a task so important in the saving of civilization from the clutches of Prussian tyranny was in some degree inhibited, unhappily, by a complete absence of cordial relations between the man whom fate had placed over us and ourselves. Or, to use the vulgar American idiom, B. and I and Mr. A. didn’t get on well. We were in fundamental disagreement as to the attitude which we, Americans, should uphold toward the poilus in whose behalf we had volunteered assistance, Mr. A. maintaining “you boys want to keep away from those dirty Frenchmen” and “we’re here to show those bastards how they do things in America,” to which we answered by seizing every opportunity for fraternization. Inasmuch as eight “dirty Frenchmen” were attached to the section in various capacities (cook, provisioner, chauffeur, mechanician, etc.) and the section itself was affiliated with a branch of the French army, fraternization was easy. Now when he saw that we had not the slightest intention of adopting his ideals, Mr. A. (together with the *sous-lieutenant* who acted as his translator﻿—for the chief’s knowledge of the French language, obtained during several years’ heroic service, consisted for the most part in “*Sar var*,” “*Sar marche*,” and “*Deet donk moan vieux*”) confined his efforts to denying us the privilege of acting as drivers, on the ground that our personal appearance was a disgrace to the section. In this, I am bound to say, Mr. A. was but sustaining the tradition conceived originally by his predecessor, a Mr. P., a Harvard man, who until his departure from *Vingt-et-Un* succeeded in making life absolutely miserable for B. and myself.

===Chinese===

1917年10月，我的朋友B.（B.）和我，作为美国红十字会（American Red Cross）诺顿-哈耶斯救护队第21卫生分队的志愿司机，六个月的服役期才熬掉将近三个月；就在这个后来被证明值得大书特书的时刻，我们刚干完一件不讨人喜欢的活儿——给分队长大人的私人破旧小汽车（flivver）清洗、上油（用法语说，*nettoyer* 才是恰当的字眼）。这位队长先生行不更名，姑且称之为A先生。借我们伟大总统的招牌腔调来说：完成了这样一项把文明从普鲁士暴政魔爪中拯救出来的重大任务，我们心中想必洋溢着活蹦乱跳的满足感；不幸的是，这份满足感多少打了折扣，因为命运安在我们头上的那位长官与我们之间，完全谈不上半点融洽。或者，用美国人的粗话来说：B.和我跟A先生处不来。在我们这些美国人应当对法国大兵（poilus，我们志愿援助的对象）抱什么态度的问题上，我们与A先生存在根本分歧。A先生的主张是「你们这帮小子离那些脏法国佬远点儿」，还有「咱们到这儿来，就是让这帮混蛋瞧瞧美国人是怎么办事的」；而我们的回应方式，则是抓住一切机会与他们亲善。由于有八名「脏法国佬」以各种身份（厨子、采买、司机、机修工等）编在分队里，而分队本身又隶属于法军的一个分支，亲善起来并不费事。等到A先生看出我们丝毫没有采纳他那些理想的意向，他便（连同充当其翻译的少尉——因为队长大人的法语知识，那可是几年英勇服役攒下来的，主要就包括「*Sar var*」（这行了）、「*Sar marche*」（这能跑）和「*Deet donk moan vieux*」（那就成啦老伙计）三句）把全部精力用来剥夺我们当司机的资格，理由是我们的个人仪表有辱分队的门面。我必须承认，在这一点上，A先生不过是继承了由前任开创的传统——那位前任是位P先生，哈佛出身，直到他离开「二十一分队」（*Vingt-et-Un*）之前，一直成功地让B.和我的日子苦不堪言。

===Original===

Before leaving this painful subject I beg to state that, at least as far as I was concerned, the tradition had a firm foundation in my own predisposition for uncouthness plus what *Le Matin* (if we remember correctly) cleverly nicknamed *La Boue Héroïque*.
Having accomplished the *nettoyage* (at which we were by this time adepts, thanks to Mr. A.’s habit of detailing us to wash any car which its driver and aide might consider too dirty a task for their own hands) we proceeded in search of a little water for personal use. B. speedily finished his ablutions. I was strolling carelessly and solo from the cook-wagon toward one of the two tents﻿—which protestingly housed some forty huddling Americans by night﻿—holding in my hand an historic *morceau de chocolat*, when a spick, not to say span, gentleman in a suspiciously quiet French uniform allowed himself to be driven up to the *bureau*, by two neat soldiers with tin derbies, in a Renault whose painful cleanliness shamed my recent efforts. This must be a general at least, I thought, regretting the extremely undress character of my uniform, which uniform consisted of overalls and a cigarette.
Having furtively watched the gentleman alight and receive a ceremonious welcome from the chief and the aforesaid French lieutenant who accompanied the section for translatory reasons, I hastily betook myself to one of the tents, where I found B. engaged in dragging all his belongings into a central pile of frightening proportions. He was surrounded by a group of fellow-heroes who hailed my coming with considerable enthusiasm. “Your bunky’s leaving” said somebody. “Going to Paris” volunteered a man who had been trying for three months to get there. “Prison you mean” remarked a confirmed optimist whose disposition had felt the effects of French climate.
Albeit confused by the eloquence of B.’s unalterable silence, I immediately associated his present predicament with the advent of the mysterious stranger, and forthwith dashed forth, bent on demanding from one of the tin-derbies the high identity and sacred mission of this personage. I knew that with the exception of ourselves everyone in the section had been given his seven days’ leave﻿—even two men who had arrived later than we and whose turn should, consequently, have come after ours.

===Chinese===

在结束这个沉痛话题之前，请容我声明：至少就我而言，这一传统是有坚实根基的——一来我天生仪容遜遢，二来还有那被《晨报》（*Le Matin*，如果我没记错的话）妙称为「英雄泥泞」（*La Boue Héroïque*）的东西。
清洗工作（*nettoyage*）完成了——多亏A先生有个习惯，凡是司机和助手嫌太脏、不愿亲自动手的车，一律派我们去洗，到这时我们已洗出了大师水准——接着我们便去找点儿水供个人使用。B.三两下就洗漱完毕。我手里拿着一块具有历史意义的巧克力（*morceau de chocolat*），独自一人晃晃悠悠地从炊事车朝两顶帐篷之一走去——夜里，四十来个美国人挤在里头，帐篷都发出了抗议——这时，一位衣着一尘不染（简直可以说光鲜齐整）的先生，穿着一身低调得可疑的法军制服，由两名戴着铁皮圆顶帽、干净利落的士兵护送，乘着一辆雷诺车来到办公处（*bureau*）门前；那辆车干净得令人痛心，把我刚下的功夫都比得黯然失色。这至少得是个将军，我一边想，一边为自己那身极不讲究的制服感到遗憾——这身制服由一条工装裤和一支香烟组成。
我偷偷看着那位先生下车，接受队长和那位因翻译需要而随分队的法国少尉的隆重欢迎，然后赶紧溜到一顶帐篷里，发现B.正忙着把他全部的家当拖到屋子中央，堆成一座规模骇人的小山。他周围围着一群英雄同志，见我来了相当热烈地打招呼。「你的小伙伴要走了」有人说。「去巴黎」一个三个月来一直想去巴黎的人自告奋勇地解释。「你是说去蹲监狱吧」一位不可救药的乐观主义者评论道——法国的气候已经熏陶过他的性情。
B.以不可更改的沉默发表了一通雄辩，把我弄得云里雾里；但我还是立刻把他眼下的处境同那位神秘来客的降临联系在一起，随即冲了出去，打定主意要向其中一顶铁皮帽追问这位大人的显赫身份和神圣使命。我知道，除了我们俩，分队里人人都轮到过七天休假——就连两个比我们晚来、按理应该排在后面的人也轮到了。

===Original===

I also knew that at the headquarters of the Ambulance, *7 rue François Premier*, was Monsieur Norton, the supreme head of the Norton Harjes fraternity, who had known my father in other days. Putting two and two together I decided that this potentate had sent an emissary to Mr. A. to demand an explanation of the various and sundry insults and indignities to which I and my friend had been subjected, and more particularly to secure our long-delayed permission. Accordingly I was in high spirits as I rushed toward the *bureau*.
I didn’t have to go far. The mysterious one, in conversation with *monsieur le sous-lieutenant*, met me halfway. I caught the words: “And Cummings” (the first and last time that my name was correctly pronounced by a Frenchman), “where is he?”
“Present,” I said, giving a salute to which neither of them paid the slightest attention.
“Ah yes” impenetrably remarked the mysterious one in positively sanitary English. “You shall put all your baggage in the car, at once”﻿—then, to tin-derby-the-first, who appeared in an occult manner at his master’s elbow﻿—“Go with him, get his baggage, at once.”
My things were mostly in the vicinity of the *cuisine*, where lodged the *cuisinier, mécanicien, menuisier*, etc., who had made room for me (some ten days since) on their own initiative, thus saving me the humiliation of sleeping with nineteen Americans in a tent which was always two-thirds full of mud. Thither I led the tin-derby, who scrutinised everything with surprising interest. I threw *mes affaires* hastily together (including some minor accessories which I was going to leave behind, but which the t-d bade me include) and emerged with a duffle-bag under one arm and a bedroll under the other, to encounter my excellent friends, the “dirty Frenchmen,” aforesaid. They all popped out together from one door, looking rather astonished. Something by way of explanation as well as farewell was most certainly required, so I made a speech in my best French:
“Gentlemen, friends, comrades﻿—I am going away immediately and shall be guillotined tomorrow.”
—“Oh hardly guillotined I should say,” remarked t-d, in a voice which froze my marrow despite my high spirits; while the cook and carpenter gaped audibly and the mechanician clutched a hopelessly smashed carburetor for support.
One of the section’s *voitures*, a FIAT, was standing ready.

===Chinese===

我还知道，在救护队总部——弗朗索瓦一世街7号——坐着诺顿先生，诺顿-哈耶斯兄弟会的最高首脑，他早年间认识我父亲。据此推理，我断定这位大人物派了使者来找A先生，要他就我和朋友遭受的种种侮辱与轻慢作出解释，尤其是要为我们争取那拖延已久的休假许可。于是我朝办公处飞奔而去，情绪高涨。
不用跑多远。那位神秘人物正与少尉先生（*monsieur le sous-lieutenant*）交谈，半路就迎上了我。我听见几个词：「还有卡明斯」（这是法国人第一次、也是最后一次把我的名字念对），「他在哪儿？」
「到。」我说着敬了个礼，两人谁也没搭理。
「啊，是的。」神秘人物用无比干净利落的英语高深莫测地说。「你把全部行李搬上车，立刻。」接着对一号铁皮帽下令——此人已悄然出现在主人肘边——「跟他去，取行李，立刻。」
我的东西大都在厨房（*cuisine*）附近，那儿住着厨子（*cuisinier*）、机修工（*mécanicien*）、木匠（*menuisier*）等等，他们十天前主动给我腾出了个铺位，使我免于同十九个美国人挤在一顶永远三分之二是泥的帐篷里睡觉的耻辱。
我领着铁皮帽往那儿去，他对一切都以惊人的兴致细细打量。我匆匆把我的东西（*mes affaires*）归拢到一起（包括几样我本打算留下的零星物件，铁皮帽吩咐一并带上），然后一手臂下夹着行李袋，一手臂下夹着铺盖卷走了出来，迎面撞上我那些杰出的朋友——前面说过的「脏法国佬」们。
他们全体从同一扇门里一齐探出来，一脸惊愕。显然有必要讲几句以作解释兼道别，于是我用我最地道的法语发表了演说：
「先生们，朋友们，同志们——我马上就要走了，明天就会被送上断头台。」
——「哦，依我看断头台倒不至于。」铁皮帽说。那嗓音冻得我骨髓发凉，高涨的情绪也没能护住我；厨子和木匠张大了嘴，呵气有声；机修工抓住一个砸得没法看的化油器，靠它撑住身子。
分队的一辆车（*voiture*）——一辆菲亚特——已经备好了。

===Original===

General Nemo sternly forbade me to approach the Renault (in which B.’s baggage was already deposited) and waved me into the FIAT, bed, bedroll and all; whereupon t-d leaped in and seated himself opposite me in a position of perfect unrelaxation, which, despite my aforesaid exultation at quitting the section in general and Mr. A. in particular, impressed me as being almost menacing. Through the front window I saw my friend drive away with t-d Number 2 and Nemo; then, having waved hasty farewell to all *les Américains* that I knew﻿—three in number﻿—and having exchanged affectionate greetings with Mr. A. (who admitted he was very sorry indeed to lose us), I experienced the jolt of the clutch﻿—and we were off in pursuit.
Whatever may have been the forebodings inspired by t-d Number 1’s attitude, they were completely annihilated by the thrilling joy which I experienced on losing sight of the accursed section and its asinine inhabitants﻿—by the indisputable and authentic thrill of going somewhere and nowhere, under the miraculous auspices of someone and no one﻿—of being yanked from the putrescent banalities of an official nonexistence into a high and clear adventure, by a deus ex machina in a grey-blue uniform, and a couple of tin derbies. I whistled and sang and cried to my vis-à-vis: “By the way, who is yonder distinguished gentleman who has been so good as to take my friend and me on this little promenade?”﻿—to which, between lurches of the groaning FIAT, t-d replied awesomely, clutching at the window for the benefit of his equilibrium: “Monsieur le Ministre de Sûreté de Noyon.”
Not in the least realizing what this might mean, I grinned. A responsive grin, visiting informally the tired cheeks of my confrère, ended by frankly connecting his worthy and enormous ears which were squeezed into oblivion by the oversize casque. My eyes, jumping from those ears, lit on that helmet and noticed for the first time an emblem, a sort of flowering little explosion, or hair-switch rampant. It seemed to me very jovial and a little absurd.
“We’re on our way to Noyon, then?”
T-d shrugged his shoulders.
Here the driver’s hat blew off. I heard him swear, and saw the hat sailing in our wake. I jumped to my feet as the FIAT came to a sudden stop, and started for the ground﻿—then checked my flight in midair and landed on the seat, completely astonished.

===Chinese===

尼摩将军严厉禁止我靠近那辆雷诺（B.的行李已经装了进去），挥手让我连人带床、带铺盖卷上菲亚特；铁皮帽随即跳上车，在我对面坐定，全身绷得纹丝不动；尽管我先前说过，能甩掉整个分队、尤其是A先生让我欢欣鼓舞，这副架势仍让我觉得近乎威胁。透过前窗，我看见我的朋友同二号铁皮帽和尼摩一起乘车离去；然后，我向我认识的所有「美国人」（*les Américains*）——共三名——匆匆挥手作别，又与A先生互道了深情厚谊的问候（他承认失去我们实在非常遗憾），接着便体验到离合器的一顿——我们出发追了上去。
不管一号铁皮帽的态度勾起了怎样的不祥预感，这些预感都被狂喜涤荡得干干净净——那个受诅咒的分队和它那群蠢驴似的居民终于从视野里消失了；那是一种货真价实、无可辩驳的战栗：在某个大人物兼无名之辈的神佑之下，驶向某处兼无处；被一位穿灰蓝制服的「解围之神」（*deus ex machina*）外加两顶铁皮帽，从官方「不存在」那腐烂透顶的平庸里猛地拽出，拽进一场高远澄澈的冒险。
我吹着口哨，唱着歌，冲我对面那位喊道：「顺便问一句，那边那位好心地带着我朋友和我出来遛弯儿的显赫绅士是哪位呀？」——对此，铁皮帽在呻吟的菲亚特东倒西歪的间隙里，紧紧抓住车窗以维持平衡，肃然答道：「努瓦永（Noyon）警务大臣先生。」（*Monsieur le Ministre de Sûreté de Noyon*）
我丝毫没意识到这话意味着什么，咧嘴一笑。对面那位也回了一笑，不拘礼节地爬上他疲惫的面颊，最后索性把他那对可敬的大耳朵连成一片——那对耳朵被超大的钢盔压得没了存在感。
我的目光从那对耳朵上跳开，落在那顶钢盔上，第一次注意到一枚徽记——像一朵怒放的小小爆炸，又像一束飞扬的马尾。我觉得它十分喜庆，又有点滑稽。
「那我们这是去努瓦永喽？」
铁皮帽耸了耸肩。
这时司机的帽子被风吹跑了。我听见他咒骂，看见帽子在我们车后飘飞。菲亚特猛地一停，我霍地站起身，正要下车——却在半空刹住了动作，一屁股坐回座位上，惊愕不已。

===Original===

T-d’s revolver, which had hopped from its holster at my first move, slid back into its nest. The owner of the revolver was muttering something rather disagreeable. The driver (being an American of *Vingt-et-Un*) was backing up instead of retrieving his cap in person. My mind felt as if it had been thrown suddenly from fourth into reverse. I pondered and said nothing.
On again﻿—faster, to make up for lost time. On the correct assumption that t-d does not understand English the driver passes the time of day through the minute window:
“For Christ’s sake, Cummings, what’s up?”
“You got me,” I said, laughing at the delicate naivete of the question.
“Did y’ do something to get pinched?”
“Probably,” I answered importantly and vaguely, feeling a new dignity.
“Well, if you didn’t, maybe B﻿—﻿— did.”
“Maybe,” I countered, trying not to appear enthusiastic. As a matter of fact I was never so excited and proud. I was, to be sure, a criminal! Well, well, thank God that settled one question for good and all﻿—no more *Section Sanitaire* for me! No more Mr. A. and his daily lectures on cleanliness, deportment, etc.! In spite of myself I started to sing. The driver interrupted:
“I heard you asking the tin lid something in French. Whadhesay?”
“Said that gink in the Renault is the head cop of Noyon,” I answered at random.
“**Goodnight.** Maybe we’d better ring off, or you’ll get in wrong with”﻿—he indicated t-d with a wave of his head that communicated itself to the car in a magnificent skid; and t-d’s derby rang out as the skid pitched t-d the length of the FIAT.
“You rang the bell then,” I commented﻿—then to t-d: “Nice car for the wounded to ride in,” I politely observed. T-d answered nothing.﻿ ﻿…
Noyon.
We drive straight up to something which looks unpleasantly like a feudal dungeon. The driver is now told to be somewhere at a certain time, and meanwhile to eat with the Head Cop, who may be found just around the corner﻿—(I am doing the translating for t-d)﻿—and, oh yes, it seems that the Head Cop has particularly requested the pleasure of this distinguished American’s company at déjeuner.
“Does he mean me?” the driver asked innocently.
“Sure,” I told him.
Nothing is said of B. or me.
Now, cautiously, t-d first and I a slow next, we descend.

===Chinese===

铁皮帽的左轮手枪——在我刚一动时已从枪套里跳了出来——滑回了它的巢。手枪的主人正嘟哝着几句相当不客气的话。司机（「二十一分队」的美国人）没亲自下车去捡帽子，反而在倒车。我的脑子仿佛猛地从四挡被摘到了倒挡。我思忖着，一言不发。
重新上路——更快了，把耽误的时间补回来。司机基于正确的判断——铁皮帽不懂英语——透过那扇一丁点大的车窗搭起话来：
「看在基督份上，卡明斯，出什么事儿了？」
「你可问着我了。」我说，被这问题天真的精妙逗笑了。
「你是不是干了什么事儿，被条子盯上了？」
「多半是。」我煞有介事又含含糊糊地答道，感到一种新的尊严。
「呃，你要是没有，那没准儿是B——干了。」
「没准儿。」我回敬道，竭力不显得兴高采烈。事实上，我从来没有这么兴奋、这么骄傲过。千真万确，我是个罪犯了！好啊，好啊，感谢上帝，这个问题一劳永逸地解决了——我再也不用待在卫生分队（*Section Sanitaire*）了！再也不用听A先生和他那关于清洁、仪容等等的每日训诫了！我不由自主地唱起歌来。司机打断了我：
「我听见你用法语跟那铁皮盖打听什么来着。他咋说的？」
「说雷诺车里那家伙是努瓦永的警察头子。」我随口答道。
「**天哪。**没准儿咱俩最好闭嘴，不然你要得罪……」——他朝铁皮帽一摆头示意，那动作传到车上，化成一次漂亮的大甩尾；铁皮帽的圆顶帽铛啷一声，人被甩出菲亚特整个车厢那么远。
「你这一下可敲响了钟。」我评论道——接着又对铁皮帽说：「好车，运伤员正合适。」我客气地评价道。铁皮帽一言不发。…
努瓦永。
我们径直开到一座建筑跟前，它长得像封建时代的地牢，令人不快。有人吩咐司机在几点钟到某处报到，在此期间去和警察头子一起吃饭，警察头子就在拐角那儿——（这通翻译是我在替铁皮帽效劳）——哦对了，警察头子似乎特意请求这位杰出的美国人赏光共进午餐（*déjeuner*）。
「他说的是我吗？」司机天真地问。
「当然。」我告诉他。
至于B.和我，则无人提起。
现在，小心翼翼地，铁皮帽先下，我慢半拍跟上，我们下了车。

===Original===

The FIAT rumbles off, with the distinguished one’s backward-glaring head poked out a yard more or less and that distinguished face so completely surrendered to mystification as to cause a large laugh on my part.
“You are hungry?”
It was the erstwhile-ferocious speaking. A criminal, I remembered, is somebody against whom everything he says and does is very cleverly made use of. After weighing the matter in my mind for some moments I decided at all cost to tell the truth, and replied:
“I could eat an elephant.”
Hereupon t-d led me to the Kitchen Itself, set me to eat upon a stool, and admonished the cook in a fierce voice:
“Give this great criminal something to eat in the name of the French Republic!”
And for the first time in three months I tasted Food.
T-d seated himself beside me, opened a huge jackknife, and fell to, after first removing his tin derby and loosening his belt.
One of the pleasantest memories connected with that irrevocable meal is of a large, gentle, strong woman who entered in a hurry, and seeing me cried out:
“What is it?”
“It’s an American, my mother,” t-d answered through fried potatoes.
“Why is he here?” the woman touched me on the shoulder, and satisfied herself that I was real.
“The good God is doubtless acquainted with the explanation,” said t-d pleasantly. “Not myself being the﻿—”
“Ah, *mon pauvre*” said this very beautiful sort of woman. “You are going to be a prisoner here. Every one of the prisoners has a *marraine*, do you understand? I am their *marraine*. I love them and look after them. Well, listen: I will be your *marraine*, too.”
I bowed and looked around for something to pledge her in. T-d was watching. My eyes fell on a huge glass of red pinard. “Yes, drink,” said my captor, with a smile. I raised my huge glass.
“*A la santé de ma marraine charmante!*”
—This deed of gallantry quite won the cook (a smallish, agile Frenchman) who shovelled several helps of potatoes on my already empty plate. The tin derby approved also: “That’s right, eat, drink, you’ll need it later perhaps.” And his knife guillotined another delicious hunk of white bread.
At last, sated with luxuries, I bade adieu to my *marraine* and allowed t-d to conduct me (I going first, as always) upstairs and into a little den whose interior boasted two mattresses, a man sitting at the table, and a newspaper in the hands of the man.

===Chinese===

菲亚特轰隆隆开走了，那位杰出人士的脑袋伸出车外约莫一码长，向后怒目而视；那张杰出的脸彻底交给了困惑，惹得我放声大笑。
「你饿吗？」
说话的是先前那位凶神。我记得，所谓罪犯，就是他说的每句话、做的每件事都会被人巧妙利用的人。我在心里掂量了片刻，决定不惜一切代价说实话，便答道：
「我能吃下一头大象。」
于是铁皮帽把我领进厨房本部，安置我坐在一只凳子上吃，又用凶狠的声音告诫厨子：
「以法兰西共和国的名义，给这个大罪犯弄点吃的！」
三个月来，我第一次尝到了食物（Food，大写的）。
铁皮帽在我旁边坐下，打开一把大折刀，先摘掉铁皮圆顶帽、松开腰带，然后大吃起来。
关于那顿不可挽回的饭食，最愉快的记忆之一是一位高大、温柔而有力的妇人，她急匆匆走进来，一看见我就喊：
「这是怎么回事？」
「是个美国人，妈妈。」铁皮帽隔着满嘴煎土豆答道。
「他为什么在这儿？」妇人碰了碰我的肩膀，确信我是真人。
「个中缘由，想必仁慈的上帝是清楚的。」铁皮帽和颜悦色地说，「在下可不——」
「啊，我可怜的孩子（*mon pauvre*）。」这位非常美丽的那类妇人说。「你要在这里当囚犯了。每个囚犯都有一位教母（*marraine*），你明白吗？我就是她们的教母。我爱她们，照顾她们。听着，我也要做你的教母。」
我鞠了一躬，四下张望，想找点什么向她敬献。铁皮帽正盯着。我的目光落在一大杯红酒（*pinard*）上。「喝吧。」我的捕获者微笑着说。我举起大杯。
「为我迷人的教母的健康干杯！」（*A la santé de ma marraine charmante!*）
——这一豪侠之举彻底赢得了厨子（一个身材矮小、手脚麻利的法国人）的心，他往我早已空了的盘子里又铲了几勺土豆。铁皮帽也表赞同：「这就对了，吃吧，喝吧，往后兴许用得着。」他的刀又斩下一段美味白面包，执行了一次断头。
终于，珍馐餍足之后，我向教母道了别，任由铁皮帽（照例我走在前面）领我上楼，进了一间小窝：室内陈设颇丰，有两副床垫、一个坐在桌旁的人，以及那人手里的一份报纸。

===Original===

“*C’est un Américain*,” t-d said by way of introduction. The newspaper detached itself from the man who said: “He’s welcome indeed: make yourself at home, Mr. American”﻿—and bowed himself out. My captor immediately collapsed on one mattress.
I asked permission to do the same on the other, which favor was sleepily granted. With half-shut eyes my Ego lay and pondered: the delicious meal it had just enjoyed; what was to come; the joys of being a great criminal﻿ ﻿… then, being not at all inclined to sleep, I read *Le Petit Parisien* quite through, even to *Les Voies Urinaires.*
Which reminded me﻿—and I woke up t-d and asked: “May I visit the *vespasienne*?”
“Downstairs,” he replied fuzzily, and readjusted his slumbers.
There was no one moving about in the little court. I lingered somewhat on the way upstairs. The stairs were abnormally dirty. When I reentered, t-d was roaring to himself. I read the journal through again. It must have been about three o’clock.
Suddenly t-d woke up, straightened and buckled his personality, and murmured: “It’s time, come on.”
*Le bureau de Monsieur le Ministre* was just around the corner, as it proved. Before the door stood the patient FIAT. I was ceremoniously informed by t-d that we would wait on the steps.
Well! Did I know any more?﻿—the American driver wanted to know.
Having proved to my own satisfaction that my fingers could still roll a pretty good cigarette, I answered: “No,” between puffs.
The American drew nearer and whispered spectacularly: “Your friend is upstairs. I think they’re examining him.”
T-d got this; and though his rehabilitated dignity had accepted the “makin’s” from its prisoner, it became immediately incensed:
“That’s enough,” he said sternly.
And dragged me *tout-à-coup* upstairs, where I met B. and his t-d coming out of the *bureau* door. B. looked peculiarly cheerful. “I think we’re going to prison all right,” he assured me.
Braced by this news, poked from behind by my t-d, and waved on from before by M. le Ministre himself, I floated vaguely into a very washed, neat, businesslike and altogether American room of modest proportions, whose door was immediately shut and guarded on the inside by my escort.
Monsieur le Ministre said:
“Lift your arms.”
Then he went through my pockets. He found cigarettes, pencils, a jackknife and several francs.

===Chinese===

「这是个美国人。」（*C'est un Américain*）铁皮帽权作介绍。报纸从那人身上脱离开来，那人说：「欢迎欢迎：请自便，美国人先生。」——说罢躬身退出。我的捕获者立刻瘡倒在一张床垫上。
我请求允许自己在另一张床垫上照办，这一恩准在睡意朦眬中获得了。我的自我半阖着眼躺着，反复思量：刚享用过的美餐；接下来会怎样；当一个大罪犯的种种乐事……然后，因为丝毫没有睡意，我把《小巴黎人报》（*Le Petit Parisien*）从头到尾读了个遍，连「尿路」（*Les Voies Urinaires*）专栏都没落下。
这倒提醒了我——我把铁皮帽叫醒，问道：「我可以去一趟公共小便池（*vespasienne*）吗？」
「楼下。」他含含糊糊地答了一句，重新调整好睡姿。
小院里一个人影也没有。上楼时我在楼梯上磨蹭了一会儿。楼梯脏得出奇。等我再进门，铁皮帽正独自打着呼噜。我把报纸又通读了一遍。想必是三点钟左右了。
铁皮帽突然醒了，把他的个性抖擞整齐、扣好，喃喃道：「到点了，走吧。」
大臣先生的办公室（*Le bureau de Monsieur le Ministre*）果然就在拐角上。门前停着那辆任劳任怨的菲亚特。铁皮帽郑重其事地通知我，我们就在台阶上等。
怎么样！又知道点什么了吗？——美国司机想知道。
我先向自己满意地证明了手指头还能卷出一支像样的香烟，然后喷云吐雾地答道：「没了。」
美国人凑近了些，煞有介事地耳语：「你的朋友在楼上。我看他们正在审他。」
铁皮帽听见了；虽说他那刚恢复起来的尊严曾接受过囚犯敬的烟丝，此刻还是立刻勃然大怒：
「够了。」他严厉地说。
说着冷不防（*tout-à-coup*）把我拖上楼，正碰上B.和他的铁皮帽从办公室门口出来。B.看上去格外快活。「我看咱们进监狱是十拿九稳了。」他向我保证。
这消息令我精神一振；后面我的铁皮帽捅着，前面大臣先生本人挥手放行，我恍恍惚惚地飘进一间洗刷得干干净净、整洁利落、公事公办、彻头彻尾美国做派的小房间；房门随即关上，由我的押送者在里面把守。
大臣先生说：
「举起手臂。」
然后他搜了我的口袋。他搜出了香烟、铅笔、一把折刀和几枚法郎。

===Original===

He laid his treasures on a clean table and said: “You are not allowed to keep these. I shall be responsible.” Then he looked me coldly in the eye and asked if I had anything else?
I told him that I believed I had a handkerchief.
He asked me: “Have you anything in your shoes?”
“My feet,” I said, gently.
“Come this way,” he said frigidly, opening a door which I had not remarked. I bowed in acknowledgment of the courtesy, and entered room number 2.
I looked into six eyes which sat at a desk.
Two belonged to a lawyerish person in civilian clothes, with a bored expression, plus a moustache of dreamy proportions with which the owner constantly imitated a gentleman ringing for a drink. Two appertained to a splendid old dotard (a face all ski-jumps and toboggan slides), on whose protruding chest the rosette of the Legion pompously squatted. Numbers five and six had reference to Monsieur, who had seated himself before I had time to focus my slightly bewildered eyes.
Monsieur spoke sanitary English, as I have said.
“What is your name?”﻿—“Edward E. Cummings.”
—“Your second name?”﻿—“E-s-t-l-i-n,” I spelled it for him.﻿—“How do you say that?”﻿—I didn’t understand.﻿—“How do you say your name?”﻿—“Oh,” I said; and pronounced it. He explained in French to the moustache that my first name was Edouard, my second “A-s-tay-l-ee-n,” and my third “Kay-u-mm-ee-n-gay-s”﻿—and the moustache wrote it all down. Monsieur then turned to me once more:
“You are Irish?”﻿—“No,” I said, “American.”﻿—“You are Irish by family?”﻿—“No, Scotch.”﻿—“You are sure that there was never an Irishman in your parents?”﻿—“So far as I know,” I said, “there never was an Irishman there.”﻿—“Perhaps a hundred years back?” he insisted﻿—“Not a chance,” I said decisively. But Monsieur was not to be denied: “Your name it is Irish?”﻿—“Cummings is a very old Scotch name,” I told him fluently, “it used to be Comyn. A Scotchman named The Red Comyn was killed by Robert Bruce in a church. He was my ancestor and a very well-known man.”﻿—“But your second name, where have you got that?”﻿—“From an Englishman, a friend of my father.” This statement seemed to produce a very favorable impression in the case of the rosette, who murmured: “*Un ami de son père, un Anglais, bon!*” several times.

===Chinese===

他把这些宝贝摊在一张干净的桌子上，说：「这些不许你留着。由我负责保管。」然后他冷冷地盯着我的眼睛，问我还有没有别的东西？
我告诉他，我记得还有一块手帕。
他问我：「你鞋里藏东西了吗？」
「只有我的脚。」我温文尔雅地说。
「这边来。」他冷冰冰地说，打开一扇我先前没留意的门。我鞠躬致谢这番好意，走进了第二号房间。
我望进六只眼睛，它们端坐在一张书桌后。
其中两只属于一个讼师模样、身着便装的人物，面带倦意，外加一撇意境悠远的小胡子——主人不住地捻它，模仿一位摇铃叫酒的绅士。另两只属于一位气派的老糊涂（一张脸全是跳台滑雪道和雪橇滑道），那枚荣誉军团的玫瑰形徽记就神气活现地蹲在他凸出的胸脯上。第五只和第六只属于大臣先生，我还没来得及对焦那双略带迷惘的眼睛，他已自行落座。
正如我说过的，大臣先生讲一口干净利落的英语。
「你叫什么名字？」——「爱德华·E·卡明斯。」
——「你的第二个名字？」——「E-s-t-l-i-n。」我替他拼写了一遍。——「怎么念？」——我没听懂。——「你的名字怎么念？」——「哦。」我说着念了出来。他用法语向小胡子解释：我的第一个名字是爱德华（Edouard），第二个是「A-s-tay-l-ee-n」，第三个是「Kay-u-mm-ee-n-gay-s」——小胡子一一记下。大臣先生随后又转向我：
「你是爱尔兰人？」——「不是。」我说，「是美国人。」——「你家祖上是爱尔兰人？」——「不是，是苏格兰人。」——「你能肯定你父母双方从来没有爱尔兰人吗？」——「据我所知，」我说，「从来没有。」——「也许一百年前有？」他坚持道。——「绝无可能。」我斩钉截铁地说。但大臣先生不肯罢休：「你的名字是爱尔兰式的？」——「卡明斯是一个非常古老的苏格兰姓氏。」我滔滔不绝地告诉他，「从前拼作科明（Comyn）。一个绰号『红科明』的苏格兰人在教堂里被罗伯特·布鲁斯杀了。他是我的祖先，一位很有名的人物。」——「可你的第二个名字是哪儿来的？」——「从一个英国人那儿，我父亲的朋友。」这话似乎给玫瑰徽记留下了极好的印象，他喃喃了好几遍：「他父亲的朋友，英国人，好！」（*Un ami de son père, un Anglais, bon!*）

===Original===

Monsieur, quite evidently disappointed, told the moustache in French to write down that I denied my Irish parentage; which the moustache did.
“What does your father in America?”﻿—“He is a minister of the gospel,” I answered. “Which church?”﻿—“Unitarian.” This puzzled him. After a moment he had an inspiration: “That is the same as a Free Thinker?”﻿—I explained in French that it wasn’t and that *mon père* was a holy man. At last Monsieur told the moustache to write: Protestant; and the moustache obediently did so.
From this point on our conversation was carried on in French, somewhat to the chagrin of Monsieur, but to the joy of the rosette and with the approval of the moustache. In answer to questions, I informed them that I was a student for five years at Harvard (expressing great surprise that they had never heard of Harvard), that I had come to New York and studied painting, that I had enlisted in New York as *conducteur volontaire*, embarking for France shortly after, about the middle of April.
Monsieur asked: “You met B﻿—﻿— on the *paquebot*?” I said I did.
Monsieur glanced significantly around. The rosette nodded a number of times. The moustache rang.
I understood that these kind people were planning to make me out the innocent victim of a wily villain, and could not forbear a smile. *C’est rigolo*, I said to myself; they’ll have a great time doing it.
“You and your friend were together in Paris?” I said “yes.” “How long?” “A month, while we were waiting for our uniforms.”
A significant look by Monsieur, which is echoed by his confrères.
Leaning forward Monsieur asked coldly and carefully: “What did you do in Paris?” to which I responded briefly and warmly: “We had a good time.”
This reply pleased the rosette hugely. He wagged his head till I thought it would have tumbled off. Even the mustache seemed amused. Monsieur le Ministre de la Sûreté de Noyon bit his lip. “Never mind writing that down,” he directed the lawyer. Then, returning to the charge:
“You had a great deal of trouble with Lieutenant A.?”
I laughed outright at this complimentary nomenclature. “Yes, we certainly did.”
He asked: “Why?”﻿—so I sketched “Lieutenant” A. in vivid terms, making use of certain choice expressions with which one of the “dirty Frenchmen” attached to the section, a Parisien, master of argot, had furnished me.

===Chinese===

大臣先生显然相当失望，用法语吩咐小胡子记下：我否认自己的爱尔兰血统；小胡子照办了。
「你父亲在美国做什么？」——「他是一位福音牧师。」我答道。「哪个教会？」——「一位论派。」这把他难住了。过了一会儿他灵光一现：「那和自由思想家是一回事吗？」——我用法语解释不是一回事，而且家父（*mon père*）是位圣洁之人。最后大臣先生让小胡子记下：新教徒；小胡子恭顺地照办了。
从这时起，我们的对话改用法语进行，这让大臣先生颇为懊恼，却让玫瑰徽记喜出望外，小胡子亦表赞同。在回答提问时，我告诉他们：我在哈佛读了五年书（对他们竟然从未听说过哈佛深表惊讶），之后到纽约学画，在纽约报名当了志愿司机（*conducteur volontaire*），大约四月中旬随后启程来法。
大臣先生问：「你是在邮船（*paquebot*）上认识B——的？」我说是在。
大臣先生意味深长地环顾左右。玫瑰徽记点了好几下头。小胡子摇了铃。
我明白了，这几位好人心底里打的算盘，是要把我描绘成一个奸诈恶棍手下天真的受害者，我忍不住笑了。真有意思（*C'est rigolo*），我对自己说；他们这么干会其乐无穷的。
「你和你朋友在巴黎待在一起？」我说「是」。「多久？」「一个月，等制服的那阵子。」
大臣先生意味深长地看了一眼，同僚们纷纷呼应。
大臣先生身体前倾，冷峻而审慎地问：「你们在巴黎干了些什么？」对此我简短而热忱地答道：「我们过得很快活。」
这个回答让玫瑰徽记乐不可支。他摇头晃脑，晃得我都担心脑袋会掉下来。连小胡子似乎也被逗乐了。努瓦永警务大臣先生咬了咬嘴唇。「这句不必记。」他指示那位法律界人士。然后重新发动攻势：
「你和A少尉闹得很不愉快？」
听到这个恭维性的称谓，我放声大笑。「对，确实不愉快。」
他问：「为什么？」——于是我用语重心长的生动字眼勾画了一通「A少尉」，用词精选自我从分队里一名「脏法国佬」——巴黎人、黑话大师——那里学来的表达。

===Original===

My phraseology surprised my examiners, one of whom (I think the moustache) observed sarcastically that I had made good use of my time in Paris.
Monsieur le Ministre asked: Was it true (a) that B. and I were always together and (b) preferred the company of the attached Frenchmen to that of our fellow-Americans?﻿—to which I answered in the affirmative. Why? he wanted to know. So I explained that we felt that the more French we knew and the better we knew the French the better for us; expatiating a bit on the necessity for a complete mutual understanding of the Latin and Anglo-Saxon races if victory was to be won.
Again the rosette nodded with approbation.
Monsieur le Ministre may have felt that he was losing his case, for he played his trump card immediately: “You are aware that your friend has written to friends in America and to his family very bad letters.” “I am not,” I said.
In a flash I understood the motivation of Monsieur’s visit to *Vingt-et-Un*: the French censor had intercepted some of B.’s letters, and had notified Mr. A. and Mr. A.’s translator, both of whom had thankfully testified to the bad character of B. and (wishing very naturally to get rid of both of us at once) had further averred that we were always together and that consequently I might properly be regarded as a suspicious character. Whereupon they had received instructions to hold us at the section until Noyon could arrive and take charge﻿—hence our failure to obtain our long-overdue permission.
“Your friend,” said Monsieur in English, “is here a short while ago. I ask him if he is up in the aeroplane flying over Germans will he drop the bombs on Germans and he say no, he will not drop any bombs on Germans.”
By this falsehood (such it happened to be) I confess that I was nonplussed. In the first place, I was at the time innocent of third-degree methods. Secondly, I remembered that, a week or so since, B.

===Chinese===

我的用词令几位审讯官大吃一惊，其中一位（我想是小胡子）讥讽地评了一句：我在巴黎的时间没有虚度。
大臣先生问：(a) 我和B.总是形影不离，(b) 相比美国同胞，我们更愿意和分队里的法国人做伴——是否属实？对此我作了肯定回答。为什么？他想知道。于是我解释说，我们觉得认识的法国人越多、对法国人了解越深，对我们越有利；还就拉丁种族与盎格鲁-撒克逊种族必须彻底相互理解方能赢得胜利这一必要性略作铺陈。
玫瑰徽记又点头赞许。
大臣先生也许感到自己的案子要输，便立刻打出了王牌：「你知道吧，你朋友给美国的朋友和家人写了一些非常恶劣的信。」「我不知道。」我说。
电光石火间，我明白了大臣先生驾临「二十一分队」的动机：法国检查官截获了B.的几封信，通知了A先生和A先生的翻译；两人感激涕零地证明了B.品行恶劣，并且（非常自然地想把我们一起打发掉）进一步声称我们总是形影不离，因此我大可以被视作嫌疑分子。于是他们奉命把我们扣在分队，直到努瓦永来人接管——这就是我们迟迟拿不到早该到手的休假许可的缘故。
「你的朋友，」大臣先生用英语说，「刚才在这里。我问他：如果你坐飞机飞到德国人上头，你会不会朝德国人扔炸弹？他说不，他不朝任何德国人扔炸弹。」
我承认，这个谎言（它恰好就是个谎言）让我方寸大乱。第一，我当时对疲劳审讯那一套还一无所知。第二，我想起来，大约一星期前，B.

===Original===

, myself and another American in the section had written a letter﻿—which, on the advice of the *sous-lieutenant* who accompanied *Vingt-et-Un* as translator, we had addressed to the Undersecretary of State in French Aviation﻿—asking that inasmuch as the American Government was about to take over the Red Cross (which meant that all the Sanitary Sections would be affiliated with the American, and no longer with the French, Army) we three at any rate might be allowed to continue our association with the French by enlisting in l’Esquadrille Lafayette. One of the “dirty Frenchmen” had written the letter for us in the finest language imaginable, from data supplied by ourselves.
“You write a letter, your friend and you, for French aviation?”
Here I corrected him: there were three of us; and why didn’t he have the third culprit arrested, might I ask? But he ignored this little digression, and wanted to know: Why not American aviation?﻿—to which I answered: “Ah, but as my friend has so often said to me, the French are after all the finest people in the world.”
This double-blow stopped Noyon dead, but only for a second.
“Did your friend write this letter?”﻿—“No,” I answered truthfully.﻿—“Who did write it?”﻿—“One of the Frenchmen attached to the section.”﻿—“What is his name?”﻿—“I’m sure I don’t know,” I answered; mentally swearing that, whatever might happen to me the scribe should not suffer. “At my urgent request,” I added.
Relapsing into French, Monsieur asked me if I would have any hesitation in dropping bombs on Germans? I said no, I wouldn’t. And why did I suppose I was fitted to become aviator? Because, I told him, I weighed 135 pounds and could drive any kind of auto or motorcycle. (I hoped he would make me prove this assertion, in which case I promised myself that I wouldn’t stop till I got to Munich; but no.)
“Do you mean to say that my friend was not only trying to avoid serving in the American Army but was contemplating treason as well?” I asked.
“Well, that would be it, would it not?” he answered coolly. Then, leaning forward once more, he fired at me: “Why did you write to an official so high?”
At this I laughed outright. “Because the excellent *sous-lieutenant* who translated when Mr. Lieutenant A. couldn’t understand advised us to do so.”

===Chinese===

连同我、以及分队里另一个美国人，曾合写过一封信——按照「二十一分队」随队翻译少尉的建议，信是写给法国航空部副国务秘书的——信中说，鉴于美国政府即将接管红十字会（这意味着所有卫生分队将改隶美军序列而非法军），恳请至少让我们三人得以通过加入拉法耶特飞行中队（*l'Esquadrille Lafayette*）继续与法国人共事。信是由一名「脏法国佬」根据我们提供的材料、用想象得到的最优美的文笔代写的。
「你和你朋友写了一封信，为的是加入法国航空队？」
这里我纠正了他：我们一共三个人；请问为什么不把第三个案犯也抓来？但他对这个小小岔题置之不理，只想知道：为什么不加入美国航空队？——对此我答道：「啊，正像我朋友常对我说的，法国人毕竟是世界上最优秀的人。」
这记双重打击把努瓦永打得当场哑火，不过只哑了一秒。
「这信是你朋友写的吗？」——「不是。」我如实回答。——「那是谁写的？」——「分队里的一名法国人。」——「他叫什么名字？」——「我还真不知道。」我答道，心里发誓无论我遭什么罪，也绝不能让那位代笔的受连累。「是我再三恳求他写的。」我补充道。
大臣先生重新改说法语，问我朝德国人扔炸弹会不会有半点犹豫？我说不会。那我凭什么觉得自己够格当飞行员？因为，我告诉他，我体重135磅，而且什么汽车摩托车都会开。（我巴不得他叫我去验证这个说法，那样的话我给自己许了愿：不一口气开到慕尼黑决不停车；可惜没有。）
「您这不是等于说，我朋友不但企图逃避在美国军队服役，还图谋叛国吗？」我问道。
「嗯，就是这么回事，不是吗？」他冷静地答道。接着又前倾身子，向我发难：「你们为什么给这么大的官写信？」
对此我放声大笑。「因为A少尉大人听不懂的时候，替他翻译的那位好心的少尉建议我们这么写。」

===Original===

Following up this sortie, I addressed the mustache: “Write this down in the testimony﻿—that I, here present, refuse utterly to believe that my friend is not as sincere a lover of France and the French people as any man living!﻿—Tell him to write it,” I commanded Noyon stonily. But Noyon shook his head, saying: “We have the very best reason for supposing your friend to be no friend of France.” I answered: “That is not my affair. I want my opinion of my friend written in; do you see?” “That’s reasonable,” the rosette murmured; and the moustache wrote it down.
“Why do you think we volunteered?” I asked sarcastically, when the testimony was complete.
Monsieur le Ministre was evidently rather uncomfortable. He writhed a little in his chair, and tweaked his chin three or four times. The rosette and the moustache were exchanging animated phrases. At last Noyon, motioning for silence and speaking in an almost desperate tone, demanded:
“*Est-ce-que vous détestez les boches?*”
I had won my own case. The question was purely perfunctory. To walk out of the room a free man I had merely to say yes. My examiners were sure of my answer. The rosette was leaning forward and smiling encouragingly. The moustache was making little *ouis* in the air with his pen. And Noyon had given up all hope of making me out a criminal. I might be rash, but I was innocent; the dupe of a superior and malign intelligence. I would probably be admonished to choose my friends more carefully next time and that would be all.﻿ ﻿…
Deliberately, I framed the answer:
“*Non. J’aime beaucoup les français.*”
Agile as a weasel, Monsieur le Ministre was on top of me: “It is impossible to love Frenchmen and not to hate Germans.”
I did not mind his triumph in the least. The discomfiture of the rosette merely amused me. The surprise of the moustache I found very pleasant.
Poor rosette! He kept murmuring desperately: “Fond of his friend, quite right. Mistaken of course, too bad, meant well.”
With a supremely disagreeable expression on his immaculate face the victorious minister of security pressed his victim with regained assurance: “But you are doubtless aware of the atrocities committed by the boches?”
“I have read about them,” I replied very cheerfully.
“You do not believe?”
“*Ça se peut.*”
“And if they are so, which of course they are” (tone of profound conviction) “you do not detest the Germans?”

===Chinese===

乘着这次突袭得手，我冲小胡子发话：「把这句记进笔录——我，在场本人，绝不相信我的朋友对法兰西和法国人民的爱不如世上任何人真诚！——让他记下来。」我冷硬地命令努瓦永。但努瓦永摇头道：「我们有十足的理由认定，你的朋友绝非法兰西之友。」我回答：「那不关我的事。我要把我对朋友的看法记进去；明白吗？」「有道理。」玫瑰徽记喃喃道；小胡子于是记下了。
笔录完毕后，我讥讽地问：「那您觉得我们为什么来当志愿兵？」
大臣先生显然颇不自在。他在椅子里扭了扭，捏了三四回下巴。玫瑰徽记和小胡子正你来我往地说着热闹话。最后，努瓦永示意肃静，用近乎绝望的语气逼问：
「你们恨德国佬吗？」（*Est-ce-que vous détestez les boches?*）
我自己的案子已经赢了。这问题纯属走过场。只要答一声「恨」，我就能以自由之身走出这个房间。几位审讯官对我的回答十拿九稳。玫瑰徽记正探着身子，笑吟吟地鼓励我。小胡子正用钢笔在空中描着一个个小小的「是」（*oui*）。努瓦永则已彻底断了把我办成罪犯的念想。我或许鲁莽，但清白无辜；不过是某个高明而邪恶的智力的应声虫罢了。多半会训诫我下回交友谨慎些，也就到此为止了。…
我不慌不忙，字斟句酌地组好了答案：
「不恨（*Non*）。我非常爱法国人（*J'aime beaucoup les français*）。」
大臣先生像黄鼠狼一样敏捷，一下子扑到我身上：「爱法国人而不恨德国人，是不可能的。」
他的得势我毫不介意。玫瑰徽记的狼狈只让我觉得好玩。小胡子的惊讶更令我心情舒畅。
可怜的玫瑰徽记！他绝望地喃喃个不停：「疼他的朋友，没错。当然是弄错了，可惜，用意是好的。」
得胜的警务大臣先生那张一尘不染的脸上摆出极致嫌恶的表情，带着重拾的自信向他的猎物施压：「不过，德国佬犯下的种种暴行，你总该知道吧？」
「读到过。」我非常爽快地答道。
「你不相信？」
「那也可能。」（*Ça se peut.*）
「倘若果真如此——当然它们是事实」（语气深信不疑）「你也不恨德国人？」

===Original===

“Oh, in that case, of course anyone must detest them,” I averred with perfect politeness.
And my case was lost, forever lost. I breathed freely once more. All my nervousness was gone. The attempt of the three gentlemen sitting before me to endow my friend and myself with different fates had irrevocably failed.
At the conclusion of a short conference I was told by Monsieur:
“I am sorry for you, but due to your friend you will be detained a little while.”
I asked: “Several weeks?”
“Possibly,” said Monsieur.
This concluded the trial.
Monsieur le Ministre conducted me into room number 1 again. “Since I have taken your cigarettes and shall keep them for you, I will give you some tobacco. Do you prefer English or French?”
Because the French (*paquet bleu*) are stronger and because he expected me to say English, I said “French.”
With a sorrowful expression Noyon went to a sort of bookcase and took down a blue packet. I think I asked for matches, or else he had given back the few which he found on my person.
Noyon, t-d and the grand criminal (alias I) now descended solemnly to the FIAT. The more and more mystified *conducteur* conveyed us a short distance to what was obviously a prison-yard. Monsieur le Ministre watched me descend my voluminous baggage.
This was carefully examined by Monsieur at the *bureau*, of the prison. Monsieur made me turn everything topsy-turvy and inside out. Monsieur expressed great surprise at a huge shell: where did I get it?﻿—I said a French soldier gave it to me as a souvenir.﻿—And several *têtes d’obus*?﻿—also souvenirs, I assured him merrily. Did Monsieur suppose I was caught in the act of blowing up the French Government, or what exactly?﻿—But here are a dozen sketchbooks, what is in them?﻿—Oh, Monsieur, you flatter me: drawings.﻿—Of fortifications? Hardly; of poilus, children, and other ruins.﻿—Ummmm. (Monsieur examined the drawings and found that I had spoken the truth.) Monsieur puts all these trifles into a small bag, with which I had been furnished (in addition to the huge duffle-bag) by the generous Red Cross. Labels them (in French): “Articles found in the baggage of Cummings and deemed *inutile* to the case at hand.” This leaves in the duffle-bag aforesaid: my fur coat, which I brought from New York; my bed and blankets and bedroll, my civilian clothes, and about twenty-five pounds of soiled linen.

===Chinese===

「哦，那种情形之下，任何人自然都得恨他们。」我彬彬有礼地断言。
就这样，我的案子输了，永远地输了。我重新畅快地呼吸。所有的紧张都消失殆尽。坐在我面前三位先生试图赐予我朋友和我两种不同命运的图谋，无可挽回地破产了。
短暂合议之后，大臣先生向我宣布：
「我为你惋惜，但看在你朋友的份上，你将被羁押一小段时间。」
我问：「好几个星期？」
「也许吧。」大臣先生说。
审判到此结束。
大臣先生把我重新领回第一号房间。「既然你的香烟被我收走、由我替你保管，我送你些烟草。你要英国的还是法国的？」
因为法国的（蓝包装，*paquet bleu*）劲儿更大，也因为他料我会说英国烟，我说：「法国的。」
努瓦永面带戚容，走到一个类似书柜的跟前，取下一包蓝色装的。我记得我讨了火柴，要不然就是他把从我身上搜出的几根还给了我。
努瓦永、铁皮帽和大罪犯（即本人）此刻庄严地下楼走向菲亚特。那位越来越摸不着头脑的司机（*conducteur*）载着我们驶了一小段路，来到一个一看便知是监狱大院的地方。大臣先生看着我卸下我那一大堆行李。
行李在监狱的办公处由大臣先生仔细查验。大臣先生命我把每样东西翻个底朝天、里外倒置。大臣先生对一枚巨大的炮弹表示了极大的惊讶：哪儿弄来的？——我说是一个法国士兵送我做纪念的。——还有几枚弹头（*têtes d'obus*）呢？——也是纪念品，我快活地向他保证。难道大臣先生以为我被当场抓获、正要炸掉法国政府，还是怎么的？——可这一打速写本里画的是什么？——哦，大臣先生，您过奖了：是画。——画的防御工事？绝非；画的是法国大兵、孩子们，还有别的废墟。——嗯嗯。（大臣先生查验了画作，发现我所言不虚。）大臣先生把这些小物件统统装进一个小口袋——那是由慷慨的红十字会配发的（在那只巨大的行李袋之外）。他用法语贴上标签：「在卡明斯行李中发现、经认定与本案无关（*inutile*）之物品。」如此一来，前述行李袋里还剩下：我从纽约带来的毛皮大衣；我的床、毯子和铺盖卷，我的便装，以及大约二十五磅脏衬衣。

===Original===

“You may take the bedroll and the folding bed into your cell”﻿—the rest of my *affaires* would remain in safe keeping at the *bureau*.
“Come with me,” grimly croaked a lank turnkey creature.
Bedroll and bed in hand, I came along.
We had but a short distance to go; several steps in fact. I remember we turned a corner and somehow got sight of a sort of square near the prison. A military band was executing itself to the stolid delight of some handfuls of ragged *civiles*. My new captor paused a moment; perhaps his patriotic soul was stirred. Then we traversed an alley with locked doors on both sides, and stopped in front of the last door on the right. A key opened it. The music could still be distinctly heard.
The opened door showed a room, about sixteen feet short and four feet narrow, with a heap of straw in the further end. My spirits had been steadily recovering from the banality of their examination; and it was with a genuine and never-to-be-forgotten thrill that I remarked, as I crossed what might have been the threshold: “*Mais, on est bien ici.*”
A hideous crash nipped the last word. I had supposed the whole prison to have been utterly destroyed by earthquake, but it was only my door closing.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「你可以带铺盖卷和折叠床进牢房」——我的其余物件（*affaires*）将留在办公处妥善保管。
「跟我来。」一个瘦长条的狱卒生物阴沉地哑声说。
我手拿铺盖卷和床，跟了上去。
我们要走的路并不远；实际上就几步。我记得我们拐过一个弯，不知怎么就望见了监狱附近某种广场。一支军乐队正在自我演奏，让几小群衣衫褴褛的百姓（*civiles*）呆呆地听得高兴。我的新捕获者停了片刻；也许他爱国的灵魂被触动了。然后我们穿过一条两侧都是上锁房门的过道，在右手最后一扇门前停住。一把钥匙打开了门。音乐仍清晰可闻。
打开的门露出一间屋子，长约莫十六英尺，宽不过四英尺，最里头堆着一垛稻草。我的精神经过那场平庸乏味的审讯，正稳步回升；当我跨过那道大概算是门槛的东西时，我怀着一种真切而终生难忘的激动说道：「嚯，这儿真不错。」（*Mais, on est bien ici.*）
一声骇人的巨响咬掉了最后一个字。我还以为整座监狱被地震彻底震塌了，原来不过是我身后的门关上了。…

## II En Route / 二 在途中
===Original===

I put the bedroll down. I stood up.
I was myself.
An uncontrollable joy gutted me after three months of humiliation, of being bossed and herded and bullied and insulted. I was myself and my own master.
In this delirium of relief (hardly noticing what I did) I inspected the pile of straw, decided against it, set up my bed, disposed the roll on it, and began to examine my cell.
I have mentioned the length and breadth. The cell was ridiculously high; perhaps ten feet. The end with the door in it was peculiar. The door was not placed in the middle of this end, but at one side, allowing for a huge iron can waist-high which stood in the other corner. Over the door and across the end, a grating extended. A slit of sky was always visible.
Whistling joyously to myself, I took three steps which brought me to the door-end. The door was massively made, all of iron or steel I should think. It delighted me. The can excited my curiosity. I looked over the edge of it. At the bottom reposefully lay a new human turd.
I have a sneaking mania for woodcuts, particularly when used to illustrate the indispensable psychological crisis of some outworn romance. There is in my possession at this minute a masterful depiction of a tall, bearded, horrified man who, clad in an anonymous rig of goat skins, with a fantastic umbrella clasped weakly in one huge paw, bends to examine an indication of humanity in the somewhat cubist wilderness whereof he had fancied himself the owner.﻿ ﻿…
It was then that I noticed the walls. Arm-high they were covered with designs, mottos, pictures. The drawing had all been done in pencil. I resolved to ask for a pencil at the first opportunity.
There had been Germans and Frenchmen imprisoned in this cell. On the right wall, near the door-end, was a long selection from Goethe, laboriously copied. Near the other end of this wall a satiric landscape took place. The technique of this landscape frightened me. There were houses, men, children. And there were trees. I began to wonder what a tree looks like, and laughed copiously.
The back wall had a large and exquisite portrait of a German officer.
The left wall was adorned with a yacht, flying a number 13. “My beloved boat” was inscribed in German underneath. Then came a bust of a German soldier, very idealized, full of unfear.

===Chinese===

我放下铺盖卷。我站直了身子。
我还是我自己。
三个月的屈辱——被人呼来喝去、驱来赶去、欺压凌辱——之后，一阵难以自抑的狂喜掏空了我。我还是我自己，我自己做主。
在这阵如释重负的迷狂中（几乎没留意自己在做什么），我查看了那垛稻草，弃之不用，支起我的床，把铺盖卷摊在上面，然后开始打量我的牢房。
长宽我已经说过了。牢房高得离谱；约莫十英尺。有门的那一端颇为特别。门不开在这一端的正中，而是偏在一侧，给另一角落里一只齐腰高的巨大铁桶腾出了地方。门上方横贯这一端，装着一道铁栅。从那里永远可以望见一线天。
我欢快地吹着口哨，三步走到有门的那一端。门造得敦实厚重，我想，全是铁的或是钢的。它让我心花怒放。那只桶勾起了我的好奇。我朝桶里张望。桶底安详地躺着一坨新鲜的人类粪便。
我私底下痴迷木刻版画，尤其是用来图解某部陈旧罗曼史中不可或缺的心理危机的那种。此刻我手头就藏有一幅大师之作：一个高个子、大胡子、惊恐万状的男人，穿着一身不知名的羊皮行头，一只巨爪有气无力地攥着一把奇形怪状的伞，正弯腰察看某种人类留下的迹象——在这片有点立体派味道的荒野里，他本自命是主人的。…
就在那时我注意到了四面墙。齐臂高的地方布满图案、题铭、绘画。画全是铅笔画的。我打定主意，一有机会就讨一支铅笔。
这间牢房里关过德国人，也关过法国人。右墙靠门一端，是一段歌德的长篇选抄，抄得艰辛备至。靠这堵墙的另一端，上演着一幅讽刺的风景画。这幅风景画的技法把我吓着了。有房子，有男人，有孩子。还有树。我开始琢磨树到底长什么样，放声大笑起来。
后墙上是一幅大而精美的德国军官肖像。
左墙的装饰是一艘游艇，飘着13号的号码旗。底下用德文题着「我心爱的小船」。接着是一尊德国士兵的半身像，被理想化得厉害，浑身是无所畏惧。

===Original===

After this, a masterful crudity﻿—a doughnut-bodied rider, sliding with fearful rapidity down the acute backbone of a totally transparent sausage-shaped horse, who was moving simultaneously in five directions. The rider had a bored expression as he supported the stiff reins in one fist. His further leg assisted in his flight. He wore a German soldier’s cap and was smoking. I made up my mind to copy the horse and rider at once, so soon, that is, as I should have obtained a pencil.
Last, I found a drawing surrounded by a scrolled motto. The drawing was a potted plant with four blossoms. The four blossoms were elaborately dead. Their death was drawn with a fearful care. An obscure deliberation was exposed in the depiction of their drooping petals. The pot tottered very crookedly on a sort of table, as near as I could see. All around ran a funereal scroll. I read: “My farewell to my beloved wife, Gaby.” A fierce hand, totally distinct from the former, wrote in proud letters above: “Punished for desertion. Six years of prison﻿—military degradation.”
It must have been five o’clock. Steps. A vast cluttering of the exterior of the door﻿—by whom? Whang opens the door. Turnkey-creature extending a piece of chocolate with extreme and surly caution. I say “*Merci*” and seize chocolate. Klang shuts the door.
I am lying on my back, the twilight does mistily bluish miracles through the slit over the whang-klang. I can just see leaves, meaning tree.
Then from the left and way off, faintly, broke a smooth whistle, cool like a peeled willow-branch, and I found myself listening to an air from Petroushka, Petroushka, which we saw in Paris at the *Châtelet, mon ami et moi*.﻿ ﻿…
The voice stopped in the middle﻿—and I finished the air. This code continued for a half-hour.
It was dark.
I had laid a piece of my piece of chocolate on the windowsill. As I lay on my back a little silhouette came along the sill and ate that piece of a piece, taking something like four minutes to do so. He then looked at me, I then smiled at him, and we parted, each happier than before.
My *cellule* was cool, and I fell asleep easily.
(Thinking of Paris.)
… Awakened by a conversation whose vibrations I clearly felt through the left wall:
Turnkey-creature: “What?”

===Chinese===

在这之后，是一件浑然天成的粗糙杰作——一个甜甜圈身材的骑手，正以骇人的速度顺着一匹全透明的香肠形马的锋利脊梁骨往下溜，而那匹马同时朝五个方向移动着。骑手一脸无聊的表情，一只拳头撑着僵直的缰绳。他那条远侧的腿助力着他的飞行。他戴一顶德国士兵帽，嘴里掃着烟。我打定主意，一拿到铅笔就立刻临摹这马和骑手。
最后，我发现一幅画，四周围着卷轴题铭。画是一盆开着四朵花的盆栽。四朵花精心地死去了。它们的死被画得无比用心。那低垂的花瓣里透出一种说不清的处心积虑。依我尽力所见，花盆歪歪斜斜地架在某种桌子上。四周环绕着丧礼般的卷轴。我读到：「与我挚爱的妻子加比永别。」另一只与前头截然不同的狂放之手，在上方以骄傲的字母写道：「因逃亡受罚。六年监禁——褫夺军衔。」
想必是五点钟了。脚步声。门外一阵大规模的窸窣作响——是谁？哐啷一声门开了。狱卒生物以一种极致而阴沉的小心递进来一块巧克力。我说「谢谢」（*Merci*）一把抓住巧克力。咣当一声门关上了。
我仰面躺着，暮色透过那扇哐啷之门上方的窄缝，做着雾蒙蒙的蓝调奇迹。我刚刚能看见树叶，树叶意味着树。
随后，从左边很远的地方，幽幽地响起一段圆润的口哨，清凉得像剥了皮的柳枝，我发现自己在听《彼得鲁什卡》（*Petroushka*）里的一段曲调——彼得鲁什卡，我和我朋友（*mon ami et moi*）在巴黎夏特莱剧院（*Châtelet*）看过的那出。…
那声音在中途停住——我便把曲子吹完。这套暗号持续了半个小时。
天黑了。
我把我的那块巧克力上掰下的一小块放在窗台上。我仰卧着，一个小小的剪影沿着窗台走来，吃掉了那一小块的一小块，足足用了大约四分钟。然后他看了看我，我朝他笑了笑，我们就此别过，各自都比先前快活。
我的小囚室（*cellule*）很凉爽，我很容易就睡着了。
（想着巴黎。）
……被一场对话惊醒，那对话的振动我隔着左墙清晰可感：
狱卒生物：「什么？」

===Original===

A moldly moldering molish voice, suggesting putrefying tracts and orifices, answers with a cob-webbish patience so far beyond despair as to be indescribable: “*La soupe.*”
“Well, the soup, I just gave it to you, Monsieur Savy.”
“Must have a little something else. My money is *chez le directeur*. Please take my money which is *chez le directeur* and give me anything else.”
“All right, the next time I come to see you today I’ll bring you a salad, a nice salad, Monsieur.”
“Thank you, Monsieur,” the voice moldered.
Klang!!﻿—and says the turnkey-creature to somebody else; while turning the lock of Monsieur Savy’s door; taking pains to raise his voice so that Monsieur Savy will not miss a single word through the slit over Monsieur Savy’s whang-klang:
“That old fool! Always asks for things. When supposest thou will he realize that he’s never going to get anything?”
Grubbing at my door. Whang!
The faces stood in the doorway, looking me down. The expression of the faces identically turnkeyish, i.e., stupidly gloating, ponderously and imperturbably tickled. Look who’s here, who let that in?
The right body collapsed sufficiently to deposit a bowl just inside.
I smiled and said: “Good morning, sirs. The can stinks.”
They did not smile and said: “Naturally.” I smiled and said: “Please give me a pencil. I want to pass the time.” They did not smile and said: “Directly.”
I smiled and said: “I want some water, if you please.”
They shut the door, saying “Later.”
Klang and footsteps.
I contemplate the bowl which contemplates me. A glaze of greenish grease seals the mystery of its content, I induce two fingers to penetrate the seal. They bring me up a flat sliver of cabbage and a large, hard, thoughtful, solemn, uncooked bean. To pour the water off (it is warmish and sticky) without committing a nuisance is to lift the cover off *Ça Pue*. I did.
Thus leaving beans and cabbage-slivers. Which I ate hurryingly, fearing a ventral misgiving.
I pass a lot of time cursing myself about the pencil, looking at my walls, my unique interior.
Suddenly I realize the indisputable grip of nature’s humorous hand. One evidently stands on *Ça Pue* in such cases. Having finished, panting with stink, I tumble on the bed and consider my next move.
The straw will do. Ouch, but it’s Dirty.﻿—Several hours elapse.﻿ ﻿…
Steps and fumble. Klang. Repetition of promise to Monsieur Savy, etc.

===Chinese===

一个发霉的、霉烂的、霉兮兮的嗓音——令人联想到腐烂的脏腑与孔窍——以蛛网般远超绝望、难以言表的耐心答道：「汤。」（*La soupe.*）
「哎呀，汤嘛，我刚给您送来过，萨维先生。」
「总得再有点别的什么。我的钱在典狱长（*chez le directeur*）那儿。请拿我的钱，就是典狱长那儿的那份，随便给我点别的。」
「好吧，今天我下回来看您的时候，给您带份沙拉，一份好沙拉，先生。」
「谢谢您，先生。」那嗓音霉烂着说。
咣当！！——狱卒生物一边拧着萨维先生门上的锁，一边对另外什么人说；他特意提高嗓门，好让萨维先生隔着门上哐啷之门上方的窄缝一个字也不漏听：
「那老傻子！老是要东西。你说他得多久才能明白，他什么也甭想得到？」
我的门上一阵扒挠。哐啷！
几张脸站在门口，居高临下地打量我。那几张脸上的表情如出一辙的狱卒相，也就是说，愚蠢地幸灾乐祸，笨重而处变不惊地自我得意。瞧瞧谁来这儿了，谁把这玩意儿放进来的？
右边那具身子弯到刚好能把一只碗撂进门里的程度。
我微笑着说：「早上好，先生们。那只桶臭气熏天。」
他们没笑，说：「那当然。」我微笑着说：「请给我一支铅笔吧。我想打发时间。」他们没笑，说：「马上。」
我微笑着说：「劳驾，我想要点水。」
他们关上门，丢下一句「回头」。
咣当，脚步声。
我端详着那只碗，那只碗也端详着我。一层绿油油的油膜封存着碗中之物的谜，我诱导两根手指戳破封印。它们给我捞上来一片扁平的白菜和一颗大而硬、若有所思、一本正经、没煮过的豆子。要把水（温吞吞、黏糊糊的）倒掉而不弄出事端，就等于揭开「臭屎桶」（*Ça Pue*）的盖子。我揭了。
如此只剩下了豆子和白菜片。我赶紧把它们吃了，生怕肚子里生出异议。
我咒骂自己不该提铅笔的事，咒骂了好一阵，一边打量我的墙，我独一无二的室内装饰。
突然，我领会到造化那只幽默之手不容置疑的把握。这种情况下，人显然得站到臭屎桶上去。完事之后，我带着一身臭气直喘，一头栽到床上，思量下一步棋。
稻草垛也凑合。哎哟，可它真脏。——几个小时过去了。…
脚步声和摸索声。咣当。对萨维先生许诺的重复，等等。

===Original===

Turnkeyish and turnkeyish. Identical expression. One body collapses sufficiently to deposit a hunk of bread and a piece of water.
“Give your bowl.”
I gave it, smiled and said: “Well, how about that pencil?”
“Pencil?” T-c looked at T-c.
They recited then the following word: “Tomorrow.” Klang and footsteps.
So I took matches, burnt, and with just 60 of them wrote the first stanza of a ballade. Tomorrow I will write the second. Day after tomorrow the third. Next day the refrain. After﻿—oh, well.
My whistling of Petroushka brought no response this evening.
So I climbed on *Ça Pue*, whom I now regarded with complete friendliness; the new moon was unclosing sticky wings in dusk, a far noise from near things.
I sang a song the “dirty Frenchmen” taught us, *mon ami et moi*. The song says *Bon soir, Madame de la Lune*.﻿ ﻿… I did not sing out loud, simply because the moon was like a mademoiselle, and I did not want to offend the moon. My friends: the silhouette and *la lune*, not counting *Ça Pue*, whom I regarded almost as a part of me.
Then I lay down, and heard (but could not see the silhouette eat something or somebody)﻿ ﻿… and saw, but could not hear, the incense of *Ça Pue* mount gingerly upon the taking air of twilight.
The next day.﻿—Promise to M. Savy. Whang. “My pencil?”﻿—“You don’t need any pencil, you’re going away.”﻿—“When?”﻿—“Directly.”﻿—“How directly?”﻿—“In an hour or two: your friend has already gone before. Get ready.”
Klang and steps.
Everyone very sore about me. I should worry, however.
One hour, I guess.
Steps. Sudden throwing of door open. Pause.
“Come out, American.”
As I came out, toting bed and bedroll, I remarked: “I’m sorry to leave you,” which made T-c furiously to masticate his insignificant moustache.
Escorted to *bureau*, where I am turned over to a very fat gendarme.
“This is the American.” The v-f-g eyed me, and I read my sins in his porklike orbs. “Hurry, we have to walk,” he ventured sullenly and commandingly.
Himself stooped puffingly to pick up the segregated sack. And I placed my bed, bedroll, blankets and ample pelisse under one arm, my 150-odd pound duffle-bag under the other; then I paused. Then I said, “Where’s my cane?”
The v-f-g hereat had a sort of fit, which perfectly became him.
I repeated gently: “When I came to the *bureau* I had a cane.”

===Chinese===

狱卒相加狱卒相。一模一样的表情。一具身子弯到刚好把一块面包和一份水撂进门里。
「把碗交出来。」
我交了碗，微笑着说：「那么，铅笔的事儿怎么样了？」
「铅笔？」狱卒生物甲看了看狱卒生物乙。
于是他们齐诵了下面这个词：「明天。」咣当，脚步声。
于是我拿出火柴，一根根烧，用整整60根写下了谣曲的第一节。明天写第二节。后天写第三节。再一天写叠句。之后——唉，罢了。
今晚我吹《彼得鲁什卡》，没得到回应。
于是我爬上臭屎桶——如今我对它已满怀友善；新月正在暮色中展开黏糊糊的翅膀，一种发自近处事物的遥远声响。
我唱起一支「脏法国佬」教我们（我和我朋友）的歌。那歌唱道：*Bon soir, Madame de la Lune*（晚安，月亮夫人）。……我没有放声唱，只因月亮像一位小姐，我不想冒犯月亮。我的朋友们：剪影和月夫人（*la lune*），还不算臭屎桶——它我已几乎视作自己身体的一部分。
然后我躺下，听见（但看不见剪影在吃什么东西或什么人）……又看见，但听不见，臭屎桶的香烟小心翼翼地袅袅升起，攀上黄昏那渐凉的空气。
第二天。——对萨维先生的许诺。哐啷。「我的铅笔呢？」——「你用不着铅笔了，你要走了。」——「什么时候？」——「马上。」——「多马上？」——「一两个钟头之内：你朋友已经先走了。准备准备。」
咣当，脚步。
人人都为我气得要命。不过，我犯得上操心吗。
大概过了一个钟头。
脚步。门猛地被推开。停顿。
「出来，美国人。」
我扛着床和铺盖卷出来时说了句：「真舍不得离开你们。」这话让狱卒生物狂怒地咀嚼起他那撇不值一提的小胡子。
被押到办公处（*bureau*），我在那里被移交给一位非常胖的宪兵。
「这就是那个美国人。」超胖宪兵打量我，我在他那对猪一样的眼珠里读出了我的罪孽。「快点，咱们得走着去。」他阴沉而又威严地冒出一句。
他自己气喘吁吁地弯腰拎起那只被单独存放的口袋。我把床、铺盖卷、毯子和宽大的毛皮大衣夹在一侧胳膊底下，把那只一百五十多磅的行李袋夹在另一侧胳膊底下；然后我停了停。然后我说：「我的手杖呢？」
超胖宪兵闻言发了一阵小小的羊痫疯，发作得恰到好处。
我温和地重复道：「我来办公处的时候，还有一根手杖。」

===Original===

“I don’t give a damn about your cane,” burbled my new captor frothily, his pink evil eyes swelling with wrath.
“I’m staying,” I replied calmly, and sat down on a curb, in the midst of my ponderous trinkets.
A crowd of gendarmes gathered. One didn’t take a cane with one to prison (I was glad to know where I was bound, and thanked this communicative gentleman); or criminals weren’t allowed canes; or where exactly did I think I was, in the Tuileries? asks a rube movie-cop personage.
“Very well, gentlemen,” I said. “You will allow me to tell you something.” (I was beet-colored.) “In America that sort of thing isn’t done.”
This haughty inaccuracy produced an astonishing effect, namely, the prestidigitatorial vanishment of the v-f-g. The v-f-g’s numerous confrères looked scared and twirled their whiskers.
I sat on the curb and began to fill a paper with something which I found in my pockets, certainly not tobacco.
Splutter-splutter-fizz-Poop﻿—the v-f-g is back, with my oak-branch in his raised hand, slithering opprobria and mostly crying: “Is that huge piece of wood what you call a cane? It is, is it? What? How? What the﻿—,” so on.
I beamed upon him and thanked him, and explained that a “dirty Frenchman” had given it to me as a souvenir, and that I would now proceed.
Twisting the handle in the loop of my sack, and hoisting the vast parcel under my arm, I essayed twice to boost it on my back. This to the accompaniment of HurryHurryHurryHurryHurryHurryHurry.﻿ ﻿… The third time I sweated and staggered to my feet, completely accoutred.
Down the road. Into the *ville*. Curious looks from a few pedestrians. A driver stops his wagon to watch the spider and his outlandish fly. I chuckled to think how long since I had washed and shaved. Then I nearly fell, staggered on a few steps, and set down the two loads.
Perhaps it was the fault of a strictly vegetarian diet. At any rate, I couldn’t move a step farther with my bundles. The sun sent the sweat along my nose in tickling waves. My eyes were blind.
Hereupon I suggested that the v-f-g carry part of one of my bundles with me, and received the answer: “I am doing too much for you as it is. No gendarme is supposed to carry a prisoner’s baggage.”
I said then: “I’m too tired.”
He responded: “You can leave here anything you don’t care to carry further; I’ll take care of it.”
I looked at the gendarme.

===Chinese===

「你那破手杖关我屁事。」我的新捕获者吐着泡沫咕哝道，他那双粉红的恶眼因狂怒而鼓胀。
「那我就不走了。」我平静地回答，在一道路边石上坐下，被我这堆沉重的破烂玩意儿包围着。
一群宪兵聚拢过来。去监狱哪有带手杖的（我很高兴终于知道了自己的去向，并向这位健谈的先生道谢）；要么就是罪犯不许用手杖；要么就是——你到底以为自己在哪儿，杜伊勒里花园吗？一个土包子电影警察式的人物发问。
「很好，先生们。」我说。「请容我告诉诸位一件事。」（我的脸红成了甜菜色。）「在美国，是不兴这一套的。」
这句高傲的无稽之谈产生了惊人的效果：超胖宪兵像变戏法一样消失了。他那一大群同僚面露惧色，捻起了各自的络腮胡。
我坐在路边石上，开始往一张纸里填我口袋里翻出来的某种东西，反正不是烟草。
噗噗——嘶——砰——超胖宪兵回来了，高举的手里攞着我那根橡木枝，嘴里淌着污言秽语，主要是喊：「这么大块木头就是你说的手杖？是吗？啊？怎么？什么——」如此等等。
我对他堆起满面笑容，谢过他，解释说那是一位「脏法国佬」送我做纪念的，然后说我这就动身。
我把杖柄拧进口袋的环里，把那个巨大的包袱甩上胳膊，两次尝试把它驮上背。伴随这一切的是快快快快快快快快快快快快快快。…第三次，我大汗淋漓、踉踉跄跄地站了起来，全副披挂。
沿路而下。进了城（*ville*）。几个行人投来好奇的目光。一个车夫停下马车，看蜘蛛和他那只奇装异服的苍蝇。想到自己已经多久没洗脸刮胡子，我暗自笑了。接着我险些摔倒，踉跄几步，放下了两件行李。
也许该怪那份严格素食的伙食。总之，我驮着行李一步也挪不动了。太阳把汗水顺着我的鼻梁一阵阵痒酥酥地送下来。我的眼睛什么也看不见了。
于是我提议超胖宪兵同我一起抬一件行李的一部分，得到的回答是：「我为你做得已经够多的了。宪兵可没有替罪犯扛行李的规矩。」
我便说：「我太累了。」
他答道：「不愿意再扛的，可以撞在这儿；我替你保管。」
我看着那宪兵。

===Original===

I looked several blocks through him. My lip did something like a sneer. My hands did something like fists.
At this crisis along comes a little boy. May God bless all males between seven and ten years of age in France!
The gendarme offered a suggestion, in these words: “Have you any change about you?” He knew, of course, that the sanitary official’s first act had been to deprive me of every last cent. The gendarme’s eyes were fine. They reminded me of﻿ ﻿… never mind. “If you have change,” said he, “you might hire this kid to carry some of your baggage.” Then he lit a pipe which was made in his own image, and smiled fattily.
But herein the v-f-g had bust his milk-jug. There is a slit of a pocket made in the uniform of his criminal on the right side, and completely covered by the belt which his criminal always wears. His criminal had thus outwitted the gumshoe fraternity.
The *gosse* could scarcely balance my smaller parcel, but managed after three rests to get it to the station platform; here I tipped him something like two cents (all I had) which, with dollar-big eyes, he took and ran.
A strongly-built, groomed apache smelling of cologne and onions greeted my v-f-g with that affection which is peculiar to gendarmes. On me he stared cynically, then sneered frankly.
With a little tooty shriek the funny train tottered in. My captors had taken pains to place themselves at the wrong end of the platform. Now they encouraged me to HurryHurryHurry.
I managed to get under the load and tottered the length of the train to a car especially reserved. There was one other criminal, a beautifully-smiling, shortish man, with a very fine blanket wrapped in a waterproof oilskin cover. We grinned at each other (the most cordial salutation, by the way, that I have ever exchanged with a human being) and sat down opposite one another﻿—he, plus my baggage which he helped me lift in, occupying one seat; the gendarme-sandwich, of which I formed the pièce de résistance, the other.
The engine got under way after several feints; which pleased the Germans so that they sent several scout planes right over the station, train, us *et tout*.

===Chinese===

我的目光穿透过他，望出去好几条街。我的嘴唇做了个类似冷笑的动作。我的双手做了个类似攞拳的动作。
就在这关口，来了一个小男孩。愿上帝保佑法国所有七到十岁的男性！
宪兵献上一计，原话如下：「你身上有零钱吗？」他当然知道，那位讲卫生的官员的头一件功绩就是把我搜刮得分文不剩。宪兵的眼睛很漂亮。它们让我想起……不提也罢。「你要是有零钱，」他说，「可以雇这小子替你扛点行李。」说着他点上一支照他自己形象制作的烟斗，肥肥地笑了。
但这一回，超胖宪兵摔破了自己的奶罐。他的罪犯的制服右侧开着一道窄缝口袋，完全被罪犯永远系着的那条腰带盖住。他的罪犯就这样智胜了那帮包打听行会。
小家伙（*gosse*）几乎扛不稳我那只小些的包裹，歇了三歇才把它弄到车站月台上；我在那儿给了他大约两分钱的小费（我的全部家当），他睁着一双一美元大小的眼睛，接过钱跑了。
一个身板结实、修饰整齐的巴黎阿飞，浑身古龙水和洋葱味，用宪兵们特有的那种深情问候我的超胖宪兵。对我，他先是冷冷地盯，继而公然嗤笑。
那列滑稽的火车呜地尖叫一声，摇摇晃晃进了站。我的捕获者们特意站到了月台错误的一端。此刻他们鼓励我快快快。
我好不容易钻进行李底下，摇摇晃晃走完列车的全长，来到一节专用车厢。里面还有一名罪犯，一个笑容可掬、个头偏矮的男人，带着一条上好的毯子，裹在防水的油布套里。我们朝彼此咧嘴一笑（顺便说，这是我与人类交换过的最诚挚的问候），面对面坐下——他加上他帮我抬上车的那堆行李占一个座位；宪兵三明治，我充当其中的主菜（*pièce de résistance*），占另一个。
机车虚晃了几枪之后终于启动；这让德国人高兴坏了，特地派了几架侦察机飞到车站、列车、我们等等（*et tout*）的正上方。

===Original===

All the French anticraft guns went off together for the sake of sympathy; the guardians of the peace squinted cautiously from their respective windows, and then began a debate on the number of the enemy while their prisoners smiled at each other appreciatively.
“*Il fait chaud*,” said this divine man, prisoner, criminal, or whatnot, as he offered me a glass of wine in the form of a huge tin cup overflowed from the canteen in his slightly unsteady and delicately made hand. He is a Belgian. Volunteered at beginning of war. Permission at Paris, overstayed by one day. When he reported to his officer, the latter announced that he was a deserter﻿—I said to him, “It is funny. It is funny I should have come back, of my own free will, to my company. I should have thought that being a deserter I would have preferred to remain in Paris.” The wine was terribly cold, and I thanked my divine host.
Never have I tasted such wine.
They had given me a chunk of war-bread in place of blessing when I left Noyon. I bit into it with renewed might. But the divine man across from me immediately produced a sausage, half of which he laid simply upon my knee. The halving was done with a large keen poilu’s knife.
I have not tasted a sausage since.
The pigs on my either hand had by this time overcome their respective inertias and were chomping cheek-murdering chunks. They had quite a layout, a regular picnic-lunch elaborate enough for kings or even presidents. The v-f-g in particular annoyed me by uttering alternate chompings and belchings. All the time he ate he kept his eyes half-shut; and a mist overspread the sensual meadows of his coarse face.
His two reddish eyes rolled devouringly toward the blanket in its waterproof roll. After a huge gulp of wine he said thickly (for his huge moustache was crusted with saliva-tinted half-moistened shreds of food), “You will have no use for that *machine là-bas*. They are going to take everything away from you when you get there, you know. I could use it nicely. I have wanted such a piece of rubber for a great while, in order to make me a raincoat. Do you see?” (Gulp. Swallow.)
Here I had an inspiration. I would save the blanket-cover by drawing these brigands’ attention to myself. At the same time I would satisfy my inborn taste for the ridiculous. “Have you a pencil?” I said.

===Chinese===

法国的全部高射炮出于同情一齐开火；和平的守护者们从各自的窗户里小心地眯眼张望，随后开始辩论敌机的数目，而他们的囚犯们则相视而笑，心照不宣。
「天真热。」（*Il fait chaud*）这位神圣的人——囚犯、罪犯或随便什么——说道，一边用他那微微不稳、做工精致的手，把一大锡杯从军用水壶里溢出来的葡萄酒递给我。他是比利时人。开战之初志愿参军。在巴黎休假，超期了一天。他回去向长官报到时，长官宣布他成了逃兵——我对他说：「真有意思。真有意思，我居然自己愿意回到连队来。要我说，当了逃兵，我宁肯留在巴黎才对。」那酒冰得厉害，我谢过我这神圣的东道主。
我从未喝过那样的美酒。
离开努瓦永（Noyon）时，他们赏了我一块战争面包以代替祝福。我重新鼓起力气咬了一口。可对面的那位神圣的人立刻变出一根香肠，简简单单地把一半放在了我的膝盖上。切半的活儿是用一把法国大兵的大号利刃完成的。
从那以后我再没尝过香肠。
我两边的猪们这时都克服了各自的惰性，大嚼起谋杀腮帮子的大块食物。他们的阵仗不小，一顿正经的野餐，丰盛得够招待国王甚至总统。超胖宪兵尤其烦人，大嚼声与打嗝声轮番出炉。吃的全过程他一直半闭着眼；一层雾气漫过他那张粗脸上肉欲的草原。
他那双淡红色眼珠贪婪地滚向那个防水套里的毯子卷。灌下一大口酒后，他口齿含混地说（因为他那撇大胡子上结满了染着唾沫色的半湿食物碎屑）：「到了那儿，那个玩意儿（*machine là-bas*）你就用不上了。他们会把你身上的一切都没收的，知道吧。我拿它倒正合适。这么一块胶皮我惦记好久了，想给自己做件雨衣。明白吗？」（咕咚。咽。）
这时我灵机一动。我可以把这两个强盗的注意力引到我自己身上，保住那个毯子套。同时还能满足我对荒唐事与生俱来的品味。「您有铅笔吗？」我说。

===Original===

“Because I am an artist in my own country, and will do your picture.”
He gave me a pencil. I don’t remember where the paper came from. I posed him in a pig-like position, and the picture made him chew his moustache. The apache thought it very droll. I should do his picture, too, at once. I did my best; though protesting that he was too beautiful for my pencil, which remark he countered by murmuring (as he screwed his moustache another notch), “Never mind, you will try.” Oh, yes, I would try all right, all right. He objected, I recall, to the nose.
By this time the divine “deserter” was writhing with joy. “If you please, Monsieur,” he whispered radiantly, “it would be too great an honor, but if you could﻿—I should be overcome.﻿ ﻿…”
