Tears (for some strange reason) came into my eyes.
He handled his picture sacredly, criticised it with precision and care, finally bestowed it in his inner pocket. Then we drank. It happened that the train stopped and the apache was persuaded to go out and get his prisoner’s canteen filled. Then we drank again.
He smiled as he told me he was getting ten years. Three years at solitary confinement was it, and seven working in a gang on the road? That would not be so bad. He wished he was not married, had not a little child. “The bachelors are lucky in this war”﻿—he smiled.
Now the gendarmes began cleaning their beards, brushing their stomachs, spreading their legs, collecting their baggage. The reddish eyes, little and cruel, woke from the trance of digestion and settled with positive ferocity on their prey. “You will have no use.﻿ ﻿…”
Silently the sensitive, gentle hands of the divine prisoner undid the blanket-cover. Silently the long, tired, well-shaped arms passed it across to the brigand at my left side. With a grunt of satisfaction the brigand stuffed it in a large pouch, taking pains that it should not show. Silently the divine eyes said to mine: “What can we do, we criminals?” And we smiled at each other for the last time, the eyes and my eyes.
A station. The apache descends. I follow with my numerous *affaires*. The divine man follows me﻿—the v-f-g him.
The blanket-roll containing my large fur-coat got more and more unrolled; finally I could not possibly hold it.
It fell. To pick it up I must take the sack off my back.
Then comes a voice, “allow me if you please, monsieur”﻿—and the sack has disappeared.

===Chinese===

「因为我在本国是个艺术家，可以给您画张像。」
他给了我一枝铅笔。纸是哪儿来的我记不清了。我让他摆了个猪式造型，画出来的像让他咀嚼起自己的胡子。阿飞觉得妙极了。我也该立刻给他画一张。我尽了全力；不过我抗议说他太俊美了，非我这支铅笔所能表现，对这话他的回应是喃喃道（一边把胡子又拧上一扣）：「不碍事，你试试嘛。」哦，试试，我当然要试，要试的。我记得，他对鼻子有意见。
这时候，那位神圣的「逃兵」已经喜悦得扭动起来。「如果您愿意，先生，」他满面生光地悄声道，「那实在是天大的荣幸，可您要是能——我真不知如何承受。…」
眼泪（出于某种奇怪的缘故）涌上了我的眼眶。
他神圣地捧着他的画像，精确而细致地品评了一番，最终把它珍藏进内袋。然后我们喝了酒。恰好火车停了，阿飞被说服下车去把他囚犯的水壶灌满。然后我们又喝了酒。
他微笑着告诉我，他判了十年。好像是三年单独监禁，七年编队修路？那还算不上太糟。他希望自己没结过婚，没有小孩。「这场战争里，光棍是有福的。」——他微笑着说。
这时宪兵们开始拾掇各自的胡子，掸各自的肚子，叉开双腿，归拢各自的行李。那双淡红色的眼睛，小小的、残忍，从消化的迷醉中醒来，以十足的凶狠盯住了它们的猎物。「你就用不上了。…」
神圣的囚犯那双敏感、温柔的手，默默地解开了毯子套。那双修长、疲惫、形状美好的手臂，默默地把它递给我左侧的强盗。强盗满意地哼了一声，把套子塞进一只大挎包，还特意不让它露出形迹。那双神圣的眼睛默默地对我的眼睛说：「我们罪犯又能怎样呢？」我们最后一次相视而笑，那双眼睛和我的眼睛。
一站到了。阿飞下了车。我带着我数不清的行李（*affaires*）跟下去。神圣的人跟着我——超胖宪兵跟着他。
裹着我那件大毛皮大衣的毯子卷越散越开；终于，我再也抱不住了。
它掉在了地上。要捡起它，我必须先卸下背上的口袋。
这时传来一个声音，「请容我效劳，先生」——口袋便没了踪影。

===Original===

Blindly and dumbly I stumble on with the roll; and so at length we come into the yard of a little prison; and the divine man bowed under my great sack.﻿ ﻿… I never thanked him. When I turned, they’d taken him away, and the sack stood accusingly at my feet.
Through the complete disorder of my numbed mind flicker jabbings of strange tongues. Some high boy’s voice is appealing to me in Belgian, Italian, Polish, Spanish and﻿—beautiful English. “Hey, Jack, give me a cigarette, Jack.﻿ ﻿…”
I lift my eyes. I am standing in a tiny oblong space. A sort of court. All around, two-story wooden barracks. Little crude staircases lead up to doors heavily chained and immensely padlocked. More like ladders than stairs. Curious hewn windows, smaller in proportion than the slits in a doll’s house. Are these faces behind the slits? The doors bulge incessantly under the shock of bodies hurled against them from within. The whole dirty nouveau business about to crumble.
Glance one.
Glance two: directly before me. A wall with many bars fixed across one minute opening. At the opening a dozen, fifteen, grins. Upon the bars hands, scraggy and bluishly white. Through the bars stretching of lean arms, incessant stretchings. The grins leap at the window, hands belonging to them catch hold, arms belonging to the hands stretch in my direction﻿ ﻿… an instant; the new grins leap from behind and knock off the first grins which go down with a fragile crashing like glass smashed: hands wither and break, arms streak out of sight, sucked inward.
In the huge potpourri of misery a central figure clung, shaken but undislodged. Clung like a monkey to central bars. Clung like an angel to a harp. Calling pleasantly in a high boyish voice: “O Jack, give me a cigarette.”
A handsome face, dark, Latin smile, musical fingers strong.
I waded suddenly through a group of gendarmes (they stood around me watching with a disagreeable curiosity my reaction to this). Strode fiercely to the window.
Trillions of hands.
Quadrillions of itching fingers.
The angel-monkey received the package of cigarettes politely, disappearing with it into howling darkness. I heard his high boy’s voice distributing cigarettes. Then he leaped into sight, poised gracefully against two central bars, saying “Thank you, Jack, good boy”﻿ ﻿… “Thanks, *merci*, *gracias*﻿ ﻿…” a deafening din of gratitude reeked from within.

===Chinese===

我又盲又哑地抱着毯子卷跌跌撞撞往前走；就这样，我们终于走进了一座小监狱的院子；神圣的人弓着身子，驮着我那只大口袋。……我一直没能谢他。等我转过身，他们已经把他带走了，口袋孤零零地立在我脚边，像在控诉。
在我那冻僵头脑的一片狼藉中，闪烁着各种奇怪语言的戳刺。某个高亢的少年嗓音正用比利时语、意大利语、波兰语、西班牙语，还有——美妙的英语向我呼吁。「嘿，杰克，给我支烟，杰克。…」
我抬起眼。我正站在一小块长方形的场地上。一种院子。四周环绕着两层楼的木棚屋。窄小的粗陋楼梯通向一道道挂着粗链、上着巨锁的门。与其说是楼梯，不如说是梯子。古怪的凿出来的窗，比例上比玩具娃娃屋的窗缝还小。那些窄缝后面是脸吗？门在从里面撞上来的身体的冲击下不停地鼓胀。整个肮脏的新派营生眼看就要崩塌。
扫视其一。
扫视其二：正前方。一堵墙，一个小小的开口上钉着许多铁条。开口处有十来张、十五张咧开的笑。铁条上是手，瘦骨嶙峋、泛着青白。铁条间伸出一条条精瘦的胳膊，不停地伸着。那些笑朝窗口扑，附着的双手抓住铁条，附着的手的胳膊朝我伸来……一瞬间；新的笑从后面扑上来，把先前的笑撞了下去，后者像砸碎的玻璃一样发出脆裂声：手枯萎、折断，胳膊倏地缩回不见，被吸了进去。
在这片苦难的大杂烩中，一个中心人物死死扒着，被撼动却未被撼落。像猴子一样扒在正中的铁条上。像天使一样抱着竖琴。用高亢的少年嗓音愉快地呼唤：「哦，杰克，给我支烟。」
一张英俊的脸，黝黑，拉丁式的微笑，弹琴般的手指强劲有力。
我突然蹚过一群宪兵（他们围在我四周，带着令人不快的兴致旁观我对此的反应）。大步冲向窗口。
万亿只手。
亿亿只发痒的手指。
天使-猴子彬彬有礼地接过那包香烟，带着它消失在嚎啸的黑暗里。我听见他高亢的少年嗓音在分发香烟。随后他跃入视野，优雅地攀在正中两根铁条上，说：「谢谢你，杰克，好小伙」……「谢谢，*merci*，*gracias*…」里面涌出一阵震耳欲聋的感激的喧嚣。

===Original===

“Put your baggage in here,” quoth an angry voice. “No, you will not take anything but one blanket in your cell, understand.” In French. Evidently the head of the house speaking. I obeyed. A corpulent soldier importantly lead me to my cell. My cell is two doors away from the monkey-angel, on the same side. The high boy-voice, centralized in a torrent-like halo of stretchings, followed my back. The head himself unlocked a lock. I marched coldly in. The fat soldier locked and chained my door. Four feet went away. I felt in my pocket, finding four cigarettes. I am sorry I did not give these also to the monkey﻿—to the angel. Lifted my eyes and saw my own harp.

===Chinese===

「把行李放这儿。」一个恼怒的声音说。「不行，进牢房只能带一条毯子，别的什么都不许带，明白吗。」说的是法语。显然是这宅子的头儿在发话。我照办了。一个肥壮的士兵煞有介事地领我走向牢房。我的牢房离猴子-天使隔两道门，在同一侧。那高亢的少年嗓音，聚在激流般伸出的手臂光环的中央，一路追着我的背影。头儿亲自开了一把锁。我冷冷地迈步进去。胖士兵锁好我的门，挂上链子。四只脚走远了。我摸了摸口袋，摸出四支香烟。很抱歉，我没有把这几支也送给猴子——送给天使。我抬起眼，看见了我自己的竖琴。

## III A Pilgrim’s Progress / 三 一个朝圣者的旅程
===Original===

Through the bars I looked into that little and dirty lane whereby I had entered; in which a sentinel, gun on shoulder, and with a huge revolver strapped at his hip, monotonously moved. On my right was an old wall overwhelmed with moss. A few growths stemmed from its crevices. Their leaves were of a refreshing colour. I felt singularly happy, and carefully throwing myself on the bare planks sang one after another all the French songs which I had picked up in my stay at the ambulance; sang *La Madelon*, sang ***Av**ec av**ec Du***, and *Les Galiots Sont Lourds Dans Sac*﻿—concluding with an inspired rendering of *La Marseillaise*, at which the guard (who had several times stopped his round in what I choose to interpret as astonishment) grounded arms and swore appreciatively. Various officials of the jail passed by me and my lusty songs; I cared no whit. Two or three conferred, pointing in my direction, and I sang a little louder for the benefit of their perplexity. Finally out of voice I stopped.
It was twilight.
As I lay on my back luxuriously, I saw through the bars of my twice padlocked door a boy and a girl about ten years old. I saw them climb on the wall and play together, obliviously and exquisitely, in the darkening air. I watched them for many minutes; till the last moment of light failed; till they and the wall itself dissolved in a common mystery, leaving only the bored silhouette of the soldier moving imperceptibly and wearily against a still more gloomy piece of autumn sky.
At last I knew that I was very thirsty; and leaping up began to clamor at my bars. “Something to drink, please.” After a long debate with the sergeant of guards who said very angrily: “Give it to him,” a guard took my request and disappeared from view, returning with a more heavily armed guard and a tin cup full of water. One of these gentry watched the water and me, while the other wrestled with the padlock. The door being minutely opened, one guard and the water painfully entered. The other guard remained at the door, gun in readiness. The water was set down, and the enterer assumed a perpendicular position which I thought merited recognition; accordingly I said “*Merci*” politely, without getting up from the planks. Immediately he began to deliver a sharp lecture on the probability of my using the tin cup to saw my way out; and commended haste in no doubtful terms.

===Chinese===

透过铁栏，我望进我进来时穿过的那条肮脏小巷；巷子里，一个哨兵肩上掃着枪，胯上绑着一支巨大的左轮手枪，单调地来回走动。我右边是一面爬满青苔的老墙。几株草木从墙缝里钻出来。叶子是醒神的颜色。我感到说不出的幸福，小心翼翼地往光板上一躺，把我在救护队待的日子里听来的法国歌一首接一首地唱了个遍；唱《玛德隆》（*La Madelon*），唱《Av**ec av**ec Du*》，唱《Les Galiots Sont Lourds Dans Sac》——最后满怀灵感地唱了一曲《马赛曲》，哨兵（他几次停下巡逻，我情愿把这理解为惊讶）放下枪托，赞叹地骂了一句粗。监狱里的各路官员从我和我雄壮的歌声旁经过；我毫不在乎。有两三个凑在一起，朝我这边指指点点，我为成全他们的困惑，唱得更响了些。终于嗓子哑了，我停下。
已是黄昏。
我舒舒服服地仰躺着，透过我那扇上了两道挂锁的门的铁栏，看见一个男孩和一个十岁上下的女孩。我看着他们爬上墙头，在渐暗的暮色里忘我而美妙地嬉戏。我看了他们好几分钟；直到最后一丝光亮湮灭；直到他们和墙本身一同溶解进共同的神秘，只剩那士兵无聊的剪影，在更阴沉的一块秋日夜空前，难以觉察地、疲倦地挪动着。
我终于觉出自己渴得厉害；跳起身来，冲着铁栏喊叫。「求点喝的。」同卫兵军士好一番争执——军士怒气冲冲地说：「给他。」一名卫兵接过我的请求，从视野中消失，回来时带来一名武装更重的卫兵和一锡杯水。这几位绅士中的一位看住水和我不放，另一位则同挂锁搏斗。门开启了一条细缝，一名卫兵和水艰难地挤进来。另一名守在门口，枪随时待命。水放下了，进来者摆出一个我认为值得表彰的直立姿势；于是我礼貌地说了声「谢谢」（*Merci*），人没从板子上起来。他立刻开始厉声训话，讲我有可能用这锡杯把门锯开逃走的概率；并用明白无误的字眼夸奖了速度的重要。

===Original===

I smiled, asked pardon for my inherent stupidity (which speech seemed to anger him) and guzzled the so-called water without looking at it, having learned something from Noyon. With a long and dangerous look at their prisoner, the gentlemen of the guard withdrew, using inconceivable caution in the relocking of the door.
I laughed and fell asleep.
After (as I judged) four minutes of slumber, I was awakened by at least six men standing over me. The darkness was intense, it was extraordinarily cold. I glared at them and tried to understand what new crime I had committed. One of the six was repeating: “Get up, you are going away. Four o’clock.” After several attempts I got up. They formed a circle around me; and together we marched a few steps to a sort of storeroom, where my great sack, small sack, and overcoat were handed to me. A rather agreeably voiced guard then handed me a half-cake of chocolate, saying (but with a tolerable grimness): “You’ll need it, believe me.” I found my stick, at which “piece of furniture” they amused themselves a little until I showed its use, by catching the ring at the mouth of my sack in the curved end of the stick and swinging the whole business unaided on my back. Two new guards﻿—or rather gendarmes﻿—were now officially put in charge of my person; and the three of us passed down the lane, much to the interest of the sentinel, to whom I bade a vivid and unreturned adieu. I can see him perfectly as he stares stupidly at us, a queer shape in the gloom, before turning on his heel.
Toward the very station whereat some hours since I had disembarked with the Belgian deserter and my former escorts, we moved. I was stiff with cold and only half awake, but peculiarly thrilled. The gendarmes on either side moved grimly, without speaking; or returning monosyllables to my few questions. Yes, we were to take the train. I was going somewhere, then? “*B’en sûr.*”﻿—“Where?”﻿—“You will know in time.”
After a few minutes we reached the station, which I failed to recognize. The yellow flares of lamps, huge and formless in the night mist, some figures moving to and fro on a little platform, a rustle of conversation: everything seemed ridiculously suppressed, beautifully abnormal, deliciously insane.

===Chinese===

我笑了笑，为我天生的愚钝请求原谅（这话似乎激怒了他），然后看也没看就把那所谓的水灌了下去——在努瓦永我学到了点什么。卫兵绅士们朝他们的囚犯投来漫长而危险的一瞥，然后以难以想象的谨慎重新锁好门，退了出去。
我笑了笑，睡着了。
据我判断只睡了四分钟，我就被至少六个站在我上方的人弄醒了。夜色浓重，冷得出奇。我怒视他们，努力理解自己又犯了什么新罪。六人中有一个在反复说：「起来，你要走了。四点钟。」试了几次之后我起来了。他们在我四周围成一圈；我们一起迈出几步，进了一间储藏室，我的大口袋、小口袋和大衣交还到我手上。接着，一个嗓音还算悦耳的卫兵递给我半块巧克力，说（语气还算过得去的严厉）：「你用得着的，信我。」我找到了我的手杖，这几位绅士对这件「家具」取笑了一会儿，直到我演示了它的用途——用杖的弯钩勾住口袋口的环，单枪匹马把全部家伙甩上背。两名新卫兵——确切说是宪兵——此时正式接管了我；我们三人顺着巷子走出去，让那哨兵大感兴趣，我同他生动地道了别，他没理我。他呆呆地瞪着我们的样子我看得清清楚楚：一个黑黢黢的怪影，在黑暗里，然后转身走了。
我们朝车站走去，几小时前我正是同那位比利时逃兵和先前的押送者在那个站下的车。我冻僵了，半睡半醒，却格外亢奋。两侧的宪兵沉默地走着，不说话；我寥寥几个问题，只换来单音节的回答。是的，我们坐火车。那我是要去某个地方了？「那可不（*B'en sûr*）。」——「去哪儿？」——「到时候你就知道了。」
几分钟后我们到了车站，我没认出它来。灯的黄焰在夜雾里又大又无形；几个身影在小月台上来回走动；一片窸窣的交谈：一切都压抑得可笑，异常得美妙，疯得可口。

===Original===

Every figure was wrapped with its individual ghostliness; a number of ghosts each out on his own promenade, yet each for some reason selecting this unearthly patch of the world, this putrescent and uneasy gloom. Even my guards talked in whispers. “Watch him, I’ll see about the train.” So one went off into the mist. I leaned dizzily against the wall nearest me (having plumped down my baggage) and stared into the darkness at my elbow, filled with talking shadows. I recognized *officiers anglais* wandering helplessly up and down, supported with their sticks; French lieutenants talking to each other here and there; the extraordinary sense-bereft station master at a distance looking like a cross between a jumping-jack and a goblin; knots of *permissionnaires* cursing wearily or joking hopelessly with one another or stalking back and forth with imprecatory gesticulations. “It’s a joke, too, you know, there are no more trains?”﻿—“The conductor is dead. I know his sister.”﻿—“Old chap, I am all in.”﻿—“Say, we are all lost.”﻿—“What time is it?”﻿—“My dear fellow, there is no more time, the French Government forbids it.” Suddenly burst out of the loquacious opacity a dozen handfuls of Algériens, their feet swaggering with fatigue, their eyes burning, apparently by themselves﻿—faceless in the equally black mist. By threes and fives they assaulted the goblin who wailed and shook his withered fist in their faces. There was no train. It had been taken away by the French Government. “How do I know how the poilus can get back to their regiments on time? Of course you’ll all of you be deserters, but is it my fault?” (I thought of my friend, the Belgian, at this moment lying in a pen at the prison which I had just quitted by some miracle.)﻿ ﻿… One of these fine people from uncivilized, ignorant, unwarlike Algeria was drunk and knew it, as did two of his very fine friends who announced that as there was no train he should have a good sleep at a farmhouse hard by, which farmhouse one of them claimed to espy through the impenetrable night. The drunk was accordingly escorted into the dark, his friends’ abrupt steps correcting his own large slovenly procedure out of earshot.﻿ ﻿… Some of the Black People sat down near me and smoked. Their enormous faces, wads of vital darkness, swooned with fatigue. Their vast gentle hands lay noisily about their knees.

===Chinese===

每个身影都裹着各自的鬼气；一群幽灵，各蹓各的，却偏偏都选中了世界上这块出尘之地，这块腐烂而不安的阴鹵。连我的押送者都压低了嗓门说话。「看着他，我去看看火车。」于是一个人走进雾里。我把行李咚地放下，晕眩地靠在最近的墙上，盯着我肘边的黑暗，那里面塞满了会说话的影子。我认出英国军官们拄着手杖，无助地踱来踱去；法国中尉们三三两两地交谈；远处那位失去了知觉的站长，看起来像活动木偶和小妖精的混种；一坨坨休假兵咒骂着、绝望地开着玩笑，或者踱来踱去打着手势发狠。「你知道吗，这也是个笑话，没有火车了？」——「车长死了。我认识他妹妹。」——「老兄，我彻底完了。」——「喂，咱们全完了。」——「几点了？」——「亲爱的，没有时间了，法国政府禁止时间。」忽然，从那咶噪的不透明里冲出十几拔阿尔及利亚人，双脚困倦地大掘大摆，眼晴燃烧着，形单影只——在同样漆黑的雾里没有脸。他们三五成群围攻那个小妖精，妖精哀嚎着朝他们挥舞干瘪的拳头。没有火车。火车被法国政府拿走了。「我怎么知道法国大兵怎么按时赶回团里？你们当然都会变成逃兵，可这难道是我的错？」（我想起我那位比利时朋友，此刻正躺在我刚刚奇迹般脱身的监狱的栏圈里。）……这群来自未开化、愚昧、不尚武的阿尔及利亚的好人里，有一个醉了，自己也知道；他两位同样出色的朋友也知道，并宣布既然没有火车，他就该在附近一家农舍好好睡一觉，那农舍其中一个声称透过不可穿透的夜色看到了。于是醉汉被护送进黑暗，朋友们急促的脚步把他那副慢腾腾的大动作纠正到听不见的地方。……几个黑皮肤的人在我近旁坐下抽烟。他们巨大的脸，一团团有生命力的黑暗，因疲倦而晕沉。他们宽大温柔的手摊在膝上，嗓音宏亮。

===Original===

The departed gendarme returned, with a bump, out of the mist. The train for Paris would arrive *de suite*. We were just in time, our movement had so far been very creditable. All was well. It was cold, eh?
Then with the ghastly miniature roar of an insane toy the train for Paris came fumbling into the station.﻿ ﻿…
We boarded it, due caution being taken that I should not escape. As a matter of fact I held up the would-be passengers for nearly a minute by my unaided attempts to boost my uncouth baggage aboard. Then my captors and I blundered heavily into a compartment in which an Englishman and two French women were seated. My gendarmes established themselves on either side of the door, a process which woke up the Anglo-Saxon and caused a brief gap in the low talk of the women. Jolt﻿—we were off.
I find myself with a *française* on my left and an *anglais* on my right. The latter has already uncomprehendingly subsided into sleep. The former (a woman of about thirty) is talking pleasantly to her friend, whom I face. She must have been very pretty before she put on the black. Her friend is also a *veuve*. How pleasantly they talk, of *la guerre*, of Paris, of the bad service; talk in agreeably modulated voices, leaning a little forward to each other, not wishing to disturb the dolt at my right. The train tears slowly on. Both the gendarmes are asleep, one with his hand automatically grasping the handle of the door. Lest I escape. I try all sorts of positions, for I find myself very tired. The best is to put my cane between my legs and rest my chin on it; but even that is uncomfortable, for the Englishman has writhed all over me by this time and is snoring creditably. I look him over; an Etonian, as I guess. Certain well-bred-well-fedness. Except for the position﻿—well, *c’est la guerre*. The women are speaking softly. “And do you know, my dear, that they had raids again in Paris? My sister wrote me.”﻿—“One has excitement always in a great city, my dear.”﻿—
Bump, slowing down. **Bump﻿—bump.**
It is light outside. One sees the world. There is a world still, the *gouvernement français* has not taken it away, and the air must be beautifully cool. In the compartment it is hot. The gendarmes smell worst. I know how I smell. What polite women.
“*Enfin, nous voilà.*” My guards awoke and yawned pretentiously. Lest I should think they had dozed off. It is Paris.

===Chinese===

走开的那位宪兵咚地从雾里回来了。去巴黎的火车马上（*de suite*）就到。我们来得正是时候，我们此前的行动非常值得表扬。一切都好。天儿真冷，嗯？
随后，随着一声疯玩具般可怕的小吼，去巴黎的火车摸索着进了站。…
我们上了车，为防我逃跑，一切必要的谨慎都用上了。实际上，我凭一己之力往车上驮那堆粗笨的行李，把想上车的乘客足足拦了将近一分钟。然后我和我的捕获者们重重地闯进一间车室，里面坐着一位英国人和两位法国女人。我的宪兵们在门两侧各就各位，这一过程惊醒了那位盎格鲁-撒克逊人，也让女人们压低的交谈出现了短暂的停顿。一顿——我们出发了。
我发现自己左边一位法国女人（*française*），右边一位英国人（*anglais*）。后者已经茫然地重新沉入睡眠。前者（三十上下的女人）正同她的朋友愉快地交谈，我正对着那位朋友。她穿上丧服之前想必非常漂亮。她的朋友也是一位寡妇（*veuve*）。她们谈得多愉快啊，谈战争（*la guerre*），谈巴黎，谈糟糕的服务；声音抑扬有致，微微前倾向对方，不想惊扰我右边那个呆子。火车慢慢地飞驰。两个宪兵都睡着了，一个的手还不由自主地抓住门把。怕我跑了。我试了各种姿势，因为我发现自己累极了。最好的办法是把手杖夹在两腿间，下巴搁在上面；可就这样也不舒服，因为那位英国人这会儿已经在我身上滚来滚去，鼾声可嘉。我打量他；依我猜是个伊顿仔。某种教养良好、喂养良好的气质。撇开姿势不谈——唉，这就是战争（*c'est la guerre*）。女人们在轻声说话。「亲爱的，你知道吗，巴黎又挨空袭了？我妹妹写信告诉我的。」——「在大城市里，人总是有热闹看，亲爱的。」——
一顿，减速。**一顿——一顿。**
外面亮了。看得见世界了。世界还在，法国政府（*gouvernement français*）还没把它拿走，空气想必凉爽宜人。车室里很热。宪兵们味儿最大。我知道我自己什么味儿。多么有礼貌的女人们。
「终于，到了。」（*Enfin, nous voilà.*）我的卫兵们醒了，装模作样地打了个哈欠。免得我以为他们睡着了。这里是巴黎。

===Original===

Some *permissionaires* cried “Paris.” The woman across from me said “Paris, Paris.” A great shout came up from every insane drowsy brain that had travelled with us﻿—a fierce and beautiful cry, which went the length of the train.﻿ ﻿… Paris, where one forgets, Paris, which is Pleasure, Paris, in whom our souls live, Paris, the beautiful, Paris at last.
The Englishman woke up and said heavily to me: “I say, where are we?”﻿—“Paris,” I answered, walking carefully on his feet as I made my baggage-laden way out of the compartment. It was Paris.
My guards hurried me through the station. One of them (I saw for the first time) was older than the other, and rather handsome with his Van Dyck blackness of curly beard. He said that it was too early for the metro, it was closed. We should take a car. It would bring us to the other station from which our next train left. We should hurry. We emerged from the station and its crowds of crazy men. We boarded a car marked something. The conductress, a strong, pink-cheeked, rather beautiful girl in black, pulled my baggage in for me with a gesture which filled all of me with joy. I thanked her, and she smiled at me. The car moved along through the morning.
We descended from it. We started off on foot. The car was not the right car. We would have to walk to the station. I was faint and almost dead from weariness and I stopped when my overcoat had fallen from my benumbed arm for the second time: “How far is it?” The older gendarme returned briefly, “Twenty minutes.” I said to him: “Will you help me carry these things?” He thought, and told the younger to carry my small sack filled with papers. The latter grunted, “*C’est défendu.*” We went a little farther, and I broke down again. I stopped dead, and said: “I can’t go any farther.” It was obvious to my escorts that I couldn’t, so I didn’t trouble to elucidate. Moreover, I was past elucidation.
The older stroked his beard. “Well,” he said, “would you care to take a cab?” I merely looked at him. “If you wish to call a cab, I will take out of your money, which I have here and which I must not give to you, the necessary sum, and make a note of it, subtracting from the original amount a sufficiency for our fare to the Gare. In that case we will not walk to the Gare, we will in fact ride.” “Please,” was all I found to reply to this eloquence.

===Chinese===

几个休假兵喊：「巴黎。」我对面的女人说：「巴黎，巴黎。」同车而来的每一颗疯而困的脑袋都爆发出一声呐喊——凶猛而美丽的呐喊，传遍了整列火车。…巴黎，让人遗忘之城；巴黎，即快乐本身；巴黎，我们的灵魂栖居于她；巴黎，美人；巴黎，终于到了。
英国人醒来，沉重地对我说：「我说，这是哪儿？」——「巴黎。」我答，一边踩着他的脚，驮着行李蹒跚出室。这里是巴黎。
我的卫兵们押着我匆匆穿过车站。其中一个（我头一次看清）比另一个年长，范代克式的黑卷髯颇为英像。他说地铁还太早，没开门。我们该坐电车。它会把我们带到另一个车站，我们下一班火车从那儿发车。我们得抓紧。我们走出车站，走出那群疯子。我们上了一辆标着什么字样的电车。女售票员，一个健壮、粉颊、相当漂亮的黑衣姑娘，伸手替我把行李拽了上来，那动作让我浑身欢喜。我谢了她，她朝我微笑。电车在晨光中前行。
我们下了车。我们开始步行。那辆电车坐错了。我们只好走到车站去。我又晕又累，几乎要死，大衣第二次从我麻木的胳膊上滑落时，我停了下来：「还有多远？」年长的宪兵简短地答：「二十分钟。」我对他说：「您帮我扛一下这些行不行？」他想了想，吩咐年轻的那个扛起我那只装满文件的小口袋。后者咕哝了一声：「这是禁止的（*C'est défendu*）。」我们又走了一小段，我又垮了。我一动不动地站住，说：「我走不动了。」我的押送者看得出我确实走不动了，所以我也不费劲解释。况且，我也解释不动了。
年长的捋着胡子。「那么，」他说，「您愿意叫辆马车吗？」我只看着他。「如果您想叫车，我就从您的钱里——钱在我这儿，不能交给您——取所需数目，记一笔账，从原额里扣除我们去车站的足够车费。那样的话我们就不走着去车站了，实际上是坐车去。」「请便。」面对这番雄辩，我只找到这一句回答。

===Original===

Several empty cabs had gone by during the peroration of the law, and no more seemed to offer themselves. After some minutes, however, one appeared and was duly hailed. Nervously (he was shy in the big city) the older asked if the driver knew where the Gare was. “*Laquelle?*” demanded the *cocher* angrily. And when he was told﻿—“Of course, I know, why not?” We got in; I being directed to sit in the middle, and my two bags and fur coat piled on top of us all.
So we drove through the streets in the freshness of the full morning, the streets full of a few divine people who stared at me and nudged one another, the streets of Paris﻿ ﻿… the drowsy ways wakening at the horses’ hoofs, the people lifting their faces to stare.
We arrived at the Gare, and I recognized it vaguely. Was it D’Orléans? We dismounted, and the tremendous transaction of the fare was apparently very creditably accomplished by the older. The *cocher* gave me a look and remarked whatever it is Paris drivers remark to Paris cab horses, pulling dully at the reins. We entered the station and I collapsed comfortably on a bench; the younger, seating himself with enormous pomposity at my side, adjusted his tunic with a purely feminine gesture expressive at once of pride and nervousness. Gradually my vision gained in focus. The station has a good many people in it. The number increases momently. A great many are girls. I am in a new world﻿—a world of chic femininity. My eyes devour the inimitable details of costume, the inexpressible nuances of pose, the indescribable démarche of the midinette. They hold themselves differently. They have even a little bold color here and there on skirt or blouse or hat. They are not talking about *La Guerre*. Incredible. They appear very beautiful, these Parisiennes.
And simultaneously with my appreciation of the crisp persons about me comes the hitherto unacknowledged appreciation of my uncouthness. My chin tells my hand of a good quarter inch of beard, every hair of it stiff with dirt. I can feel the dirt-pools under my eyes. My hands are rough with dirt. My uniform is smeared and creased in a hundred thousand directions. My puttees and shoes are prehistoric in appearance.﻿ ﻿…
My first request was permission to visit the *vespasienne*. The younger didn’t wish to assume any unnecessary responsibilities; I should wait till the older returned. There he was now.

===Chinese===

在这番法律的雄辩进行期间，几辆空马车驶了过去，再没有新的出现。不过几分钟后，来了一辆，被适时地叫住。年长的有些紧张地（他在这大城市里局促）问车夫知不知道车站在哪儿。「哪个（*Laquelle*）？」车夫愤怒地问。被告知之后——「我当然知道，怎么啦？」我们上了车；我被指定坐中间，我那两只口袋和毛皮大衣全堆在我们三人头顶。
就这样，我们在清冽的清晨里驱车穿过街道，街上稀稀落落几个神明般的人盯着我看，彼此碰肘示意，巴黎的街道…睡意沉沉的路在马蹄下醒来，人们抬起脸来张望。
我们到了车站，我依稀认出了它。是奥尔良站？我们下车，车费的那笔隆重交易由年长的办得显然非常体面。车夫看了我一眼，说了句巴黎车夫对巴黎马车必然要说的那句话，闷闷地拉着繉绳。我们进了车站，我舒服地瘫在长凳上；年轻的那个以极度的威严在我旁边坐下，用一个十足女性的动作整了整军装上衣，既流露骄傲，又流露紧张。渐渐地，我的视线有了焦距。车站里有不少人。人数每一刻都在增加。很多是姑娘。我到了一个新世界——一个时撜女性的世界。我的眼睛贪螬着那无可模仿的衣装细节、不可言传的姿态微妙、难以形容的小女工步态（*démarche*）。她们自有一种仪态。她们的裙子上、衣衫上、帽子上，这里那里甚至有一点大胆的颜色。她们不谈战争（*La Guerre*）。难以置信。她们显得非常美，这些巴黎女人。
与此同时，在我欣赏周围这些爽利人儿的当口，我对自己那副粗饬相的鉴赏也油然而生。下巴告诉我的手：那儿有足足四分之一英寸的胡子，每一根都硬挺着污垢。我能感觉到眼睛下面的污垢池。我的手粗糙全是泥。我的制服朝十万八千个方向抹皱交加。我的绑腿和鞋看起来属于史前时代。…
我的第一个请求是准许我去趟公共小便池（*vespasienne*）。年轻的那个不愿承担任何不必要的责任；我应该等年长的回来。喏，他这会儿回来了。

===Original===

I might ask him. The older benignly granted my petition, nodding significantly to his fellow-guard, by whom I was accordingly escorted to my destination and subsequently back to my bench. When we got back the gendarmes held a consultation of terrific importance; in substance, the train which should be leaving at that moment (six something) did not run today. We should therefore wait for the next train, which leaves at twelve-something-else. Then the older surveyed me and said almost kindly: “How would you like a cup of coffee?”﻿—“Much,” I replied sincerely enough.﻿—“Come with me,” he commanded, resuming instantly his official manner. “And you” (to the younger) “watch his baggage.”
Of all the very beautiful women whom I had seen the most very beautiful was the large and circular lady who sold a cup of perfectly hot and genuine coffee for two cents, just on the brink of the station, chatting cheerfully with her many customers. Of all the drinks I ever drank, hers was the most sacredly delicious. She wore, I remember, a tight black dress in which enormous and benignant breasts bulged and sank continuously. I lingered over my tiny cup, watching her swift big hands, her round nodding face, her large sudden smile. I drank two coffees, and insisted that my money should pay for our drinks. Of all the treating which I shall ever do, the treating of my captor will stand unique in pleasure. Even he half appreciated the sense of humor involved; though his dignity did not permit a visible acknowledgment thereof.
*Madame la vendeuse de café*, I shall remember you for more than a little while.
Having thus consummated breakfast, my guardian suggested a walk. Agreed. I felt I had the strength of ten because the coffee was pure. Moreover it would be a novelty to *me promener sans* 150-odd pounds of baggage. We set out.
As we walked easily and leisurely the by this time well peopled streets of the vicinity, my guard indulged himself in pleasant conversation. Did I know Paris much? He knew it all. But he had not been in Paris for several (eight was it?) years. It was a fine place, a large city to be sure. But always changing. I had spent a month in Paris while waiting for my uniform and my assignment to a *section sanitaire*? And my friend was with me? H-mmm-mm.
A perfectly typical runt of a Paris bull eyed us.

===Chinese===

我可以去问他。年长的仁慈地批准了我的请求，朝同僚意味深长地点了点头，于是我被后者押送到目的地，又被押回长凳。回来之后，宪兵们开了一场极其重要的会议；实质内容是：本应此刻（六点几分）发车的火车今天停开。所以我们得等下一班，十二点几分发车。然后年长的打量了我一番，几乎和蔼地说：「来杯咖啡怎么样？」——「好啊。」我够真诚地答道。——「跟我来。」他命令道，立刻恢复了官员派头。「你（对年轻的那个）看着他的行李。」
在我见过的所有非常美丽的女人里，最美丽的是车站门口那位高大圆润的妇人，她以两分钱的价格卖一杯滚烫纯正的咖啡，一边同她的众多顾客愉快地聊天。在我喝过的所有饮料里，她那杯是最神圣的美味。我记得她穿一件紧身的黑裙，巨大而慈祥的胸脯不停地起伏。我慢慢抿着那小小的杯子，看她灵巧的大手，看她圆圆的、不住点动的脸，看她突然而至的大笑。我喝了两杯，并坚持用我的钱付账。在我此生所有的请客里，请我的捕获者这一遭将永远是独一无二的乐事。连他都多少体会到了其中的幽默感；只是他的尊严不允许他公开承认。
卖咖啡的女士（*Madame la vendeuse de café*），我会记得你，不止一时半刻。
早餐如此大功告成之后，我的监护人提议散散步。同意。我觉得自己有十个人的力气，因为那咖啡是纯的。何况不用驮一百五十多磅行李地散步（*me promener sans*）对我来说也是新鲜事。我们出发了。
当我们轻松悠闲地走过这时已人烟渐稠的附近街道时，我的卫兵兴致勃勃地聊了起来。我很熟悉巴黎吗？他熟透了。不过他有好几年（八年？）没来巴黎了。好地方，当然是个大城市。但总是在变。我在等制服和卫生分队（*section sanitaire*）分配的那一个月是在巴黎度过的？我朋友和我在一起？嗯——嗯。
一个十足的巴黎条子式的小矬子盯上了我们。

===Original===

The older saluted him with infinite respect, the respect of a shabby rube deacon for a well-dressed burglar. They exchanged a few well-chosen words, in French of course. “What ya got there?”﻿—“An American.”﻿—“What’s wrong with him?”﻿—“H-mmm” mysterious shrug of the shoulders followed by a whisper in the ear of the city thug. The latter contented himself with “Ha-aaa”﻿—plus a look at me which was meant to wipe me off the earth’s face (I pretended to be studying the morning meanwhile). Then we moved on, followed by ferocious stares from the Paris bull. Evidently I was getting to be more of a criminal every minute; I should probably be shot tomorrow, not (as I had assumed erroneously) the day after. I drank the morning with renewed vigor, thanking heaven for the coffee, Paris; and feeling complete confidence in myself. I should make a great speech (in Midi French). I should say to the firing squad: “Gentlemen, *c’est de la blague, tu sais? Moi, je connais la soeur du conducteur.*”﻿ ﻿… They would ask me when I preferred to die. I should reply, “Pardon me, you wish to ask me when I prefer to become immortal?” I should answer: “What matter? It’s all the same to me, because there isn’t any more time﻿—the French Government forbids it.”
My laughter surprised the older considerably. He would have been more astonished had I yielded to the well-nigh irrepressible inclination, which at the moment suffused me, to clap him heartily upon the back.
Everything was blague. The driver, the café, the police, the morning, and least and last the excellent French Government.
We had walked for a half hour or more. My guide and protector now inquired of a workingman the location of the *boucheries*? “There is one right in front of you,” he was told. Sure enough, not a block away. I laughed again. It was eight years all right.
The older bought a great many things in the next five minutes: sausage, cheese, bread, chocolate, *pinard rouge*. A bourgeoise with an unagreeable face and suspicion of me written in headlines all over her mouth served us with quick hard laconicisms of movement. I hated her and consequently refused my captor’s advice to buy a little of everything (on the ground that it would be a long time till the next meal), contenting myself with a cake of chocolate﻿—rather bad chocolate, but nothing to what I was due to eat during the next three months.

===Chinese===

年长的以无限的敬意向他敬礼，那是一个衣衫褴褛的乡下教堂执事对衣着体面的盗贼的敬意。他们用法语寒暄了几句精选的话。「你那儿是什么人？」——「一个美国人。」——「他怎么了？」——「嗯——」神秘地耸耸肩，接着凑到这城市恶棍耳边一阵低语。后者只回了声「哈——啊——」——外加一个意在把我从地球表面抹掉的眼神（我则假装在研究早晨）。然后我们继续前进，身后追随着巴黎条子凶狠的瞪视。显然，我每一分钟都在变得更像一个罪犯；多半我明天就要被枪毙，而不是（我先前误以为的）后天。我以新的活力畅饮着这个早晨，为那咖啡、为巴黎感谢上苍；对自己满怀信心。我将发表一篇伟大的演说（用南方法语）。我要对行刑队说：「先生们，这是个玩笑，知道吧？（*c'est de la blague, tu sais?*）我啊，我认识车长的妹妹。（*Moi, je connais la soeur du conducteur.*）」…他们会问我愿意什么时候死。我会答：「对不起，您是想问我愿意什么时候不朽？」我会回答：「有什么要紧？对我来说都一样，因为已经没有时间了——法国政府禁止时间。」
我的笑声让年长的大吃一惊。倘若我屈服于当时几乎按捺不住的冲动——热情地拍拍他的背——他还会更吃惊。
一切都是玩笑（blague）。车夫，咖啡馆，警察，早晨，以及最末最小最妙的：那个无可挑剔的法国政府。
我们走了半个钟头或者更多。我的向导兼保护人这时向一个工人打听肉铺（*boucheries*）在哪儿？「您眼前就有一家。」人家告诉他。果然，不到一个街区。我又笑了。确实是八年了。
接下来的五分钟里年长的买了许多东西：香肠、奶酪、面包、巧克力、红酒（*pinard rouge*）。一位面色不讨喜的资产阶级主妇——怀疑我用大标题写满了她整个嘴角——用快捷、生硬、言简意赅的动作招待我们。我恨她，于是拒绝了捕获者每样都买点的建议（理由是距下顿饭还早得很），只给自己买了一块巧克力——相当差的巧克力，但比起接下来三个月我要吃的东西还算不上什么。

===Original===

Then we retraced our steps, arriving at the station after several mistakes and inquiries, to find the younger faithfully keeping guard over my two *sacs* and overcoat.
The older and I sat down, and the younger took his turn at promenading. I got up to buy a *Fantasio* at the stand ten steps away, and the older jumped up and escorted me to and from it. I think I asked him what he would read? and he said “Nothing.” Maybe I bought him a journal. So we waited, eyed by everyone in the Gare, laughed at by the officers and their *marraines*, pointed at by sinewy dames and decrepit *bonhommes*﻿—the centre of amusement for the whole station. In spite of my reading I felt distinctly uncomfortable. Would it never be Twelve? Here comes the younger, neat as a pin, looking fairly sterilized. He sits down on my left. Watches are ostentatiously consulted. It is time. *En avant.* I sling myself under my bags.
“Where are we going now?” I asked the older. Curling the tips of his mustachios, he replied, “Mah-say.”
Marseilles! I was happy once more. I had always wanted to go to that great port of the Mediterranean, where one has new colors and strange customs, and where the people sing when they talk. But how extraordinary to have come to Paris﻿—and what a trip lay before us. I was much muddled about the whole thing. Probably I was to be deported. But why from Marseilles? Where was Marseilles anyway? I was probably all wrong about its location. Who cared, after all? At least we were leaving the pointings and the sneers and the half-suppressed titters.﻿ ﻿…
Two fat and respectable *bonhommes*, the two gendarmes, and I, made up one compartment. The former talked an animated stream, the guards and I were on the whole silent. I watched the liquidating landscape and dozed happily. The gendarmes dozed, one at each door. The train rushed lazily across the earth, between farmhouses, into fields, along woods﻿ ﻿… the sunlight smacked my eye and cuffed my sleepy mind with colour.
I was awakened by a noise of eating. My protectors, knife in hand, were consuming their meat and bread, occasionally tilting their *bidons* on high and absorbing the thin streams which spurted therefrom. I tried a little chocolate. The *bonhommes* were already busy with their repast. The older gendarme watched me chewing away at the chocolate, then commanded, “Take some bread.”

===Chinese===

然后我们原路返回，走错几处、又问了几次路之后回到车站，发现年轻的那个忠实地看守着我的两只口袋（*sacs*）和大衣。
年长的和我坐下，年轻的去蹓他的班。我起身到十步外的报摊买本《 Fantasio 》，年长的跳起来，押着我去又押着我回。我想我问过他看点什么？他说「什么都不看」。也许我给他买了份报。我们就这样等着，全车站的人都看着我们，军官们和他们的教母们（*marraines*）笑我们，筋健的太太们和老朽的好好先生们（*bonhommes*）对我们指指点点——整个车站的笑料中心。尽管有读物，我还是明显地不自在。十二点怎么还不到？年轻的来了，干净得一丝不苟，简直是消了毒的样子。他在我左边坐下。表被郑重其事地看了看。到点了。出发（*En avant*）。我把身子钻到口袋底下。
「现在去哪儿？」我问年长的。他捻着髭尖，答：「马赛（Mah-say）。」
马赛！我又幸福了。我一直想去那个地中海大港，那里有新的色彩、奇怪的习俗，那里的人说话像唱歌。可是竟然取道巴黎——而前方的旅途又是何等漫长。我对整件事稀里糊涂。多半是要把我驱逐出境。可为什么从马赛走？马赛到底在哪儿？我对它的方位八成全弄错了。管他呢，谁在乎？至少我们正离开那些指指点点、嗤笑和憋着的窃笑。…
两个胖而体面的好好先生、两个宪兵，加上我，凑成一间车室。前两位聊得热火朝天，宪兵们和我总体上沉默。我看着液化的风景出神，幸福地打起了盹。宪兵们也打盹，一人守一扇门。火车懒洋洋地掠过大地，掠过农舍，掠进田野，沿树林而行…阳光拍打我的眼睛，用色彩掴我困倦的头脑。
我被一阵吃东西的声音吵醒。我的保护人们手持刀子，正大嚼面包夹肉，不时把水壶（*bidons*）举高，吸吮里面喷出的细流。我尝了点巧克力。好好先生们早已忙于自己的餐饭。年长的宪兵看我嚼着巧克力，便命令道：「拿点面包吃。」

===Original===

This astonished me, I confessed, beyond anything which had heretofore occurred. I gazed mutely at him, wondering whether the *gouvernement français* had made away with his wits. He had relaxed amazingly: his cap lay beside him, his tunic was unbuttoned, he slouched in a completely undisciplined posture﻿—his face seemed to have been changed for a peasant’s, it was almost open in expression and almost completely at ease. I seized the offered hunk, and chewed vigorously on it. Bread was bread. The older appeared pleased with my appetite; his face softened still more, as he remarked: “Bread without wine doesn’t taste good,” and proffered his *bidon*. I drank as much as I dared, and thanked him: “*Ça va mieux.*” The pinard went straight to my brain, I felt my mind cuddled by a pleasant warmth, my thoughts became invested with a great contentment. The train stopped; and the younger sprang out, carrying the empty canteens of himself and his comrade. When they and he returned, I enjoyed another cup. From that moment till we reached our destination at about eight o’clock the older and I got on extraordinarily well. When the gentlemen descended at their station he waxed almost familiar. I was in excellent spirits; rather drunk; extremely tired. Now that the two guardians and myself were alone in the compartment, the curiosity which had hitherto been stifled by etiquette and pride of capture came rapidly to light. Why was I here, anyway? I seemed well enough to them.﻿—Because my friend had written some letters, I told them.﻿—But I had done nothing myself?﻿—I explained that we used to be together all the time, *mon ami et moi*; that was the only reason which I knew of.﻿—It was very funny to see how this explanation improved matters. The older in particular was immensely relieved.﻿—I would without doubt, he said, be set free immediately upon my arrival. The French government didn’t keep people like me in prison.﻿—They fired some questions about America at me, to which I imaginatively replied. I think I told the younger that the average height of buildings in America was nine hundred metres. He stared and shook his head doubtfully, but I convinced him in the end. Then in my turn I asked questions, the first being: Where was my friend?﻿—It seems that my friend had left Gré (or whatever it was) the morning of the day I had entered it.

===Chinese===

我承认，这让我比此前发生的任何事情都吃惊。我哑哑地盯着他，纳闷法国政府（*gouvernement français*）是不是把他的脑子也收走了。他松弛得惊人：帽子摘在旁边，上衣敞着扣子，整个人歪成一副毫无纪律的坐姿——他的脸像换成了庄稼汉的，表情几乎坦荡，几乎彻底放松。我接过递来的面包，大嚼起来。面包就是面包。年长的似乎对我的胃口很满意；他的脸更柔和了，一边说：「光吃面包不就酒，没味儿。」一边递上他的水壶。我放量喝了一回，谢他：「好多了（*Ça va mieux*）。」酒直冲脑门，我觉得脑子被一股暖意抱着，思绪里充盈起巨大的满足。火车一停；年轻的跳出去，拎着他和同伴的空水壶。等他和水壶回来，我又享了一杯。从那一刻直到八点左右抵达目的地，我和年长的处得出奇地好。到站时，好好先生们下车，他几乎显得亲热了。我情绪极佳；微醺；累到极点。车室里只剩下两位监护人和我，先前被礼节和捕获者的自尊压下去的好奇，此刻迅速抬头。我到底为什么在这儿？他们看我不像坏人。——因为我朋友写了些信，我告诉他们。——可我自己什么也没干？——我解释说，我和我朋友从前总是形影不离（*mon ami et moi*）；我知道的原因只有这一个。——看到这个解释让事态好转，真是有趣极了。年长的尤其如释重负。——他说，我一到地方准会立刻获释。法国政府不会把像我这样的人关在监狱里。——他们朝我发射了几个关于美国的问题，我发挥想象一一作答。我想我告诉过年轻的那个，美国房子的平均高度是九百米。他瞪着眼、迟疑地摇头，但我最终还是说服了他。然后轮到我问问题，第一个是：我朋友在哪儿？——似乎我朋友在我进那地方的头一天早上就离开了格雷（Gré，或者别的什么名字）。

===Original===

﻿—Did they know where my friend was going?﻿—They couldn’t say. They had been told that he was very dangerous.﻿—So we talked on and on: How long had I studied French? I spoke very well. Was it hard to learn English?﻿—
Yet when I climbed out to relieve myself by the roadside one of them was at my heels.
Finally watches were consulted, tunics buttoned, hats donned. I was told in a gruff voice to prepare myself; that we were approaching the end of our journey. Looking at the erstwhile participants in conversation, I scarcely knew them. They had put on with their caps a positive ferocity of bearing. I began to think that I had dreamed the incidents of the preceding hours.
We descended at a minute, dirty station which possessed the air of having been dropped by mistake from the bung of the *gouvernement français*. The older sought out the station master, who having nothing to do was taking a siesta in a miniature waiting-room. The general countenance of the place was exceedingly depressing; but I attempted to keep up my spirits with the reflection that after all all this was but a junction, and that from here we were to take a train for Marseilles herself. The name of the station, Briouse, I found somewhat dreary. And now the older returned with the news that our train wasn’t running today, and that the next train didn’t arrive till early morning and should we walk to Marseilles? I could check my great *sac* and overcoat. The small *sac* I should carry along﻿—it was only a step, after all.
With a glance at the desolation of Briouse I agreed to the stroll. It was a fine night for a little promenade; not too cool, and with a promise of a moon stuck into the sky. The *sac* and coat were accordingly checked by the older; the station master glanced at me and haughtily grunted (having learned that I was an American); and my protectors and I set out.
I insisted that we stop at the first café and have some wine on me. To this my escorts agreed, making me go ten paces ahead of them, and waiting until I was through before stepping up to the bar﻿—not from politeness, to be sure, but because (as I soon gathered) gendarmes were not any too popular in this part of the world, and the sight of two gendarmes with a prisoner might inspire the habitués to attempt a rescue.

===Chinese===

——他们知道我朋友去哪儿了吗？——说不上来。上头告诉他们，此人非常危险。——于是我们继续聊啊聊：我法语学了多久？我讲得很好。英语难学吗？——
可当我爬下车到路边方便时，其中一个还是跟在我屁股后头。
终于，表被看了看，上衣扣上，帽子戴上。一个粗厉的声音通知我做好准备；我们快到旅程的终点了。看看方才那两位交谈的参与者，我几乎认不得他们了。戴上帽子，他们平添了一股凶狠的架势。我开始以为前几个钟头里的事是我梦见的。
我们在一个细小、肮脏的车站下了车，那车站一副被法国政府从酒桶塞子里失手掉下来的气质。年长的去找站长，站长无事可做，正在一间微型候车室里打盹。这地方的整体面貌沮丧透顶；但我努力振作精神，安慰自己说这毕竟只是个接驳站，我们还要在这儿换乘去马赛本塞的火车。车站的名字，布里乌斯（Briouse），我觉得有些凄凉。这时年长的回来了，带来的消息是：我们的火车今天不开，下一班要等到清晨才到——要不我们走着去马赛？我的大口袋（*sac*）和大衣可以寄存。小口袋我得随身带——毕竟只是一步路。
我瞥了一眼布里乌斯的荒凉，同意遛达一趟。这样的夜晚正适合小小散步；不算太冷，天上还插着一枚月亮的期票。口袋和大衣由年长的寄存了；站长瞥了我一眼，傲慢地哼了一声（他已得知我是美国人）；我和我的保护人们出发了。
我坚持在第一家咖啡馆停下，由我做东喝点酒。对此我的押送者们同意了，让我走在前面十步，等我喝完了他们才走近柜台——当然不是出于礼貌，而是因为（我很快便咂摸出来）宪兵在世界的这一带不太得人心，两个宪兵押着一个囚犯的景象，没准会激发常客们实施一次营救。

===Original===

Furthermore, on leaving the café (a desolate place if I ever saw one, with a fearful *patronne*) I was instructed sharply to keep close to them but on no account to place myself between them, there being sundry villagers to be encountered before we struck the high road for Marseilles. Thanks to their forethought and my obedience the rescue did not take place, nor did our party excite even the curiosity of the scarce and soggy inhabitants of the unlovely town of Briouse.
The high road won, all of us relaxed considerably. The *sac* full of suspicious letters which I bore on my shoulder was not so light as I had thought, but the kick of the Briouse pinard thrust me forward at a good clip. The road was absolutely deserted; the night hung loosely around it, here and there tattered by attempting moonbeams. I was somewhat sorry to find the way hilly, and in places bad underfoot; yet the unknown adventure lying before me, and the delicious silence of the night (in which our words rattled queerly like tin soldiers in a plush-lined box) boosted me into a condition of mysterious happiness. We talked, the older and I, of strange subjects. As I suspected, he had been not always a gendarme. He had seen service among the Arabs. He had always liked languages and had picked up Arabian with great ease﻿—of this he was very proud. For instance﻿—the Arabian way of saying “Give me to eat” was this; when you wanted wine you said so-and-so; “Nice day” was something else. He thought I could pick it up inasmuch as I had done so creditably with French. He was absolutely certain that English was much easier to learn than French, and would not be moved. Now what was the American language like? I explained that it was a sort of Argot-English. When I gave him some phrases he was astonished﻿—“It sounds like English!” he cried, and retailed his stock of English phrases for my approval. I tried hard to get his intonation of the Arabian, and he helped me on the difficult sounds. America must be a strange place, he thought.﻿ ﻿…
After two hours walking he called a halt, bidding us rest. We all lay flat on the grass by the roadside. The moon was still battling with clouds. The darkness of the fields on either side was total. I crawled on hands and knees to the sound of silver-trickling water and found a little spring-fed stream. Prone, weight on elbows, I drank heavily of its perfect blackness.

===Chinese===

此外，离开咖啡馆（如果说我见过荒凉的地方，这就是一个，还有个可怕的老板娘）之后，我被严厉地告知：要紧跟他们，但绝不能走在他们两人中间，因为在踏上马赛大道之前还要遇上三三两两的村民。多亏他们的深谋远虑和我的顺从，营救没有发生，我们这一行也未能激起布里乌斯这座丑镇上稀少而湿漉漉的居民哪怕一点点好奇。
上了大道，我们大家都放松了不少。我肩上那袋装满嫌疑信件的口袋并不像我想的那么轻，但布里乌斯红酒的劲儿推着我迈开不错的步子。路上绝无人迹；夜松垮垮地挂在路两侧，这里那里被挣扎的月光撕出破口。我发现路竟是丘陵起伏，有几处脚下还不平整，多少有些遗憾；然而前方未知的冒险，加上夜那可口的寂静（我们的谈话声在其中古怪地叮当作响，像丝绒衬盒里的锡兵），把我助推进一种神秘的幸福。我和年长的聊起各种奇奇怪怪的话题。正如我猜的，他并非一直是宪兵。他在阿拉伯人中间服过役。他一向喜欢语言，轻而易举就学会了阿拉伯语——对此他非常自豪。比如——阿拉伯语「给我吃的」是这样说的；想要酒时你这样说；「天气不错」又是另一种说法。他相信我也能学会，因为我法语学得那么出色。他绝对确信英语比法语好学得多，且不为所动。那美国话是什么样的？我解释说那是一种黑话英语（Argot-English）。我给了他几个短语，他惊呆了——「听着怎么像英语！」他喊道，并把他库存的英语短语兜售出来求我认可。我努力学他的阿拉伯语腔调，遇到难音他还帮我纠正。美国想必是个奇怪的地方，他觉得。…
走了两个钟头，他喊了暂停，让我们休息。我们全都平躺在路边的草地上。月亮还在同云搏斗。两侧田野的黑暗是全然的黑暗。我手脚并用爬向一泓银亮的水声，找到一条泉眼小溪。我俯卧下去，用胳膊肘撑着身子，痛饮它完美的黑。

===Original===

It was icy, talkative, minutely alive.
The older presently gave a perfunctory “*alors*”; we got up; I hoisted my suspicious utterances upon my shoulder, which recognized the renewal of hostilities with a neuralgic throb. I banged forward with bigger and bigger feet. A bird, scared, swooped almost into my face. Occasionally some night-noise pricked a futile, minute hole in the enormous curtain of soggy darkness. Uphill now. Every muscle thoroughly aching, head spinning, I half-straightened my no longer obedient body; and jumped: face to face with a little wooden man hanging all by itself in a grove of low trees.
—The wooden body, clumsy with pain, burst into fragile legs with absurdly large feet and funny writhing toes; its little stiff arms made abrupt cruel equal angles with the road. About its stunted loins clung a ponderous and jocular fragment of drapery. On one terribly brittle shoulder the droll lump of its neckless head ridiculously lived. There was in this complete silent doll a gruesome truth of instinct, a success of uncanny poignancy, an unearthly ferocity of rectangular emotion.
For perhaps a minute the almost obliterated face and mine eyed one another in the silence of intolerable autumn.
Who was this wooden man? Like a sharp black mechanical cry in the spongy organism of gloom stood the coarse and sudden sculpture of his torment; the big mouth of night carefully spurted the angular actual language of his martyred body. I had seen him before in the dream of some medieval saint, with a thief sagging at either side, surrounded with crisp angels. Tonight he was alone; save for myself, and the moon’s minute flower pushing between slabs of fractured cloud.
I was wrong, the moon and I and he were not alone.﻿ ﻿… A glance up the road gave me two silhouettes at pause. The gendarmes were waiting. I must hurry to catch up or incur suspicions by my sloth. I hastened forward, with a last look over my shoulder﻿ ﻿… the wooden man was watching us.
When I came abreast of them, expecting abuse, I was surprised by the older’s saying quietly “We haven’t far to go,” and plunging forward imperturbably into the night.
Nor had we gone a half hour before several dark squat forms confronted us: houses.

===Chinese===

它冰凉、健谈、微末而活泼。
过了一会儿年长的例行公事地说了声「那么（*alors*）」；我们爬起来；我把那一袋嫌疑言论重新驮上肩，肩膀以一阵神经痛承认战事重启。我迈着越来越大的脚往前砸。一只受惊的鸟几乎扑到我脸上。偶尔有什么夜声在湿透的黑暗巨幕上扎出一个徒劳的小孔。上坡了。每一块肌肉都痛透了，脑袋发晕，我把不再听话的身子半直起来；随即一惊：面对面撞上一个木刻小人，独自悬在一丛矮树的中间。
——那木刻的身躯，笨拙得痛苦，爆出两条细腿、两只大得荒唐的脚和扭动滑稽的脚趾；僵硬的小胳膊同路面构成突兀而残忍的直角。矮短的腰间坠着一块笨重而谐谑的破布。在一边脆得可怕的小肩膀上，那没有脖子的脑袋滑稽地顶着一坨，荒唐地活着。在这个彻底沉默的木偶身上，有一种可怖的直觉之真，一种惊心刺骨的成，一种感情被掰成直角的地狱般的凶狠。
在那难以忍受的秋夜寂静里，那张几乎磨没了的脸和我的脸彼此对视了大约一分钟。
这个木人是谁？他那受难的粗粝而突兀的雕像，像一声尖锐的黑色机械哭喊，戳在阴鹵的海绵状肌体里；夜的大嘴仔细地喷出他那殉难躯体的棱角分明的实体语言。我从前在某个中世纪圣徒的梦里见过他，两侧各坠着一名盗贼，四周绕着脆亮的友天使。今夜他独自一人；除了我，和那朵从碎云的板缝间挤出来的月亮的小花。
我错了，月亮、我和他并非独自。……朝路上一瞥，我看见两个暂停的剪影。宪兵们在等。我得赶紧跟上，否则我的怠惰要招疑。我加紧向前，回头望了最后一眼……那木人在目送我们。
等我赶上他们，准备好挨骂，年长的却平静地说了句「没多远了」，让我吃了一惊，随即不动声色地扎进夜色。
走了不到半个钟头，几团黑黢黢的矮墩形拦住了我们：房子。

===Original===

I decided that I did not like houses﻿—particularly as now my guardian’s manner abruptly changed; once more tunics were buttoned, holsters adjusted, and myself directed to walk between and keep always up with the others. Now the road became thoroughly afflicted with houses, houses not, however, so large and lively as I had expected from my dreams of Marseilles. Indeed we seemed to be entering an extremely small and rather disagreeable town. I ventured to ask what its name was. “Mah-say” was the response. By this I was fairly puzzled. However the street led us to a square, and I saw the towers of a church sitting in the sky; between them the round, yellow, big moon looked immensely and peacefully conscious﻿ ﻿… no one was stirring in the little streets, all the houses were keeping the moon’s secret.
We walked on.
I was too tired to think. I merely felt the town as a unique unreality. What was it? I knew﻿—the moon’s picture of a town. These streets with their houses did not exist, they were but a ludicrous projection of the moon’s sumptuous personality. This was a city of Pretend, created by the hypnotism of moonlight.﻿—Yet when I examined the moon she too seemed but a painting of a moon and the sky in which she lived a fragile echo of colour. If I blew hard the whole shy mechanism would collapse gently with a neat soundless crash. I must not, or lose all.
We turned a corner, then another. My guides conferred concerning the location of something, I couldn’t make out what. Then the older nodded in the direction of a long dull dirty mass not a hundred yards away, which (as near as I could see) served either as a church or a tomb. Toward this we turned. All too soon I made out its entirely dismal exterior. Grey long stone walls, surrounded on the street side by a fence of ample proportions and uniformly dull colour. Now I perceived that we made toward a gate, singularly narrow and forbidding, in the grey long wall. No living soul appeared to inhabit this desolation.
The older rang at the gate. A gendarme with a revolver answered his ring; and presently he was admitted, leaving the younger and myself to wait. And now I began to realize that this was the gendarmerie of the town, into which for safekeeping I was presently to be inducted for the night.

===Chinese===

我断定我不喜欢房子——尤其因为此时我监护人的态度骤变；上衣重新扣上，枪套重新调正，我被命令走在两人中间并时刻跟上。这时公路已被房子彻底感染，但这些房子不如我梦想马赛时想象的那么高大热闹。实际上我们像在进一座极小而相当讨嫌的镇子。我冒昧问它叫什么名字。「马——赛（Mah-say）」是回答。这让我相当纳闷。不过街道把我们引到一个广场，我看见一座教堂的双塔坐在天上；双塔之间，浑圆、澄黄、硕大的月亮显得浩瀚而安宁地自觉……小街上无人走动，所有的房子都保守着月亮的秘密。
我们继续走。
我累得不能思考。我只是把这镇子感觉为一种独一无二的不真实。它是什么？我知道——月亮的一幅镇子画。这些街和街上的房子并不存在，它们不过是月亮华艳人格的一个荒唐投影。这是一座假装之城，由月光的催眠术创造。——然而我端详月亮时，她也像只是一幅月亮的画，她所居的天空是色彩的脆弱回声。只要我用力吹口气，这整套羞怯的机关就会温柔地坍塌，一声干净的、无声的碎响。我万万不能吹，否则全都没有了。
我们拐过一道弯，又一道。我的向导们商量着什么的位置，我听不清是什么。然后年长的朝不到百码外一团晦暗肮脏的长形东西扬了扬下巴，那东西（依我尽力所见）要么当教堂用，要么当坟墓用。我们朝它转去。很快我就看满了它彻底阴郁的外表。灰色的长石墙，临街一面围着宽大而色调一律晦暗的栅栏。这时我看清我们是朝墙上的一道门走去，那门窄得出奇、拒人千里。这荒凉里不见一个活人。
年长的在门上按铃。一个挎左轮手枪的宪兵应了铃；很快他被放了进去，留下年轻的和我等待。这时我才明白，这是镇上的宪兵队，我马上就要被送进去安全过夜。

===Original===

My heart sank, I confess, at the thought of sleeping in the company of that species of humanity which I had come to detest beyond anything in hell or on earth. Meanwhile the doorman had returned with the older, and I was bidden roughly enough to pick up my baggage and march. I followed my guides down a corridor, up a staircase, and into a dark, small room where a candle was burning. Dazzled by the light and dizzied by the fatigue of my ten or twelve mile stroll, I let my baggage go; and leaned against a convenient wall, trying to determine who was now my tormentor.
Facing me at a table stood a man of about my own height, and, as I should judge, about forty years old. His face was seedy sallow and long. He had bushy semicircular eyebrows which drooped so much as to reduce his eyes to mere blinking slits. His cheeks were so furrowed that they leaned inward. He had no nose, properly speaking, but a large beak of preposterous widthlessness, which gave his whole face the expression of falling gravely downstairs, and quite obliterated the unimportant chin. His mouth was made of two long uncertain lips which twitched nervously. His cropped black hair was rumpled; his blouse, from which hung a croix de guerre, unbuttoned; and his unputteed shanks culminated in bed-slippers. In physique he reminded me a little of Ichabod Crane. His neck was exactly like a hen’s: I felt sure that when he drank he must tilt his head back as hens do in order that the liquid may run down their throats. But his method of keeping himself upright, together with certain spasmodic contractions of his fingers and the nervous “uh-ah, uh-ah” which punctuated his insecure phrases like uncertain commas, combined to offer the suggestion of a rooster; a rather moth-eaten rooster, which took itself tremendously seriously and was showing off to an imaginary group of admiring hens situated somewhere in the background of his consciousness.
“*Vous êtes, uh-ah, l’Am-é-ri-cain?*”
“*Je suis Américain*,” I admitted.
“*Eh-bi-en uh-ah uh-ah*﻿—We were expecting you.” He surveyed me with great interest.
Behind this seedy and restless personage I noted his absolute likeness, adorning one of the walls. The rooster was faithfully depicted à la Rembrandt at half-length in the stirring guise of a fencer, foil in hand, and wearing enormous gloves.

===Chinese===

我承认，一想到要同那种人类同桌共寝——那种我在地狱内外最最厌恶的人类——我的心就沉了下去。这时司门人同年长的一起回来了，我被打发——语气够粗鲁的——扛起行李开步。我跟着向导们走过一条走廊，爬上一道楼梯，进了一间点着蜡烛的黑暗小屋。我被光眩了眼，又被十来英里散步的疲惫晕了头，一松手让行李落了地；靠在一面现成的墙上，努力辨清现在轮到哪位来折磨我。
桌前对着我站着一个人，同我差不多高，依我看四十岁上下。他的脸枯黄而长。浓密的半圆形眉毛奋拉得很厉害，把眼睛挤成了两个眨巴的细缝。他的两颊沟壑深得向里倾陷。严格说他没有鼻子，只有一只宽得离谱的大啄，这让他的整张脸呈现出一种庄严滚下楼梯的表情，并把不重要的下巴彻底抹了。他的嘴由两片犹疑的长嘴唇构成，紧张地抽动。他剪短的黑色乱发蓬着；胸前挂着战争十字勋章的上衣敞着扣；没打绑腿的两条细腿终结于一双趿拉的拖鞋。体形上他让我想起一点伊卡博德·克莱恩。他的脖子活像一只母鸡的：我确信他喝水时必定像母鸡那样把头向后一仰，好让水流进喉咙。可他保持直立的方式，加上手指某些痉挛性的收缩，以及用不定的「呃——啊，呃——啊」给不牢靠的句子打上不确定逗号的习惯，合起来给出一只公鸡的意象；一只颇有些虫蛀的公鸡，自以为了不起，正在向他意识背景里想象中的一群爱慕的母鸡炫耀。
「您就是，呃——啊，那个美——利——坚人？」（*Vous êtes, uh-ah, l'Am-é-ri-cain?*）
「我是美国人。」（*Je suis Américain*）我承认。
「呃——啊，呃——啊——我们正等您呢。」他大感兴趣地打量我。
在这位枯槁而躁动的人物身后，我注意到他本人的绝对肖像，装饰在其中一面墙上。那只公鸡被忠实地画成伦勃朗风格的半身像，摆着击剑手的英姿，手持花剑，戴一双巨大的手套。

===Original===

The execution of this masterpiece left something to be desired; but the whole betokened a certain spirit and verve, on the part of the sitter, which I found difficulty in attributing to the being before me.
“*Vous êtes uh-ah **Kew-Mangz**?*”
“What?” I said, completely baffled by this extraordinary dissyllable.
“*Comprenez vous fran-çais?*”
“*Un peu.*”
“*Bon. Alors, vous vous ap-pel-lez **Kew Mangz**, n’est-ce pas? Edouard **Kew-Mangz**?*”
“Oh,” I said, relieved, “yes.” It was really amazing, the way he writhed around the *G*.
“*Comment ça se prononce en anglais?*”
I told him.
He replied benevolently, somewhat troubled “uh-ah uh-ah uh-ah﻿—why are you here, **Kew-Mangz**?”
At this question I was for one moment angrier than I had ever before been in all my life. Then I realized the absurdity of the situation, and laughed.﻿—“*Sais pas.*”
The questionnaire continued:
“You were in the Red Cross?”﻿—“Surely, in the Norton Harjes Ambulance, *Section Sanitaire Vingt-et-Un*.”﻿—“You had a friend there?”﻿—“Naturally.”﻿—“*Il a écrit, votre ami, des bêtises, n’est ce pas?*”﻿—“So they told me. *N’en sais rien.*”﻿—“What sort of person was your friend?”﻿—“He was a magnificent person, always *très gentil* with me.”﻿—(With a queer pucker the fencer remarked) “Your friend got you into a lot of trouble, though.”﻿—(To which I replied with a broad grin) “*N’importe*, we are *camarades*.”
A stream of puzzled uh-ahs followed this reply. The fencer, or rooster or whatever he might be, finally, picking up the lamp and the lock, said: “*Alors, viens avec moi, **Kew-Mangz**.*” I started to pick up the *sac*, but he told me it would be kept in the office (we being in the office). I said I had checked a large *sac* and my fur overcoat at Briouse, and he assured me they would be sent on by train. He now dismissed the gendarmes, who had been listening curiously to the examination. As I was conducted from the *bureau* I asked him point-blank: “How long am I to stay here?”﻿—to which he answered “*Oh, peut-être un jour, deux jours, je ne sais pas.*”
Two days in a gendarmerie would be enough, I thought. We marched out.
Behind me the bedslippered rooster uhahingly shuffled. In front of me clumsily gamboled the huge imitation of myself. It descended the terribly worn stairs. It turned to the right and disappeared.﻿ ﻿…
We were standing in a chapel.

===Chinese===

这件大师之作的执行不无可议之处；但整体上显示出坐画人的一种精神和锐气，我很难把它归给我面前这位存在。
「你是呃——啊**屈——芒**？」（*Vous êtes uh-ah **Kew-Mangz**?*）
「什么？」我说，被这个非同寻常的双音节彻底难住了。
「你懂法——语吗？」（*Comprenez vous fran-çais?*）
「一点（*Un peu*）。」
「好。那么，你叫**屈·芒**（**Kew Mangz**），对吧？爱德华**屈——芒**？」（*Edouard **Kew-Mangz**?*）
「哦，」我松了口气说，「对。」他在那个G上打转的方式真叫绝。
「英文里怎么念？」（*Comment ça se prononce en anglais?*）
我告诉了他。
他和蔼地、略带困扰地答道：「呃——啊——呃——啊——你怎么在这儿，**屈——芒**？」
听到这个问题，我有生以来头一次那么愤怒。随即我意识到处境的荒唐，笑了。——「不知道（*Sais pas*）。」
问答继续：
「你在红十字会待过？」——「当然，诺顿-哈耶斯救护队，第二十一卫生分队。」——「你在那儿有个朋友？」——「自然。」——「你朋友写了，呃，浑话，对吧？」（*Il a écrit, votre ami, des bêtises, n'est ce pas?*）——「他们这么说的。我什么都不知道（*N'en sais rien*）。」——「你朋友是什么样的人？」——「是个了不起的人，对我一向非常和气（*très gentil*）。」——（击剑手怪模怪样地皱起脸）「不过你朋友给你惹了大麻烦。」——（对此我咧嘴一笑）「不要紧（*N'importe*），我们是同志（*camarades*）。」
这个回答之后是一连串困惑的呃——啊。击剑手，或者说公鸡，或者随便他是什么，终于提起灯和锁，说：「那么，跟我来，**屈——芒**。」（*Alors, viens avec moi, **Kew-Mangz**.*）我伸手去提口袋，他告诉我口袋要存在办公室（我们正在办公室里）。我说我在布里乌斯寄存了一只大口袋和毛皮大衣，他向我保证会由火车转送过来。他随即打发走宪兵们，他们一直好奇地听着这场审问。当我被带出办公室（*bureau*）时，我直截了当地问他：「我要在这儿待多久？」——他的回答是：「哦，也许一天，两天，说不好（*peut-être un jour, deux jours, je ne sais pas*）。」
在宪兵队蹲两天也就够了，我想。我们走出去。
身后，趿拖鞋的公鸡呃啊呃啊地拖着步子。身前，我的巨大仿品笨拙地蹦跳。它下了那道磨得可怕的楼梯。它向右一拐，不见了。…
我们站在一座小教堂里。

===Original===

The shrinking light which my guide held had become suddenly minute; it was beating, senseless and futile, with shrill fists upon a thick enormous moisture of gloom. To the left and right through lean oblongs of stained glass burst dirty burglars of moonlight. The clammy stupid distance uttered dimly an uncanny conflict﻿—the mutterless tumbling of brutish shadows. A crowding ooze battled with my lungs. My nostrils fought against the monstrous atmospheric slime which hugged a sweet unpleasant odour. Staring ahead, I gradually disinterred the pale carrion of the darkness﻿—an altar, guarded with the ugliness of unlit candles, on which stood inexorably the efficient implements for eating God.
I was to be confessed, then, of my guilty conscience, before retiring? It boded well for the morrow.
… the measured accents of the fencer: “*Prenez votre paillasse.*” I turned. He was bending over a formless mass in one corner of the room. The mass stretched halfway to the ceiling. It was made of mattress-shapes. I pulled at one﻿—burlap, stuffed with prickly straw. I got it on my shoulder. “*Alors.*” He lighted me to the doorway by which we had entered. (I was somewhat pleased to leave the place.)
Back, down a corridor, up more stairs; and we were confronted by a small scarred pair of doors from which hung two of the largest padlocks I had ever seen. Being unable to go further, I stopped: he produced a huge ring of keys. Fumbled with the locks. No sound of life: the keys rattled in the locks with surprising loudness; the latter, with an evil grace, yielded﻿—the two little miserable doors swung open.
Into the square blackness I staggered with my paillasse. There was no way of judging the size of the dark room which uttered no sound. In front of me was a pillar. “Put it down by that post, and sleep there for tonight, in the morning *nous allons voir*,” directed the fencer. “You won’t need a blanket,” he added; and the doors clanged, the light and fencer disappeared.
I needed no second invitation to sleep. Fully dressed, I fell on my paillasse with a weariness which I have never felt before or since. But I did not close my eyes: for all about me there rose a sea of most extraordinary sound﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

我的向导手中那盏畏缩的光忽然变小了；它正用尖锐的小拳头，徒劳而无谓地捶打一堵厚得惊人的阴湿。左右两侧，脏兮兮的月光窃贼穿过瘦长的彩窗破囧而入。黏冷而愚蠢的远方暗暗发出一场诡异的冲突——无言地翻滚的兽性黑影。一股拥挤的黏液同我的肺搏斗。我的鼻孔抗击着那团搂着甜腻不快气味的巨大的大气黏泥。我睁大眼向前看，渐渐从黑暗中发掘出苍白的尸骸——一座祭坛，由未点的蜡烛丑陋地守卫着，上面不屈地立着进食用上帝的全套器具。
那么，就寝之前，我负罪的良心还须先受一番告解？这对明天是个好兆头。
……击剑手抑扬的音调：「拿你的草垫（*Prenez votre paillasse*）。」我转身。他正俯身于屋角一团无形的东西。那团东西堆到半个天花板高。由一块块床垫形状组成。我拽出一块——麻袋布，塞着扎人的稻草。我把它扛上肩。「那么（*Alors*）。」他照我进来时的门道引我过去。（离开那地方我多少有些乐意。）
往回走，下一条走廊，再爬几级楼梯；我们面前立着一对疤疤拉拉的小门，门上挂着两把我此生见过最大的挂锁。我走不过去了，停下：他掏出一大串钥匙。同锁搏斗了一番。没有生命的声息：钥匙在锁孔里响得出奇地大；锁以邪恶的优雅屈服了——两扇可怜的小门开了。
我扛着草垫蹒跚进那方黑暗。没法判断这间不吭一声的黑屋子有多大。我面前立着一根柱子。「搁在那根柱子旁边，今晚就在那儿睡，明儿咱们瞧着办（*nous allons voir*）。」击剑手指示道。「你用不着毯子。」他补了一句；门哐啷一声，光和击剑手一起消失了。
睡觉的邀请我不需要第二次。和衣而卧，我扑倒在草垫上，带着一种我此前此后从未感到过的疲惫。但我没有闭眼：因为我的四周升起了一片无比奇异的声音之海…

===Original===

the hitherto empty and minute room became suddenly enormous: weird cries, oaths, laughter, pulling it sideways and backward, extending it to inconceivable depth and width, telescoping it to frightful nearness. From all directions, by at least thirty voices in eleven languages (I counted as I lay Dutch, Belgian, Spanish, Turkish, Arabian, Polish, Russian, Swedish, German, French﻿—and English) at distances varying from seventy feet to a few inches, for twenty minutes I was ferociously bombarded. Nor was my perplexity purely aural. About five minutes after lying down, I saw (by a hitherto unnoticed speck of light which burned near the doors which I had entered) two extraordinary looking figures﻿—one a well-set man with a big, black beard, the other a consumptive with a bald head and sickly moustache, both clad only in their knee-length chemises, hairy legs naked, feet bare﻿—wander down the room and urinate profusely in the corner nearest me. This act accomplished, the figures wandered back, greeted with a volley of ejaculatory abuse from the invisible co-occupants of my new sleeping-apartment; and disappeared in darkness.
I remarked to myself that the gendarmes of this gendarmerie were peculiarly up in languages, and fell asleep.

===Chinese===

这间先前空空荡荡的斗室骤然变得巨大：怪异的喊叫、咒骂、大笑，把它扯向侧面和后方，抻到不可想象的深与宽，又叠缩到骇人的近旁。来自四面八方、至少三十条嗓音、十一种语言（我躺着数过：荷兰语、比利时语、西班牙语、土耳其语、阿拉伯语、波兰语、俄语、瑞典语、德语、法语——还有英语），距离从七十英尺到几英寸不等，整整二十分钟，我遭到猛烈的轰炸。我的困惑也并非只在耳朵。躺下约五分钟后，我看见（借着我进来那道门旁一点此前未曾留意的光斑）两个长相非凡的人物——一个是精壮的黑胡子大汉，另一个是秃顶、病髭的痨病鬼，两人都只穿着齐膝的衬衣，毛腿赤裸，光着脚——蹶到屋那头，在离我最近的角落里大量小便。此事完毕，两个人影蹶回去，迎接他们的是我这新卧室里那些看不见的同居者一阵急促如连珠的叫骂；然后没入黑暗。
我自语道，这宪兵队里的宪兵们在语言上可真有两下子，然后睡着了。

## IV Le Nouveau / 四 新来的人
===Original===

“*Vous ne voulez pas de café?*”
The threatening question recited in a hoarse voice woke me like a shot. Sprawled half on and half off my paillasse, I looked suddenly up into a juvenile pimply face with a red tassel bobbing in its eyes. A boy in a Belgian uniform was stooping over me. In one hand a huge pail a third full of liquid slime. I said fiercely: “*Au contraire, je veux bien.*” And collapsed on the mattress.
“*Pas de quart, vous?*” the face fired at me.
“*Comprends pas*,” I replied, wondering what on earth the words meant.
“English?”
“American.”
At this moment a tin cup appeared mysteriously out of the gloom and was rapidly filled from the pail, after which operation the tassel remarked: “Your friend here” and disappeared.
I decided I had gone completely crazy.
The cup had been deposited near me. Not daring to approach it, I boosted my aching corpse on one of its futile elbows and gazed blankly around. My eyes, wading laboriously through a dark atmosphere, a darkness gruesomely tactile, perceived only here and there lively patches of vibrating humanity. My ears recognised English, something which I took to be low-German and which was Belgian, Dutch, Polish, and what I guessed to be Russian.
Trembling with this chaos, my hand sought the cup. The cup was not warm; the contents, which I hastily gulped, were not even tepid. The taste was dull, almost bitter, clinging, thick, nauseating. I felt a renewed interest in living as soon as the deathful swallow descended to my abdomen, very much as a suicide who changes his mind after the fatal dose. I decided that it would be useless to vomit. I sat up. I looked around.
The darkness was rapidly going out of the sluggish stinking air. I was sitting on my mattress at one end of a sort of room, filled with pillars; ecclesiastical in feeling. I already perceived it to be of enormous length. My mattress resembled an island: all around it on the floor at distances varying from a quarter of an inch to ten feet (which constituted the limit of distinct vision) reposed startling identities. There was blood in some of them. Others consisted of a rind of blueish matter sustaining a core of yellowish froth. From behind me a chunk of hurtling spittle joined its fellows. I decided to stand up.
At this moment, at the far end of the room, I seemed to see an extraordinary vulture-like silhouette leap up from nowhere.

===Chinese===

「您不要咖啡吗？」（*Vous ne voulez pas de café?*）
一个粗嗓子念出的威胁性问题把我像枪一样打醒了。我半在半不在草垫上摊着，猛一抬头，正对上一张长满粉刺的少年脸，眼前晃着一撮红缨。一个穿比利时制服的男孩正俯身看我。一只手提着一只盛了三分之一液体黏浆的大桶。我凶狠地说：「正相反，我要（*Au contraire, je veux bien*）。」然后瘫回床垫上。
「没有杯（*Pas de quart*），您？」那张脸向我开火。
「听不懂（*Comprends pas*）。」我答，纳闷这几个词到底是什么意思。
「英语？」
「美国人。」
这时一只锡杯从幽暗中神秘出现，被从桶里迅速斟满；这个动作之后，那撮红缨说：「你朋友在这儿。」随后消失。
我断定自己彻底疯了。
杯子已放在我旁边。我不敢靠近它，用一条不中用的胳膊肘撑起我疼痛的尸体，茫然四顾。我的眼睛在一片黑暗的大气里艰难蹚行——一种触感可怖的黑暗——只在这里那里看到一块块活动震颤的人味儿。我的耳朵辨认出英语、一种我以为是低地德语实为比利时语的东西，还有荷兰语、波兰语，以及我猜是俄语的语言。
我的手抖着伸向杯子，被这片乱象抖着。杯子不温；我匆忙灌下去的内容物甚至算不上温吞。那味道发闷、近乎发苦、黏着、稠厚、令人作呕。那致命的一咽一下到腹部，我顿时对活着重新起了兴致——很像吞下致命剂量后回心转意的自杀者。我断定吐出来也无用。我坐起来。我环顾四周。
黑暗正从那迟滞发臭的空气里迅速退去。我坐在我的床垫上，在一间满是柱子的类似房间的尽头；感觉上颇像教堂。我已经觉出它长得惊人。我的床垫像一座岛：四周的地板上，从四分之一英寸到十英尺不等（即清晰视觉的极限）卧着一排排惊人的同类物。有的里面带血。另一些由一圈青色物质托着一核黄色泡沫构成。从我身后又飞来一大块疾唾，加入了同伴。我决定站起来。
就在这时，在房间远端，我似乎看见一个非凡的、秃鹫似的剪影凭空跃起。

===Original===

It rushed a little way in my direction crying hoarsely “*Corvée d’eau!*”﻿—stopped, bent down at what I perceived to be a paillasse like mine, jerked what was presumably the occupant by the feet, shook him, turned to the next, and so on up to six. As there seemed to be innumerable paillasses, laid side by side at intervals of perhaps a foot with their heads to the wall on three sides of me, I was wondering why the vulture had stopped at six. On each mattress a crude imitation of humanity, wrapped ear-high in its blanket, lay and drank from a cup like mine and spat long and high into the room. The ponderous reek of sleepy bodies undulated toward me from three directions. I had lost sight of the vulture in a kind of insane confusion which arose from the further end of the room. It was as if he had touched off six high explosives. Occasional pauses in the minutely crazy din were accurately punctuated by exploding bowels; to the great amusement of innumerable somebodies, whose precise whereabouts the gloom carefully guarded.
I felt that I was the focus of a group of indistinct recumbents who were talking about me to one another in many incomprehensible tongues. I noticed beside every pillar (including the one beside which I had innocently thrown down my mattress the night before) a good sized pail, overflowing with urine, and surrounded by a large irregular puddle. My mattress was within an inch of the nearest puddle. What I took to be a man, an amazing distance off, got out of bed and succeeded in locating the pail nearest to him after several attempts. Ten invisible recumbents yelled at him in six languages.
All at once a handsome figure rose from the gloom at my elbow. I smiled stupidly into his clear hardish eyes. And he remarked pleasantly:
“Your friend’s here, Johnny, and wants to see you.”
A bulge of pleasure swooped along my body, chasing aches and numbness, my muscles danced, nerves tingled in perpetual holiday.
B. was lying on his camp-cot, wrapped like an Eskimo in a blanket which hid all but his nose and eyes.
“Hello, Cummings,” he said smiling. “There’s a man here who is a friend of Vanderbilt and knew Cézanne.”
I gazed somewhat critically at B. There was nothing particularly insane about him, unless it was his enthusiastic excitement, which might almost be attributed to my jack-in-the-box manner of arriving.

===Chinese===

它朝我这边冲了一小段，粗声喊着「打水队（*Corvée d'eau*）！」——停下，朝一张在我看来同我一样的草垫弯下腰去，拽住大概是睡主的双脚，摇醒他，转向下一张，如此共六张。既然草垫似乎数不胜数——以大约一英尺的间隔并排铺着，头朝墙，在我三面围开——我纳闷秃鹫为什么停在六张。每张床垫上，一个粗糙的人类仿品，裹着毯子齐耳高，躺着，用一只同我一样的杯子喝水，朝屋里又长又高地吐痰。困倦身体那沉重的汗臭从三个方向朝我涌来。秃鹫消失在从房间远端升起的一种疯狂的混乱中，我失去了他的踪迹。他像是引爆了六颗高爆炸药。那细密疯狂的喧阗里偶有的停顿，被炸响的肠子精确地打着标点；逗乐了数不清的什么人，他们的确切位置由黑暗小心保守着。
我觉得自己是一群模糊卧者的话题中心，他们正用许多听不懂的语言谈论我。我注意到每根柱子旁（包括我前夜无辜撂下床垫的那根）都有一只不小的桶，尿漫出边沿，四周一大摊不规则的水洼。我的床垫离最近的水洼不到一英寸。一个我勉强算作人的东西，在惊人地远处下了床，试了几次才找到离他最近的桶。十个看不见的卧者用六种语言朝他大喊。
蓦地，一个英俊的身影从我肘边的幽暗中立起。我傻笑着望进他那双清亮而略硬的眼睛。他愉快地说：
「你朋友在这儿呢，约翰尼，想见你。」
一团欢喜沿着我的身体掠过，追跑了疼痛和麻木，我的肌肉起舞，神经在一場永久假日里發麻。
B.正躺在他的行军床上，像个爱斯基摩人似的裹在毯子里，只露出鼻子和眼睛。
「哈罗，卡明斯。」他微笑着说。「这儿有个人是范德比尔特的朋友，还认识塞尚。」
我带点挑剔地打量B.。他身上没什么特别疯的地方，除非算上他那热切的兴奋——而这几乎可以归因于我那玩偶盒似的出场方式。

===Original===

He said: “There are people here who speak English, Russian, Arabian. There are the finest people here! Did you go to Gré? I fought rats all night there. Huge ones. They tried to eat me. And from Gré to Paris? I had three gendarmes all the way to keep me from escaping, and they all fell asleep.”
I began to be afraid that I was asleep myself. “Please be frank,” I begged. “Strictly *entre nous*: am I dreaming, or is this a bug-house?”
B. laughed, and said: “I thought so when I arrived two days ago. When I came in sight of the place a lot of girls waved from the window and yelled at me. I no sooner got inside than a queer looking duck whom I took to be a nut came rushing up to me and cried: ‘Too late for soup!’﻿—This is Campe de Triage de la Ferté Macé, Orne, France, and all these fine people were arrested as spies. Only two or three of them can speak a word of French, and that’s *soupe*!”
I said, “My God, I thought Marseilles was somewhere on the Mediterranean Ocean, and that this was a gendarmerie.”
“But this is M-a-c-é. It’s a little mean town, where everybody snickers and sneers at you if they see you’re a prisoner. They did at me.”
“Do you mean to say we’re *espions* too?”
“Of course!” B. said enthusiastically. “Thank God! And in to stay. Every time I think of the *section sanitaire*, and A. and his thugs, and the whole rotten red-taped *Croix Rouge*, I have to laugh. Cummings, I tell you this is the finest place on earth!”
A vision of the *Chef de la section Sanitaire Ving-et-Un* passed through my mind. The doughy face. Imitation-English-officer swagger. Large calves, squeaking puttees. The daily lecture: “I doughno what’s th’matter with you fellers. You look like nice boys. Well-edjucated. But you’re so dirty in your habits. You boys are always kickin’ because I don’t put you on a car together. I’m ashamed to do it, that’s why. I doughtwanta give this section a black eye. We gotta show these lousy Frenchmen what Americans are. We gotta show we’re superior to ’em. Those bastards doughno what a bath means. And you fellers are always hangin’ ’round, talkin’ with them dirty frog-eaters that does the cookin’ and the dirty work ’round here. How d’you boys expect me to give you a chance? I’d like to put you fellers on a car, I wanta see you boys happy. But I don’t dare to, that’s why.

===Chinese===

他说：「这儿有人讲英语、俄语、阿拉伯语。这儿有最棒的人！你去格雷了吗？我在那儿同老鼠斗了一整夜。大个的。它们想把我吃了。从格雷到巴黎呢？一路上三个宪兵看着我，怕我跑了，结果全睡着了。」
我开始担心睡着的是我自己。「请坦白说，」我恳求道。「天知地知你知我知（*entre nous*）：我是在做梦，还是这是个疯人院？」
B.笑了，说：「我两天前刚来的时候也这么想。我一望见这地方，一群姑娘就从窗口朝我招手喊叫。我刚进门，一个样子古怪的家伙——我当他是疯子——就冲我喍过来，喊：『赶不上喝汤了！』——这里是法国奥恩省拉费尔泰马塞甄别营（Campe de Triage de la Ferté Macé），这些好人全是以间谍罪抓来的。他们里头只有两三个会说一句法语，那就是『汤（soupe）』！」
我说：「天哪，我还以为马赛在地中海边上，以为这是宪兵队。」
「可这里是马——塞（M-a-c-é）。一个又小又坏的镇子，谁要是看出你是囚犯，人人都喍笑你。他们就喍笑过我。」
「你是说我们俩也是间谍（*espions*）？」
「当然！」B.热情地说。「谢天谢地！而且要住下去。每当我想起卫生分队（*section sanitaire*）、A.和他的那帮打手、整个烂透了的红十字会（*Croix Rouge*）官様文章，我就忍不住笑。卡明斯，我告诉你，这是世界上最棒的地方！」
第21卫生分队队长（Chef de la section Sanitaire Ving-et-Un）的身影从我脑中掠过。那张面团脸。冒牌英国军官的摇摇摆摆。粗大的小腿，吱吱作响的绑腿。每日训话：「俺不知道你们这帮小子咋回事。看着像好孩子。受过良好教育。可你们习惯太脏了。你们这帮小子老抱怨俺不把你们俩安排上一辆车。俺那是不好意思，就为这个。俺不想给分队丢脸。俺们得让这帮烂法国人瞧瞧美国人是啥样。俺们得显得比他们高一头。那帮混蛋不懂洗澡是啥意思。可你们这帮小子老轉悠，跟这帮干厨子脏活的脏吃蛤蟆的聊。你们叫俺咋给你们机会？俺想送你们上车，俺想看你们小子高兴。可俺不敢，就为这个。

===Original===

If you want me to send you out, you gotta shave and look neat, and *keep away from them dirty Frenchmen*. We Americans are over here to learn them lousy bastards something.”
I laughed for sheer joy.
A terrific tumult interrupted my mirth. “*Par ici!*”﻿—“Get out of the way you damn Polak!”﻿—“M’sieu, M’sieu!”﻿—“Over here!”﻿—“*Mais non!*”﻿—“*Gott-ver-dummer!*” I turned in terror to see my paillasse in the clutches of four men who were apparently rending it in as many directions.
One was a clean-shaven youngish man with lively eyes, alert and muscular, whom I identified as the man who had called me “Johnny.” He had hold of a corner of the mattress and was pulling against the possessor of the opposite corner: an incoherent personage enveloped in a buffoonery of amazing rags and patches, with a shabby head on which excited wisps of dirty hair stood upright in excitement, and the tall, ludicrous, extraordinary, almost noble figure of a dancing bear. A third corner of the paillasse was rudely grasped by a six-foot combination of yellow hair, red hooligan face, and sky-blue trousers; assisted by the undersized tasseled mucker in Belgian uniform, with a pimply rogue’s mug and unlimited impertinence of diction, who had awakened me by demanding if I wanted coffee. Albeit completely dazed by the uncouth vocal fracas, I realised in some manner that these hostile forces were contending, not for the possession of the mattress, but merely for the privilege of presenting the mattress to myself.
Before I could offer any advice on this delicate topic, a childish voice cried emphatically beside my ear: “Put the mattress here! What are you trying to do? There’s no use destroy‑ing a mat‑tress!”﻿—at the same moment the mattress rushed with cobalt strides in my direction, propelled by the successful efforts of the Belgian uniform and the hooligan visage, the clean-shaven man and the incoherent bear still desperately clutching their respective corners; and upon its arrival was seized with surprising strength by the owner of the child’s voice﻿—a fluffy little gnome-shaped man with a sensitive face which had suffered much﻿—and indignantly deposited beside B.’s bed in a space mysteriously cleared for its reception. The gnome immediately kneeled upon it and fell to carefully smoothing certain creases caused by the recent conflict, exclaiming slowly syllable by syllable: “*Mon Dieu.

===Chinese===

你们要是想让俺派你们出去，就得刮脸、拾掇干净，离那些脏法国佬远点。俺们美国人到这儿来，就是要教那帮烂混蛋点东西。」
我纯粹出于快乐而笑。
一阵可怕的骚动打断了我的欢畅。「这边（*Par ici*）！」——「让开，你他妈的波兰佬！」——「先生，先生！」——「这边！」——「不是（*Mais non*）！」——「他妈的（*Gott-ver-dummer*）！」我惊恐地转身，看见四个男人正拽着我的草垫，朝显然同样多的方向撕扯。
一个是脸刮得干净的年轻人，眼睛灵动，机敏而矫健——我认出他就是喊我「约翰尼」的那位。他攞着床垫一角，同对角的占有者对拉：那是一位语无伦次的人物，裹在一身惊人破烂的滑稽戏里，头顶脏兮兮的头发一绺绺激动地竖着，身材高挑、滑稽、非凡、几乎高贵，活一头跳舞的熊。草垫的第三个角被一个六英尺的组合粗鲁地攞住：黄头发、红透一个流氓相的脸、天蓝裤子；帮他的是那个戴缨的矬子，比利时制服，一脸粉刺的贼相，开口闭口没个正经——就是拿咖啡把我吵醒的那个。我虽然被这场粗野的声浪彻底轟得发晕，还是多少明白过来：这几股敌对势力争的并非床垫的归属，而仅仅是把床垫呈献给我本人的荣光。
我还没来得及在这个微妙议题上发表意见，一个童音在我耳边斩钉截铁地喊：「把床垫放这儿！你们想干嘛？毁一个床——垫没有用！」——同一瞬间，床垫在比利时制服和流氓脸的合力推动下迈着钴蓝色的大步朝我冲来，干净脸和语无伦次的熊仍死死攞着各自的角；床垫一到，就被那个童音的主人——一个毛茸茸的小地精模样的男人，一张他受尽苦难的敏感的脸——以惊人的力气一把抓住，愤然安放在B.床边一块被神秘清理出来的空地上。地精立刻跪上去，动手细心抚平刚才那场冲突造成的折痕，一个音节一个音节地慢慢感叹：「我的天（*Mon Dieu*），

===Original===

* Now, that’s better, you mustn’t do things like that.” The clean-shaven man regarded him loftily with folded arms, while the tassel and the trousers victoriously inquired if I had a cigarette?﻿—and upon receiving one apiece (also the gnome, and the clean-shaven man, who accepted his with some dignity) sat down without much ado on B.’s bed﻿—which groaned ominously in protest﻿—and hungrily fired questions at me. The bear meanwhile, looking as if nothing had happened, adjusted his ruffled costume with a satisfied air and (calmly gazing into the distance) began with singularly delicate fingers to stuff a stunted and ancient pipe with what appeared to be a mixture of wood and manure.
I was still answering questions, when a gnarled voice suddenly threatened, over our head: “Broom? You. Everybody. Clean. *Surveillant* says. Not me, no?”﻿—I started, expecting to see a parrot.
It was the silhouette.
A vulture-like figure stood before me, a demoralised broom clenched in one claw or fist: it had lean legs cased in shabby trousers, muscular shoulders covered with a rough shirt open at the neck, knotted arms, and a coarse insane face crammed beneath the visor of a cap. The face consisted of a rapid nose, droopy moustache, ferocious watery small eyes, a pugnacious chin, and sunken cheeks hideously smiling. There was something in the ensemble at once brutal and ridiculous, vigorous and pathetic.
Again I had not time to speak; for the hooligan in azure trousers hurled his butt at the bear’s feet, exclaiming: “There’s another for you, Polak!”﻿—jumped from the bed, seized the broom, and poured upon the vulture a torrent of *Gott-ver-dummers*, to which the latter replied copiously and in kind. Then the red face bent within a few inches of my own, and for the first time I saw that it had recently been young﻿—“I say I do your sweep for you” it translated pleasantly. I thanked it; and the vulture, exclaiming: “Good. Good. Not me. Surveillant. Harree does it for everybody. Hee, hee”﻿—rushed off, followed by Harree and the tassel. Out of the corner of my eye I watched the tall, ludicrous, extraordinary, almost proud figure of the bear stoop with quiet dignity, the musical fingers close with a singular delicacy upon the moist indescribable eighth-of-an-inch of tobacco.
I did not know that this was a Delectable Mountain.﻿ ﻿…

这就好了，你们可不能这样。」干净脸抱着胳膊高高地打量他，缨子和裤子则胜利地问我有没有香烟？——一人得了一支（地精和干净脸也各得一支，后者接得颇有威严）之后，他们不多客气就在B.的床上坐下了——床不祥地吱呀抗议——然后饿狼般地朝我发射问题。那头熊 meanwhile，一脸若无其事，满意地整了整他凌乱的行头，（平静地望着远方）用一双出奇灵巧的手指往一支短小古老的烟斗里塞一种似乎是木屑加肥料的混合物。
我还在答问，一个粗厉的嗓音突然在我们头顶上威胁道：「扫帚？你。所有人。打扫。监守（*Surveillant*）说的。不是我，嗯？」——我一惊，以为要看见一只鹦鹉。
原来是那剪影。
一个秃鹫模样的人立在我面前，一只爪子或拳头里攥着一把士气涣散的扫帚：細腿裹在破裤子里，肌肉发达的肩膀罩着一件领口敞开的粗布衬衫，疙疙瘩瘩的胳膊，一张粗野疯狂的疯脸塞在帽檐下。那脸由一只急促的鼻子、八字胡、凶猛水汪汪的小眼睛、一个好斗的下巴和两片可怕微笑着的陷颊组成。整体上有些东西既残暴又滑稽，既狂暴又可怜。
我又没来得及说话；因为天蓝裤子的流氓把烟头朝熊的脚边一扔，喊：「再赏你一个，波兰佬！」——从床上跳下，夺过扫帚，朝秃鹫倾泻了一串他妈的，后者也源源本本地回敬同类。然后那张红脸凑到我面前几英寸之内，我头一次看出它不久前还年轻——「我说，我替你扫。」它愉快地翻译道。我谢了它；秃鹫喊：「好。好。不是我。监守。阿累替所有人扫。嘻，嘻。」——冲了出去，阿累（Harree）和缨子跟上。我用眼角看见那头高挑、滑稽、非凡、几乎骄傲的熊带着安静的尊严弯下腰，那双音乐般的手指以奇异的精巧捏住那湿漉漉不可名状的八分之一英寸烟丝。
我还不知道这是一座悦目山（Delectable Mountain）。…

===Original===

The clean-shaven man (who appeared to have been completely won over by his smoke), and the fluffy gnome, who had completed the arrangement of my paillasse, now entered into conversation with myself and B.; the clean-shaven one seating himself in Harree’s stead, the gnome declining (on the grounds that the bed was already sufficiently loaded) to occupy the place left vacant by the tassel’s exit, and leaning against the drab, sweating, poisonous wall. He managed, however, to call our attention to the shelf at B.’s head which he himself had constructed, and promised me a similar luxury *toute de suite*. He was a Russian, and had a wife and *gosse* in Paris. “My name is Monsieur Au-guste, at your service”﻿—and his gentle pale eyes sparkled. The clean-shaven talked distinct and absolutely perfect English. His name was Fritz. He was a Norwegian, a stoker on a ship. “You mustn’t mind that feller that wanted you to sweep. He’s crazy. They call him John the Baigneur. He used to be the bathman. Now he’s *Maître de Chambre*. They wanted me to take it﻿—I said, ‘F﻿—﻿— it, I don’t want it.’ Let him have it. That’s no kind of a job, everyone complaining and on top of you morning till night. ‘Let them that wants the job take it’ I said. That crazy Dutchman’s been here for two years. They told him to get out and he wouldn’t, he was too fond of the booze” (I jumped at the slang) “and the girls. They took it away from John and give it to that little Ree-shar feller, that doctor. That was a swell job he had, *baigneur*, too. All the bloody liquor you can drink and a girl every time you want one. He ain’t never had a girl in his life, that Ree-shar feller.” His laughter was hard, clear, cynical. “That Pompom, the little Belgian feller was just here, he’s a great one for the girls. He and Harree. Always getting *cabinot*. I got it twice myself since I been here.”
All this time the enormous room was filling gradually with dirty light. In the further end six figures were brooming furiously, yelling to each other in the dust like demons. A seventh, Harree, was loping to and fro splashing water from a pail and enveloping everything and everybody in a ponderous and blasphemous fog of *Gott-ver-dummers*.

===Chinese===
