干净脸（似乎已被那口烟彻底收买）和替我安置完草垫的毛草地精，此刻同我和B.聊了起来；干净脸坐了阿累的位子，地精则婉拒（理由是床上已足够超载）缨子退出留下的空位，靠在昏暗、冒汗、有毒的墙上。不过他还是没法不让我们注意B.床头那块搁板——他自己造的——并答应马上（*toute de suite*）也给我弄一套同样的奢侈品。他是俄国人，妻子和孩子（*gosse*）在巴黎。「鄙人奥古斯特先生（Monsieur Au-guste），听候差遣。」——他温柔苍白的眼睛发着光。干净脸讲一口清晰绝伦的英语。他叫弗里茨（Fritz），挪威人，船上司炉。「你别往心里去，刚才那个要你扫地的家伙。他疯了。他们叫他洗浴约翰（John the Baigneur）。他从前是澡堂工。现在是侍寝总管（*Maître de Chambre*）。他们本来要我干——我说：『去他妈的，我才不干。』让他干去吧。那活儿不是人干的，人人抱怨，从早到晚骑你脖子上。『谁稀罕谁干去』我说。那个疯荷兰人待了两年了。他们叫他走他不走，他太贪那口酒」（听到俚语我精神一振）「还有娘们。他们把差事从约翰手里收走，给了那个小李-沙（Ree-shar）家伙，那个大夫。他那份澡堂工（*baigneur*）也是份肥差。血酿的酒随便喝，想要姑娘回回有。那个李-沙家伙这辈子没碰过姑娘。」他的笑声硬朗、清亮、愤也。「那个彭彭（Pompom），比利时小个子刚才还在这儿，他是娘们方面的大行家。他和阿累。老蹲小号（*cabinot*）。我来以后也蹲过两回。」
这一路，那巨大的房间正渐渐充滿肮脏的光。远端六条人影在狂暴地挥帚，在尘土里彼此叫喊如群魔。第七位，阿累，正来回疾走，从桶里泼水，把一切人等裹进一片沉重而渎神的他妈的（*Gott-ver-dummers*）雾里。

===Original===

Along three sides (with the exception, that is, of the nearer end, which boasted the sole door) were laid, with their lengths at right angles to the wall, at intervals of three or four feet, something like forty paillasses. On each, with half a dozen exceptions (where the occupants had not yet finished their coffee or were on duty for the *corvée*) lay the headless body of a man smothered in its blanket, only the boots showing.
The demons were working towards our end of the room. Harree had got his broom and was assisting. Nearer and nearer they came; converging, they united their separate heaps of filth in a loudly stinking single mound at the door. Brooms were stacked against the wall in the corner. The men strolled back to their mattresses.
Monsieur Auguste, whose French had not been able to keep pace with Fritz’s English, saw his chance, and proposed “now that the Room is all clean, let us go take a little walk, the three of us.” Fritz understood perfectly, and rose, remarking as he fingered his immaculate chin “Well, I guess I’ll take a shave before the bloody *planton* comes”﻿—and Monsieur Auguste, B., and I started down the room.
It was in shape oblong, about 80 feet by 40, unmistakably ecclesiastical in feeling; two rows of wooden pillars, spaced at intervals of fifteen feet, rose to a vaulted ceiling 25 or 30 feet above the floor. As you stood with your back to the door, and faced down the room, you had in the near right-hand corner (where the brooms stood) six pails of urine. On the right-hand long wall, a little beyond the angle of this corner, a few boards, tacked together in any fashion to make a two-sided screen four feet in height, marked the position of a *cabinet d’aisance*, composed of a small coverless tin pail identical with the other six, and a board of the usual design which could be placed on the pail or not as desired. The wooden floor in the neighborhood of the booth and pails was of a dark colour, obviously owing to the continual overflow of their contents.
The right-hand long wall contained something like ten large windows, of which the first was commanded by the somewhat primitive cabinet. There were no other windows in the remaining walls; or they had been carefully rendered useless.

===Chinese===

沿三面墙（也就是说，除了有唯一一道门的近端之外），以同墙壁成直角的方向、三四英尺的间隔，铺着约莫四十张草垫。每张上面——六七张除外（主人还没喝完咖啡，或者正在打水队值班）——躺着一个无头的人体，闷在毯子里，只露出靴子。
群魔朝我们这头扫过来。阿累也抄起了扫帚帮着扫。越来越近；他们汇合，把各自的污堆在门口合并成一座轰然发臭的大坟。扫帚靠墙堆在墙角。人们踱回各自的床垫。
奥古斯特先生的法语跟不上弗里茨的英语，见缝插针，提议「既然屋子都干净了，咱们仨出去遛个小弯儿吧」。弗里茨完全听懂了，站起身，摸着他一尘不染的下巴说：「好，趁那要命的卫兵（*planton*）还没来，我先刮个脸。」——于是奥古斯特先生、B.和我朝屋子那头走去。
它呈长方形，约八十英尺长、四十英尺宽，气韵无疑是教堂式的；两列木柱以十五英尺的间距排开，升到离地二十五或三十英尺的穹顶。背门而立、面朝屋里，右手近角（扫帚立处）有六只尿桶。右手长墙上，越过这个角不远处，几块木板以随便什么方式钉成四英尺高的两面屏风，标出一间便所（*cabinet d'aisance*）的位置：由一只同另外六只一模一样、没有盖的小锡桶，和一块随人高兴搁不搁上去的常规设计木板组成。小棚和几只桶附近的木地板颜色发黑，显然归功于桶中物的常年漫溢。
右手长墙上开着大约十扇大窗，第一扇被那个颇为原始的便所征用了。其余墙上再无窗户；或者已被细心地废了武功。

===Original===

In spite of this fact, the inhabitants had contrived a couple of peepholes﻿—one in the door-end and one in the left-hand long wall; the former commanding the gate by which I had entered, the latter a portion of the street by which I had reached the gate. The blocking of all windows on three sides had an obvious significance: *les hommes* were not supposed to see anything which went on in the world without; *les hommes* might, however, look their fill on a little washing-shed, on a corner of what seemed to be another wing of the building, and on a bleak lifeless abject landscape of scrubby woods beyond﻿—which constituted the view from the ten windows on the right. The authorities had miscalculated a little in one respect: a merest fraction of the barbwire pen which began at the corner of the above-mentioned building was visible from these windows, which windows (I was told) were consequently thronged by fighting men at the time of the girl’s promenade. A *planton*, I was also told, made it his business, by keeping *les femmes* out of this corner of their *cour* at the point of the bayonet to deprive them of the sight of their admirers. In addition, it was dry bread or *cabinot* for any of either sex who were caught communicating with each other. Moreover the promenades of the men and the women occurred at roughly speaking the same hour, so that a man or woman who remained upstairs on the chance of getting a smile or a wave from his or her girl or lover lost the promenade thereby.﻿ ﻿…
We had in succession gazed from the windows, crossed the end of the room, and started down the other side, Monsieur Auguste marching between us﻿—when suddenly B. exclaimed in English “Good morning! How are you today?” And I looked across Monsieur Auguste, anticipating another Harree or at least a Fritz. What was my surprise to see a spare majestic figure of manifest refinement, immaculately apparelled in a crisp albeit collarless shirt, carefully mended trousers in which the remains of a crease still lingered, a threadbare but perfectly fitting swallowtail coat, and newly varnished (if somewhat ancient) shoes. Indeed for the first time since my arrival at La Ferté I was confronted by a perfect type: the apotheosis of injured nobility, the humiliated victim of perfectly unfortunate circumstances, the utterly respectable gentleman who had seen better days.

===Chinese===

尽管如此，住户们还是琢磨出了两个窥孔——一个在门那端，一个在左手长墙上；前者俯瞰我进来时走的大门，后者扫视我通向大门的那段街。三面封窗的含义昭然若揭：男人们（*les hommes*）不该看见外界的任何动静；不过他们可以把这些男人们看个够的，是一座小浣洗棚、建筑另一翼的一角，以及再往外一片荒凉死灭卑屈的灌木林——这就是右侧十扇窗的全部风景。当局在一个地方略有失算：从这些窗可以看见上述建筑转角处开始的铁丝网栏的一个小小尖角，因此（我听说）每逢姑娘们放风，这些窗便挤满了好汉。还有一个卫兵（我被告知）以刺刀尖把女人们（*les femmes*）挡出她们的院子（*cour*）的这个角落，剥夺她们一瞻爱慕者的眼福，以此为业。此外，无论男女，凡被抓住彼此通风报信的，一律干面包或小号伺候。而且男女放风大致同一钟点，于是哪个男人或女人若抱着得一颦一笑或一招手的指望赖在楼上，便白白失了放风。…
我们依次凭窗眺望过，穿过房间尽头，沿另一侧往回走，奥古斯特先生行在我们中间——B.忽然用英语喊：「早上好！您今天可好？」我从奥古斯特先生头顶望去，以为又要见到一个阿累，至少一个弗里茨。出乎我意料，我看见一个瘦削、庄严、显然有教养的身影，穿着一件挺括 albeit 无领的衬衫，仔细补缀的裤子上残存着一道裤线，一件磨损却極其合身的燕尾服，一双新上过漆（虽然有些年头）的鞋。确实，这是我到拉费尔泰以来头一回面对面见到一个完美的类型：受伤贵族之神圣化，倒霉透顶处境之受辱牺牲品，见过好日子的、彻头彻尾可敬的绅士。

===Original===

There was about him, moreover, something irretrievably English, nay even pathetically Victorian﻿—it was as if a page of Dickens was shaking my friend’s hand. “Count Bragard, I want you to meet my friend Cummings”﻿—he saluted me in modulated and courteous accents of indisputable culture, gracefully extending his pale hand. “I have heard a great deal about you from B., and wanted very much to meet you. It is a pleasure to find a friend of my friend B., someone congenial and intelligent in contrast to these swine”﻿—he indicated the room with a gesture of complete contempt. “I see you were strolling. Let us take a turn.” Monsieur Auguste said tactfully, “I’ll see you soon, friends,” and left us with an affectionate shake of the hand and a sidelong glance of jealousy and mistrust at B.’s respectable friend.
“You’re looking pretty well today, Count Bragard,” B. said amiably.
“I do well enough,” the Count answered. “It is a frightful strain﻿—you of course realise that﻿—for anyone who has been accustomed to the decencies, let alone the luxuries, of life. This filth”﻿—he pronounced the word with indescribable bitterness﻿—“this herding of men like cattle﻿—they treat us no better than pigs here. The fellows drop their dung in the very room where they sleep. What is one to expect of a place like this? *Ce n’est pas une existence*”﻿—his French was glib and faultless.
“I was telling my friend that you knew Cézanne,” said B. “Being an artist he was naturally much interested.”
Count Bragard stopped in astonishment, and withdrew his hands slowly from the tails of his coat. “Is it possible!” he exclaimed, in great agitation. “What an astonishing coincidence! I am myself a painter. You perhaps noticed this badge”﻿—he indicated a button attached to his left lapel, and I bent and read the words: On War Service. “I always wear it,” he said with a smile of faultless sorrow, and resumed his walk. “They don’t know what it means here, but I wear it all the same. I was a special representative for *The London Sphere* at the front in this war. I did the trenches and all that sort of thing. They paid me well; I got fifteen pounds a week. And why not? I am an R.A. My specialty was horses. I painted the finest horses in England, among them the King’s own entry in the last Derby. Do you know London?” We said no.

===Chinese===

他身上还有种无可挽回的英国味儿，甚至可以说是维多利亚式的可怜——仿佛狄更斯的一页正握我朋友的手。「布拉加尔伯爵（Count Bragard），我给您介绍我的朋友卡明斯。」——他以训练有素、温文尔雅、无疑问有教养的声调向我致意，优雅地伸出他苍白的手。「久闻大名，是B.告诉我的，一直很想见您。能结识我朋友B.的朋友，一位同这些猪猡相比如此投契而聪明的人，真是幸事。」——他以十足轻蔑的手势指了指整间屋子。「我看您在散步。我们转一圈吧。」奥古斯特先生乖巧地说：「回头见，朋友们。」同我们握手作别，朝B.的可敬朋友投去一瞥嫉妒与不信任的斜眼。
「您今天气色不错，布拉加尔伯爵。」B.和蔼地说。
「还凑合。」伯爵答。「这是可怕的煎熬——您自然明白——对任何过惯了体面日子（更别说奢侈日子）的人而言。这污秽」——他以难以形容的苦涩念出这个词——「这样把人当牲口圈——他们在这儿待我们连猪都不如。家伙们就在睡觉的屋里拉屎。这种地方还能指望什么？这不算生活（*Ce n'est pas une existence*）」——他的法语流利而无瑕疵。
「我正跟我的朋友说您认识塞尚呢。」B.说。「他是画家，自然特别感兴趣。」
布拉加尔伯爵惊讶地停下，缓缓把手从燕尾后襟抽回来。「可能吗！」他激动地喊。「多么惊人的巧合！我自己就是画家。您也许注意到了这枚徽章」——他指了指左翻领上的一枚徽章，我俯身读到：战时服务（On War Service）。「我一直戴着它。」他以完美无瑕的忧伤微笑说，继续踱步。「这儿的人不知道它是什么意思，但我照戴不误。这场战争里我是《伦敦新闻画报》（*The London Sphere*）驻前线的特派代表。我画过战壕以及诸如此类的一切。他们给我丰厚的报酬；一星期十五镑。何乐不为？我是皇家美协会员（R.A.）。我的专长是马。我画过英格兰最骏的马，包括上次德比马赛上国王亲自参赛的那一匹。您二位了解伦敦吗？」我们说不了解。

===Original===

“If you are ever in London, go to the” (I forget the name) “Hotel﻿—one of the best in town. It has a beautiful large bar, exquisitely furnished in the very best taste. Anyone will tell you where to find the —﻿—﻿—. It has one of my paintings over the bar: *Straightjacket*” (or some such name) “the Marquis of —﻿—﻿—’s horse, who won last time the race was run. I was in America in 1910. You know Cornelius Vanderbilt perhaps? I painted some of his horses. We were the best of friends, Vanderbilt and I. I got handsome prices, you understand, three, five, six thousand pounds. When I left, he gave me this card﻿—I have it here somewhere﻿—” he again stopped, sought in his breastpocket a moment, and produced a visiting card. On one side I read the name “Cornelius Vanderbilt”﻿—on the other, in bold handwriting﻿—“to my very dear friend Count F. A. de Bragard” and a date. “He hated to have me go.”
I was walking in a dream.
“Have you your sketchbooks and paints with you? What a pity. I am always intending to send to England for mine, but you know﻿—one can’t paint in a place like this. It is impossible﻿—all this dirt and these filthy people﻿—it stinks! Ugh!”
I forced myself to say: “How did you happen to come here?”
He shrugged his shoulders. “How indeed, you may well ask! I cannot tell you. It must have been some hideous mistake. As soon as I got here I spoke to the *Directeur* and to the Surveillant. The Directeur said he knew nothing about it; the Surveillant told me confidentially that it was a mistake on the part of the French government; that I would be out directly. He’s not such a bad sort. So I am waiting; every day I expect orders from the English government for my release. The whole thing is preposterous. I wrote to the Embassy and told them so. As soon as I set foot outside this place, I shall sue the French government for ten thousand pounds for the loss of time it has occasioned me. Imagine it﻿—I had contracts with countless members of The Lords﻿—and the war came. Then I was sent to the front by *The Sphere*﻿—and here I am, every day costing me dear, rotting away in this horrible place. The time I have wasted here has already cost me a fortune.”
He paused directly in front of the door and spoke with solemnity: “A man might as well be dead.”

===Chinese===

「你们若去伦敦，一定要到」（我忘了名字）「饭店——城里数一数二的。它有一间漂亮的大酒吧，陈设雅致，品味一流。谁都能告诉你们————在哪几。酒吧上方挂着我的一幅画：《直夹克》（*Straightjacket*）」（或类似的名字）「———侯爵的马，上届比赛夺冠的那匹。我1910年在美国。您也许认识科尼利厄斯·范德比尔特？我画过他的几匹马。我们是至交，范德比尔特和我。我得的价钱可观，您要明白，三千、五千、六千镑。我临走时，他给了我这张名片——就带在身上什么地方。」他又停下，在胸前口袋摸了片刻，掏出一张名片。一面印着「科尼利厄斯·范德比尔特」——另一面是遒劲的手书：「赠我挚友 F.A.德·布拉加尔伯爵」，还有一个日期。「他舍不得我走呢。」
我走在梦里。
「您的速写本和颜料带了吗？真遗憾。我一直打算让人从英国寄来我的，可您知道——这种地方没法作画。不可能——满地污秽，满屋邋遢人——臭气熏天！呕！」
我勉强问：「您是怎么到这儿来的？」
他耸耸肩。「可不是嘛，问得好！我告诉不了您。一定是某个可怕的误会。我一到就找了所长（*Directeur*）和监守。所长说一概不知；监守私下告诉我，是法国政府搞错了；我很快就能出去。他不算太坏的那种人。所以我就等着；我天天盼着英国政府放我出去的命令。整件事荒谬绝伦。我写信告诉了大使馆。我一跨出这个地方，就要为它耽误我的时间向法国政府索赔一万镑。想想看——我同上议院无数议员签着合同——战争爆发了。接着《画报》（*The Sphere*）派我去前线——结果我到了这儿，每天损失惨重，在这可怕的地方烂下去。我在这儿浪费的时间已经损失了一笔财产。」
他在门前站定，庄严地说：「人这样还不如死了干净。」

===Original===

Scarcely had the words passed his lips when I almost jumped out of my skin, for directly before us on the other side of the wall arose the very noise which announced to Scrooge the approach of Marley’s ghost﻿—a dismal clanking and rattling of chains. Had Marley’s transparent figure walked straight through the wall and up to the Dickensian character at my side, I would have been less surprised than I was by what actually happened.
The doors opened with an uncanny bang and in the bang stood a fragile minute queer figure, remotely suggesting an old man. The chief characteristic of the apparition was a certain disagreeable nudity which resulted from its complete lack of all the accepted appurtenances and prerogatives of old age. Its little stooping body, helpless and brittle, bore with extraordinary difficulty a head of absurd largeness, yet which moved on the fleshless neck with a horrible agility. Dull eyes sat in the clean-shaven wrinkles of a face neatly hopeless. At the knees a pair of hands hung, infantile in their smallness. In the loose mouth a tiny cigarette had perched and was solemnly smoking itself.
Suddenly the figure darted at me with a spiderlike entirety.
I felt myself lost.
A voice said mechanically from the vicinity of my feet: “*Il vous faut prendre la douche*”﻿—I stared stupidly. The spectre was poised before me; its averted eyes contemplated the window. “Take your bath,” it added as an afterthought, in English﻿—“Come with me.” It turned suddenly. It hurried to the doorway. I followed. Its rapid deadly doll-like hands shut and skillfully locked the doors in a twinkling. “Come,” its voice said.
It hurried before me down two dirty flights of narrow mutilated stairs. It turned left, and passed through an open door.
I found myself in the wet sunless air of morning.
To the right it hurried, following the wall of the building. I pursued it mechanically. At the corner, which I had seen from the window upstairs, the barbed-wire fence eight feet in height began. The thing paused, produced a key and unlocked a gate. The first three or four feet of wire swung inward. He entered. I after him.
In a flash the gate was locked behind me, and I was following along a wall at right angles to the first. I strode after the thing. A moment before I had been walking in a free world: now I was again a prisoner.

===Chinese===

话音未落，我几乎魂飞魄散：正前方的墙外，升起了向斯克鲁奇预告马利鬼魂临近的那个声响——一阵凄凉的铁链锒铛。就算马利透明的身影径直穿墙而入，走到我身边这位狄更斯式人物跟前，我也不会比实际发生的更吃惊。
门带着不祥的巨响开了，响声里立着一个脆弱、细小、古怪的身影，隐隐约约像个老头。这幽灵的首要特征是一种令人不快的赤裸——源于它彻底缺少老年一切公认的配备与特权。它那佝偻细小、无助而脆弱的身子，异乎寻常吃力地顶着一颗大得荒唐的头，那头却在无肉的脖子上灵活得可怕。迟钝的眼睛嵌在一张刮得干干净净、沟壑整齐而无望的脸里。膝头垂着一双手，小得婴孩似的。松弛的嘴里叼着一支小小的香烟，正一本正经地自燃自吸。
那身影忽然以蜘蛛般的整体向我窜来。
我觉得自己完了。
一个声音从我脚边机械地发出：「您得去洗淋浴（*Il vous faut prendre la douche*）」——我呆呆地瞪着。幽灵在我面前定住；它侧过的眼睛凝视窗外。「去洗澡。」它又用英语补了一句，像是事后想起——「跟我来。」它猛地转身。它匆匆走向门口。我跟上。它那双飞快、致命、玩偶似的手一眨眼关好门并熟练上锁。「来。」它的声音说。
它匆匆在我前面下了两段又窄又破、肮脏的楼梯。它左转，穿过一扇开着的门。
我发现自己置身于早晨那湿漉漉、无日光的空气里。
它朝右匆匆而行，顺着建筑的墙。我机械地跟着。在拐角——就是我从楼上窗口见过的那个——八英尺高的铁丝网开始了。那东西停下，掏出钥匙开了一道门。头三四英尺的网向内摆开。他进去了。我随后。
一眨眼门在我身后锁上，我沿着一段同第一堵墙成直角的墙走着。我大步追赶那东西。片刻之前我还走在一个自由的世界里：此刻我又是个囚犯了。

===Original===

The sky was still over me, the clammy morning caressed me; but walls of wire and stone told me that my instant of freedom had departed. I was in fact traversing a lane no wider than the gate; on my left, barbed-wire separated me from the famous *cour* in which *les femmes se promenent*﻿—a rectangle about 50 feet deep and 200 long, with a stone wall at the further end of it and otherwise surrounded by wire;﻿—on my right, grey sameness of stone, the ennui of the regular and the perpendicular, the ponderous ferocity of silence.﻿ ﻿…
I had taken automatically some six or eight steps in pursuit of the fleeing spectre when, right over my head, the grey stone curdled with a female darkness; the hard and the angular softening in a putrescent explosion of thick wriggling laughter. I started, looked up, and encountered a window stuffed with four savage fragments of crowding Face: four livid, shaggy disks focusing hungrily; four pair of uncouth eyes rapidly smouldering; eight lips shaking in a toothless and viscous titter. Suddenly above and behind these terrors rose a single horror of beauty﻿—a crisp vital head, a young ivory actual face, a night of firm, alive, icy hair, a white, large, frightful smile.
… The thing was crying two or three paces in front of me: “Come!” The heads had vanished as by magic.
I dived forward; followed through a little door in the wall into a room about fifteen feet square, occupied by a small stove, a pile of wood, and a ladder. He plunged through another even smaller door, into a bleak rectangular place, where I was confronted on the left by a large tin bath and on the right by ten wooden tubs, each about a yard in diameter, set in a row against the wall. “Undress” commanded the spectre. I did so. “Go into the first one.” I climbed into the tub. “You shall pull the string,” the spectre said, hurriedly throwing his cigarette into a corner. I stared upward, and discovered a string dangling from a kind of reservoir over my head: I pulled: and was saluted by a stabbing crash of icy water. I leaped from the tub. “Here is your napkin. Make dry yourself”﻿—he handed me a piece of cloth a little bigger than a handkerchief. “Hurree.” I donned my clothes, wet and shivering and altogether miserable. “Good. Come now!” I followed him, through the room with the stove, into the barbed-wire lane.

===Chinese===

天空还在我头顶，黏湿的早晨爱抚着我；但铁丝与石头的墙告诉我，我那自由的瞬间已然逝去。我实际上正走在一条不比那道门宽多少的夹道里：左边，铁丝网把我同那个女人们散步（*les femmes se promenent*）的著名院子（*cour*）隔开——一个约五十英尺深、二百英尺长的长方场，远端一堵石墙，其余各面围着铁丝；——右边，灰色划一的石头，规则与垂直的单调，沉默那沉重的凶暴。…
我下意识地又追了那逃遁的幽灵六七步，忽然，正对着我头顶，灰石头凝出一团女性的黑暗；坚硬与棱角在一场黏稠蠕动大笑的腐烂爆炸中软了下来。我一惊，抬头撞见一扇塞满四块凶猛的拥挤之脸的窗：四张青灰、蓬乱的圆盘饥饿地聚焦；四双粗野的眼睛急速阴燃；八片嘴唇在无牙而黏稠的窃笑中发抖。蓦地，这些恐怖之上、之后，升起一个美的孤恐——一颗爽利而有生气的头，一张年轻象牙般真切的脸，一头浓密、鲜活、冰冷的黑夜之发，一个大大的、白色的、骇人的微笑。
…那东西在我前面两三步喊着：「来！」那些头已魔法般消失。
我一头扎上前；跟着穿过墙上一扇小门，进了一间十五英尺见方的屋子，里面有一只小炉子、一堆木柴和一架梯子。他一头又钻进一扇更小的门，进了一处荒凉的长方形所在，左边迎着我一只大锡浴缸，右边贴墙一列十只木盆，每只直径约一码。「脱衣服。」幽灵命令。我照办。「进第一只。」我爬进盆里。「你拉那根绳。」幽灵说，匆匆把烟扔进角落。我向上望，发现一根绳从头顶一个什么储水器里垂下来：我一拉：迎接我的是一记冰水针刺的倾泻。我从盆里蹦出来。「这是您的毛巾。擦干自己（Make dry yourself）」——他递给我一块比手帕大不了多少的布。「快点（Hurree）。」我穿上衣服，湿着，抖着，浑身凄惨。「好。现在来！」我跟着他，穿过有炉子的屋子，回到铁丝夹道。

===Original===

A hoarse shout rose from the yard﻿—which was filled with women, girls, children, and a baby or two. I thought I recognised one of the four terrors who had saluted me from the window, in a girl of 18 with a soiled slobby body huddling beneath its dingy dress; her bony shoulders stifled in a shawl upon which excremental hair limply spouted; a huge empty mouth; and a red nose, sticking between the bluish cheeks that shook with spasms of coughing. Just inside the wire a figure reminiscent of Gré, gun on shoulder, revolver on hip, moved monotonously.
The apparition hurried me through the gate, and along the wall into the building, where instead of mounting the stairs he pointed down a long, gloomy corridor with a square of light at the end of it, saying rapidly, “Go to the promenade”﻿—and vanished.
With the laughter of the Five still ringing in my ears, and no very clear conception of the meaning of existence, I stumbled down the corridor; bumping squarely into a beefy figure with a bull’s neck and the familiar revolver who demanded furiously: “What are you doing there? *Nom de Dieu!*”﻿—“*Pardon. Les douches*,” I answered, quelled by the collision.﻿—He demanded in wrathy French “Who took you to the douches?”﻿—For a moment I was at a complete loss﻿—then Fritz’s remark about the new *baigneur* flashed through my mind: “Ree-shar” I answered calmly.﻿—The bull snorted satisfactorily. “Get into the *cour* and hurry up about it,” he ordered.﻿—“*C’est par là?*” I inquired politely.﻿—He stared at me contemptuously without answering; so I took it upon myself to use the nearest door, hoping that he would have the decency not to shoot me. I had no sooner crossed the threshold when I found myself once more in the welcome air; and not ten paces away I espied B. peacefully lounging, with some thirty others, within a *cour* about one quarter the size of the women’s. I marched up to a little dingy gate in the barbed-wire fence, and was hunting for the latch (as no padlock was in evidence) when a scared voice cried loudly “*Qu’est ce que vous faites là!*” and I found myself stupidly looking into a rifle. B.

===Chinese===

院子里升起一片粗哑的欢呼——院里满是女人、姑娘、孩子，还有一两个婴儿。我想我认出了窗口向我致意的四大恐怖之一：一个十八岁的姑娘，邋遢松弛的身子缩在昏暗的裙子里；瘦骨嶙峋的肩膀闷在一条披肩下，排泄物似的头发无力地喷薄其上；一张巨大空洞的嘴；一只红鼻子，戳在两片发青、被咳嗽的痉挛抖个不停的腮中间。铁丝网内一个令人想起格雷的身影，掃着枪，挎着左轮，单调地走动。
那幽灵押着我穿过大门，沿墙进楼，但他没有上楼，而是朝下指了指一条尽头亮着一方光的漫长阴暗走廊，急急地说：「去放风（Go to the promenade）」——就不见了。
五张笑脸的笑声还在我耳边回响，我对存在的意义也还不甚了了，一路跌撞着下了走廊；迎头撞上一个牛颈肉感的身影，带着熟悉的老式左轮，狂怒地喝问：「你在那儿干什么？他妈妈的（*Nom de Dieu*）！」——「对不起。淋浴（*Pardon. Les douches*）。」我被撞得没了脾气，答道。——他用怒气冲冲的法语追问：「谁带你去淋浴的？」——我一时刻全然不知所措——随即弗里茨那句关于新澡堂工的话闪过脑际：「李-沙（Ree-shar）。」我平静地答。——那头牛满意地哼了哼。「进院子（*cour*）去，麻利点。」他命令。——「是在那边吗（*C'est par là?*）」我礼貌地问。——他轻蔑地瞪着我，不答；于是我自作主张用了最近的那道门，心里指望他还有点体面不至于枪毙我。我刚一跨过门槛便重新回到了受欢迎的空气里；不出十步，我望见B.正同另外约三十个人，在一个约有女院四分之一大的院子里（*cour*）安闲地溜达。我朝铁丝网上一扇小破门走去，正摸索门闩（因为没见着挂锁），一个惊惶的声音大喊：「你在那儿干什么！（*Qu'est ce que vous faites là!*）」我发现自己傻傻地望进一杆步枪。B.

===Original===

, Fritz, Harree, Pompom, Monsieur Auguste, The Bear, and the last but not least Count de Bragard immediately informed the trembling *planton* that I was a Nouveau who had just returned from the *douches* to which I had been escorted by Monsieur Reeshar, and that I should be admitted to the *cour* by all means. The cautious watcher of the skies was not, however, to be fooled by any such fol-de-rol and stood his ground. Fortunately at this point the beefy *planton* yelled from the doorway “Let him in,” and I was accordingly let in, to the gratification of my friends, and against the better judgment of the guardian of the *cour*, who muttered something about having more than enough to do already.
I had not been mistaken as to the size of the men’s yard: it was certainly not more than twenty yards deep and fifteen wide. By the distinctness with which the shouts of *les femmes* reached my ears I perceived that the two *cours* adjoined. They were separated by a stone wall ten feet in height, which I had already remarked (while en route to *les douches*) as forming one end of the *cour des femmes*. The men’s *cour* had another stone wall slightly higher than the first, and which ran parallel to it; the two remaining sides, which were property ends, were made by the familiar barbed-wire.

===Chinese===

、弗里茨、阿累、彭彭、奥古斯特先生、熊，以及最后但同样重要的布拉加尔伯爵，立刻向那个发抖的卫兵（*planton*）说明：我是个新来的（Nouveau），刚由李沙尔先生（Monsieur Reeshar）押去洗了淋浴回来，无论如何应当放进院子。不过，这位谨慎的天空守望者岂会被这种鬼把戏哄骗，寸步不让。幸好这时那个肉感的卫兵从门口喊了一嗓子「放他进来」，于是我被放了进来，朋友们皆大欢喜，院子的守护者则咕哝了句他手头的事已经多得干不完。
我对院子大小的估计没有错：深肯定不过二十码，宽不过十五码。从女人们（*les femmes*）的叫喊声入耳的清晰程度，我察觉两个院子（*cours*）彼此紧邻。中间隔一堵十英尺高的石墙，我先前（在去淋浴的路上）已注意到它构成女院（*cour des femmes*）的一端。男院另有一堵比第一堵略高的石墙，与之平行；剩下两侧属于地界墙，由熟悉的铁丝网构成。

===Original===

The furniture of the *cour* was simple: in the middle of the further end, a wooden sentry-box was placed just inside the wire; a curious contrivance, which I discovered to be a sister to the booth upstairs, graced the wall on the left which separated the two *cours*, while further up on this wall a horizontal iron bar projected from the stone at a height of seven feet and was supported at its other end by a wooden post, the idea apparently being to give the prisoners a little taste of gymnastics; a minute wooden shed filled the right upper corner and served secondarily as a very partial shelter for the men and primarily as a stable for an extraordinary water-wagon, composed of a wooden barrel on two wheels with shafts which would not possibly accommodate anything larger than a diminutive donkey (but in which I myself was to walk not infrequently, as it proved); parallel to the second stone wall, but at a safe distance from it, stretched a couple of iron girders serving as a barbarously cold seat for any unfortunate who could not remain on his feet the entire time; on the ground close by the shed lay amusement devices numbers two and three﻿—a huge iron cannonball and the six-foot iron axle of a departed wagon﻿—for testing the strength of the prisoners and beguiling any time which might lie heavily on their hands after they had regaled themselves with the horizontal bar; and finally, a dozen mangy apple-trees, fighting for their very lives in the angry soil, proclaimed to all the world that the *cour* itself was in reality a *verger*.

> “Les pommiers sont pleins de pommes;

Allons au verger, Simone.”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

院子的陈设很简单：远端正中，一座木制哨亭立在铁丝网内侧；一件我后来发现同楼上那位是姊妹的奇妙装置，装点在隔开两个院子的左侧墙上；再往上，同一面墙七英尺高处伸出一根水平铁杠，另一头由一根木柱支撑，用意显然是让囚徒们稍尝体操的滋味；一个小小的木棚占满右上角，次要地充当聊胜于无的遮蔽，主要地充作一辆非凡水车的马厩——那车由一只安在两轮上的木桶和两根车辕组成，辕间充其量容得下一头迷你毛驴（但事实证明我自己将不止一次驾辕其间）；同第二堵石墙平行、但保持着安全距离，横着两根铁梁，为任何不幸无法全程站立的人充当一条冷得野蛮的坐凳；棚子近旁的地上躺着娱乐器械二号和三号——一颗巨大的铁炮弹，和一根六英尺长的、某辆已故马车的铁轴——供囚徒们测试膂力，并消遣他们在玩过单杠之后仍然沉重的时光；最后，一打癞皮苹果树在愤怒的泥土里挣扎求生，向全世界宣告：这院子（*cour*）其实是一座果园（*verger*）。

> 「苹果树儿苹果满枝；

咱们去果园吧，西蒙娜。」…

===Original===

A description of the *cour* would be incomplete without an enumeration of the manifold duties of the *planton* in charge, which were as follows: to prevent the men from using the horizontal bar, except for chinning, since if you swung yourself upon it you could look over the wall into the women’s *cour*; to see that no one threw anything over the wall into said *cour*; to dodge the cannonball which had a mysterious habit of taking advantage of the slope of the ground and bounding along at a prodigious rate of speed straight for the sentry-box; to watch closely anyone who inhabited the *cabinet d’aisance*, lest he should make use of it to vault over the wall; to see that no one stood on the girders, for a similar reason; to keep watch over anyone who entered the shed; to see that everyone urinated properly against the wall in the general vicinity of the cabinet; to protect the apple-trees into which well-aimed pieces of wood and stone were continually flying and dislodging the sacred fruit; to mind that no one entered or exited by the gate in the upper fence without authority; to report any signs, words, tokens, or other immoralities exchanged by prisoners with girls sitting in the windows of the women’s wing (it was from one of these windows that I had recently received my salutation), also names of said girls, it being forbidden to exhibit any part of the female person at a window while the males were on promenade; to quell all fights and especially to prevent people from using the wagon axle as a weapon of defense or offense; and last, to keep an eye on the sweeper when he and his wheelbarrow made use of a secondary gate situated in the fence at the further end, not far from the sentry-box, to dump themselves.
Having acquainted me with the various *défendus* which limited the activities of a man on promenade, my friends proceeded to enliven the otherwise somewhat tedious morning by shattering one after another all rules and regulations.

===Chinese===

不历数当班卫兵（*planton*）的种种职责，对院子的描述便不完全。其职责如下：防止男人们把单杠当荡杠用（只许引体向上），因为若荡上去便可越过墙头看女院；确保没人朝墙那边的院子里扔东西；躲闪那颗炮弹——它有个神秘的习惯，专爱借地势的坡度，以惊人的速度笔直扑向哨亭；严密监视任何光顾便所的人，谨防他借之翻墙；基于同理，确保没人站上铁梁；看管任何进棚子的人；确保人人都在便所附近一带对着墙规规矩矩地小便；保护苹果树——瞄得准的木块石块不断飞进树里，摇落神圣的果实；留心没人擅自从上端栅栏的门进出；上报囚徒们同女生楼窗口姑娘们（我方才的礼遇即来自其中一扇窗）交换的任何暗号、字条、信物及其他不道德行为，同时上报上述姑娘的姓名——男生放风期间，女性身体的任何部位露出窗口均属违禁；平息一切斗殴，尤其防止人们把车轴当攻防武器；最后，在扫地的同他那辆独轮车经由远端栅栏上离哨亭不远的另一道门倾倒自身时，盯紧他。
朋友们让我领教了限制放风者活动的各项禁令（*défendus*）之后，便着手打破一条又一条规章制度，为这个否则颇为沉闷的早晨添趣。

===Original===

Fritz, having chinned himself fifteen times, suddenly appeared astride of the bar, evoking a reprimand; Pompom bowled the *planton* with the cannonball, apologising in profuse and vile French; Harree the Hollander tossed the wagon-axle lightly half the length of the *cour*, missing The Bear by an inch; The Bear bided his time and cleverly hurled a large stick into one of the holy trees, bringing to the ground a withered apple for which at least twenty people fought for several minutes; and so on. The most open gestures were indulged in for the benefit of several girls who had braved the official wrath and were enjoying the morning at their windows. The girders were used as a racetrack. The beams supporting the shed-roof were shinned. The water-wagon was dislocated from its proper position. The cabinet and urinal were misused. The gate was continually admitting and emitting persons who said they were thirsty, and must get a drink at a tub of water which stood around the corner. A letter was surreptitiously thrown over the wall into the *cour des femmes*.
The *planton* who suffered all these indignities was a solemn youth with wise eyes situated very far apart in a mealy expressionless ellipse of face, to the lower end of which clung a piece of down, exactly like a feather sticking to an egg. The rest of him was fairly normal with the exception of his hands, which were not mates; the left being considerably larger, and made of wood.
I was at first somewhat startled by this eccentricity; but soon learned that with the exception of two or three, who formed the Surveillant’s permanent staff and of whom the beefy one was a shining example, all the *plantons* were supposed to be unhealthy; they were indeed the disabled whom *le gouvernement français* sent from time to time to La Ferté and similar institutions for a little outing, and as soon as they had recovered their health under these salubrious influences they were shipped back to do their bit for world-safety, democracy, freedom, etc., in the trenches. I also learned that, of all the ways of attaining *cabinot*, by far the simplest was to apply to a *planton*, particularly to a permanent *planton*, say the beefy one (who was reputed to be peculiarly touchy on this point) the term *embusqué*. This method never failed.

===Chinese===

弗里茨引体向上十五次之后，忽然翻上杠骑坐，招来一声申斥；彭彭用炮弹保龄了一下卫兵，用滔滔而恶毒的法语道歉；荷兰人阿累把车轴轻轻抛出半个院子长，离熊只差一英寸；熊伺准时机，巧妙地把一根大棍投进圣树之一，打下一只蔫苹果，至少二十人为它激战了好几分钟；凡此种种。为几位不惧官怒、正在窗口享受早晨的姑娘，人们尽情表演最公开的手势。铁梁被当作跑道。撑棚顶的梁柱被爬上爬下。水车被挪离了原位。便所和小便处被滥用。那道门不停地放人进出，进出者都说口渴，必须到拐角一桶水那儿喝一口。一封信被偷偷扔过墙，扔进了女院。
忍受这一切凌辱的卫兵是个严肃的青年，一双智慧的眼睛生得很开，嵌在一张粉质、无表情的椭圆脸上，椭圆下端粘着一撮绒毛，活像粘在鸡蛋上的一根羽毛。他其余部位还算正常，除了那双手：两只不配对；左边的明显大些，而且是木头做的。
起初这古怪让我有点吃惊；但很快便了解到，除了两三个组成监守常任班子的（肉感的那位便是光辉榜样），所有卫兵（*plantons*）都理应不健康；他们其实是些病残者，被法国政府（*le gouvernement français*）不时送到拉费尔泰及同类机构来小小疗养，一旦在这等有益健康的影响下恢复了健康，便被装运回去，在战壕里为世界安全、民主、自由等等尽自己的一份力。我还了解到，在通往小号（*cabinot*）的一切途径中，最简便的莫过于对一个卫兵——尤其是常任卫兵，比如那位肉感的（据说他对此点特别炸毛）——使用「后方老爷（*embusqué*）」一词。此法从不落空。

===Original===

To its efficacy many of the men and more of the girls (by whom the *plantons*, owing to their habit of taking advantage of the weaker sex at every opportunity, were even more despised) attested by not infrequent spasms of consumptive coughing, which could be plainly heard from the further end of one *cour* to the other.
In a little over two hours I learned an astonishing lot about La Ferté itself: it was a coeducational receiving station whither were sent from various parts of France (a) males suspected of espionage and (b) females of a well-known type found in the zone of the armies. It was pointed out to me that the task of finding such members of the human race was *pas difficile:* in the case of the men, any foreigner would do provided his country was neutral (e.g. Holland); as for the girls, inasmuch as the armies of the Allies were continually retreating, the *zone des armées* (particularly in the case of Belgium) was always including new cities, whose *petites femmes* became automatically subject to arrest. It was not to be supposed that all the women of La Ferté were *putains*: there were a large number of respectable women, the wives of prisoners, who met their husbands at specified times on the floor below the men’s quarters, whither man and woman were duly and separately conducted by *plantons*. In this case no charges had been preferred against the women; they were voluntary prisoners, who had preferred to freedom this living in proximity to their husbands. Many of them had children; some babies. In addition there were certain *femmes honnêtes* whose nationality, as in the case of the men, had cost them their liberty; Marguerite the washerwoman, for example, was a German.
La Ferté Macé was not properly speaking a prison, but a Porte or Detention Camp: that is to say, persons sent to it were held for a Commission, composed of an official, a lawyer, and a captain of gendarmes, which inspected the Camp and passed upon each case in turn for the purpose of determining the guiltiness of the suspected party. If the latter were found guilty by the Commission, he or she was sent off to a regular prison camp for the duration of the war; if not guilty, he or she was (in theory) set free. The Commission came to La Ferté once every three months.

===Chinese===

其效力如何，许多男人和更多姑娘（由于卫兵们逮着机会就占弱者便宜的习惯，她们尤其鄙视他们）可以作证：她们那不难从院子这头听到那头的痨病式的咳嗽阵发，便是明证。
在两小时多一点的时间里，我对拉费尔泰本身有了惊人的了解：这是一所男女合校的收容站，从法国各地送来的有 (a) 涉嫌间谍罪的男性和 (b) 在军队区发现的某个众所周知类型之女性。有人向我指出，寻找此类人类成员的任务并不难（*pas difficile*）：就男人而言，只要是中立国的（例如荷兰）外国人，谁都可以充数；至于姑娘们，既然协约国军队不断撤退，军队区（*zone des armées*，比利时尤其如此）便不断纳入新的城市，那些城市的小女人们（*petites femmes*）便自动成为拘捕对象。不能以为拉费尔泰的女人全是妓女（*putains*）：有为数不少的可敬妇女，是囚犯的妻子，在规定时间去男监楼下同丈夫相会，由卫兵分别妥加押送前往。这种情形下对女人们并无任何指控；她们是自愿的囚犯，宁愿放弃自由，选择守在丈夫近旁。许多人带着孩子；有些带着婴儿。此外还有一些诚实的女人（*femmes honnêtes*），同男人一样因国籍失去了自由；比如洗衣妇玛格丽特（Marguerite）就是德国人。
严格说来，拉费尔泰马塞不是监狱，而是一道「门」，即拘留营：也就是说，送来的人由一个委员会审处——委员会由一名官员、一名律师和一名宪兵上尉组成，巡视营中，逐案审定，以确定嫌疑者之罪嫌。若后者被委员会认定有罪，他或她将被送往正规战俘营关押至战争结束；若无罪，则（理论上）开释。委员会每三个月来拉费尔泰一次。

===Original===

It should be added that there were prisoners who had passed the Commission, two, three, four, and even five times, without any appreciable result; there were *prisonnierès* who had remained in La Ferté a year, and even eighteen months.
The authorities at La Ferté consisted of the Directeur, or general overlord, the Surveillant, who had the *plantons* (orderlies) under him and was responsible to the Directeur for the administration of the camp, and the *Gestionnaire* (who kept the accounts). As assistant, the Surveillant had a mail clerk who acted as translator on occasion. Twice a week the camp was visited by a regular French army doctor (*médecin major*) who was supposed to prescribe in severe cases and to give the women venereal inspection at regular intervals. The daily routine of attending to minor ailments and injuries was in the hands of Monsieur Ree-shar (Richard), who knew probably less about medicine than any man living and was an ordinary prisoner like all of us, but whose impeccable conduct merited cosy quarters. A sweeper was appointed from time to time by the Surveillant, acting for the Directeur, from the inhabitants of La Ferté; as was also a cook’s assistant. The regular cook was a fixture, and a Boche like the other fixtures, Marguerite and Richard. This fact might seem curious were it not that the manner, appearance and actions of the Directeur himself proved beyond a shadow of a doubt that he was all which the term Boche could possibly imply.
“He’s a son-of-a-bitch,” B. said heartily. “They took me up to him when I came two days ago. As soon as he saw me he bellowed: ‘*Imbécile et inchrétien!*’; then he called me a great lot of other things, including Shame of my country, Traitor to the sacred cause of Liberty, Contemptible coward and Vile sneaking spy. When he got all through I said ‘I don’t understand French.’ You should have seen him then.”
Separation of the sexes was enforced, not, it is true, with success, but with a commendable ferocity. The punishments for both men and girls were dry bread and *cabinot*.
“What on earth is *cabinot?*” I demanded.
There were various *cabinots*: each sex had its regular *cabinot*, and there were certain extra ones. B. knew all about them from Harree and Pompom, who spent nearly all their time in the *cabinot*. They were rooms about nine feet square and six feet high.

===Chinese===

还应当补充：有的囚犯已过堂两回、三回、四回乃至五回，毫无可观的效果；有的女囚（*prisonnières*）在拉费尔泰待了一年，甚至十八个月。
拉费尔泰的当局由所长（Directeur，即总霸主）、监守（Surveillant，其下有卫兵们即勤务，对所长负责管理营务）和账房（*Gestionnaire*，管账）组成。监守有一名助手，即邮务文书，兼临时翻译。一名正规法军军医（*médecin major*）每周来营两次，负责重症处方，并定期给女人们做性病检查。日常小病小伤的处置则操在李沙尔先生（Monsieur Ree-shar，即理查德 Richard）手里——他对医学的了解大概少于世上任何活人，同我们大家一样是普通囚犯，只是行为无可挑剔，才挣得一份安逸差事。扫地的由监守代表所长不时从拉费尔泰住户中指派；帮厨的亦然。正厨是固定编制，同其他固定编制玛格丽特和理查德一样是个德国佬（Boche）。这一事实若非所长本人的做派、相貌与行径已不容置疑地证明他集德国佬一词可能暗示的一切于一身，倒也许显得奇怪。
「他是个狗娘养的。」B.由衷地说。「我两天前来的时候他们把我带到他跟前。他一见我就咆哮：『白痴兼异教徒（*Imbécile et inchrétien*）！』；接着又给我扣了一大串别的帽子，包括祖国之耻、自由神圣事业之叛徒、可鄙的懦夫、卑劣的鬼祟间谍。他骂完了，我说：『我不懂法语。』你真该看看他当时那张脸。」
男女隔绝被执行着——虽无成效，凶猛程度却堪嘉奖。对男人和姑娘们的惩罚一律是干面包和小号。
「小号（*cabinot*）到底是什么玩意？」我追问。
小号名目繁多：男女各有各自的常备小号，另有若干额外的。B.从阿累和彭彭那儿对它们了如指掌——那两位几乎常年住在小号里。那是些九英尺见方、六英尺高的房间。

===Original===

There was no light and no floor, and the ground (three were on the ground floor) was always wet and often a good many inches under water. The occupant on entering was searched for tobacco, deprived of his or her mattress and blanket, and invited to sleep on the ground on some planks. One didn’t need to write a letter to a member of the opposite sex to get *cabinot*, or even to call a *planton embusqué*﻿—there was a woman, a foreigner, who, instead of sending a letter to her embassy through the *bureau* (where all letters were read by the mail clerk to make sure that they said nothing disagreeable about the authorities or conditions of La Ferté) tried to smuggle it outside, and got twenty-eight days of *cabinot*. She had previously written three times, handing the letters to the Surveillant, as per regulations, and had received no reply. Fritz, who had no idea why he was arrested and was crazy to get in touch with his embassy, had likewise written several letters, taking the utmost care to state the facts only and always handing them in; but he had never received a word in return. The obvious inference was that letters from a foreigner to his embassy were duly accepted by the Surveillant (Warden), but rarely, if ever, left La Ferté.
B. and I were conversing merrily apropos the God-sent miracle of our escape from *Vingt-et-Un*, when a benign-faced personage of about fifty with sparse greyish hair and a Benjamin Franklin expression appeared on the other side of the fence, from the direction of the door through which I had passed after bumping the beefy bull. “*Planton*” it cried heavily to the wooden-handed one, “Two men to go get water.” Harree and Pompom were already at the gate with the archaic water-wagon, the former pushing from behind and the latter in the shafts. The guardian of the *cour* walked up and opened the gate for them, after ascertaining that another *planton* was waiting at the corner of the building to escort them on their mission. A little way from the *cour*, the stone wall (which formed one of its boundaries and which ran parallel to the other stone wall dividing the two *cours*) met the prison building; and here was a huge double door, twice padlocked, through which the waterseekers passed on to the street. There was a sort of hydrant up the street a few hundred yards, I was told. The cook (Benjamin F.

===Chinese===

没有灯，没有地板，地面（其中三间在底层）永远是湿的，常常积水好几英寸。入住者入内时先被搜走烟草，褫夺床垫和毯子，被邀请睡在几块木板上的地面。要得小号，未必要给异性写信，甚至未必要骂卫兵「后方老爷」——有个女人，外国人，本该经由办公处（*bureau*，所有信件在此由邮务文书阅读，以确保对拉费尔泰当局或状况无一句不敬之词）把信发往她的使馆，她却试图把信偷带出去，得了二十八天小号。此前她规规矩矩按规定把三封信交到监守手里，均石沉大海。弗里茨完全不知道自己为何被捕，急疯了想同使馆联系，也写过好几封信，字斟句酌只陈述事实，一律照章上交；但从未收到只言片语的回音。显而易见的推论是：外国人写给其使馆的信件由监守（狱监）如数收下，却极少——如果曾经有过——离开拉费尔泰。
B.和我正为我们逃出「二十一分队」（*Vingt-et-Un*）这场天赐奇迹谈得高兴，一个面目慈祥、五十上下、头发灰白稀疏、一脸本杰明·富兰克林式表情的人物出现在栅栏那头，来自我撞过那头肉牛之后穿过的那道门的方向。「卫兵。」他冲木头手那位沉沉地喊，「出两个人打水。」阿累和彭彭已经推着那辆古董水车到了门口，前者在后头推，后者驾着辕。院子的守护者走上前去给他们开门，先确认了另一名卫兵已等在建筑拐角、将押送他们执行任务。离院子不远，石墙（构成院子一界、与分隔两院的那堵石墙平行）同监狱建筑相接；这里有一扇上了两道锁的巨大对开门，打水人由此上街。街上几百码外有一个类似水栓的东西，我被告知。厨子（就是那位本杰明·富兰克林）

===Original===

, that is) required from three to six wagonfuls of water twice a day, and in reward for the labour involved in its capture was in the habit of giving a cup of coffee to the captors. I resolved that I would seek water at the earliest opportunity.
Harree and Pompom had completed their third and final trip and returned from the kitchen, smacking their lips and wiping their mouths with the backs of their hands. I was gazing airily into the muddy sky, when a roar issued from the doorway:
“*Montez les hommes!*” or “Send the men up!”
It was the beefy-necked. We filed from the *cour*, through the door, past a little window which I was told belonged to the kitchen, down the clammy corridor, up the three flights of stairs, to the door of The Enormous Room. Padlocks were unlocked, chains rattled, and the door thrown open. We entered. The Enormous Room received us in silence. The door was slammed and locked behind us by the *planton*, whom we could hear descending the gnarled and filthy stairs.
In the course of a half-hour, which time, as I was informed, intervened between the just-ended morning promenade and the noon meal which was the next thing on the program, I gleaned considerable information concerning the daily schedule of La Ferté. A typical day was divided by planton-cries as follows:
“*Café*,” “*Corvée d’eau*,” “*Nettoyage de Chambre*,” “*Montez les Hommes*,” “*A la soupe les hommes.*”
The most terrible cry of all, and which was not included in the regular program of planton-cries, consisted of the words:
“*Aux douches les hommes*”﻿—when all, sick, dead and dying not excepted, descended to the baths. Although *les douches* came only once in 15 days, such was the terror they inspired that it was necessary for the *planton* to hunt under mattresses for people who would have preferred death itself.
Upon remarking that *corvée d’eau* must be excessively disagreeable, I was informed that it had its bright side, viz., that in going to and from the sewer one could easily exchange a furtive signal with the women who always took pains to be at their windows at that moment. Influenced perhaps by this, Harree and Pompom were in the habit of doing their friends’ *corvées* for a consideration.

===Chinese===

每天需要三到六车水，一天两趟，作为采水劳苦的报酬，习惯上赏采水人一杯咖啡。我打定主意，一有机会就去采水。
阿累和彭彭完成了第三趟、也是最后一趟，从厨房回来，咂着嘴，用手背抹着嘴。我正悠然凝望泥泞的天空，门口炸出一声吼：
「男人们上楼（*Montez les hommes!*）！」或者说「把男人们送上去！」
是那位肉感脖子的。我们鱼贯出了院子，穿过那道门，经过一扇小窗（听说属于厨房），沿着黏湿的走廊，上三段楼梯，来到巨室（The Enormous Room）门前。挂锁被打开，铁链哗啦作响，门被推开。我们走进去。巨室默默接收了我们。门在我们身后哐当锁上，是那卫兵锁的，我们听得见他走下那疙疙瘩瘩、肮脏的楼梯。
在半个小时里——我被告知，这是刚刚结束的晨间放风同下一项节目午饭之间的间隔——我采集到了关于拉费尔泰每日日程的可观情报。典型的一天由卫兵的呼喊划分如下：
「咖啡（*Café*）」，「打水（*Corvée d'eau*）」，「扫屋（*Nettoyage de Chambre*）」，「男人们上楼（*Montez les Hommes*）」，「喝汤了男人们（*A la soupe les hommes.*）」
其中最可怕的呼喊，不在卫兵呼喊的常规节目之内，是这样几个词：
「男人们洗澡（*Aux douches les hommes*）」——届时全体，病者、死者与垂死者概不例外，统统下楼入浴。淋浴（*les douches*）虽十五天才来一回，其引发的恐怖却大到卫兵必须钻到床垫底下去搜人，被搜者宁死不愿。
当我评论说打水想必极其讨厌时，我被告知它也有光明面：即，往返于下水道之间时，可以轻易同女人们偷偷交换信号——她们总特意在那个钟点守在窗口。也许正是受了这点影响，阿累和彭彭习惯于有偿替朋友们代打水。

===Original===

The girls, I was further instructed, had their *corvée* (as well as their meals) just after the men; and the miraculous stupidity of the *plantons* had been known to result in the coincidence of the two.
At this point somebody asked me how I had enjoyed my shower?
I was replying in terms of unmeasured opprobrium when I was interrupted by that gruesome clanking and rattling which announced the opening of the door. A moment later it was thrown wide, and the beefy-neck stood in the doorway, a huge bunch of keys in his paw, and shouted:
“*A la soupe les hommes.*”
The cry was lost in a tremendous confusion, a reckless thither-and-hithering of humanity, everyone trying to be at the door, spoon in hand, before his neighbour. B. said calmly, extracting his own spoon from beneath his mattress on which we were seated: “They’ll give you yours downstairs and when you get it you want to hide it or it’ll be pinched”﻿—and in company with Monsieur Bragard, who had refused the morning promenade, and whose gentility would not permit him to hurry when it was a question of such a low craving as hunger, we joined the dancing roaring throng at the door. I was not too famished myself to be unimpressed by the instantaneous change which had come over The Enormous Room’s occupants. Never did Circe herself cast upon men so bestial an enchantment. Among these faces convulsed with utter animalism I scarcely recognised my various acquaintances. The transformation produced by the *planton’s* shout was not merely amazing; it was uncanny, and not a little thrilling. These eyes bubbling with lust, obscene grins sprouting from contorted lips, bodies unclenching and clenching in unctuous gestures of complete savagery, convinced me by a certain insane beauty. Before the arbiter of their destinies some thirty creatures, hideous and authentic, poised, cohering in a sole chaos of desire; a fluent and numerous cluster of vital inhumanity. As I contemplated this ferocious and uncouth miracle, this beautiful manifestation of the sinister alchemy of hunger, I felt that the last vestige of individualism was about utterly to disappear, wholly abolished in a gambolling and wallowing throb.
The beefy-neck bellowed:
“Are you all here?”
A shrill roar of language answered. He looked contemptuously around him, upon the thirty clamouring faces each of which wanted to eat him﻿—puttees, revolver and all.

===Chinese===

我还被告知，姑娘们的打水（*corvée*）（连同她们的开饭）紧排在男人们之后；而卫兵们那种奇迹般的愚蠢，有时竟能促成二者相逢。
正说到这儿，有人问我淋浴洗得可尽兴？
我正用毫不留情的鄙夷之词作答，就被那预示开门的可怕铁链锒铛声打断了。片刻之后门大开，肉脖子立在门口，爪里攥着一大串钥匙，喊道：
「喝汤了男人们（*A la soupe les hommes.*）」
呼喊淹没在一场惊天动地的混乱里，一股不管不顾的人流左冲右突，人人都想手掃勺子赶在邻居之前冲到门口。B.不动声色地说——一边从我们坐着的床垫底下抽出他自己的勺子：「他们会发你一把，下楼领；领了就得藏好，不然会被人顺走。」——于是我们同布拉加尔先生一道加入门口那支手舞足蹈、咆哮如雷的人马，布拉加尔先生拒绝了晨间放风，他的绅士风度不允许他在饥饿这种低级欲求面前失了从容。我自己还没饿到对巨室住户这番瞬息万变的变化无动于衷的地步。喀耳刻本人也从未对人类施过如此兽性的魔法。在这一片彻底动物性痉挛的嘴脸中间，我几乎认不出我那几位熟人了。卫兵一声吼制造的变身不仅是惊人；它诡异，而且颇令人心颤。这一双双沸腾着贪欲的眼，这一张张歪嘴上生出的淫笑，这一具具身体以全然野蛮的油腻手势开合收放，以一种疯狂的美丽说服了我。在她们命运的主宰者面前，约莫三十个生物，丑陋而本真，蓄势而立，凝聚成单一的欲望混沌；一团流利而众多、生命力充沛的非人。当我凝视这凶猛粗野的奇迹，这饥饿那阴暗炼金术的美丽显现，我感到个人主义的最后残余即将彻底消失，将在一场蹦跳与打滚的悸动中被全然取消。
肉脖子吼道：
「都到齐了吗？」
一声尖锐的多语言咆哮作了回答。他轻蔑地环顾左右，望着三十张喧哗的、张张都想把他连绑腿带左轮一口吃掉的脸。

===Original===

Then he cried:
“*Allez, descendez.*”
Squirming, jostling, fighting, roaring, we poured slowly through the doorway. Ridiculously. Horribly. I felt like a glorious microbe in huge absurd din irrevocably swathed. B. was beside me. A little ahead Monsieur Auguste’s voice protested. Count Bragard brought up the rear.
When we reached the corridor nearly all the breath was knocked out of me. The corridor being wider than the stairs allowed me to inhale and look around. B. was yelling in my ear:
“Look at the Hollanders and the Belgians! They’re always ahead when it comes to food!”
Sure enough: John the Bathman, Harree and Pompom were leading this extraordinary procession. Fritz was right behind them, however, and pressing the leaders hard. I heard Monsieur Auguste crying in his child’s voice:
“If every‑body goes slow‑er we will ar‑rive soon‑er. You mustn’t act like that!”
Then suddenly the roar ceased. The melee integrated. We were marching in orderly ranks. B. said:
“The Surveillant!”
At the end of the corridor, opposite the kitchen window, there was a flight of stairs. On the third stair from the bottom stood (teetering a little slowly back and forth, his lean hands joined behind him and twitching regularly, a kepi tilted forward on his cadaverous head so that its visor almost hid the weak eyes sunkenly peering from under droopy eyebrows, his pompous rooster-like body immaculately attired in a shiny uniform, his puttees sleeked, his cross polished)﻿—The Fencer. There was a renovated look about him which made me laugh. Also his pose was ludicrously suggestive of Napoleon reviewing the armies of France.
Our column’s first rank moved by him. I expected it to continue ahead through the door and into the open air, as I had myself done in going from *les douches* to *le cour;* but it turned a sharp right and then sharp left, and I perceived a short hall, almost hidden by the stairs. In a moment I had passed The Fencer myself and entered the hall. In another moment I was in a room, pretty nearly square, filled with rows of pillars. On turning into the hall the column had come almost to a standstill. I saw that the reason for this slowing-down lay in the fact that on entering the room every man in turn passed a table and received a piece of bread from the chef. When B. and I came opposite the table the dispenser of bread smiled pleasantly and nodded to B.

===Chinese===

然后他喊：
「走吧，下去（*Allez, descendez.*）」
扭着，挤着，斗着，吼着，我们缓缓涌出门洞。荒唐地。可怕地。我觉得自己像一个光荣的微生物，裹在一场巨大而荒唐的喧器里，无法自拔。B.在我身旁。前面一点，奥古斯特先生的嗓音在抗议。布拉加尔伯爵压后。
到了走廊，我几乎喘不过气来。走廊比楼梯宽些，容我吸口气四下张望。B.在我耳边大喊：
「瞧那些荷兰人和比利时人！一到吃的他们就冲最前头！」
果然：澡堂约翰、阿累和彭彭正率领着这支非凡的游行队伍。不过弗里茨紧跟在他们身后，对领头人穷追不舍。我听见奥古斯特先生用他的童音喊：
「如——果大家慢——一点，我们就能早——点到。你们可不能这样！」
忽然咆哮停了。乱军成列。我们竟迈着整齐的队伍行进。B.说：
「监守！」
走廊尽头，厨房窗对过，有一段楼梯。自下数第三级上站着（缓缓前后晃着，瘦手背在身后规律地抽动，制帽前倾压在死人似的头上，帽檐几乎遮住低眉下深陷窥视的弱眼，他那神气活现的公鸡身板穿着一尘不染的亮制服，绑腿梳得溜光，十字勋章擦得锃亮）——击剑手。他周身焕然一新，看得我直想笑。他那姿势又活脱脱是拿破仑检阅法兰西大军。
我们这一列的第一排从他面前走过。我以为队伍会径直穿过大门走进露天，像我先前从淋浴去院子那样；但它一个急右转，又一个急左转，我看见一段几乎被楼梯遮住的短过厅。一眨眼我也从击剑手面前经过，进了过厅。再一眨眼我已置身一间近乎正方形的屋子，一列列柱子排满其中。拐进过厅时队伍几乎停了下来。我看出减速的原因在于：进门时每个人依次经过一张桌子，从厨子手里领一块面包。轮到B.和我走到桌前时，那位面包分发者朝B.亲切地笑了笑，点了点头。

===Original===

, then selected a large hunk and pushed it rapidly into B.’s hands with an air of doing something which he shouldn’t. B. introduced me, whereupon the smile and selection was repeated.
“He thinks I’m a German,” B. explained in a whisper, “and that you are a German too.” Then aloud, to the cook: “My friend here needs a spoon. He just got here this morning and they haven’t given him one.”
The excellent person at the bread table hereupon said to me: “You shall go to the window and say I tell you to ask for spoon and you will catch one spoon”﻿—and I broke through the waiting line, approached the kitchen-window, and demanded of a roguish face within:
“A spoon, please.”
The roguish face, which had been singing in a high faint voice to itself, replied critically but not unkindly:
“You’re a new one?”
I said that I was, that I had arrived late last night.
It disappeared, reappeared, and handed me a tin spoon and cup, saying:
“You haven’t a cup?”﻿—“No,” I said.
“Here. Take this. Quick.” Nodding in the direction of the Surveillant, who was standing all this time on the stairs behind me.
I had expected from the cook’s phrase that something would be thrown at me which I should have to catch, and was accordingly somewhat relieved at the true state of affairs. On reentering the *salle à manger* I was greeted by many cries and wavings, and looking in their direction perceived everybody uproariously seated at wooden benches which were placed on either side of an enormous wooden table. There was a tiny gap on one bench where a place had been saved for me by B., with the assistance of Monsieur Auguste, Count Bragard, Harree and several other fellow-convicts. In a moment I had straddled the bench and was occupying the gap, spoon and cup in hand, and ready for anything.
The din was perfectly terrific. It had a minutely large quality. Here and there, in a kind of sonal darkness, solid sincere unintelligible absurd wisps of profanity heavily flickered. Optically the phenomenon was equally remarkable: seated waggingly swaying corpselike figures, swaggering, pounding with their little spoons, roaring, hoarse, unkempt. Evidently Monsieur le Surveillant had been forgotten. All at once the roar bulged unbearably. The roguish man, followed by the chef himself, entered with a suffering waddle, each of them bearing a huge bowl of steaming something.

===Chinese===

随即挑出一大块，飞快塞进B.手里，一脸干了什么不该干的事的神情。B.介绍了我，于是微笑和挑选又重复了一遍。
「他当我是德国人，」B.压低嗓音解释，「也当你是德国人。」然后大声对厨子说：「我这位朋友需要一把勺子。他今儿早上刚到，还没发给勺子。」
面包桌前那位出色人物于是对我说：「你到窗口去，说是我让你去讨勺子的，你就会接到一把勺子。」——于是我挤出等候的队伍，走到厨房窗口，向窗内一张淘气的脸开口：
「劳驾，来把勺子。」
那张淘气的脸正用又高又细的嗓音自顾自唱着歌，挑剔却不无善意地答：
「新来的？」
我说是，昨晚半夜刚到。
它消失，又出现，递给我一把锡勺和一只杯子，说：
「你没有杯子？」——「没有。」我说。
「拿着。快。」朝监守的方向点点头，监守一直站在我身后的楼梯上。
我原以为照厨子的说法，会有什么东西朝我抛来、得由我去接，因此事到临头反觉宽慰。重新进餐厅（*salle à manger*）时，无数叫喊和挥手迎接我，循声望去，只见人人都喧喧囔囔地坐在长木凳上，凳子摆在一张巨大木桌的两侧。一条凳子上有个小小的空档，是B.在奥古斯特先生、布拉加尔伯爵、阿累和其他几位难友的协助下为我占下的座位。转眼我已跨上长凳，占住空档，一手勺子一手杯，万事俱备。
喧声大得可怕。它有一种细密而宏大的质地。这里那里，在一种声响的黑暗中，一绺绺结实、诚恳、不知所云、荒唐的脏话沉甸甸地闪动。视觉上的奇观同样可观：坐着的、摇晃的、尸体般的人形，神气活现，敲着小勺，吼着，嗌着，蓬头垢面。监守先生显然已被抛在脑后。咆哮声忽然涨到不可忍耐。那淘气的人来了，厨子本人殿后，迈着受罪的八字步，一人捧一大盆冒着热气的什么。

===Original===

At least six people immediately rose, gesturing and imploring: “*Ici*”﻿—“*Mais non, ici*”﻿—“*Mettez par ici*”﻿—
The bearers plumped their burdens carefully down, one at the head of the table and one in the middle. The men opposite the bowls stood up. Every man seized the empty plate in front of him and shoved it into his neighbour’s hand; the plates moved toward the bowls, were filled amid uncouth protestations and accusations﻿—“*Mettez plus que ça*”﻿—“*C’est pas juste, alors*”﻿—“*Donnez-moi encore de pommes*”﻿—“*Nom de Dieu, il n’y a pas assez*”﻿—“*Cochon, qu’est-ce qu’il veut?*”﻿—“Shut up”﻿—“*Gott-ver-dummer*”﻿—and returned one by one. As each man received his own, he fell upon it with a sudden guzzle.
Eventually, in front of me, solemnly sat a faintly-smoking urine-coloured circular broth, in which soggily hung half-suspended slabs of raw potato. Following the example of my neighbours, I too addressed myself to *La Soupe*. I found her lukewarm, completely flavourless. I examined the hunk of bread. It was almost bluish in colour; in taste mouldy, slightly sour. “If you crumb some into the soup,” remarked B., who had been studying my reactions from the corner of his eye, “they both taste better.” I tried the experiment. It was a complete success. At least one felt as if one were getting nourishment. Between gulps I smelled the bread furtively. It smelled rather much like an old attic in which kites and other toys gradually are forgotten in a gentle darkness.
B. and I were finishing our soup together when behind and somewhat to the left there came the noise of a lock being manipulated. I turned and saw in one corner of the *salle à manger* a little door, shaking mysteriously. Finally it was thrown open, revealing a sort of minute bar and a little closet filled with what appeared to be groceries and tobacco; and behind the bar, standing in the closet, a husky, competent-looking lady. “It’s the canteen,” B. said. We rose, spoon in hand and breadhunk stuck on spoon, and made our way to the lady. I had, naturally, no money; but B.

===Chinese===

至少六个人立刻站起，打着手势恳求：「这儿（*Ici*）」——「不，这儿（*Mais non, ici*）」——「放这边（*Mettez par ici*）」——
捧盆人小心翼翼地撂下重负，一盆在桌上首，一盆在中段。对盆而坐的人起立。人人抓起面前的空盘塞进邻座手里；盘子朝大盆传去，在粗野的抗议和指控中盛满——「多来点（*Mettez plus que ça*）」——「这也太不公道了（*C'est pas juste, alors*）」——「再给我点土豆（*Donnez-moi encore de pommes*）」——「他妈妈的，不够分（*Nom de Dieu, il n'y a pas assez*）」——「猪，他想要什么？（*Cochon, qu'est-ce qu'il veut?*）」——「闭嘴」——「他妈的（*Gott-ver-dummer*）」——再一一传回。人人一拿到自己的那份，便一头扎进去猛吞。
终于，我面前端端正正地坐下了一碗微冒热气、尿色的圆汤，里面半悬半泡地搭着几片生土豆。我效法邻座，也向汤大人（*La Soupe*）下手了。我发现她温吞吞的，全然无味。我端详那块面包。颜色近乎发蓝；味道发霉，微微带酸。「如果你掰点碎进去泡着，」B.说——他一直用眼角研究我的反应——「两样都会好吃些。」我做了实验。大获成功。至少让人觉得是在汲取营养了。吞咽的间隙，我偷偷嗅那面包。它闻起来很像一间老旧的阁楼，风筝和别的玩具在温柔的黑暗里渐渐被遗忘。
B.和我正一起喝完汤，身后偏左传来摆弄锁具的声响。我回头，看见餐厅一角一扇小门神秘地颤动。终于它被推开，露出一方小小的柜台和一间堆满似乎是杂货烟草的小屋；柜台后头，屋里立着一位健壮、干练模样的女士。「是小卖部。」B.说。我们起身，一手掃勺，勺尖戳着一块面包，朝那位女士走去。我自然是身无分文；但B.

===Original===

reassured me that before the day was over I should see the Gestionnaire and make arrangements for drawing on the supply of ready cash which the gendarmes who took me from Gré had confided to The Surveillant’s care; eventually I could also draw on my account with Norton-Harjes in Paris; meantime he had *quelques sous* which might well go into chocolate and cigarettes. The large lady had a pleasant quietness about her, a sort of simplicity, which made me extremely desirous of complying with B.’s suggestion. Incidentally I was feeling somewhat uncertain in the region of the stomach, due to the unique quality of the lunch which I had just enjoyed, and I brightened at the thought of anything as solid as chocolate. Accordingly we purchased (or rather B. did) a *paquet jaune* and a cake of something which was not Meunier. And the remaining *sous* we squandered on a glass apiece of red acrid *pinard*, gravely and with great happiness pledging the hostess of the occasion and then each other.
With the exception of ourselves hardly anyone patronized the canteen, noting which I felt somewhat conspicuous. When, however, Harree Pompom and John the Bathman came rushing up and demanded cigarettes my fears were dispelled. Moreover the *pinard* was excellent.
“Come on! Arrange yourselves!” the bull-neck cried hoarsely as the five of us were lighting up; and we joined the line of fellow-prisoners with their breads and spoons, gaping, belching, trumpeting fraternally, by the doorway.
“*Tout le monde en haut!*” this *planton* roared.
Slowly we filed through the tiny hall, past the stairs (empty now of their Napoleonic burden), down the corridor, up the creaking gnarled damp flights, and (after the inevitable pause in which the escort rattled chains and locks) into The Enormous Room.
This would be about ten thirty.
Just what I tasted, did, smelled, saw, and heard, not to mention touched, between ten thirty and the completion of the evening meal (otherwise the four o’clock soup) I am quite at a loss to say. Whether it was that glass of *pinard* (plus, or rather times, the astonishing exhaustion bequeathed me by my journey of the day before) which caused me to enter temporarily the gates of forgetfulness, or whether the sheer excitement attendant upon my ultra-novel surroundings proved too much for an indispensable part of my so-called mind﻿—I do not in the least know.

===Chinese===

安慰我说，今天之内我就会见到账房，安排支取那笔现款——押送我离开格雷的宪兵们把它托付给了监守保管；往后我还可以支取我在巴黎诺顿-哈耶斯的账户；眼下他有几个子儿（*quelques sous*），尽可以换巧克力和香烟。这位大个子女士身上有一种愉快的安静，一种朴素，使我对B.的建议充满执行的渴望。顺便一提，我刚享用过的那顿品质独特的午饭，让我的胃部多少有些拿不定主意，一想到巧克力这样扎实的东西，我便精神一振。于是我们买了（确切说是B.买了）一包黄包装（*paquet jaune*）和一块算不上默尼埃（Meunier）牌的可可制品。剩下的子儿挥霍在每人一杯红色酸涩的红酒（*pinard*）上，我们庄重而幸福地举杯，先敬本次的女东道，再彼此互敬。
除了我们，几乎没人光顾小卖部，见此情形我自觉有几分惹眼。不过，待阿累、彭彭和澡堂约翰冲过来讨烟，我的顾虑便烟消云散了。何况那红酒确实不错。
「快！各就各位！」牛脖子哑着嗓子喊，我们五个正在点烟；我们便加入难友们排成的队——人人掃着面包和勺子，张着嘴，打着嗝，兄弟般地吹着牛——在门边。
「全都上楼（*Tout le monde en haut!*）」这位卫兵吼道。
我们缓缓鱼贯穿过小小的过厅，经过楼梯（此刻已卸下它拿破仑的重负），沿走廊而下，登上吱呀作响、疙疙瘩瘩、潮湿的楼梯，再（经过押送者摆弄铁链挂锁的例行停顿）进入巨室。
这大约是十点半。
从十点半到晚饭（又称四点钟的汤）结束之间，我尝了什么、做了什么、嗅了什么、看了什么、听了什么，更不必说摸了什么，我实在无从说起。不知是那杯红酒（乘上前一天旅途留给我的惊人疲惫）使我临时走入了遗忘之门，还是这超级新奇的环境所带来的纯粹兴奋，压垮了我那所谓头脑中一个不可或缺的部件——我一概不知。

===Original===

I am fairly certain that I went on afternoon promenade. After which I must surely have mounted to await my supper in The Enormous Room. Whence (after the due and proper interval) I doubtless descended to the clutches of *La Soupe Extraordinaire*﻿ ﻿… yes, for I perfectly recall the cry which made me suddenly to reenter the dimension of distinctness﻿ ﻿… and by Jove I had just finished a glass of *pinard*﻿ ﻿… somebody must have treated me﻿ ﻿… we were standing together, spoon in hand﻿ ﻿… when we heard﻿—
“*A la promenade*,”﻿ ﻿… we issued *en queue*, firmly grasping our spoons and bread, through the dining-room door. Turning right we were emitted, by the door opposite the kitchen, from the building itself into the open air. A few steps and we passed through the little gate in the barbed wire fence of the *cour*.
Greatly refreshed by my second introduction to the canteen, and with the digestion of the somewhat extraordinary evening meal apparently assured, I gazed almost intelligently around me. Count Bragard had declined the evening promenade in favour of The Enormous Room, but I perceived in the crowd the now familiar faces of the three Hollanders﻿—John, Harree and Pompom﻿—likewise of The Bear, Monsieur Auguste, and Fritz. In the course of the next hour I had become, if not personally, at least optically acquainted with nearly a dozen others.
Somewhat overawed by the animals Harree and Pompom (but nevertheless managing to overawe a goodly portion of his fellow-captives) an extraordinary human being paced the *cour*. On gazing for the first time directly at him I experienced a feeling of nausea. A figure inclined to corpulence, dressed with care, remarkable only above the neck﻿—and then what a head! It was large, and had a copious mop of limp hair combed back from the high forehead﻿—hair of a disagreeable blond tint, dutch-cut behind, falling over the pinkish soft neck almost to the shoulders. In this pianist’s or artist’s hair, which shook en masse when the owner walked, two large and outstanding and altogether brutal white ears tried to hide themselves. The face, a cross between classic Greek and Jew, had a Reynard expression, something distinctly wily and perfectly disagreeable.

===Chinese===

我大体确定自己参加了下午放风。此后想必是上楼回巨室等晚饭了。然后（在合乎规定的间隔之后）我无疑又落入了非凡汤大人的魔掌……对，因为我清楚地记得那声使我骤然重返清晰维度的高喊……而且老天，我刚喝完一杯红酒……想必有人请了我……我们正一起站着，人手一勺……这时我们听见——
「放风了（*A la promenade*）」，……我们排在队尾（*en queue*），紧掃着勺子和面包，从饭堂的门鱼贯而出。右转，经厨房对面的门出了楼，走进露天。几步之后，我们穿过院子铁丝网上那道小门。
经小卖部二次滋润，那顿非同寻常的晚饭似乎也消化无虞，我几乎算得上聪明地环顾四周。布拉加尔伯爵谢绝了晚间放风，偏心于巨室；但我在人群中认出了三位荷兰人现已熟悉的脸——约翰、阿累和彭彭——还有熊、奥古斯特先生和弗里茨。接下来的一个钟头里，即便谈不上亲自，至少在视觉上我结识了另外十来个人。
在畜生阿累和彭彭面前颇有些敬畏（却也成功地震住了相当一部分难友），一个非凡的人类正在院子里踱步。头一眼直视他时，我竟感到一阵恶心。一个发福倾向的身材，穿着讲究，唯独脖子以上不同凡响——那是一颗怎样的头颅！它很大，一头浓软的头发从高高的前额向后梳去——一种发灰的金色，脑后剪成荷兰式，散落在粉红的软脖颈上，几乎及肩。在这头钢琴家兼艺术家的头发里——主人一走动它便整体颤动——两只又大又招风、白得全然粗暴的耳朵试图藏身。那张脸，古典希腊与犹太人的混血，长着一副列那狐（Reynard）式的表情，某种分明的狡黠和十足的不快。

===Original===

An equally with the hair blond moustache﻿—or rather mustachios projectingly important﻿—waved beneath the prominent nostrils, and served to partially conceal the pallid mouth, weak and large, whose lips assumed from time to time a smile which had something almost foetal about it. Over the even weaker chin was disposed a blond goatee. The cheeks were fatty. The continually perspiring forehead exhibited innumerable pinkish pockmarks. In conversing with a companion this being emitted a disgusting smoothness, his very gestures were oily like his skin. He wore a pair of bloated wristless hands, the knuckles lost in fat, with which he smoothed the air from time to time. He was speaking low and effortless French, completely absorbed in the developing ideas which issued fluently from his mustachios. About him there clung an aura of cringing. His hair whiskers and neck looked as if they were trick neck whiskers and hair, as if they might at any moment suddenly disintegrate, as if the smoothness of his eloquence alone kept them in place.
We called him Judas.
Beside him, clumsily keeping the pace but not the step, was a tallish effeminate person whose immaculate funereal suit hung loosely upon an aged and hurrying anatomy. He wore a big black cap on top of his haggard and remarkably clean-shaven face, the most prominent feature of which was a red nose, which sniffed a little now and then as if its owner was suffering from a severe cold. This person emanated age, neatness and despair. Aside from the nose which compelled immediate attention, his face consisted of a few large planes loosely juxtaposed and registering pathos. His motions were without grace. He had a certain refinement. He could not have been more than forty-five. There was worry on every inch of him. Possibly he thought that he might die. B. said “He’s a Belgian, a friend of Count Bragard, his name is Monsieur Pet-airs.” From time to time Monsieur Pet-airs remarked something delicately and pettishly in a gentle and weak voice. His adam’s-apple, at such moments, jumped about in a longish slack wrinkled skinny neck which was like the neck of a turkey. To this turkey the approach of Thanksgiving inspired dread. From time to time M. Pet-airs looked about him sidewise as if he expected to see a hatchet. His hands were claws, kind, awkward and nervous. They twitched.

===Chinese===

一道同头发一样发黄的髭——或者说两撇趾高气扬的大髭——在突出的鼻孔下飘动，恰好半掩着那张苍白、阔大而软弱的嘴，那嘴唇不时咧出一个几乎带着胎儿气的微笑。软得更甚的下巴上，蓄着一撮黄色的山羊胡。两颊多肉。不断冒汗的前额布满无数粉红的痘疤。同同伴交谈时，这存在散发出一种令人作呕的圆滑，他的一举一动都油光光的，同他的皮肤一样。他长着一双肿胀而无腕的手，指节陷在脂肪里，不时用它们抚平空气。他低声说着毫不费力的法语，全然沉浸于从髭间流利涌出的思路。他周身缭绕着一层谄媚之气。他的头发、胡子和脖颈看上去像假脖、假须、假发，随时可能突然解体，仿佛全靠他雄辩的油光才勉强粘在原处。
我们叫他犹大（Judas）。
他身旁，勉强跟上速度却踩不上步点的，是个偏高的脂粉气人物，一身一尘不染的丧服松松垮垮罩在一副上了年纪、行色匆匆的骨架上。他那憔悴而刮得异柰干净的脸上一顶黑色大帽，脸上最突出的特征是一只红鼻子，不时抽动两下，仿佛主人正患者重感冒。此人浑身发散着年岁、整洁与绝望。除了那只非看不可的鼻子，他的脸由几块松松拼合的大平面构成，写满哀婉。他的动作毫无风度。他有某种雅致。他顶多四十五岁。他浑身上下每一寸都写着忧愁。他多半想着自己可能会死。B.说：「他是比利时人，布拉加尔伯爵的朋友，名叫佩泰尔先生（Monsieur Pet-airs）。」佩泰尔先生不时用温柔而虚弱的嗓音细声细气、略带嗔怪地评论些什么。这种时刻，他的喉结便在一截偏长、松弛、起皱、皮包骨的脖子里上蹿下跳，活像火鸡的脖子。一想到感恩节临近，这只火鸡便恐惧不已。佩泰尔先生不时朝身侧张望，仿佛指望看见一把斧头。他的双手是爪子，善良、笨拙而紧张。它们抽搐着。

===Original===

The bony and wrinkled things looked as if they would like to close quickly upon a throat.
B. called my attention to a figure squatting in the middle of the *cour* with his broad back against one of the more miserable trees. This figure was clothed in a remarkably picturesque manner: it wore a dark sombrero-like hat with a large drooping brim, a bright red gipsy shirt of some remarkably fine material with huge sleeves loosely falling, and baggy corduroy trousers whence escaped two brown, shapely, naked feet. On moving a little I discovered a face﻿—perhaps the handsomest face that I have ever seen, of a gold brown color, framed in an amazingly large and beautiful black beard. The features were finely formed and almost fluent, the eyes soft and extraordinarily sensitive, the mouth delicate and firm beneath a black moustache which fused with the silky and wonderful darkness falling upon the breast. The face contained a beauty and dignity which, as I first saw it, annihilated the surrounding tumult without an effort. Around the carefully formed nostrils there was something almost of contempt. The cheeks had known suns of which I might not think. The feet had travelled nakedly in countries not easily imagined. Seated gravely in the mud and noise of the *cour*, under the pitiful and scraggly *pommier*﻿ ﻿… behind the eyes lived a world of complete strangeness and silence. The composure of the body was graceful and Jovelike. This being might have been a prophet come out of a country nearer to the sun. Perhaps a god who had lost his road and allowed himself to be taken prisoner by *le gouvernement français*. At least a prince of a dark and desirable country, a king over a gold-skinned people who would return when he wished to his fountains and his houris. I learned upon inquiry that he travelled in various countries with a horse and cart and his wife and children, selling bright colours to the women and men of these countries. As it turned out, he was one of the Delectable Mountains; to discover which I had come a long and difficult way. Wherefore I shall tell you no more about him for the present, except that his name was Joseph Demestre.
We called him The Wanderer.
I was still wondering at my good luck in occupying the same miserable yard with this exquisite personage when a hoarse, rather thick voice shouted from the gate: “*L’américain!

===Chinese===

这两把骨头与皱皮，看上去很想朝某个喉咙疾速合拢。
B.提醒我注意院子正中一个蹲踞的身影，宽阔的脊背抵着其中一棵最不幸的树。这身影衣着出奇地如画：一顶宽檐低垂的深色墨西哥帽，一件料子极好、大袖宽垂的鲜红吉卜赛衬衫，一条宽松的灯芯绒裤，裤脚下逃出两只棕色、匀称、赤裸的脚。稍稍挪动，我看见了一张脸——也许是我此生见过最英俊的脸，金棕色，镶在一部大得惊人、美极了的黑胡子里。五官精致而近乎流畅，眼睛温柔而格外敏感，黑髭下那张嘴纤美而坚定，髭与垂在胸前那丝缎般美妙的暗色融为一体。那张脸自有一种美丽与尊严，我初见之下，它不费吹灰之力便抹平了周围的喧器。那精心轮廓的鼻孔旁，藏着一丝近乎轻蔑的东西。那双颊知道我不敢想象的太阳。那双脚赤裸地走过不易想象的国度。他端坐于院子的泥泞与嗓音里，坐在可怜兮兮、瘦骨嶙峋的苹果树下……那双眼睛后面，活着一个全然陌生而沉默的世界。身体的仪态优雅而近乎朱庇特式。这个存在，本可能是走出某个离太阳更近的国度的先知。或是一位迷了路、任凭自己被法国政府（*le gouvernement français*）俘虏的神。至少是某个幽暗而可欲之国的王子，一个金肤色民族之王，想何时回归他的泉水与天女，便可何时回归。一经打听我得知，他赶着一匹马、一辆车，携着妻儿游历各国，向各国男女兜售鲜亮的颜色。后来事实证明，他是悦目山之一；为发现他，我走了一段漫长而艰险的路。因此，眼下关于他我不再多讲，只说一句：他名叫约瑟夫·德梅斯特（Joseph Demestre）。
我们叫他漫游者（The Wanderer）。
我还沉浸在与这位精致人物同处一个可怜院子的好运里，门口忽然响起一个粗哑、含混的嗓音：「美国人！（*L'américain!*」

===Original===

*”
It was a *planton*, in fact the chief *planton* for whom all ordinary *plantons* had unutterable respect and whom all mere men unutterably hated. It was the *planton* into whom I had had the distinguished honour of bumping shortly after my visit to *le bain*.
The Hollanders and Fritz were at the gate in a mob, all shouting “Which” in four languages.
This *planton* did not deign to notice them. He repeated roughly “*L’américain.*” Then, yielding a point to their frenzied entreaties: “*Le nouveau.*”
B. said to me “Probably he’s going to take you to the Gestionnaire. You’re supposed to see him when you arrive. He’s got your money and will keep it for you, and give you an allowance twice a week. You can’t draw more than 20 francs. I’ll hold your bread and spoon.”
“Where the devil is the American?” cried the *planton*.
“Here I am.”
“Follow me.”
I followed his back and rump and holster through the little gate in the barbed wire fence and into the building, at which point he commanded “Proceed.”
I asked “Where?”
“Straight ahead,” he said angrily.
I proceeded. “Left!” he cried. I turned. A door confronted me. “*Entrez*,” he commanded. I did. An unremarkable looking gentleman in a French uniform, sitting at a sort of table. “*Monsieur le médecin, le nouveau.*” The doctor got up. “Open your shirt.” I did. “Take down your pants.” I did. “All right.” Then, as the *planton* was about to escort me from the room: “English?” he asked with curiosity. “No,” I said, “American.” “*Vraiment*”﻿—he contemplated me with attention. “South American are you?” “United States” I explained. “*Vraiment*”﻿—he looked curiously at me, not disagreeably in the least. “*Pourquoi vous êtes ici?*” “I don’t know,” I said smiling pleasantly, “except that my friend wrote some letters which were intercepted by the French censor.” “Ah,” he remarked. “*C’est tout.*”
And I departed. “Proceed!” cried the Black Holster. I retraced my steps, and was about to exit through the door leading to the *cour*, when “Stop! *Nom de Dieu!* Proceed!”
I asked “Where?” completely bewildered.
“Up,” he said angrily.
I turned to the stairs on the left, and climbed.
“Not so fast there,” he roared behind me.
I slowed up. We reached the landing. I was sure that the Gestionnaire was a very fierce man﻿—probably a lean slight person who would rush at me from the nearest door saying “Hands up” in French, whatever that may be.

===Chinese===

」
是个卫兵，而且是卫兵长，普通卫兵对他怀有难以言喻的敬畏，而普通人类对他怀有难以言喻的仇恨。正是我洗澡归来后不久有幸撞上的那位。
荷兰人和弗里茨在门口挤成一团，用四种语言齐喊「哪个」。
这位卫兵不屑于搭理他们。他粗声重复：「美国人。」随后拗不过他们发狂的恳求，让了一步：「新来的。」
B.对我说：「多半是要带你去见账房。按规矩到达就该去见他。他替你收着钱，替你保管，每周发你两次零用。最多只能取二十法郎。我帮你拿着面包和勺子。」
「那美国人在哪儿？」卫兵喊。
「我就是。」
「跟上。」
我跟着他的后背、后臀和枪套，穿过铁丝网上的小门进了楼，进楼时他下令：「往前走。」
我问：「去哪儿？」
「直走。」他怒气冲冲地说。
我直走。「左！」他喊。我转过去。一扇门迎着我。「进。」他命令。我进了。一位穿法国制服、模样平常的先生，坐在一张类似桌子的东西后面。「军医先生，新来的（*Monsieur le médecin, le nouveau*）。」医生站起来。「解开衬衫。」我解了。「脱裤子。」我脱了。「好了。」随后，卫兵正要押我出屋，他好奇地问：「英语？」我说是，我是美国人。「真的（*Vraiment*）」——他专注地打量我。「南美的吧？」「美利坚合众国。」我解释。「真的（*Vraiment*）」——他好奇地看着我，丝毫不令人不快。「您为什么在这儿？（*Pourquoi vous êtes ici?*）」「不知道，」我愉快地笑着说，「只知道我朋友写了些信，被法国检查官截了。」「啊，」他说。「就是这样（*C'est tout*）。」
我便出来了。「继续走！」黑枪套喊。我原路折返，正要从通往院子的门出去，只听「站住！他妈妈的！继续走！」
我彻底蒙了，问：「去哪儿？」
「上楼。」他怒气冲冲地说。
我转向左边的楼梯，往上爬。
「别那么快。」他在我身后吼。
我放慢了。我们到了楼梯口。我断定账房是个非常凶悍的人——多半是个精瘦细条的狠角色，会从最近的门里朝我猛扑过来，用法语喊「举起手来」，管它法语怎么讲。

===Original===

The door opposite me stood open. I looked in. There was the Surveillant standing, hands behind back, approvingly regarding my progress. I was asking myself, Should I bow? when a scurrying and a tittering made me look left, along a dark and particularly dirty hall. Women’s voices﻿ ﻿… I almost fell with surprise. Were not those shadows’ faces peering a little boldly at me from doors? How many girls were there﻿—it sounded as if there were a hundred﻿—
“*Qu’est-ce que vous faites*,” etc., and the *planton* gave me a good shove in the direction of another flight of stairs. I obligingly ascended; thinking of the Surveillant as a spider, elegantly poised in the centre of his nefarious web, waiting for a fly to make too many struggles.﻿ ﻿…
At the top of this flight I was confronted by a second hall. A shut door indicated the existence of a being directly over the Surveillant’s holy head. Upon this door, lest I should lose time in speculating, was in ample letters inscribed:

> **Gestionnaire**

===Chinese===

对面那扇门开着。我朝里看。监守站在那里，双手背后，赞许地注视着我的进展。我正自问要不要鞠躬，一阵碎步和窃笑使我朝左望去，沿一条黑暗而特别肮脏的过道。女人的声音……我惊得几乎跌倒。那些从门后大胆窥视我的，不是一张张影子的脸吗？姑娘们有多少——听着足足有一百个——
「你在干什么（*Qu'est-ce que vous faites*）」等等，卫兵朝另一段楼梯的方向把我狠狠一搡。我殷勤地爬了上去；一边把监守想成一只蜘蛛，优雅地伏在他那邪恶蛛网的中央，等着哪只苍蝇挣扎过头。…
这段楼梯顶上，又一条过道迎着我。一扇关着的门表明，正对监守圣顶之上，住着一位存在。这门上，怕我在猜测上浪费时间，用宽大的字母写着：

> **Gestionnaire（账房）**

===Original===

I felt unutterably lost. I approached the door. I even started to push it.
“*Attends, Nom de Dieu.*” The *planton* gave me another shove, faced the door, knocked twice, and cried in accents of profound respect: “Monsieur le Gestionnaire”﻿—after which he gazed at me with really supreme contempt, his neat pig-like face becoming almost circular.
I said to myself: This Gestionnaire, whoever he is, must be a very terrible person, a frightful person, a person utterly without mercy.
From within a heavy, stupid, pleasant voice lazily remarked:
“*Entrez.*”
The *planton* threw the door open, stood stiffly on the threshold, and gave me the look which *plantons* give to eggs when *plantons* are a little hungry.
I crossed the threshold, trembling with (let us hope) anger.
Before me, seated at a table, was a very fat personage with a black skull cap perched upon its head. Its face was possessed of an enormous nose, on which pince-nez precariously roosted; otherwise the face was large, whiskered, very German and had three chins. Extraordinary creature. Its belly, as it sat, was slightly dented by the tabletop, on which tabletop rested several enormous tomes similar to those employed by the recording angel on the Day of Judgment, an inkstand or two, innumerable pens and pencils, and some positively fatal looking papers. The person was dressed in worthy and semi-dismal clothes amply cut to afford a promenade for the big stomach. The coat was of that extremely thin black material which occasionally is affected by clerks and dentists and more often by librarians. If ever I looked upon an honest German jowl, or even upon a caricature thereof, I looked upon one now. Such a round fat red pleasant beer-drinking face as reminded me only and immediately of huge meerschaum pipes, Deutsche Verein mottos, sudsy seidels of Wurtzburger, and Jacob Wirth’s (once upon a time) brachwurst. Such pin-like pink merry eyes as made me think of Kris Kringle himself. Such extraordinarily huge reddish hands as might have grasped six seidels together in the Deutsche Küchen on 13th street. I gasped with pleasurable relief.

===Chinese===

我感到无以言表的迷惘。我走近那扇门。我甚至已经开始推它。
「等着，他妈妈的（*Attends, Nom de Dieu*）。」卫兵又推了我一把，对着门敲了两下，用无限恭敬的腔调喊：「账房先生（Monsieur le Gestionnaire）」——喊完用真正至上的轻蔑瞪了我一眼，他那张干净的猪脸几乎瞪成了圆形。
我自忖：这位账房，不管他是谁，必定是个非常可怕的人，一个凶神恶煞，一个全无慈悲之辈。
门里一个沉重、愚钝而和气的嗓音懒洋洋地说：
「进（*Entrez*）。」
卫兵把门推开，僵硬地立在门植上，给了我一个卫兵们肚子微饿时给鸡蛋的那种眼神。
我跨过门槛，浑身发抖——但愿是因愤怒。
我面前，桌后坐着一位非常肥胖的人物，头上扣着一顶黑色小便帽。那张脸上盘踞着一只巨大的鼻子，鼻上摇摇欲坠地栖着一副夹鼻眼镜；脸的其余部分阔大、多须、非常德国，共三层下巴。奇异的生物。他坐着，肚子被桌沿微微压出一道凹痕；桌面上摆着几本同末日审判时记录天使所用类似的大部头，一两个墨水台，数不清的钢笔铅笔，还有几份看起来绝对致命的文件。此君穿着体面而半显阴沉的衣裳，裁剪宽绰，足够让大肚子散步。那件上衣是极薄的黑呢料，偶尔受文书、牙医青睐，更受图书馆员青睐。若说我见过一张诚实的德国脸盘，甚至见过它的漫画，此刻我就见着了一张。那样一张又圆又肥又红又可亲的喝啤酒的脸，让我立刻且仅仅想起大海泡烟斗、德国同盟会格言、泡沫翻滚的维尔茨堡大杯，以及雅各布·沃思餐馆（想当年）的布朗香肠。那样一对针尖般粉红快活的小眼，让我想起圣诞老人他本人。那样一双大得出奇的红手，简直能一手在第十三街的德国后厨里同掃六只大啤酒杯。我如释重负地舒了一口气。

===Original===

Monsieur le Gestionnaire looked as if he was trying very hard, with the aid of his beribboned glasses and librarian’s jacket (not to mention a very ponderous gold watch-chain and locket that were supported by his copious equator) to appear possessed of the solemnity necessarily emanating from his lofty and responsible office. This solemnity, however, met its Waterloo in his frank and stupid eyes, not to say his trilogy of cheerful chins﻿—so much so that I felt like crying “*Wie gehts!*” and cracking him on his huge back. Such an animal! A contented animal, a bulbous animal; the only living hippopotamus in captivity, fresh from the Nile.
He contemplated me with a natural, under the circumstances, curiosity. He even naively contemplated me. As if I were hay. My hay-coloured head perhaps pleased him, as a hippopotamus. He would perhaps eat me. He grunted, exposing tobacco-yellow tusks, and his tiny eyes twittered. Finally he gradually uttered, with a thick accent, the following extremely impressive dictum:
“*C’est l’américain.*”
I felt much pleased, and said “*Oui, j’suis américain, Monsieur.*”
He rolled half over backwards in his creaking chair with wonderment at such an unexpected retort. He studied my face with a puzzled air, appearing slightly embarrassed that before him should stand *l’américain* and that *l’américain* should admit it, and that it should all be so wonderfully clear. I saw a second dictum, even more profound than the first, ascending from his black vest. The chain and fob trembled with anticipation. I was wholly fascinated. What vast blob of wisdom would find its difficult way out of him? The bulbous lips wiggled in a pleasant smile.
“*Voo parlez français.*”
This was delightful. The *planton* behind me was obviously angered by the congenial demeanour of Monsieur le Gestionnaire, and rasped with his boot upon the threshold. The maps to my right and left, maps of France, maps of the Mediterranean, of Europe, even, were abashed. A little anaemic and humble biped whom I had not previously noted, as he stood in one corner with a painfully deferential expression, looked all at once relieved. I guessed, and correctly guessed, that this little thing was the translator of La Ferté. His weak face wore glasses of the same type as the hippopotamus’, but without a huge black ribbon.

===Chinese===

账房先生的神情，仿佛正借助他那副系带眼镜和图书馆员外套（更不必说由他丰富的赤道支撑着的、一根极其厚重的金表链和坠盒），竭力显出他那崇高要职所必有的庄严。然而这庄严在他那坦率而愚钝的眼睛里——更遑论那欢快的下巴三部曲——迎来了它的滑铁卢；以致我很想喊一声「你好吗（*Wie gehts!*）」再在他宽阔的背上拍一记。这样一头牲口！一头心满意足的牲口，一头球茎状的牲口；世上唯一被囚的活河马，刚从尼罗河运来。
他以一种在此情形下理所当然的好奇打量我。他甚至天真地打量我。仿佛我是一捆干草。我这一头干草色的脑袋也许讨他喜欢，身为河马。他没准会把我吃了。他哼了一声，露出烟草黄的獠牙，小小的眼睛叽喳乱动。终于，他带着浓重的口音，缓缓发布出如下一条极深刻的名言：
「这就是那个美国人（*C'est l'américain*）。」
我大为受用，说：「对，我是美国人，先生（*Oui, j'suis américain, Monsieur*）。」
他在这意外的应答之下，连人带咯吱作响的椅子向后翻了一半，惊叹不已。他困惑地研究我的脸，似乎略感尴尬：他面前竟站着美国人本人，而美国人本人竟自认是美国人，而且这一切竟如此清楚得美妙。我看见第二条名言，比第一条更深刻，正从他的黑马甲里升上来。表链和坠盒因期待而颤抖。我彻底入了迷。何等巨大的一团智慧将从他体内艰难地破壳而出？那球茎状的嘴唇愉快地扭动着笑了。
「你会讲法语（*Voo parlez français*）。」
这太令人愉快了。我身后的卫兵显然被账房先生的亲切态度激怒了，在门槛上蹭响靴子。我左右两侧的地图——法兰西的地图、地中海的、乃至欧洲的——都臊了。一个我先前未曾留意的、贫血而谦卑的两足小生物，一直带着痛苦的恭顺立在角落里，此刻忽然如释重负。我猜——并且猜对了——这个小东西就是拉费尔泰的翻译。他虚弱的脸上架着一副同河马同款的眼镜，只是没有黑色大缎带。

===Original===

I decided to give him a tremor; and said to the hippo “*Un peu, Monsieur*,” at which the little thing looked sickly.
The hippopotamus benevolently remarked “*Voo parlez bien*,” and his glasses fell off. He turned to the watchful *planton*:
“*Voo poovez aller. Je vooz appelerai.*”
The watchful *planton* did a sort of salute and closed the door after him. The skullcapped dignitary turned to his papers and began mouthing them with his huge hands, grunting pleasantly. Finally he found one, and said lazily:
“*De quelle endroit que vooz êtes?*”
“*De Massachusetts*,” said I.
He wheeled round and stared dumbly at the weak faced one, who looked at a complete loss, but managed to stammer simperingly that it was a part of the United States.
“**Uh.**” The hippopotamus said.
Then he remarked that I had been arrested, and I agreed that I had been arrested.
Then he said “Have you got any money?” and before I could answer clambered heavily to his feet and, leaning over the table before which I stood, punched me gently.
“Uh,” said the hippopotamus, sat down, and put on his glasses.
“I have your money here,” he said. “You are allowed to draw a little from time to time. You may draw 20 francs, if you like. You may draw it twice a week.”
