The rear rank marched behind in a somewhat asymmetrical manner: a young, stupid-looking, clear-complexioned fellow (obviously a farmer, and having expensive black puttees and a handsome cap with a shiny black leather visor) slightly preceded a tall, gliding, thinnish, unjudgeable personage who peeped at everyone quietly and solemnly from beneath the visor of a somewhat large slovenly cloth cap showing portions of a lean, long, incognisable face upon which sat, or rather drooped, a pair of mustachios identical in character with those which are sometimes pictorially attributed to a Chinese dignitary﻿—in other words, the mustachios were exquisitely narrow, homogeneously downward, and made of something like black corn-silk. Behind *les nouveaux* staggered four paillasses motivated mysteriously by two pair of small legs belonging (as it proved) to Garibaldi and the little Machine-Fixer; who, coincident with the tumbling of the mattresses to the floor, perspiringly emerged to sight.
The first thing the shifty-eyed Hollander did was to exclaim *Gottverdummer*. The first thing the whiskery Belgian did was to grab his paillasse and stand guard over it. The first thing the youth in the leggings did was to stare helplessly about him, murmuring something whimperingly in Polish. The first thing the fourth nouveau did was pay no attention to anybody; lighting a cigarette in an unhurried manner as he did so, and puffing silently and slowly as if in all the universe nothing whatever save the taste of tobacco existed.
A bevy of Hollanders were by this time about the triangle, asking him all at once Was he from so-and-so, What was in his box. How long had he been in coming, etc. Half a dozen stooped over the box itself, and at least three pairs of hands were on the point of trying the lock﻿—when suddenly with incredible agility the unperturbed smoker shot a yard forward, landing quietly beside them; and exclaimed rapidly and briefly through his nose.
“*Mang.*”
He said it almost petulantly, or as a child says “Tag! You’re it.”

===Chinese===

凭海盗式络腮胡半掩的粗野五官，以及那双沉重、卑劣、游移的眼睛，我以同等的速度认出是比利时人。前列肩上扛着一只大箱子，黑黢黢，做工好，显然死沉，他们把它如释重负地哼的一声撂在便所旁边。后列以某种不对称的方式跟在后面：一个年轻、蠢相、面色清爽的家伙（显然是庄稼人，绑腿昂贵，帽子讲究，黑漆皮帽檐）稍稍超前于一个高挑、滑行、偏瘦、无从判断的人物；后者从一顶偏大而邋遢的布帽帽檐下，安静而庄严地窥视每个人，帽子下露出一张瘦长、无从辨认的脸的部分，脸上坐落着——不如说下垂着——两撇髭，其性格同画里有时归于中国达官贵人名下的那种一模一样——换句话说，这髭精美地窄，均质地垂，材质像黑色的玉米须。在新人们（*les nouveaux*）背后，四张草垫被两双小腿神秘地推进着，蹒跚而来——那两双小腿（后来证明）属于加里波第和小个子修机匠；草垫一倒地，他们俩便汗涔涔地冒了出来。
那位眼色游移的荷兰人干的第一件事是喊了一声他妈的（*Gottverdummer*）。那位大胡子的比利时人干的第一件事是抢过自己的草垫，站岗似的守住。那个打绑腿的青年干的第一件事是无助地环顾四周，用波兰语呜咽着什么。第四位新人干的第一件事是谁也不理；不慌不忙地点上一支烟，默默地缓缓喷吐，仿佛全宇宙除了烟味什么都不存在。
这当口一群荷兰人已经围住那三角形，七嘴八舌问他是不是某某地方来的，箱子里是什么，路上走了多久，等等。半打人俯到箱子上，至少有三双手正要试那把锁——那不动声色的吸烟人忽然以难以置信的敏捷朝前射出一码，轻轻巧巧落在他们旁边；从鼻子里迅速而简短地蹦出一个词：
「曼（*Mang*）。」
他说这词几乎是赌气式的，像小孩说「抓到啦，该你了」。

===Original===

The onlookers recoiled, completely surprised. Whereat the frightened youth in black puttees sidled over and explained with a pathetic, at once ingratiating and patronising, accent:
“He is not nasty. He’s a good fellow. He’s my friend. He wants to say that it’s his, that box. He doesn’t speak French.”
“It’s the *Gottverdummer* Polak’s box,” said the Triangular Man exploding in Dutch. “They’re a pair of Polakers; and this man” (with a twist of his pale-blue eyes in the direction of the Bewhiskered One) “and I had to carry it all the *Gottverdummer* way to this *Gottverdummer* place.”
All this time the incognizable nouveau was smoking slowly and calmly, and looking at nothing at all with his black buttonlike eyes. Upon his face no faintest suggestion of expression could be discovered by the hungry minds which focused unanimously upon its almost stern contours. The deep furrows in the cardboardlike cheeks (furrows which resembled slightly the gills of some extraordinary fish, some unbreathing fish) moved not an atom. The moustache drooped in something like mechanical tranquillity. The lips closed occasionally with a gesture at once abstracted and sensitive upon the lightly and carefully held cigarette; whose curling smoke accentuated the poise of the head, at once alert and uninterested.
Monsieur Auguste broke in, speaking, as I thought, Russian﻿—and in an instant he and the youth in puttees and the Unknowable’s cigarette and the box and the Unknowable had disappeared through the crowd in the direction of Monsieur Auguste’s paillasse, which was also the direction of the paillasse belonging to the Cordonnier as he was sometimes called﻿—a diminutive man with immense mustachios of his own who promenaded with Monsieur Auguste, speaking sometimes French but, as a general rule, Russian or Polish.
Which was my first glimpse, and is the reader’s, of the Zulu; he being one of the Delectable Mountains. For which reason I shall have more to say of him later, when I ascend the Delectable Mountains in a separate chapter or chapters; till when the reader must be content with the above, however unsatisfactory description.﻿ ﻿…
One of the most utterly repulsive personages whom I have met in my life﻿—perhaps (and on second thought I think certainly) the most utterly repulsive﻿—was shortly after this presented to our midst by the considerate French government.

===Chinese===

围观者齐齐后缩，彻底愣住。于是那吓坏了的黑绑腿青年側身过来，用一种可怜巴巴、既讨好又居高临下的口音解释：
「他不是坏。他是好人。他是我朋友。他想说那是他的，那口箱子。他不会讲法语。」
「是那个他妈的波兰佬的箱子，」三角先生用荷兰语炸开，「他们是一对波兰佬；这个人」（他那双淡蓝眼睛朝大胡子那位一拧）「和我，抬着它抬了一路他妈的路，到这他妈的地方。」
这当口那位无法辨认的新人缓缓静静抽着烟，黑钮扣似的眼睛什么也不看。那些一齐聚焦在他那张近乎严峻的轮廓上的饥饿头脑，在他脸上找不出一丝最微弱的表情迹象。纸板似脸颊上的深沟（那沟微微像某种非凡的鱼——某种不呼吸的鱼——的鳃）纹丝不动。髭以某种机械的宁静垂着。双唇偶尔合拢，带着既抽象又敏感的手势，轻而小心地掃住香烟；烟的盘旋勾出头颅的从容，既警觉又无兴趣。
奥古斯特先生插了进来，说的（我想）是俄语——转瞬之间，他同绑腿青年、同那不可知者的香烟和箱子、连同不可知者本人，一起消失在人丛中，朝奥古斯特先生的草垫方向去了；那也是鞋匠（Cordonnier，他有时被这么称呼）的草垫的方向——一个留着自家一部大髭的小个子，同奥古斯特先生一起放风，有时说法语，但照例说俄语或波兰语。
这是我对祖鲁人（the Zulu）的第一瞥，也是读者的第一瞥；他正是悦目山之一。为此我稍后还有更多关于他的话要说，等我用单独一章或几章攀登悦目山的时候；在那之前，读者只能满足于上述无论如何不令人满意的描述。……
我此生见过的最最彻头彻尾令人作呕的人物之一——也许（再想想，我认为肯定）就是最彻头彻尾的那个——不久之后就被体贴的法国政府呈送到了我们中间。

===Original===

I refer to The Fighting Sheeney. Whether or no he arrived after the Spanish Whoremaster I cannot say. I remember that Bill the Hollander﻿—which was the name of the triangular rope-belted man with shifty blue eyes (co-*arrivé* with the whiskey Belgian; which Belgian, by the way, from his not to be exaggerated brutal look, B. and myself called The Baby-snatcher)﻿—upon his arrival told great tales of a Spanish millionaire with whom he had been in prison just previous to his discovery of La Ferté. “He’ll be here too in a couple o’ days,” added Bill the Hollander, who had been fourteen years in These United States, spoke the language to a T, talked about “The America Lakes,” and was otherwise amazingly well acquainted with The Land of The Free. And sure enough, in less than a week one of the fattest men whom I have ever laid eyes on, overdressed, much beringed and otherwise wealthy-looking, arrived﻿—and was immediately played up to by Judas (who could smell cash almost as far as *le gouvernement français* could smell sedition) and, to my somewhat surprise, by the utterly respectable Count Bragard. But most emphatically **not** by Mexique, who spent a half-hour talking to the nouveau in his own tongue, then drifted placidly over to our beds and informed us:
“You see dat feller over dere, dat fat feller? I speak Spanish to him. He no good. Tell me he make fifty thousand franc last year runnin’ whorehouse in” (I think it was) “Brest. Son of bitch!”
“Dat fat feller” lived in a perfectly huge bed which he contrived to have brought up for him immediately upon his arrival. The bed arrived in a knockdown state and with it a mechanician from *la ville*, who set about putting it together, meanwhile indulging in many glances expressive not merely of interest but of amazement and even fear. I suppose the bed had to be of a special size in order to accommodate the circular millionaire and being an extraordinary bed required the services of a skilled artisan﻿—at all events, “dat fat feller’s” couch put the Skipper’s altogether in the shade. As I watched the process of construction it occurred to me that after all here was the last word in luxury﻿—to call forth from the metropolis not only a special divan but with it a special slave, the Slave of the Bed.﻿ ﻿… “Dat fat feller” had one of the prisoners perform his *corvée* for him.

===Chinese===

我指的是打架的犹太佬（The Fighting Sheeney）。他是否在西斑牙老鸨之后到，我说不准。我记得荷兰人比尔（Bill）——就是那个系麻绳的三角先生，眼色游移蓝眼睛（同威士忌比利时人同批到达；后者，顺便说一句，因那副夸张无余的凶相，被B.和我唤作抢婴犯〔The Baby-snatcher〕）——到达时大讲特讲一位西班牙百万富翁的故事，说他发现自己这座拉费尔泰之前正同那人蹲一处监。「过两天他也要来这儿。」荷兰人比尔补充道；比尔在合众国待过十四年，英语讲得地道，谈「美洲湖」，而且在自由之地见多识广，令人惊叹。果然，不出一星期，我此生见过最胖的人之一到来了——衣着过度，戒指成把，通身富贵相——而且立刻被犹大（他嗅钞票的本事几乎赶得上法国政府嗅叛国的本事）大献殷勤，而且——让我有点意外——还得到了彻头彻尾可敬的布拉加尔伯爵的捧场。但绝对**没有**被墨西哥捧场；后者用自己的母语同这位新人谈了半个钟头，然后平静地踱到我们床边，通知我们：
「你们瞧那边那个家伙，那个胖子？我跟他说西班牙语。他不咋地。跟我说他去年靠在」（我想是）「布雷斯特开窑子挣了五万法郎。狗娘养的！」
「那个胖子」住一张大得完美的床，那是他一到就想方设法立刻让人抬上来的。床以散件状态抵达，随之而来的是镇上（*la ville*）的一名机修工，着手组装，期间频频投去不仅是有兴趣、而且是惊讶甚至恐惧的目光。我猜那张床得特制尺寸才装得下这位滚圆的百万富翁，而非凡的床需要熟练工匠的服务——总之，「那个胖子」的卧榻把船长的床彻底比了下去。看着组装过程，我心想这毕竟才是奢侈的终极：不仅从都市召来一张特制睡榻，还随榻附赠一名专属奴隶，床之奴。……「那个胖子」让一个囚犯替他出劳役。

===Original===

“Dat fat feller” bought enough at the canteen twice every day to stock a transatlantic liner for seven voyages, and never ate with the prisoners. I will mention him again apropos the Mecca of respectability, the Great White Throne of purity, Three rings Three﻿—alias Count Bragard, to whom I have long since introduced my reader.
So we come, willy-nilly, to The Fighting Sheeney.
The Fighting Sheeney arrived carrying the expensive suitcase of a livid, strangely unpleasant-looking Romanian gent, who wore a knit sweater of a strangely ugly red hue, impeccable clothes, and an immaculate velour hat which must have been worth easily fifty francs. We called this gent Rockyfeller. His personality might be faintly indicated by the adjective Disagreeable. The porter was a creature whom Ugly does not even slightly describe. There are some specimens of humanity in whose presence one instantly and instinctively feels a profound revulsion, a revulsion which﻿—perhaps because it is profound﻿—cannot be analysed. The Fighting Sheeney was one of these specimens. His face (or to use the good American idiom, his mug) was exceedingly coarse-featured and had an indefatigable expression of sheer brutality﻿—yet the impression which it gave could not be traced to any particular plane or line. I can and will say, however, that this face was most hideous﻿—perhaps that is the word﻿—when it grinned. When The Fighting Sheeney grinned you felt that he desired to eat you, and was prevented from eating you only by a superior desire to eat everybody at once. He and Rockyfeller came to us from, I think it was, the Santé; both accompanied B. to Précigne. During the weeks which The Fighting Sheeney spent at La Ferté Macé, the nonexistence of the inhabitants of The Enormous Room was rendered something more than miserable. It was rendered well-nigh unbearable.
The night Rockyfeller and his slave arrived was a night to be remembered by everyone. It was one of the wildest and strangest and most perfectly interesting nights I, for one, ever spent. Rockyfeller had been corralled by Judas, and was enjoying a special bed to our right at the upper end of The Enormous Room.

===Chinese===

「那个胖子」每天两次在小卖部的采购量够 stocked 一艘横渡大西洋的邮轮跑七趟，而且从不与囚犯同桌。关于他我还要再提一次，届时将谈及体面之麦加、纯洁之大白座、三环记三——即布拉加尔伯爵，读者早已被我介绍给这位了。
于是我们便身不由己地来到了打架的犹太佬跟前。
打架的犹太佬是拎着一位青灰色、相貌奇丑的罗马尼亚绅士的昂贵手提箱到达的；那位绅士穿一件出奇丑陋的红色针织衫，衣服 impeccable，戴一顶一尘不染的绒帽，少说值五十法郎。我们管这位绅士叫洛克菲勒（Rockyfeller）。他的个性大可用「讨厌」二字略加暗示。而那位搬箱工，丑字连形容都形容不上。有些人形标本，人一置身其前便本能地、即刻地感到一种深刻的憎恶——这憎恶也许正因为深刻而无从分析。打架的犹太佬便是此类标本之一。他的脸（或用美国好话讲，他的脸蛋子〔mug〕）五官极粗，带着一种不知疲倦的纯然凶残的表情——然而这印象追不到任何具体的面或线。我能够也愿意说的是：这张脸咧嘴笑的时候最可怖——也许这正是那个词。打架的犹太佬朝你一笑，你觉得他想吃了你，而阻止他吃了你的，只是更想一口气吃掉所有人的那个更强的欲望。他和洛克菲勒（我想）是从桑泰监狱来的；两人后来都陪B.去了普雷西涅。打架的犹太佬在拉费尔泰马塞盘桓的那几个星期里，巨室住户们的不存在被弄成了比悲惨更多的东西。被弄成了几乎无法忍受。
洛克菲勒同他的奴隶到达的那一夜，是人人该记住的一夜。那是我本人此生度过的最狂野、最奇怪、也最最有趣的一夜之一。洛克菲勒已被犹大圈了栏，正在巨室上端我们右手一张特供床上享受。

===Original===

At the canteen he had purchased a large number of candles in addition to a great assortment of dainties which he and Judas were busily enjoying﻿—when the *planton* came up, counted us twice, divided by three, gave the order “*Lumières éteintes*,” and descended, locking the door behind him. Everyone composed himself for miserable sleep. Everyone except Judas, who went on talking to Rockyfeller, and Rockyfeller, who proceeded to light one of his candles and begin a pleasant and conversational evening. The Fighting Sheeney lay stark-naked on a paillasse between me and his lord. The Fighting Sheeney told everyone that to sleep stark-naked was to avoid bugs (whereof everybody, including myself, had a goodly portion). The Fighting Sheeney was, however, quieted by the *planton’s* order; whereas Rockyfeller continued to talk and munch to his heart’s content. This began to get on everybody’s nerves. Protests in a number of languages arose from all parts of The Enormous Room. Rockyfeller gave a contemptuous look around him and proceeded with his conversation. A curse emanated from the darkness. Up sprang The Fighting Sheeney, stark naked; strode over to the bed of the curser, and demanded ferociously:
“*Boxe? Vous!*”
The curser was apparently fast asleep, and even snoring. The Fighting Sheeney turned away disappointed, and had just reached his paillasse when he was greeted by a number of uproariously discourteous remarks uttered in all sorts of tongues. Over he rushed, threatened, received no response, and turned back to his place. Once more ten or twelve voices insulted him from the darkness. Once more The Fighting Sheeney made for them, only to find sleeping innocents. Again he tried to go to bed. Again the shouts arose, this time with redoubled violence and in greatly increased number. The Fighting Sheeney was at his wits’ end. He strode about challenging everyone to fight, receiving not the slightest recognition, cursing, reviling, threatening, bullying. The darkness always waited for him to resume his mattress, then burst out in all sorts of maledictions upon his head and the sacred head of his lord and master. The latter was told to put out his candle, go to sleep and give the rest a chance to enjoy what pleasure they might in forgetfulness of their woes. Whereupon he appealed to The Sheeney to stop this.

===Chinese===

他在小卖部买了大量蜡烛，外加一大堆精美点心，正同犹大忙着享用——这时卫兵上来，把我们点了两遍，除以三，下令「熄灯（*Lumières éteintes*）」，锁门下楼。人人安顿下来准备可怜的睡眠。除了犹大，他继续同洛克菲勒交谈；还有洛克菲勒，他点起一支蜡烛，开始一个愉快而健谈的夜晚。打架的犹太佬光着身子躺在我同他主子之间的一张草垫上。他向所有人宣布，光身睡可以防臭虫（这种虫人手一份，包括我）。不过，打架的犹太佬被卫兵的命令压住了；洛克菲勒却继续又说又嚼，随心所欲。这开始磨所有人的神经。巨室四面八方升起了多种语言的抗议。洛克菲勒轻蔑地环顾一圈，继续他的谈话。黑暗里飞出一句咒骂。打架的犹太佬一跃而起，光着身子；大步走到骂人者的床前，凶狠地要求：
「拳击？你！（*Boxe? Vous!*）」
骂人者显然睡熟了，还打着鼾。打架的犹太佬失望地转身，刚回到自己草垫，又被一片各种语言发出的喧哗无礼的问候迎接。他冲过去，威胁，无人应答，转身回位。十来条嗓音再次从黑暗里辱骂他。他再次扑向那些方向，只找到熟睡的无辜者。他又试图睡觉。喊声又起，这回变本加厉，人数大增。打架的犹太佬束手无策了。他大步走来走去，向所有人挑战，得不到丝毫理会，咒骂，辱骂，威胁，威吓。那黑暗总是等他一回到床垫上，就朝他和他主子神圣的脑袋爆发各种诅咒。有人奉劝后者熄掉蜡烛，睡觉去，给别人一个机会，在忘却各自不幸中享受可能的一点乐子。于是他请求犹太佬阻止这一切。

===Original===

The Sheeney (almost weeping) said he had done his best, that everyone was a pig, that nobody would fight, that it was disgusting. Roars of applause. Protests from the less strenuous members of our circle against the noise in general: Let him have his *foutue* candle, Shut up, Go to sleep yourself, etc. Rockyfeller kept on talking (albeit visibly annoyed by the ill-breeding of his fellow-captives) to the smooth and oily Judas. The noise, or rather noises, increased. I was for some reason angry at Rockyfeller﻿—I think I had a curious notion that if I couldn’t have a light after “*lumières éteintes*,” and if my very good friends were none of them allowed to have one, then, by God! neither should Rockyfeller. At any rate, I passed a few remarks calculated to wither the by this time a little nervous Übermensch; got up, put on some enormous sabots (which I had purchased from a horrid little boy whom the French Government had arrested with his parent, for some cause unknown﻿—which horrid little boy told me that he had “found” the sabots “in a train” on the way to La Ferté) shook myself into my fur coat, and banged as noisemakingly as I knew how over to One Eyed Dah-veed’s paillasse, where Mexique joined us. “It is useless to sleep,” said One Eyed Dah-veed in French and Spanish. “True,” I agreed; “therefore, let’s make all the noise we can.”
Steadily the racket bulged in the darkness. Human cries, quips and profanity had now given place to wholly inspired imitations of various, not to say sundry, animals. Afrique exclaimed﻿—with great pleasure I recognised his voice through the impenetrable gloom:
“*Agahagahagahagahagah!*”
—“perhaps,” said I, “he means a machine gun; it sounds like either that or a monkey.” The Wanderer crowed beautifully. Monsieur Auguste’s bosom friend, *le Cordonnier*, uttered an astonishing:
“*Meeee-oooooo**ow**!*”
which provoked a tornado of laughter and some applause. Mooings, chirpings, cacklings﻿—there was a superb hen﻿—neighings, he-hawing, roarings, bleatings, growlings, quackings, peepings, screamings, bellowings, and﻿—something else, of course﻿—set The Enormous Room suddenly and entirely alive. Never have I imagined such a menagerie as had magically instated itself within the erstwhile soggy and dismal four walls of our chamber. Even such staid characters as Count Bragard set up a little bawling.

===Chinese===

犹太佬（几乎哭了）说他尽了力了，人人都是猪，没人肯打，恶心透了。哄堂喝彩。我们圈子里不那么血性的成员们提出对噪音的总抗议：让他点他的蜡烛（*foutue*）吧，闭嘴，你自己睡去，等等。洛克菲勒继续（尽管对难友们的失教明显恼火）同油光光的犹大交谈。噪音，或者说诸噪音，在上涨。我不知为什么对洛克菲勒恼火——我想我心里有个奇怪的念头：「熄灯」之后我不能点灯，我的好友们谁也不许点，那么老天作证！洛克菲勒也不许。总之，我说了几句意在蔫死这位这时已有点紧张的超级人的话；起身，蹬上一双大木鞋（从一个可怕的小男孩那里买的——法国政府不知为何把他连同家长一起抓了来——那可怕的小男孩告诉我，木鞋是他来拉费尔泰路上「在一列火车里」捡的），抖开身子钻进毛皮大衣，以我所会的最大响动，咣当咣当地踱到独眼大卫-维德的草垫，墨西哥也加入了我们。「睡觉是没用的。」独眼大卫-维德用法语和西班牙语说。「同意，」我说，「所以，咱们能弄多大动静弄多大动静。」
黑暗里那喧器稳步上涨。人类的叫喊、俏皮话和脏话，如今已让位于各种——不止是各种——动物的全然灵感迸发的模仿。阿弗里克喊了一嗓子——隔着不透光的幽暗，我愉快地听出了他的声音：
「阿嘎哈嘎哈嘎哈嘎哈嘎！（*Agahagahagahagahagah!*）」
——「也许，」我说，「他指的是机枪；听着像机枪，要不就是猴子。」漫游者漂亮地打了鸣。奥古斯特先生的知交，鞋匠，发出了一声惊人的：
「喵——————嗷！（*Meeee-ooooooow!*）」
引来一阵龙卷风般的笑和几阵掌声。哞哞，啾啾，咯咯——有一只顶好的母鸡——嘶鸣，驴叫，吼叫，咩咩，低吼，嘎嘎，唧唧，尖叫，哞叫，还有——当然——别的什么，让巨室突然间、整个地活了过来。我从未想象过这样一座动物园，它魔法般进驻了我们这间往日湿软阴惨的四壁之内。连布拉加尔伯爵这般稳重的人物也放声嚎了一嗓子。

===Original===

Monsieur Pet-airs uttered a tiny aged crowing to my immense astonishment and delight. The dying, the sick, the ancient, the mutilated, made their contributions to the common pandemonium. And then, from the lower left darkness, sprouted one of the very finest noises which ever fell on human ears﻿—the noise of a little dog with floppy ears who was tearing after something on very short legs and carrying his very fuzzy tail straight up in the air as he tore; a little dog who was busier than he was wise, louder than he was big; a red-tongued foolish breathless intent little dog with black eyes and a great smile and woolly paws﻿—which noise, conceived and executed by The Lobster, sent The Enormous Room into an absolute and incurable hysteria.
The Fighting Sheeney was at a standstill. He knew not how to turn. At last he decided to join with the insurgents, and wailed brutally and dismally. That was the last straw: Rockyfeller, who could no longer (even by shouting to Judas) make himself heard, gave up conversation and gazed angrily about him; angrily yet fearfully, as if he expected some of these numerous bears, lions, tigers and baboons to leap upon him from the darkness. His livid super-disagreeable face trembled with the flickering cadence of the candle. His lean lips clenched with mortification and wrath. “*Vous êtes chef de chambre*,” he said fiercely to Judas﻿—“why don’t you make the men stop this? *C’est emmerdant.*” “Ah,” replied Judas smoothly and insinuatingly﻿—“They are only men, and boors at that; you can’t expect them to have any manners.” A tremendous group of Something Elses greeted this remark together with cries, insults, groans and linguistic trumpetings. I got up and walked the length of the room to the cabinet (situated as always by this time of night in a pool which was in certain places six inches deep, from which pool my sabots somewhat protected me) and returned, making as loud a clattering as I was able. Suddenly the voice of Monsieur Auguste leaped through the din in an
“*Alors! c’est as-sez.*”
The next thing we knew he had reached the window just below the cabinet (the only window, by the way, not nailed up with good long wire nails for the sake of warmth) and was shouting in a wild, high, gentle, angry voice to the sentinel below:
“*Plan-ton!* It is im-pos-si-ble to sleep!”
A great cry: “Yes! I am coming!”

===Chinese===

佩泰尔先生发出了一声细小的、上了年纪的啼鸣，令我大为吃惊又大为欢喜。垂死的、害病的、年迈的、伤残的，都为这场共同的魔鬼大合唱出了力。接着，从左下方的黑暗里，冒出了人类耳朵听过的最美妙的噪音之一——一只耷拉耳朵的小狗，正迈着极短的腿追逐着什么，跑的时候那根毛茸茸的尾巴笔直朝天；一只比聪明还忙、比个头还响的小狗；一只红舌头、傻乎乎、气喘吁吁、全神贯注的小狗，黑眼睛，大笑脸，毛爪子——这噪音由龙虾构思并执行，把巨室送入了绝对的、无可救药的歇斯底里。
打架的犹太佬僵住了。他不知往哪儿转。最后他决定加入起义者，凄惨而粗野地嗥了一声。这是最后一根稻草：洛克菲勒再也（哪怕朝犹大喊）无法让人听见自己，放弃了谈话，愤怒地环顾四周；愤怒而恐惧，仿佛期待这些数不清的熊、狮、虎、狒狒中的哪只会从黑暗里朝他扑来。他那青灰、超级讨厌的脸随蜡烛的摇曳节奏发抖。瘦嘴唇因羞愤和狂怒而紧抿。「你是侍寝总管（*Vous êtes chef de chambre*），」他凶狠地对犹大说——「为什么不让这些人停下来？烦死了（*C'est emmerdant*）。」「啊，」犹大油滑而缠绵地答——「他们不过是些男人，而且是粗人；您总不能指望他们有什么教养。」一大群「别的什么」连同叫喊、辱骂、呻吟和各种语言的喇叭声齐声迎接这句话。我起身，穿过整间屋子去便所（深夜的这个时辰，它照例坐落在某些地方深达六英寸的水洼里，我的木鞋多少保护了我），再走回来，一路弄出我所能的最大咣当。忽然，奥古斯特先生的声音跃过喧器，喊了一声
「好了！够——了。（*Alors! c'est as-sez.*）」
下一刻他已冲到便所旁边那扇窗（顺便说，唯一一扇没为了保暖用长长的粗铁钉钉死的窗）前，用一种狂野、高亢、温柔而愤怒的嗓音朝楼下的哨兵喊：
「卫——兵！（*Plan-ton!*）没法睡——觉了！」
一声大喊：「好！我这就来！」

===Original===

floated up﻿—every single noise dropped﻿—Rockyfeller shot out his hand for the candle, seized it in terror, blew it out as if blowing it out were the last thing he would do in this life﻿—and The Enormous Room hung silent; enormously dark, enormously expectant.﻿ ﻿…
**Bang!** Open the door. “*Alors, qui, m’appelle? Qu’est-ce qu’on a foutu ici.*” And the Black Holster, revolver in hand, flashed his torch into the inky stillness of the chamber. Behind him stood two *plantons* white with fear; their trembling hands clutching revolvers, the barrels of which shook ludicrously.
“*C’est moi, plan-ton!*” Monsieur Auguste explained that no one could sleep because of the noise, and that the noise was because “*ce monsieur là*” would not extinguish his candle when everyone wanted to sleep. The Black Holster turned to the room at large and roared: “You children of *Merde* don’t let this happen again or I’ll fix you every one of you.”﻿—Then he asked if anyone wanted to dispute this assertion (he brandishing his revolver the while) and was answered by peaceful snorings. Then he said by X Y and Z he’d fix the noisemakers in the morning and fix them good﻿—and looked for approbation to his trembling assistants. Then he swore twenty or thirty times for luck, turned, and thundered out on the heels of his fleeing confrères who almost tripped over each other in their haste to escape from The Enormous Room. Never have I seen a greater exhibition of bravery than was afforded by The Black Holster, revolver in hand, holding at bay the snoring and weaponless inhabitants of The Enormous Room. *Vive les plantons.* He should have been a gendarme.
Of course Rockyfeller, having copiously tipped the officials of La Ferté upon his arrival, received no slightest censure nor any hint of punishment for his deliberate breaking of an established rule﻿—a rule for the breaking of which anyone of the common scum (e.g., thank God, myself) would have got *cabinot de suite*. No indeed. Several of *les hommes*, however, got *pain sec*﻿—not because they had been caught in an act of vociferous protestation by the Black Holster, which they had not﻿—but just on principle, as a warning to the rest of us and to teach us a wholesome respect for (one must assume) law and order. One and all, they heartily agreed that it was worth it. Everyone knew, of course, that the Spy had peached.

===Chinese===

飘了上来——每一处噪音应声而落——洛克菲勒伸手抓向蜡烛，惊恐地攥住，吹熄它，仿佛吹熄它是此生最后一桩壮举——巨室悬在寂静里；巨大的黑暗，巨大的期待。……
**砰！**门开。「那么，谁，在叫我？这儿他妈搞的什么鬼。（*Alors, qui, m'appelle? Qu'est-ce qu'on a foutu ici.*）」黑枪套手里握着左轮，把电筒闪进屋里墨染的寂静。他身后立着两名吓得发白的卫兵；颤抖的手攥着左轮，枪管可笑地摇晃。
「是我，卫——兵！（*C'est moi, plan-ton!*）」奥古斯特先生解释说，吵得谁也睡不着，吵是因为「那位先生（*ce monsieur là*）」在人人想睡的时候不肯熄蜡烛。黑枪套转向全屋咆哮：「你们这帮屎（Merde）孩子，再敢来这么一出，我一个一个收拾你们。」——然后他问有没有谁想对此断言表示异议（一边挥舞左轮），回答他的是一片安详的鼾声。然后他说，凭X Y和Z，他明早要收拾闹事的，好好收拾——并向他颤抖的助手们寻求嘉许。然后为了讨彩头他咒骂了二三十遍，转身，雷鸣般地冲出去，追着他逃跑的同僚们的脚后跟；那两位争先恐后逃出巨室，差点互相绊倒。我从未见过比这更伟大的英勇表演：黑枪套，手持左轮，威慑住巨室里鼾睡而无武的住户们。卫兵万岁（*Vive les plantons*）。他本该去当宪兵的。
当然，洛克菲勒到达时对拉费尔泰的官员们打点颇丰，因此他蓄意破坏既定规矩的行为没受到丝毫训斥，也没有半点惩罚的暗示——这条规矩，倘是任何普通渣滓（比如，感谢上帝，我本人）违反了，立刻就是小号伺候（*cabinot de suite*）。才不呢。倒是男人们中有几个领了干面包——不是因为他们被黑枪套逮住了高声抗议的现行（他们没有）——而只是原则上讲，以做其余诸君的殷鉴，教我们对（只能这么设想了）法律与秩序怀有健全的敬意。他们个个都衷心同意：值。当然，人人都知道，是间谍（the Spy）告的密。

===Original===

For, by Jove, even in The Enormous Room there was a man who earned certain privileges and acquired a complete immunity from punishment by squealing on his fellow-sufferers at each and every opportunity. A really ugly person, with a hard knuckling face and treacherous hands, whose daughter lived downstairs in a separate room apart from *les putains* (against which “dirty,” “filthy,” “whores” he could not say enough﻿—“Hi’d rather die than ’ave my daughter with them stinkin’ ’ores,” remarked once to me this strictly moral man, in Cockney English) and whose daughter (aged thirteen) was generally supposed to serve in a pleasurable capacity. One did not need to be warned against the Spy (as both B. and I were warned, upon our arrival)﻿—a single look at that phiz was enough for anyone partially either intelligent or sensitive. This phiz or mug had, then, squealed. Which everyone took as a matter of course and admitted among themselves that hanging was too good for him.
But the vast and unutterable success achieved by the *Menagerie* was this﻿—Rockyfeller, shortly after, left our ill-bred society for “*l’hôpital*”; the very same “hospital” whose comforts and seclusion Monsieur le Surveillant had so dextrously recommended to B. and myself. Rockyfeller kept The Fighting Sheeney in his way, in order to defend him when he went on promenade; otherwise our connection with him was definitely severed, his new companions being Muskowitz the Cockeyed Millionaire, and The Belgian Song Writer﻿—who told everyone to whom he spoke that he was a government official (“*de la blague*” cried the little Machine-Fixer, “*c’est un menteur!*” Adding that he knew of this person in Belgium and that this person was a man who wrote popular ditties). Would to Heaven we had got rid of the slave as well as the master﻿—but unfortunately The Fighting Sheeney couldn’t afford to follow his lord’s example. So he went on making a nuisance of himself, trying hard to curry favour with B. and me, getting into fights and bullying everyone generally.
Also this lionhearted personage spent one whole night shrieking and moaning on his paillasse after an injection by Monsieur Richard﻿—for syphilis. Two or three men were, in the course of a few days, discovered to have had syphilis for some time. They had it in their mouths. I don’t remember them particularly, except that at least one was a Belgian.

===Chinese===

因为老天作证，即使在巨室里也有这么一位，靠一有机会就告发难友，挣得某些特权，并获得对惩罚的完全豁免。一个真正丑陋的人，一张硬疙瘩脸，一双阴险的手；他女儿住在楼下一个单间里，同妓女们（*les putains*）隔开（对那些「脏」「烂」「野鸡」他骂不绝口——「俺宁死也不让俺闺女跟那帮臭婊子住一块儿，」这位严格道德的先生有一回用伦敦腔英语对我说）；而他女儿（十三岁）公认在从事一项愉快的行当。不需要谁提醒防着间谍（B.和我一到就有人提醒了）——朝那张脸看一眼，对任何稍有智力或敏感的人就够了。这张脸、这副尊容，告了密。大家理所当然地照单全收，并彼此承认：绞死他都嫌便宜了他。
但那座动物园取得的浩大而不可言喻的成功在于——不久之后，洛克菲勒就离开了我们这群没教养的社会，进了「医院（*l'hôpital*）」；正是监守先生那么灵巧地推荐给B.和我的那座「医院」。洛克菲勒把打架的犹太佬留在原来的位置上，为的是他出去放风时好保护他；除此之外我们同他的联系算是断了，他的新伙伴成了斜眼百万富翁穆斯科维茨和比利时歌曲作家——后者逢人便自称政府官员（「扯淡（*de la blague*），」小个子修机匠喊，「他是个骗子！（*c'est un menteur!*）」并补充说他在比利时就认识此人，此人是个写小曲的）。但愿上天连主子带奴隶一起替我们除了——不幸的是，打架的犹太佬拿不出钱来效法他的主子。于是他继续讨人嫌，死命巴结B.和我，打架，欺负所有人。
此外，这位狮心人物在理查德先生给他打了一针之后——治梅毒的——在他的草垫上嚎叫呻吟了一整夜。几天之内又发现两三个人早已得了梅毒。长在嘴里。我记不大清他们了，只记得至少一个是比利时人。

===Original===

Of course they and The Fighting Sheeney had been using the common dipper and drink pail. *Le gouvernement français* couldn’t be expected to look out for a little thing like venereal disease among prisoners: didn’t it have enough to do curing those soldiers who spent their time on permission trying their best to infect themselves with both gonorrhea and syphilis? Let not the reader suppose I am daydreaming: let him rather recall that I had had the honour of being a member of *Section Sanitaire Vingt-et-Un*, which helped evacuate the venereal hospital at Ham, with whose inhabitants (in odd moments) I talked and walked and learned several things about *la guerre*. Let the reader﻿—if he does not realise it already﻿—realise that This Great War for Humanity, etc., did not agree with some people’s ideas, and that some people’s ideas made them prefer to the glories of the front line the torments (I have heard my friends at Ham screaming a score of times) attendant upon venereal diseases. Or as one of my aforesaid friends told me﻿—after discovering that I was, in contrast to *les américains*, not bent upon making France discover America but rather upon discovering France and *les français* myself:
“*Mon vieux*, it’s quite simple. I go on leave. I ask to go to Paris, because there are prostitutes there who are totally diseased. I catch syphilis, and, when possible gonorrhea also. I come back. I leave for the front line. I am sick. The hospital. The doctor tells me: you must not smoke or drink, then you will be cured quickly. ‘Thanks, doctor!’ I drink all the time and I smoke all the time and I do not get well. I stay five, six, seven weeks. Perhaps a few months. At last, I am well. I rejoin my regiment. And now it is my turn to go on leave. I go. Again the same thing. It’s very pretty, you know.”
But about the syphilitics at La Ferté: they were, somewhat tardily to be sure, segregated in a very small and dirty room﻿—for a matter of, perhaps, two weeks. And the Surveillant actually saw to it that during this period they ate *la soupe* out of individual china bowls.
I scarcely know whether The Fighting Sheeney made more of a nuisance of himself during his decumbiture or during the period which followed it﻿—which period houses an astonishing number of fights, rows, bullyings, etc.

===Chinese===

当然，他们同打架的犹太佬一直共用同一只公用水瓢和水桶。法国政府（*le gouvernement français*）哪能指望它操心囚犯间性病这样的小事：它光治那些休假期间拼命让自己同时染上淋病和梅毒的士兵，不就够忙了吗？请读者别以为我在白日做梦：请他回想一下，我曾有幸身为「第二十一卫生分队」的一员，协助撤离过阿姆的性病医院，同那里的住院者（在闲暇时刻）谈过天散过步，学到过关于战争（*la guerre*）的几件事。请读者——如果他还没有意识到——意识到：这场为了人类等等的伟大战争，同某些人的想法不合；某些人的想法使他们宁舍前线的光荣，而取性病的折磨（我听过我在阿姆的朋友们嚎叫不下二十回）。或者，正如我上述朋友之一对我说的——在他发现我不像那些美国人（*les américains*）那样一心想让法国发现美洲、而是想亲自发现法国和法国人之后：
「老伙计（*Mon vieux*），很简单。我去休假。我申请去巴黎，因为那儿有病全了的妓女。我染上梅毒，有机会的话连淋病一起。我回来。我开赴前线。我病了。医院。医生跟我说：不许抽烟不许喝酒，好得就快。『多谢，医生！』我照抽照喝，就是不好。我住五六个七个星期。也许几个月。终于，我好了。我归队。这回又轮到我休假。我又去。老一套。挺美的，您知道的。」
至于拉费尔泰的梅毒们：他们确实——尽管迟了些——被隔离进了一间极小极脏的屋子——为期大约两星期。监守居然还关照：此期间他们的汤（*la soupe*）用各自单独的瓷碗喝。
我几乎说不准，打架的犹太佬是在他卧病期间更讨嫌，还是在其后的时期——那一时期装下了数量惊人的打架、争吵、霸凌，等等。

===Original===

He must have had a light case for he was cured in no time, and on everyone’s back as usual. Well, I will leave him for the nonce; in fact, I will leave him until I come to The Young Pole, who wore black puttees and spoke of The Zulu as “*mon ami*”﻿—the Young Pole whose troubles I will recount in connection with the second Delectable Mountain Itself. I will leave the Sheeney with the observation that he was almost as vain as he was vicious; for with what ostentation, one day when we were in the kitchen, did he show me a postcard received that afternoon from Paris, whereon I read “*Comme vous êtes beau*” and promises to send more money as fast as she earned it and, hoping that he had enjoyed her last present, the signature (in a big, adoring hand)
“*Ta môme. Alice.*”
and when I had read it﻿—sticking his map up into my face, The Fighting Sheeney said with emphasis:
“*No travailler moi. Femme travaille, fait la noce, tout le temps. Toujours avec officiers anglais. Gagne beaucoup, cent francs, deux cent francs, trois cent francs, toutes les nuits. Anglais riches. Femme me donne tout. Moi no travailler. Bon, eh?*”
Grateful for this little piece of information, and with his leer an inch from my chin, I answered slowly and calmly that it certainly was. I might add that he spoke Spanish by preference (according to Mexique very bad Spanish); for The Fighting Sheeney had made his home for a number of years in Rio, and his opinion thereof may be loosely translated by the expressive phrase, “it’s a swell town.”
A charming fellow, The Fighting Sheeney.
Now I must tell you what happened to the poor Spanish Whoremaster. I have already noted the fact that Count Bragard conceived an immediate fondness for this rolypoly individual, whose belly﻿—as he lay upon his back of a morning in bed﻿—rose up with the sheets, blankets and quilts as much as two feet above the level of his small, stupid head studded with chins. I have said that this admiration on the part of the admirable Count and R.A. for a personage of the Spanish Whoremaster’s profession somewhat interested me. The fact is, a change had recently come in our own relations with Vanderbilt’s friend. His cordiality toward B. and myself had considerably withered. From the time of our arrivals the good nobleman had showered us with favours and advice. To me, I may say, he was even extraordinarily kind.

===Chinese===

他的病想必不重，因为没多久就治好了，照旧骑到所有人头上。好，他且搁下；实际上，我要一直搁到谈年轻的波兰人（The Young Pole）的时候——就是那个打黑绑腿、管祖鲁人叫「我朋友（*mon ami*）」的年轻人——他的种种遭遇，我将连同第二座悦目山本尊一并叙述。关于犹太佬我只再补充一句评语：他的虚荣几乎赶得上他的恶毒；因为有一天我们在厨房，他多么炫耀地给我看一张当天下午从巴黎收到的明信片，上面我读到「你可真俊（*Comme vous êtes beau*）」，还有她一挣到钱就多寄的许诺，以及希望他喜欢她上次的礼物，落款（一手大大的、崇拜的字）：
「你的妞。爱丽丝。（*Ta môme. Alice.*）」
我读完——他把那张脸凑到我脸前一英寸，打架的犹太佬着重地说：
「不干活，我。女人干活，吃喝玩乐，全天候。老跟英国军官。挣得多，一百法郎，两百法郎，三百法郎，夜夜。英国人阔。女人全给我。我不干活。棒，嗯？（*No travailler moi. Femme travaille, fait la noce, tout le temps. Toujours avec officiers anglais. Gagne beaucoup, cent francs, deux cent francs, trois cent francs, toutes les nuits. Anglais riches. Femme me donne tout. Moi no travailler. Bon, eh?*）」
承蒙这片小情报，他那副媚眼离我下巴一英寸，我缓慢而平静地回答：确实棒。也许还该补充：他偏好讲西班牙语（据墨西哥说，讲得很糟）；因为打架的犹太佬在里约安过多年家，他对里约的看法大致可译为那句富有表现力的话：「那是个排场的地方。」
一个迷人的家伙，打架的犹太佬。
现在我必须告诉你们那位可怜的西班牙老鸨的下场。我已经指出过：布拉加尔伯爵对这位滚圆人物一见倾心——此人早晨仰躺在床上，肚子连床单毯子被子一起，在他那颗镶满下巴的、小而愚蠢的脑袋之上隆起足有两英尺。我说过，这位可敬的伯爵兼皇家美协会员对西班牙老鸨这一职业人士的倾慕使我颇有兴致。事实是，我们同范德比尔特的朋友的关系近来起了变化。他对B.和我的热乎劲儿相当程度地枯萎了。自我们到达起，这位好贵族对我们恩惠与忠告齐下。对我，我可以说，他甚至是格外地好。

===Original===

We talked painting, for example: Count Bragard folded a piece of paper, tore it in the centre of the folded edge, unfolded it carefully, exhibiting a good round hole, and remarking: “Do you know this trick? It’s an English trick, Mr. Cummings,” held the paper before him and gazed profoundly through the circular aperture at an exceptionally disappointing section of the altogether gloomy landscape, visible thanks to one of the ecclesiastical windows of The Enormous Room. “Just look at that, Mr. Cummings,” he said with quiet dignity. I looked. I tried my best to find something to the left. “No, no, straight through,” Count Bragard corrected me. “There’s a lovely bit of landscape,” he said sadly. “If I only had my paints here. I thought, you know, of asking my housekeeper to send them on from Paris﻿—but how can you paint in a bloody place like this with all these bloody pigs around you? It’s ridiculous to think of it. And it’s tragic, too,” he added grimly, with something like tears in his grey, tired eyes.
Or we were promenading The Enormous Room after supper﻿—the evening promenade in the *cour* having been officially eliminated owing to the darkness and the cold of the autumn twilight﻿—and through the windows the dull bloating colours of sunset pouring faintly; and the Count stops dead in his tracks and regards the sunset without speaking for a number of seconds. Then﻿—“it’s glorious, isn’t it?” he asks quietly. I say “Glorious indeed.” He resumes his walk with a sigh, and I accompany him. “*Ce n’est pas difficile à peindre, un coucher du soleil*, it’s not hard,” he remarks gently. “No?” I say with deference. “Not hard a bit,” the Count says, beginning to use his hands. “You only need three colours, you know. Very simple.” “Which colours are they?” I inquire ignorantly. “Why, you know of course,” he says surprised. “Burnt sienna, cadmium yellow, and﻿—er﻿—there! I can’t think of it. I know it as well as I know my own face. So do you. Well, that’s stupid of me.”
Or, his worn eyes dwelling benignantly upon my duffle-bag, he warns me (in a low voice) of Prussian Blue.
“Did you notice the portrait hanging in the *bureau* of the Surveillant?” Count Bragard inquired one day. “That’s a pretty piece of work, Mr. Cummings. Notice it when you get a chance. The green moustache, particularly fine. School of Cézanne.”﻿—“Really?” I said in surprise.

===Chinese===

比如我们谈绘画：布拉加尔伯爵折起一张纸，在折边中央撕开，小心展开，亮出一个浑圆的好洞，说道：「您知道这个戏法吗？这是个英国戏法，卡明斯先生。」然后把纸举在面前，透过那个圆孔，深深凝视窗外那片特别令人失望的、全然阴郁的风景——多亏巨室的一扇教堂式窗子才看得见。「您瞧那一处，卡明斯先生。」他安静而尊严地说。我瞧了。我尽力往左边找点什么。「不，不，直着看过去。」布拉加尔伯爵纠正我。「那儿有一小块可爱的风景，」他悲哀地说，「要是我的颜料在这儿就好了。您知道，我本想叫我的管家从巴黎寄来的——可这么个血腥的地方，四周全是血腥的猪猡，怎么画画？想想都荒唐。而且也是悲剧。」他阴郁地补充，灰色的倦眼里几乎有了泪。
又或者，晚饭后我们在巨室里踱步——傍晚的院子放风已因秋暮的暗与冷被官方取消——窗外斜斜地洒进落日那阴沉虚胀的颜色；伯爵突然驻足，一言不发地端详落日好几秒。然后——「壮丽，不是吗？」他轻声问。我说「确实壮丽」。他叹口气继续踱步，我陪着他。「画一幅日落不难的（*Ce n'est pas difficile à peindre, un coucher du soleil*），」他温和地说。「是吗？」我恭恭敬敬地说。「一点也不难，」伯爵说着，开始用手比划，「您知道，只要三种颜色。非常简单。」「哪三种呢？」我无知地问。「咦，您当然知道，」他惊讶地说，「熟赭、镉黄，还有——呃——瞧！我想不起来了。我对它熟得跟自己的脸一样。您也一样。唉，我真是笨。」
又或者，他仁慈地盯住我的行李袋，低声警告我提防普鲁士蓝。
「您注意到监守办公室里挂的那幅肖像了吗？」有一天布拉加尔伯爵问。「那是件漂亮的作品，卡明斯先生。有机会留意一下。绿胡子，尤其精彩。塞尚一派。」——「真的？」我惊讶地说。

===Original===

﻿—“Yes, indeed,” Count Bragard said, extracting his tired-looking hands from his tired-looking trousers with a cultured gesture. “Fine young fellow painted that. I knew him. Disciple of the master. Very creditable piece of work.”﻿—“Did you ever see Cézanne?” I ventured.﻿—“Bless you, yes, scores of times,” he answered almost pityingly.﻿—“What did he look like?” I asked, with great curiosity.﻿—“Look like? His appearance, you mean?” Count Bragard seemed at a loss. “Why he was not extraordinary looking. I don’t know how you could describe him. Very difficult in English. But you know a phrase we have in French, ‘*l’air pesant*’; I don’t think there’s anything in English for it; *il avait l’air pesant*, Cézanne, if you know what I mean.
“I should work, I should not waste my time,” the Count would say almost weepingly. “But it’s no use, my things aren’t here. And I’m getting old too; couldn’t concentrate in this stinking hole of a place, you know.”
I did some hasty drawings of Monsieur Pet-airs washing and rubbing his bald head with a great towel in the dawn. The R.A. caught me in the act and came over shortly after, saying, “Let me see them.” In some perturbation (the subject being a particular friend of his) I showed one drawing. “Very good, in fact, excellent,” the R.A. smiled whimsically. “You have a real talent for caricature, Mr. Cummings, and you should exercise it. You really got Peters. Poor Peters, he’s a fine fellow, you know; but this business of living in the muck and filth, *c’est malheureux*. Besides, Peters is an old man. It’s a dirty bloody shame, that’s what it is. A bloody shame that all of us here should be forced to live like pigs with this scum!
“I tell you what, Mr. Cummings,” he said, with something like fierceness, his weary eyes flashing, “I’m getting out of here shortly, and when I do get out (I’m just waiting for my papers to be sent on by the French consul) I’ll not forget my friends. We’ve lived together and suffered together and I’m not a man to forget it. This hideous mistake is nearly cleared up, and when I go free I’ll do anything for you and your chum. Anything I can do for you I’d be only too glad to do it. If you want me to buy you paints when I’m in Paris, nothing would give me more pleasure.

===Chinese===

——「千真万确，」布拉加尔伯爵说着，以一个有文化的手势从他那倦怠的裤子里抽出他那倦怠的双手。「画那画的是个好青年。我认识他。大师的门徒。很拿得出手的作品。」——「您见过塞尚吗？」我斗胆问。——「老天保佑，见过，几十回呢。」他几乎是怜悯地答。——「他长得什么样？」我极好奇地问。——「什么样？您是说他的相貌？」布拉加尔伯爵似乎为难了。「嗯，他相貌并不出众。我不知道该怎么形容您听。英文很难。不过您知道我们法语里有句话，『l'air pesant』（沉重的神气）；我想英文里没有对应词；il avait l'air pesant，塞尚就有那么一股沉重的神气，如果您懂我的意思。」
「我该工作，不该浪费光阴，」伯爵几乎要哭地说，「可是没用，我的东西不在这儿。而且我也老了；这么个臭气熏天的鬼地方，没法集中心思，您知道的。」
我匆匆画了几张佩泰尔先生在黎明中用一条大毛巾擦洗、揉搓秃头的速写。皇家会员逮住了我，不久踱过来，说：「让我瞧瞧。」我有些不安（画的主题是他的至交），给他看了一张。「很好，事实上，出色，」皇家会员古怪地笑了。「您有漫画的真天分，卡明斯先生，应该勤加练习。您真把彼得斯抓住了。可怜的彼得斯，他是个好人，您知道；可是住在这样的污秽里，真不幸（c'est malheureux）。何况彼得斯上了年纪。这是肮脏的血腥耻辱，就是这样。我们大家全被迫同这些人渣像猪一样过活，是血腥的耻辱！
「我告诉您吧，卡明斯先生，」他说，带着近乎凶狠的口气，倦眼放光，「我不久就要离开这儿了，等我出去（我只等法国领事把我的证件转来），我不会忘记我的朋友们。我们一起生活过、一起受过苦，我不是那种忘事的人。这个可怕的误会就要澄清了，我一获自由，为您和您的伙伴做什么都行。凡是能为您做的，我都乐意至极。您要是想让我在巴黎给您买颜料，没有比这更让我高兴的了。

===Original===

I know French as well as I know my own language” (he most certainly did) “and whereas you might be cheated, I’ll get you everything you need *à bon marché*. Because you see they know me there, and I know just where to go. Just give me the money for what you need and I’ll get you the best there is in Paris for it. You needn’t worry”﻿—I was protesting that it would be too much trouble﻿—“my dear fellow, it’s no trouble to do a favour for a friend.”
And to B. and myself *ensemble* he declared, with tears in his eyes, “I have some marmalade at my house in Paris, real marmalade, not the sort of stuff you buy these days. We know how to make it. You can’t get an idea how delicious it is. In big crocks”﻿—the Count said simply﻿—“well, that’s for you boys.” We protested that he was too kind. “Nothing of the sort,” he said, with a delicate smile. “I have a son in the English Army,” and his face clouded with worry, “and we send him some now and then, he’s crazy about it. I know what it means to him. And you shall share in it too. I’ll send you six crocks.” Then, suddenly looking at us with a pleasant expression, “By Jove!” the Count said, “do you like whiskey? Real Bourbon whiskey? I see by your look that you know what it is. But you never tasted anything like this. Do you know London?” I said no, as I had said once before. “Well, that’s a pity,” he said, “for if you did you’d know this bar. I know the barkeeper well, known him for thirty years. There’s a picture of mine hanging in his place. Look at it when you’re in London, drop in to —﻿—﻿— Street, you’ll find the place, anyone will tell you where it is. This fellow would do anything for me. And now I’ll tell you what I’ll do: you fellows give me whatever you want to spend and I’ll get you the best whiskey you ever tasted. It’s his own private stock, you understand. I’ll send it on to you﻿—God knows you need it in this place. I wouldn’t do this for anyone else, you understand,” and he smiled kindly; “but we’ve been prisoners together, and we understand each other, and that’s enough for gentlemen. I won’t forget you.” He drew himself up. “I shall write,” he said slowly and distinctly, “to Vanderbilt about you. I shall tell him it’s a dirty bloody shame that you two young Americans, gentlemen born, should be in this foul place. He’s a man who’s quick to act.

我通晓法语就跟我通晓母语一样」（他确实通晓）「，您自己去买兴许挨宰，我能给您弄到一切所需，便宜（*à bon marché*）。因为您瞧，那边的人都认得我，我知道该去哪儿。只要把钱给我，我就能给您弄到巴黎最好的。您不必担心」——我正推说太麻烦——「我亲爱的朋友，为朋友效劳谈不上麻烦。」
然后他对B.和我合起来宣布，眼里含着泪：「我在巴黎家里有一些橘子酱，真正的橘子酱，不是如今买的那种货色。我们懂得怎么做。它有多美味您根本想象不到。装在大瓦罐里」——伯爵简单地说——「呃，就归你们小伙子了。」我们推说他太客气。「哪里的话，」他精致地微笑道，「我有个儿子在英国军队里，」他的脸因担忧而阴下来，「我们时不时给他寄些，他为它着迷。我知道它对他的意义。你们也来分享。我给你们寄六罐。」接着，他忽然带着愉快的表情看着我们：「老天！」伯爵说，「你们喜欢威士忌吗？真正的波本威士忌？我看你们的眼神就知道你们懂行。可你们从没尝过这样的。您了解伦敦吗？」我说不了解，同上次一样。「唉，可惜，」他说，「否则您就知道那家酒吧了。我跟酒保熟得很，认识三十年了。我的一幅画就挂在他那儿。您到伦敦时去看看，到———街一拐就找到，谁都能告诉您。那家伙为我什么都肯干。现在我要告诉您我打算怎么办：你们两位把想花的钱给我，我给你们弄你们此生尝过的最好的威士忌。是他私藏的陈货，您明白。我给你们寄来——天知道你们在这地方多需要它。这种事我谁都不给办，您明白，」他和蔼地微笑；「可我们一起蹲过监狱，我们彼此了解，对绅士来说这就够了。我不会忘记你们的。」他挺起身。「我要写信，」他缓慢而清晰地一字一顿说，「给范德比尔特，写你们的事。我要告诉他：你们两个年轻的美国人，天生的绅士，居然蹲在这个肮脏地方，这是桩血腥的耻辱。他是个说干就干的人。

===Original===

He’ll not tolerate a thing like this﻿—an outrage, a bloody outrage, upon two of his own countrymen. We shall see what happens then.”
It was during this period that Count Bragard lent us for our personal use his greatest treasure, a water glass. “I don’t need it,” he said simply and pathetically.
Now, as I have said, a change in our relations came.
It came at the close of one soggy, damp, raining afternoon. For this entire hopeless grey afternoon Count Bragard and B. promenaded The Enormous Room. Bragard wanted the money﻿—for the whiskey and the paints. The marmalade and the letter to Vanderbilt were, of course, gratis. Bragard was leaving us. Now was the time to give him money for what we wanted him to buy in Paris and London. I spent my time rushing about, falling over things, upsetting people, making curious and secret signs to B., which signs, being interpreted, meant be careful! But there was no need of telling him this particular thing. When the *planton* announced *la soupe*, a fiercely weary face strode by me en route to his mattress and his spoon. I knew that B. had been careful. A minute later he joined me, and told me as much.﻿ ﻿…
On the way downstairs we ran into the Surveillant. Bragard stepped from the ranks and poured upon the Surveillant a torrent of French, of which the substance was: you told them not to give me anything. The Surveillant smiled and bowed and wound and unwound his hands behind his back and denied anything of the sort.
It seems that B. had heard that the kindly nobleman wasn’t going to Paris at all.
Moreover, Monsieur Pet-airs had said to B. something about Count Bragard being a suspicious personage﻿—Monsieur Pet-airs, the R.A.’s best friend.
Moreover, as I have said, Count Bragard had been playing up to the poor Spanish Whoremaster to beat the band. Every day had he sat on a little stool beside the rolypoly millionaire, and written from dictation letter after letter in French﻿—with which language the rolypoly was sadly unfamiliar.﻿ ﻿… And when next day Count Bragard took back his treasure of treasures, his personal water glass, remarking briefly that he needed it once again, I was not surprised. And when, a week or so later, he left﻿—I was not surprised to have Mexique come up to us and placidly remark:
“I give dat feller five francs. Tell me he send me overcoat, very good overcoat.

===Chinese===

他不会容忍这种事——对他自己同胞的一场暴行，一场血腥暴行。到那时我们瞧吧。」
正是在这个时期，布拉加尔伯爵把他最大的宝贝，一只水杯，借给了我们个人使用。「我用不着它了。」他简单而可怜地说。
于是，如我所说，我们关系的变化来了。
它发生在一个湿透、阴潮、下雨的下午的尾声。整整这个绝望的灰色下午，布拉加尔伯爵和B.在巨室里踱步。布拉加尔想要钱——买威士忌和颜料的钱。橘子酱和给范德比尔特的信当然是免费的。布拉加尔要离开我们了。此刻正是把钱交给他、托他在巴黎和伦敦代购的最后时机。我满屋子乱转，绊倒东西，撞翻人，朝B.做着古怪而隐秘的暗号——那暗号翻译过来就是：当心！可这件事根本不用提醒他。当卫兵喊「喝汤」时，一张凶狠而疲惫的脸从我身边大步走过，走向他的床垫和勺子。我知道B.一直当心着。一分钟后他到我这边来，告诉我确实如此。……
下楼路上我们撞见监守。布拉加尔出列，朝监守倾泻了一股法语洪流，大意是：您叫他们什么也别给我。监守微笑、鞠躬，把背在身后的手缠了又解、解了又缠，矢口否认。
原来，B.已经听说，这位和善的贵族压根儿不去巴黎。
再者，佩泰尔先生对B.说过些布拉加尔伯爵形迹可疑的话——佩泰尔先生，皇家会员的至交。
再者，如我说过的，布拉加尔一直死命巴结那位可怜的西班牙老鸨。他天天坐在滚圆百万富翁旁的一张小凳上，听写代笔，用法语一封接一封地写信——这门语言，滚圆富翁可悲地不通。……所以，第二天布拉加尔伯爵收回他那宝藏中的宝藏、他的私人水杯、简短地说他又需要它时，我不惊讶。一星期左右之后他离开时——墨西哥踱到我们跟前，平静地说：「我给了那家伙五法郎。跟我说他给我寄大衣，很好的大衣。

===Original===

But say: Please no tell anybody come from me. Please tell everybody your family send it.” And with a smile, “I t’ink dat feller fake.”
Nor was I surprised to see, some weeks later, the poor Spanish Whoremaster rending his scarce hair as he lay in bed of a morning. And Mexique said with a smile:
“Dat feller give dat English feller one hundred francs. Now he sorry.”
All of which meant merely that Count Bragard should have spelt his name, not Bra-, but with an l.
And I wonder to this day that the only letter of mine which ever reached America and my doting family should have been posted by this highly entertaining personage en ville, whither he went as a trusted inhabitant of La Ferté to do a few necessary errands for himself; whither he returned with a good deal of colour in his cheeks and a good deal of *vin rouge* in his guts; going and returning with Tommy, the *planton* who brought him *The Daily Mail* every day until Bragard couldn’t afford it, after which either B. and I or Jean le Nègre took it off Tommy’s hands﻿—Tommy, for whom we had a delightful name which I sincerely regret being unable to tell, Tommy, who was an Englishman for all his French *planton’s* uniform and worshipped the ground on which the Count stood; Tommy, who looked like a boiled lobster and had tears in his eyes when he escorted his idol back to captivity.﻿ ﻿… *Mirabile dictu*, so it was.
Well, such was the departure of a great man from among us.
And now, just to restore the reader’s faith in human nature, let me mention an entertaining incident which occurred during the latter part of my stay at La Ferté Macé. Our society had been gladdened﻿—or at any rate galvanized﻿—by the biggest single contribution in its history; the arrival simultaneously of six purely extraordinary persons, whose names alone should be of more than general interest: The Magnifying Glass, The Trick Raincoat, The Messenger Boy, The Hat, The Alsatian, The Whitebearded Raper and His Son. In order to give the reader an idea of the situation created by these *arrivés*, which situation gives the entrance of the Washing Machine Man﻿—the entertaining incident, in other words﻿—its full and unique flavour, I must perforce sketch briefly each member of a truly imposing group.

===Chinese===

可是说：请别告诉任何人是我给的。请告诉大家是你们家里寄的。」然后一笑，「我寻思那家伙是假的。」——我毫不惊讶。
几个星期后，看见可怜的西班牙老鸨早晨躺在床上扯他稀缺的头发，我也毫不惊讶。墨西哥微笑着说：
「那家伙给了那个英国家伙一百法郎。现在他后悔了。」
这一切只意味着：布拉加尔伯爵的姓氏，拼法不该是Bra-，而该多一个l（意为Blagard，骗子）。
而我至今纳闷：我此生唯一寄抵美国、送到我慈爱家人手里的信，竟是由这位极具娱乐性的人物在镇上（en ville）寄出的；他作为拉费尔泰受信任的住户进城，为自己办几件必要差事；回来时两颊颇见血色，肚里颇见红酒（*vin rouge*）；往返都由汤米（Tommy）陪同——就是那个每天给伯爵送《每日邮报》直到伯爵付不起、之后由B.和我或黑人让（Jean le Nègre）接手买下的卫兵汤米——我们给汤米起过一个绝妙的外号，我真诚遗憾不能说出来；汤米，他除了那身法国卫兵制服，骨子里是个英国人，崇拜伯爵脚下的地皮；汤米，长得像只煮熟的龙虾，护送他的偶像重返囚笼时眼里含着泪。……说来神奇（Mirabile dictu），事情就是这样。
唉，一位伟人就这样从我们中间离去了。
现在，为了让读者恢复一点对人性的信心，容我提一桩发生在我拉费尔泰马塞之行后期的有趣事件。我们的社会迎来——或至少被电了一下——它历史上最大的单笔进账：六位纯粹非凡人物的同时到来，光他们的大名就应能引起超出一般的兴趣：放大镜、戏法雨衣、电报童、帽子、阿尔萨斯人、白胡子强奸犯及其子。为了让读者对这批来客（arrivés）所造成的局面有一点概念——正是这局面给洗衣机人（the Washing Machine Man）的登场，换言之即那桩有趣事件——添足了独一无二的风味，我不得不简要勾画这支真正威风的队伍的每一位成员。

===Original===

Let me say at once that, so terrible an impression did the members make, each inhabitant of The Enormous Room rushed at breakneck speed to his paillasse; where he stood at bay, assuming as frightening an attitude as possible. The Enormous Room was full enough already, in all conscience. Between sixty and seventy mattresses, with their inhabitants and, in nearly every case, baggage, occupied it so completely as scarcely to leave room for *le poêle* at the further end and the card table in the centre. No wonder we were struck with terror upon seeing the six nouveaux. Judas immediately protested to the *planton* who brought them up that there were no places, getting a roar in response and the door slammed in his face to boot. But the reader is not to imagine that it was the number alone of the arrivals which inspired fear and distrust﻿—their appearance was enough to shake anyone’s sanity. I do protest that never have I experienced a feeling of more profound distrust than upon this occasion; distrust of humanity in general and in particular of the following individuals:
An old man shabbily dressed in a shiny frock coat, upon whose peering and otherwise very aged face a pair of dirty spectacles rested. The first thing he did, upon securing a place, was to sit upon his mattress in a professorial manner, tremulously extract a journal from his left coat pocket, tremblingly produce a large magnifying glass from his upper right vest pocket, and forget everything. Subsequently, I discovered him promenading the room with an enormous expenditure of feeble energy, taking tiny steps flat-footedly and leaning in when he rounded a corner as if he were travelling at terrific speed. He suffered horribly from rheumatism, could scarcely move after a night on the floor, and must have been at least sixty-seven years old.
Second, a palish, foppish, undersized, prominent-nosed creature who affected a deep musical voice and the cut of whose belted raincoat gave away his profession﻿—he was a pimp, and proud of it, and immediately upon his arrival boasted thereof, and manifested altogether as disagreeable a species of bullying vanity as I ever (save in the case of The Fighting Sheeney) encountered. He got his from Jean le Nègre, as the reader will learn later.
Third, a super-Western-Union-Messenger type of ancient-youth, extraordinarily unhandsome if not positively ugly.

===Chinese===

让我立刻声明：这队人马给人的印象如此可怕，以致巨室每位住户都以不要命的速度冲向自己的草垫；在那里背水而立，摆出尽可能吓人的姿势。平心而论，巨室本来已经够满了。六七十张床垫，连同其住户，且几乎张张带行李，把它占得密不透风，几乎没给远端的炉子（*le poêle*）和中央的牌桌留地方。难怪我们一见这六位新客就吓得魂飞魄散。犹大立刻向押他们上来的卫兵抗议说没有位子了，换来一声怒吼，外加当面摔门。但读者切莫以为仅仅是来客的人数引发了恐惧与不信任——他们的尊容就够动摇任何人的理智。我郑重声明，我从未体验过比这回更深刻的不信任感——对人类全体的不信任，尤其对以下各位：
一位衣衫寒酸、穿一件亮光光长礼服的老人，一张窥探而别提多老迈的脸上架着一副脏眼镜。他一占到位子干的第一件事，就是以教授派头往床垫上一坐，颤巍巍地从左襟口袋里抽出一份报纸，颤抖着从右上背心口袋里掏出一面巨大的放大镜，然后把一切忘到脑后。后来我发现他在屋里放风，以虚弱能量的巨大消耗迈着碎步，平脚掌落地，转弯时向内倾，仿佛正以骇人的速度疾驰。他风湿得厉害，在地板上过一夜几乎动弹不得，怎么也有六十七岁了。
第二位，一个偏白、脂粉气、矮小、大鼻子的生物，爱用一种深沉的音乐性嗓音，他那件束带雨衣的剪裁出卖了他的职业——他是个皮条客，并引以为荣，一到就自吹自擂，展现出一种总体上其讨厌程度堪比打架的犹太佬的、恃强凌空的虚荣。他的报应来自黑人让，读者稍后便知。
第三位，一个超级西部联合电报童类型的老青年，不说绝对丑吧，也是非同寻常地不俊。

===Original===

He had a weak pimply grey face, was clad in a brownish uniform, puttees (on pipestem calves), and a regular Messenger Boy cap. Upon securing a place he instantly went to the card-table, seated himself hurriedly, pulled out a batch of blanks, and wrote a telegram to (I suppose) himself. Then he returned to his paillasse, lay down with apparently supreme contentment, and fell asleep.
Fourth, a tiny old man who looked like a caricature of an East Side secondhand clothes dealer﻿—having a long beard, a long, worn and dirty coat reaching just to his ankles, and a small derby hat on his head. The very first night his immediate neighbour complained that “Le Chapeau” (as he was christened by The Zulu) was guilty of fleas. A great tempest ensued immediately. A *planton* was hastily summoned. He arrived, heard the case, inspected The Hat (who lay on his paillasse with his derby on, his hand far down the neck of his shirt, scratching busily and protesting occasionally his entire innocence), uttered (being the Black Holster) an oath of disgust, and ordered The Frog to “*couper les cheveux de suite et la barbe aussi; après il va au bain, le vieux*.” The Frog approached and gently requested The Hat to seat himself upon a chair﻿—the better of two chairs boasted by The Enormous Room. The Frog, successor to The Barber, brandished his scissors. The Hat lay and scratched. “*Allez, Nom de Dieu*” the *planton* roared. The poor Hat arose trembling, assumed a praying attitude; and began to talk in a thick and sudden manner. “*Asseyez-vous là, tête de cochon.*” The pitiful Hat obeyed, clutching his derby to his head in both withered hands. “Take off your hat, you son of a bitch,” the *planton* yelled. “I don’t want to,” the tragic Hat whimpered. **Bang!** the derby hit the floor, bounded upward and lay still. “Proceed,” the orderly thundered to The Frog, who regarded him with a perfectly inscrutable expression on his extremely keen face, then turned to his subject, snickered with the scissors, and fell to. Locks ear-long fell in crisp succession. Pete the Shadow, standing beside the barber, nudged me; and I looked; and I beheld upon the floor the shorn locks rising and curling with a movement of their own.﻿ ﻿… “Now for the beard,” said the Black Holster.﻿—“No, no, *Monsieur, s’il vous plaît, pas ma barbe, monsieur*”﻿—The Hat wept, trying to kneel.

===Chinese===

他有一张虚弱、多粉刺的灰脸，穿一身棕褐色制服、绑腿（绑在麻秆小腿上）、戴一顶地道的电报童帽。一占到位子，他立刻走到牌桌前，匆匆坐下，抽出一叠空白单子，给（我猜）他自己写了一封电报。然后回到草垫上，带着显然至上的满足躺下，睡着了。
第四位，一个tiny老头，长得像东区旧衣商的漫画——长胡子，一件长及踝的破脏大衣，头上一顶小圆顶帽。第一夜刚过，他的紧邻就控告「帽子」（祖鲁人给他起的圣名）有跳蚤。一场大风暴随即爆发。卫兵被急召而来。他到了，听完案情，验看帽子（那人躺在草垫上，圆顶帽照戴，一只手深深探进衬衫领口，忙着挠，不时申辩自己全然无辜），（来的是黑枪套）厌恶地骂了句粗口，命令青蛙「立刻剪掉头发，胡子也剪；剪完送老头去洗澡（*couper les cheveux de suite et la barbe aussi; après il va au bain, le vieux*）」。青蛙走上前，温和地请帽子先生坐到椅子上——那是巨室所夸耀的两把椅子中较好的一把。青蛙，理发师的继任者，挥舞着剪刀。帽子躺着挠。「快点儿，他妈妈的（*Allez, Nom de Dieu*）。」卫兵吼。可怜的帽子颤抖着爬起来，摆出祈祷的姿势；开始又急又含糊地说话。「坐到那儿去，猪头（*tête de cochon*）。」可怜的帽子从了，两只枯手把圆顶帽死死掃在头上。「摘帽，你这狗娘养的。」卫兵叫嚷。「我不想摘。」悲壮的帽子抽噎道。**砰！**圆顶帽砸在地板上，弹起，静止。「动手。」勤务兵对青蛙雷鸣道；青蛙用那张极其鋭利的脸上一个全然高深莫测的表情看了看他，然后转向他的对象，拿剪刀窃笑了一下，动手了。齐耳的发绺脆生生地次第落下。影子皮特站在理发师旁边，用肘碰碰我；我看了；我看见地板上剪下的发绉自行立起、卷曲，有一种它们自己的动静。……「现在剃胡子。」黑枪套说。——「不，不，先生，求您了，别动我的胡子，先生（*Monsieur, s'il vous plaît, pas ma barbe, monsieur*）」——帽子哭了，试图下跪。

===Original===

﻿—“*Ta gueule* or I’ll cut your throat,” the *planton* replied amiably; and The Frog, after another look, obeyed. And lo, the beard squirmed gently upon the floor, alive with a rhythm of its own; squirmed and curled crisply as it lay.﻿ ﻿… When The Hat was utterly shorn, he was bathed and became comparatively unremarkable, save for the worn long coat which he clutched about him, shivering. And he borrowed five francs from me twice, and paid me punctually each time when his own money arrived, and presented me with chocolate into the bargain, tipping his hat quickly and bowing (as he always did whenever he addressed anyone). Poor Old Hat, B. and I and the Zulu were the only men at La Ferté who liked you.
Fifth, a fat, jolly, decently dressed man.﻿—He had been to a camp where everyone danced, because an entire ship’s crew was interned there, and the crew were enormously musical, and the captain (having sold his ship) was rich and tipped the Director regularly; so everyone danced night and day, and the crew played, for the crew had brought their music with them.﻿—He had a way of borrowing the paper (*Le Matin*) which we bought from one of the lesser *plantons* who went to the town and got *Le Matin* there; borrowing it before we had read it﻿—by the sunset. And his favourite observations were:
“It’s a rotten country. Dirty weather.”
Fifth and sixth, a vacillating, staggering, decrepit creature with wildish white beard and eyes, who had been arrested﻿—incredibly enough﻿—for “rape.” With him his son, a pleasant youth quiet of demeanour, inquisitive of nature, with whom we sometimes conversed on the subject of the English Army.
Such were the individuals whose concerted arrival taxed to its utmost the capacity of The Enormous Room. And now for my incident:
In the doorway, one day shortly after the arrival of the gentlemen mentioned, quietly stood a well-dressed handsomely middle-aged man, with a sensitive face culminating in a groomed Van Dyck beard. I thought for a moment that the Mayor of Orne, or whatever his title is, had dropped in for an informal inspection of The Enormous Room. Thank God, I said to myself, it has never looked so chaotically filthy since I have had the joy of inhabiting it.

===Chinese===

——「闭嘴（*Ta gueule*），不然割了你喉咙。」卫兵和蔼地答；青蛙再看了一眼，从了。于是瞧啊，那把胡子在地板上温柔地蠕动，因一种自身的节律而活着；蠕动着，脆生生地卷曲着，就那么躺着。……帽子被剃了个精光，洗了澡，就比较不起眼了，只剩那件他紧紧裹在身上发抖的破长大衣。他前后两次向我借了五法郎，每次他自己的钱一到就准时还我，还额外送我巧克力，飞快地碰一下帽檐、鞠一躬（他每次开口同谁说话都这样）。可怜的老帽子，B.和我还有祖鲁人是拉费尔泰仅有的喜欢你的人。
第五位，一个胖乎乎、乐呵呵、穿得体面的男人。——他待过一个人人都跳舞的营地，因为整船船员都被拘在那儿，船员个个精通音乐，船长（把船卖了之后）有钱，定期给所长打点；于是人人日夜跳舞，船员奏乐，因为船员把他们的乐器随身带来了。——他有个习惯，老向我们借那份《晨报》（*Le Matin*）——报纸是我们从一个小些的卫兵那里买的，那卫兵进城代购——总赶在我们看之前——在日落时分——借走。他的口头禅是：
「这是个烂国家。破天气。」
第五和第六位，一个摇摇晃晃、跌跌撞撞、老朽不堪的生物，白胡子白眼睛乱蓬蓬，被捕的罪名——令人难以置信——竟是「强奸」。跟着他的儿子，一个讨人喜欢的青年，举止安静，天性好奇，我们有时同他聊聊英国军队。
这便是那几位先生光临之后不久的一天，静静立在门口的那伙人；如今站在门口的，是一位衣着体面、英俊的中年男子，一张敏感的脸，尖端是一部修整过的范代克式胡须。有一瞬间我以为奥恩省省长（不管他叫什么头衔）前来对巨室做非正式视察。感谢上帝，我对自己说，自从我有幸居住以来，它还从没这么混乱地脏过。

===Original===

And *sans blague*, The Enormous Room *was* in a state of really supreme disorder; shirts were thrown everywhere, a few twine clothes lines supported various pants, handkerchiefs and stockings, the stove was surrounded by a gesticulating group of nearly undressed prisoners, the stink was actually sublime.
As the door closed behind him, the handsome man moved slowly and vigorously up The Enormous Room. His eyes were as big as turnips. His neat felt hat rose with the rising of his hair. His mouth opened in a gesture of unutterable astonishment. His knees trembled with surprise and terror, the creases of his trousers quivering. His hands lifted themselves slowly outward and upward till they reached the level of his head; moved inward till they grasped his head: and were motionless. In a deep awestruck resonant voice he exclaimed simply and sincerely:
“*Nom de nom de nom de nom de nom de **dieu**!*”
Which introduces the reader to The Washing Machine Man, a Hollander, owner of a store at Brest where he sold the highly *utiles* contrivances which gave him his name. He, as I remember, had been charged with aiding and abetting in the case of escaping deserters﻿—but I know a better reason for his arrest: undoubtedly *le gouvernement français* caught him one day in the act of inventing a super-washing machine, in fact, a Whitewashing machine, for the private use of the Kaiser and His Family.﻿ ﻿…
Which brings us, if you please, to the first Delectable Mountain.

===Chinese===

而且不开玩笑（*sans blague*），巨室确实处于一种真正至高的失序状态；衬衫扔得四处都是，几根麻绳晾衣线上挂着形形色色的裤子、手帕和长袜，炉子被一群几乎光身的囚犯围着手舞足蹈，那臭实在崇高。
门在他身后关上，那英俊的男人沿着巨室缓慢而有力地走上来。他的眼睛瞪得像萝卜。他那顶整洁的毡帽随头发的竖起而竖起。他的嘴张成一个不可言喻的惊愕。他的膝盖因惊异与恐怖而发抖，裤线颤抖。他的双手缓缓向外向上抬起，直到与头齐平；向内移动，直到掃住头颅：然后静止。以一种深沉的、敬畏的、洪亮的嗓音，他简单而真诚地感叹：
「名的名的名的名的名的神哪！（*Nom de nom de nom de nom de nom de **dieu**!*）」
这便向读者介绍了洗衣机人（The Washing Machine Man），荷兰人，在布雷斯特开着一家铺子，卖那些使他得名的极为「实用」（*utiles*）的器械。他——如果我没记错——被控协助及教唆逃兵越狱——但我更知道他被捕的一个更好的理由：毫无疑问，法国政府有一天当场逮住了他，当时他正在发明一台超级洗衣机——实际上是一台粉饰机——供德皇及其家族私人使用。……
如果诸位允许，这就把我们带到了第一座悦目山。

## VIII The Wanderer / 八 漫游者
===Original===

One day somebody and I were “catching water” for Monsieur the Chef.
“Catching water” was ordinarily a mixed pleasure. It consisted, as I have mentioned, in the combined pushing and pulling of a curiously primitive two-wheeled cart over a distance of perhaps three hundred yards to a kind of hydrant situated in a species of square upon which the medieval structure known as Porte (or Camp) de Triage faced stupidly and threateningly. A *planton* always escorted the catchers through a big door, between the stone wall, which backed the men’s *cour* and the end of the building itself, or, in other words, the canteen. The ten-foot stone wall was, like every other stone wall, connected with La Ferté, topped with three feet of barbed wire. The door by which we exited with the water-wagon to the street outside was at least eight feet high, adorned with several large locks. One pushing behind, one pulling in the shafts, we rushed the wagon over a sort of threshold or sill and into the street; and were immediately yelled at by the *planton*, who commanded us to stop until he had locked the door. We waited until told to proceed; then yanked and shoved the reeling vehicle up the street to our right, that is to say, along the wall of the building, but on the outside. All this was pleasant and astonishing. To feel oneself, however temporarily, outside the eternal walls in a street connected with a rather selfish and placid looking little town (whereof not more than a dozen houses were visible) gave the prisoner an at once silly and uncanny sensation, much like the sensation one must get when he starts to skate for the first time in a dozen years or so. The street met two others in a moment, and here was a very flourishing sumach bush (as I guess) whose berries shocked the stunned eye with a savage splash of vermilion. Under this colour one discovered the Mecca of water-catchers in the form of an iron contrivance operating by means of a stubby lever which, when pressed down, yielded grudgingly a spout of whiteness. The contrivance was placed in sufficiently close proximity to a low wall so that one of the catchers might conveniently sit on the wall and keep the water spouting with a continuous pressure of his foot, while the other catcher manipulated a tin pail with telling effect.

===Chinese===

有一天，某人和我正在为厨子先生「打水」。
「打水」照例是一种悲喜交加的乐趣。如我所说，它的内容是：合力推拉一辆奇特原始的两轮车，走大约三百码，到一种广场上的一处水栓跟前；那座被称为甄别营（Porte〔或Camp〕de Triage）的中世纪建筑，就愚蠢而凶狠地面朝那个广场。总有一名卫兵押着打水人穿过一扇大门——门开在男院背后那堵石墙同建筑本身尽端（换言之即小卖部那一端）之间。那堵十英尺的石墙同拉费尔泰的每一堵石墙一样，顶上加装三英尺铁丝网。我们拉着水车出街上街的那道门至少八英尺高，缀着几把大锁。一人在后推，一人在辕里拉，我们推着车猛冲过一道门槛或窗台似的坎，冲上街去；随即被卫兵大吼一声喝止，命我们等他锁好门。我们等到放行的命令；然后把那辆摇摇晃晃的车辆连拖带拽弄上右手的街，也就是说沿建筑的外墙走。这一切既愉快又令人惊叹。让自己——哪怕暂时地——置身永恒的墙外、置身一条通向一个相当自私而安详的小镇的街上（看得见的房子不过十几座），给囚徒一种既傻气又瘆人的感觉，很像一个人时隔十几年重新开始滑冰时的感觉。街走不远便同另外两条街相接，那里有一丛相当茂盛的漆树（我猜），浆果以一记凶猛的朱红飞溅震骇发懵的眼睛。这片颜色底下，打水人发现了他们的麦加：一台铁装置，靠一根短粗的压杆工作，往下一压，不情愿地吐出一股白色。装置紧挨着一堵矮墙安放，一名打水人可以方便地坐在墙上，用脚持续踩住压杆让水一直喷，另一名打水人则操作一只锡桶，卓有成效。

===Original===

Having filled the barrel which rode on the two wagon wheels, we turned it with some difficulty and started it down the street with the tin pail on top; the man in the shafts leaning back with all his might to offset a certain velocity promoted by the down grade, while the man behind tugged helpingly at the barrel itself. On reaching the door we skewed the machine skillfully to the left, thereby bringing it to a complete standstill, and waited for the *planton* to unlock the locks; which done, we rushed it violently over the threshold, turned left, still running, and came to a final stop in front of the kitchen. Here stood three enormous wooden tubs. We backed the wagon around; then one man opened a spigot in the rear of the barrel, and at the same time the other elevated the shafts in a clever manner, inducting the *jet d’eau* to hit one of the tubs. One tub filled, we switched the stream wittily to the next. To fill the three tubs (they were not always all of them empty) required as many as six or eight delightful trips. After which one entered the *cuisine* and got his well-earned reward﻿—coffee with sugar.
I have remarked that catching water was a mixed pleasure. The mixedness of the pleasure came from certain highly respectable citizens, and more often citizenesses, of *la ville* de La Ferté Macé; who had a habit of endowing the poor water-catchers with looks which I should not like to remember too well, at the same moment clutching whatever infants they carried or wore or had on leash spasmodically to them. I never ceased to be surprised by the scorn, contempt, disgust and frequently sheer ferocity manifested in the male and particularly in the female faces. All the ladies wore, of course, black; they were wholly unbeautiful of face or form, some of them actually repellant; not one should I, even under more favourable circumstances, have enjoyed meeting. The first time I caught water everybody in the town was returning from church, and a terrific sight it was. *Vive la bourgeoisie*, I said to myself, ducking the shafts of censure by the simple means of hiding my face behind the moving water barrel.

===Chinese===

装满安在两只车轮上的木桶后，我们费点劲把它调过头，顺着街往下走，锡桶搁在桶顶；驾辕的那人拼命后仰，以抵消下坡助长的那种速度，后面那人则帮着拽桶本身。一到门口，我们熟练地把机器一歪，让它彻底停住，等卫兵开锁；锁一开，我们猛推它过门槛，左转，一路小跑，在厨房前最终停住。这里立着三只巨大的木盆。我们把车倒过去；一人打开桶尾的水嘴，同时另一人巧妙地抬起车辕，引导水柱（jet d'eau）射进其中一只盆。一盆满了，我们机智地把水流切到下一盆。要灌满三盆（它们并非总是全空着）需要多达六至八趟愉快的行程。之后便可进厨房领取应得的报酬——加了糖的咖啡。
我说过打水是一种悲喜交加的乐趣。悲的部分来自拉费尔泰马塞镇（la ville）上某些极为可敬的公民，尤其是女公民；她们有个习惯，一面朝可怜的打水人投来我不愿记得太清楚的目光，一面把怀里的、抱着的、拴着的婴儿猛地搂紧。镇上男女脸上——尤其女人们脸上——流露的轻蔑、鄙夷、嫌恶乃至纯粹的凶狠，从未让我停止惊讶。女士们当然一律穿黑；脸蛋身段全然无美可言，有的实在可憎；哪怕在更有利的情况下，她们中也没有一位我乐意结识。我头一回打水，正赶上全镇人从教堂出来，那场面实在壮观。资产阶级万岁（Vive la bourgeoisie），我对自己说，一边把脸藏进移动的水桶后面，以这一简单手段躲过非难的辕木。

===Original===

But one day﻿—as I started to inform the reader﻿—somebody and I were catching water, and, in fact, had caught our last load, and were returning with it down the street; when I, who was striding rapidly behind trying to lessen with both hands the impetus of the machine, suddenly tripped and almost fell with surprise﻿—
On the curb of the little unbeautiful street a figure was sitting, a female figure dressed in utterly barbaric pinks and vermilions, having a dark shawl thrown about her shoulders; a positively Arabian face delimited by a bright coif of some tenuous stuff, slender golden hands holding with extraordinary delicacy what appeared to be a baby of not more than three months old; and beside her a black-haired child of perhaps three years and beside this child a girl of fourteen, dressed like the woman in crashing hues, with the most exquisite face I had ever known.
*Nom de Dieu*, I thought vaguely. Am I or am I not completely asleep? And the man in the shafts craned his neck in stupid amazement, and the *planton* twirled his moustache and assumed that intrepid look which only a *planton* (or a gendarme) perfectly knows how to assume in the presence of female beauty.
That night The Wanderer was absent from *la soupe*, having been called by Apollyon to the latter’s office upon a matter of superior import. Everyone was abuzz with the news. The gypsy’s wife and three children, one a baby at the breast, were outside demanding to be made prisoners. Would the Directeur allow it? They had been told a number of times by *plantons* to go away, as they sat patiently waiting to be admitted to captivity. No threats, pleas nor arguments had availed. The wife said she was tired of living without her husband﻿—roars of laughter from all the Belgians and most of the Hollanders, I regret to say Pete included﻿—and wanted merely and simply to share his confinement. Moreover, she said, without him she was unable to support his children! and it was better that they should grow up with their father as prisoners than starve to death without him. She would not be moved. The Black Holster told her he would use force﻿—she answered nothing. Finally she had been admitted pending judgment. *Also sprach*, highly excited, the *balayeur*.

===Chinese===

但有一天——我正要告诉读者——某人和我在打水，而且实际上已经打完了最后一趟，正拉着重载沿街返回；我那时正在后面大步疾走，用双手努力削减机器的冲力，忽然绊了一下，惊得几乎跌倒——
在那条不美的小街的路缘上，坐着一个身影，一个女性身影，穿一身彻底蛮荒的粉红与朱红，肩上搭一条深色披巾；一张地道的阿拉伯脸，界以一方轻薄料子的鲜亮头巾；纤细的金色双手以非凡的轻柔攥着看来是一个不过三个月的婴儿；她旁边是一个黑发小孩，约莫三岁；小孩旁边是个十四岁的姑娘，衣着同那女人一样响亮刺目，长着我此生所知最精致的一张脸。
他妈妈的（Nom de Dieu），我迷迷糊糊地想。我到底是睡熟了还是没睡熟？驾辕那人伸长脖子，傻傻地惊叹；卫兵捻着胡子，摆出那副只有卫兵（或宪兵）在女性美貌面前才完全懂得摆的无畏表情。
那晚，漫游者缺席了汤（la soupe），他被亚波伦唤去后者办公室，商谈一件高级要务。人人都为这消息嗡嗡作响。吉卜赛人的妻子和三个孩子，一个还在吃奶，正在外面要求把他们收监。所长会准吗？他们坐在外面耐心等着入狱，卫兵们已劝了好几回。威胁、恳求、讲理一概无效。妻子说，她厌倦了没有丈夫的日子——全体比利时人和大多数荷兰人哄堂大笑，我很遗憾地说包括皮特——她只是想，单纯地想，分担他的监禁。何况她说，没有他，她养不活他的孩子们！与其没有他而饿死，不如跟着父亲当囚犯长大。她不为所动。黑枪套告诉她要动武——她一言不答。最后她获准先行收押，候审。扫地人（balayeur）如此激动地宣称。

===Original===

“Looks like a﻿—hoor,” was the Belgian-Dutch verdict, a verdict which was obviously due to the costume of the lady in question almost as much as to the untemperamental natures sojourning at La Ferté. B. and I agreed that she and her children were the most beautiful people we had ever seen, or would ever be likely to see. So *la soupe* ended, and everybody belched and gasped and trumpeted up to The Enormous Room as usual.
That evening, about six o’clock, I heard a man crying as if his heart were broken. I crossed The Enormous Room. Half-lying on his paillasse, his great beard pouring upon his breast, his face lowered, his entire body shuddering with sobs, lay The Wanderer. Several of the men were about him, standing in attitudes ranging from semi-amusement to stupid sympathy, listening to the anguish which﻿—as from time to time he lifted his majestic head﻿—poured slowly and brokenly from his lips. I sat down beside him. And he told me: “I bought him for six hundred francs, and I sold him for four hundred and fifty﻿ ﻿… it was not a horse of this race, but of the race” (I could not catch the word) “as long as from here to that post. I cried for a quarter of an hour just as if my child were dead﻿ ﻿… and it is seldom I weep over horses﻿—I say: you are going, Jewel, *au r’oir et bon jour*.”﻿ ﻿…
The vain little dancer interrupted about “broken-down horses”﻿ ﻿… “*Excuses donc*﻿—this was no disabled horse, such as goes to the front﻿—these are some horses﻿—pardon, whom you give eat, this, it is colique, that, the other, it’s colique﻿—this never﻿—he could go forty kilometres a day.﻿ ﻿…”
One of the strongest men I have seen in my life is crying because he has had to sell his favourite horse. No wonder *les hommes* in general are not interested. Someone said: “Be of good cheer, Demestre, your wife and kids are well enough.”
“Yes﻿—they are not cold; they have a bed like that” (a high gesture toward the quilt of many colours on which we were sitting, such a quilt as I have not seen since; a feathery deepness soft to the touch as air in Spring), “which is worth three times this of mine﻿—but *tu comprends*, it’s not hot these mornings”﻿—then he dropped his head, and lifted it again, crying, crying.
“*Et mes outils*, I had many﻿—and my garments﻿—where are they put, *où﻿—où? Kis!* And I had chemises﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「看着像个——婊子。」这是比利时-荷兰联合法庭的判词，此判词显然一半归功于当事女士的装束，一半归功于羁留拉费尔泰的诸君那缺乏艺术气质的天性。B.和我则一致认为，她和她的孩子们是我们此生见过、以及此生可能再见到的最美的人。汤就这样收了场，人人打着嗝、喘着气、吹着号，照旧上楼回巨室。
那天傍晚六点来钟，我听见一个人哭得肝肠寸断。我穿过巨室。漫游者半躺在草垫上，大胡子倾泻在胸前，脸低着，整个身体随哽咽发抖。几个男人围着他，姿势从半看热闹到傻同情不等，听那痛苦——每当他抬起威严的头——从他唇间缓慢而断续地倾泻。我在他身边坐下。于是他告诉我：「我花六百法郎买的他，四百五十卖了……不是这一种的马，是那种」（我没听清那个词）「从这儿到那根柱子那么长的马。我哭了一刻钟，就像我的孩子死了一样……我很少为马哭——我说：你走吧，宝贝儿，回头见，日安（au r'oir et bon jour）。」……
那个爱虚荣的小舞男插进来，说什么「废马」……「得了吧（Excuses donc）——这可不是什么残废马，送到前线去的那种——有些马，对不起，你给它们吃的，这个，是绞痛，那个，还是绞痛——这匹从来不——他一天能走四十公里。……」
我此生见过的最强壮的男人之一，正因为他被迫卖掉了心爱的马而哭。难怪男人们（les hommes）总体上不感兴趣。有人说：「想开点，德梅斯特，你老婆孩子都好好的。」
「是啊——他们不冷；他们有一床那样的被子」（朝我们正坐着的那床多彩被子高高一举；那样的被子我此后再没见过；一种羽毛般的深厚，摸上去软如春日的空气），「值我那床的三倍——可是你懂（tu comprends），这几天的早晨不暖」——然后他垂下头，又抬起来，哭着，哭着。
「还有我的工具（Et mes outils），我有好多——还有我的衣裳——它们放到哪儿去了，哪——哪儿？（où—où? Kis!）我还有衬衫……

===Original===

this is poor” (looking at himself as a prince might look at his disguise)﻿—“and like this, that﻿—where?”
“*Si* the wagon is not sold﻿ ﻿… I never will stay here for *la durée de la guerre*. No﻿—bahsht! To resume, that is why I need.﻿ ﻿…”
(more than upright in the priceless bed﻿—the twice streaming darkness of his beard, his hoarse sweetness of voice﻿—his immense perfect face and deeply softnesses eyes﻿—pouring voice)
“my wife sat over there, she spoke to No one and bothered Nobody﻿—why was my wife taken here and shut up? Had she done anything? There is a wife who *fait la putain* and turns, to everyone and another, whom I bring another tomorrow﻿ ﻿… but a woman who loves only her husband, who waits for no one but her husband﻿—”
(the tone bulged, and the eyes together)
“﻿—*Ces cigarettes ne fument pas!*” I added an apology, having presented him with the package. “Why do you shell out these? They cost fifteen sous, you may spend for them if you like, you understand what I’m saying? But some time when you have nothing” (extraordinary gently) “what then? Better to save for that day﻿ ﻿… better to buy *du tabac* and *faire* yourself; these are made of tobacco dust.”
And there was someone to the right who was saying: “Tomorrow is Sunday”﻿ ﻿… wearily. The King, lying upon his huge quilt, sobbing now only a little, heard:
“So﻿—ah﻿—he was born on a Sunday﻿—my wife is nursing him, she gives him the breast” (the gesture charmed) “she said to them she would not eat if they gave her that﻿—that’s not worth anything﻿—meat is necessary every day﻿ ﻿…” he mused. I tried to go.
“Sit there” (graciousness of complete gesture. The sheer kingliness of poverty. He creased the indescribably soft *couverture* for me and I sat and looked into his forehead bounded by the cube of square sliced hair. Blacker than Africa. Than imagination).
After this evening I felt that possibly I knew a little of The Wanderer, or he of me.
The Wanderer’s wife and his two daughters and his baby lived in the women’s quarters. I have not described and cannot describe these four. The little son of whom he was tremendously proud slept with his father in the great quilts in The Enormous Room. Of The Wanderer’s little son I may say that he had lolling buttons of eyes sewed on gold flesh, that he had a habit of turning cartwheels in one-third of his father’s trousers, that we called him The Imp.

这些就寒碜了」（像王子打量自己的化装服那样打量自己）——「都是这样，那样——在哪儿呢？」
「要是（Si）车还没卖……我绝不会在这儿待到战争结束（la durée de la guerre）。不——呸！（bahsht!）总之，正因如此我才需要。……」
（在那床无价的被子里坐得笔直还多——胡须两度倾泻的黑暗，嗓音的沙哑的甜——他那巨大完美的脸和深深柔软的眼——倾泻的嗓音）

（嗓音涨起来，眼睛也一起涨）
「——这些烟抽不着（Ces cigarettes ne fument pas）！」我为递给他的那包烟补了个歉。「你干嘛掳这个？值十五个苏呢，你高兴当然可以花，你听懂我的话没有？可等哪天你什么都没有了」（无比温柔地）「怎么办？不如省到那一天……不如买烟丝（du tabac）自己卷（faire）；这些是烟末子做的。」
右边有谁在说：「明天是礼拜天」……有气无力地。卧在他巨大被子里的国王，此刻只偶尔抽噎了，听见了：
「对——啊——他生在礼拜天——我妻子在喂他，给他吃奶」（那手势令人迷醉）「她跟他们说了，要是给她那个她就不吃——那东西一文不值——每天都得有肉……」他沉吟。我想要走。
「坐那儿」（一个完整手势的雍容。贫穷那纯粹的王者气派。他替我折了折那不可言喻的软被子〔couverture〕，我坐下，凝望他被方形切齐的头发之立方所界的前额。比非洲还黑。比想象还黑）。
这晚之后，我感到我也许对漫游者懂得了一点点，或他对我懂得了一点点。
漫游者的妻子、他的两个女儿和他的婴儿住在女监。这四位我未曾描述，也无法描述。他为之无比自豪的小儿子睡在巨室里，同父亲一起裹在巨大的被子里。关于漫游者的小儿子，我可以这样说：他长着两只缝在金色肌肤上的、晃荡的纽扣眼；他有个习惯，钻进他父亲三分之一大的裤子里侧手翻；我们管他叫小魔鬼（The Imp）。

===Original===

He ran, he teased, he turned handsprings, he got in the way, and he even climbed the largest of the scraggly trees in the *cour* one day. “You will fall,” Monsieur Peters (whose old eyes had a fondness for this irrepressible creature) remarked with conviction.﻿—“Let him climb,” his father said quietly. “I have climbed trees. I have fallen out of trees. I am alive.” The Imp shinnied like a monkey, shouting and crowing, up a lean gnarled limb﻿—to the amazement of the very *planton* who later tried to rape Celina and was caught. This *planton* put his gun in readiness and assumed an eager attitude of immutable heroism. “Will you shoot?” the father inquired politely. “Indeed it would be a big thing of which you might boast all your life: I, a *planton*, shot and killed a six-year-old child in a tree.”﻿—“*C’est emmerdant*,” the *planton* countered, in some confusion﻿—“he may be trying to escape. How do I know?”﻿—“Indeed, how do you know anything?” the father murmured quietly. “It’s a *mystère*.” The Imp, all at once, fell. He hit the muddy ground with a disagreeable thud. The breath was utterly knocked out of him. The Wanderer picked him up kindly. His son began, with the catching of his breath, to howl uproariously. “Serves him right, the —﻿—﻿— jackanapes,” a Belgian growled.﻿—“I told you so, didn’t I?” Monsieur Pet-airs worringly cried: “I said he would fall out of that tree!”﻿—“Pardon, you were right, I think,” the father smiled pleasantly. “Don’t be sad, my little son, everybody falls out of trees, they’re made for that by God,” and he patted The Imp, squatting in the mud and smiling. In five minutes The Imp was trying to scale the shed. “Come down or I fire,” the *planton* cried nervously﻿ ﻿… and so it was with The Wanderer’s son from morning till night. “Never,” said Monsieur Pet-airs with solemn desperation, “have I seen such an incorrigible child, a perfectly incorrigible child,” and he shook his head and immediately dodged a missile which had suddenly appeared from nowhere.
Night after night The Imp would play around our beds, where we held court with our chocolate and our candles; teasing us, cajoling us, flattering us, pretending tears, feigning insult, getting lectures from Monsieur Peters on the evil of cigarette smoking, keeping us in a state of perpetual inquietude.

===Chinese===

他跑，他闹，他翻筋斗，他碍事，有一天他甚至爬上了院子里最大的一棵瘦树。「你要摔的。」佩泰尔先生说（他的老眼挺喜欢这个按捤不住的小生物），语气笃定。——「让他爬，」他父亲安静地说，「我爬过树。我从树上摔下来过。我还活着。」小魔鬼像猴子一样攀上了一条瘦而疙疙瘩瘩的枝——看得那名后来试图强奸塞利娜而被抓的卫兵目瞪口呆。那卫兵把枪一端，摆出一副雷打不动的英雄姿态。「您要开枪吗？」父亲礼貌地问。「那可是件能吹一辈子的大事：我，一个卫兵，开枪打死了一个树上六岁的孩子。」——「真烦人（C'est emmerdant），」卫兵有些狼狈地反驳——「他兴许是想越狱。我怎么知道？」——「是啊，您又怎么知道任何事呢？」父亲平静地咕哝，「这是个谜（mystère）。」小魔鬼忽然掉了下来。他闷响一声砸在泥地上。气全被砸了出来。漫游者好心地把他抱起来。儿子缓过气来，随即嚎啕大哭。「活该，这个小——猴崽子。」一个比利时人咆哮。——「我说什么来着？」佩泰尔先生担忧地喊，「我说过他会从那棵树上掉下来！」——「抱歉，我想您说对了，」父亲愉快地微笑，「别难过，我的小儿子，人人都会从树上掉下来，树是上帝造来给人掉的。」他拍拍小魔鬼，蹲在泥里微笑。五分钟后，小魔鬼已在试图攀登棚子了。「下来，不然开枪了。」卫兵紧张地喊……漫游者的儿子从早到晚就是这样。「从未，」佩泰尔先生庄重而绝望地说，「见过这么不可救药的孩子，一个彻头彻尾不可救药的孩子。」他摇着头，随即闪身躲开一枚不知从哪儿突然飞来的暗器。
一夜又一夜，小魔鬼在我们的床边玩耍；我们就在那里以巧克力和蜡烛开朝；他逗我们，哄我们，捧我们，装哭，装受辱，听佩泰尔先生讲授吸烟之恶的课，让我们处在永恒的不安之中。

===Original===

When he couldn’t think of anything else to do he sang at the top of his clear bright voice:

> “C’est la guerre

faut pas t’en faire”

and turned a handspring or two for emphasis.﻿ ﻿… Mexique once cuffed him for doing something peculiarly mischievous, and he set up a great crying﻿—instantly The Wanderer was standing over Mexique, his hands clenched, his eyes sparkling﻿—it took a good deal of persuasion to convince the parent that his son was in error, meanwhile Mexique placidly awaited his end﻿ ﻿… and neither B. nor I, despite the Imp’s tormentings, could keep from laughing when he all at once with a sort of crowing cry rushed for the nearest post, jumped upon his hands, arched his back, and poised head-downward; his feet just touching the pillar. Barefooted, in a bright chemise and one-third of his father’s trousers.﻿ ﻿…
Being now in a class with “*les hommes mariés*” The Wanderer spent most of the day downstairs, coming up with his little son every night to sleep in The Enormous Room. But we saw him occasionally in the *cour*; and every other day when the dreadful cry was raised
