On the contrary, I merely state what interested me peculiarly in the course of my sojourn at La Ferté: I mention that I was extremely moved to find that, however busy sixty men may be kept suffering in common, there is always one man or two or three men who can always find time to make certain that their comrades enjoy a little extra suffering. In the case of Surplice, to be the butt of everyone’s ridicule could not be called precisely suffering; inasmuch as Surplice, being unspeakably lonely, enjoyed any and all insults for the simple reason that they constituted or at least implied a recognition of his existence. To be made a fool of was, to this otherwise completely neglected individual, a mark of distinction; something to take pleasure in; to be proud of. The inhabitants of The Enormous Room had given to Surplice a small but essential part in the drama of La Misère: he would play that part to the utmost of his ability; the cap-and-bells should not grace a head unworthy of their high significance. He would be a great fool, since that was his function; a supreme entertainer, since his duty was to amuse. After all, men in La Misère as well as anywhere else rightly demand a certain amount of amusement; amusement is, indeed, peculiarly essential to suffering; in proportion as we are able to be amused we are able to suffer; I, Surplice, am a very necessary creature after all.
I recall one day when Surplice beautifully demonstrated his ability to play the fool. Someone had crept up behind him as he was stalking to and fro, head in air proudly, hands in pockets, pipe in teeth, and had (after several heartbreaking failures) succeeded in attaching to the back of his jacket by means of a pin a huge placard carefully prepared beforehand, bearing the numerical inscription

> 606

===Chinese===

恰恰相反，我只是陈述我在拉费尔泰逗留期间特别感到兴味的事：我提及，我极为动容地发现，六十个人无论怎样忙于集体受苦，总有一两个或三个人，永远找得出时间确保他们的难友多受一点额外的苦。就白袍而言，当众人的笑柄还称不上是受苦；因为白袍，由于说不出的孤独，享受任何以及一切侮辱，理由很简单：侮辱构成、至少暗含对他的存在的承认。被当傻瓜耍，对这个 otherwise 彻底遭忽视的个体，是一种殊荣；一件可乐的事；一件可傲的事。巨室的住户们已在苦难（La Misère）的大戏里分派给白袍一个小而不可缺的角色：他将倾其所能演好这个角色；那顶系铃铛的帽子绝不佩戴在不配其崇高意义的头上。他将是一个伟大的傻子，因为那是他的职能；一个至高的艺人，因为他的本分是娱人。毕竟，苦难中的人同别处的人一样，理直气壮地要求一定分量的娱乐；娱乐实在是受苦所特需的；我们越能被逗乐，就越能受苦；我，白袍，毕竟是一个非常必要的存在。
我记得有一天，白袍漂亮地演示了他装傻的本事。有人趁他昂着头、手插兜、叼着烟斗来回踱步时，从他背后猫过来，（经历了几次痛心的失败后）成功用别针在他上衣背面别上一张预先精心制作的大告示，上书巨幅数字

> 606

===Original===

in vast writing. The attacher, having accomplished his difficult feat, crept away. So soon as he reached his paillasse a volley of shouts went up from all directions, shouts in which all nationalities joined, shouts or rather jeers which made the pillars tremble and the windows rattle﻿—
“**Six cent six! Syph’lis!**”
Surplice started from his reverie, removed his pipe from his lips, drew himself up proudly, and﻿—facing one after another the sides of The Enormous Room﻿—blustered in his bad and rapid French accent:
“*Pas syph’lis! Pas syph’lis!*”
at which, rocking with mirth, everyone responded at the top of his voice:
“**Six cent six!**”
Whereat, enraged, Surplice made a dash at Pete The Shadow and was greeted by
“Get away, you bloody Polak, or I’ll give you something you’ll be sorry for”﻿—this from the lips of America Lakes. Cowed, but as majestic as ever, Surplice attempted to resume his promenade and his composure together. The din bulged:
“*Six cent six! Syph’lis! Six cent Six!*”
—increasing in volume with every instant. Surplice, beside himself with rage, rushed another of his fellow-captives (a little old man, who fled under the table) and elicited threats of:
“Come on now, you Polak hoor, and quit that business or I’ll kill you,” upon which he dug his hands into the pockets of his almost transparent pantaloons and marched away in a fury, literally frothing at the mouth.﻿—
“*Six Cent Six!*”
everyone cried. Surplice stamped with wrath and mortification. “*C’est dommage*,” Monsieur Auguste said gently beside me. “*C’est un bon-homme, le pauvre, il ne faut pas l’em-merd-er.*”
“Look behind you!”
somebody yelled. Surplice wheeled, exactly like a kitten trying to catch its own tail, and provoked thunders of laughter. Nor could anything at once more pitiful and ridiculous, more ludicrous and horrible, be imagined.
“On your coat! Look on your jacket!”
Surplice bent backward, staring over his left, then his right, shoulder, pulled at his jacket first one way then the other﻿—thereby making his improvised tail to wag, which sent The Enormous Room into spasms of merriment﻿—finally caught sight of the incriminating appendage, pulled his coat to the left, seized the paper, tore it off, threw it fiercely down, and stamped madly on the crumpled 606; spluttering and blustering and waving his arms; slavering like a mad dog.

===Chinese===

别针客大功告成，猫走了。他刚回到草垫，四面八方爆发出齐声呐喊，各国国籍一齐加盟，喊声——不如说嘲骂——震得柱子发抖、窗子作响——
「**六百零六！梅毒！（Six cent six! Syph'lis!）**」
白袍从冥想中惊起，从唇上取下烟斗，骄傲地一挺——依次面向巨室四面——用他那糟糕而急促的法语腔咆哮：
「不是梅毒！不是梅毒！（Pas syph'lis! Pas syph'lis!）」
闻言人人笑得东倒西歪，扯着嗓子回敬：
「**六百零六！**」
于是白袍怒极，朝影子皮特猛冲，得到的是「滚开，你这血波兰佬，不然给你点厉害尝尝」——这话出自美洲湖之口。白袍慑住了，却威严如故，试图同时恢复踱步与镇定。喧声暴涨：
「六百零六！梅毒！六百零六！（Six cent six! Syph'lis! Six cent Six!）」
——分秒增值。白袍怒不可遏，又冲向另一位难友（一个小老头，钻到了桌子底下），换来了「现在就滚，你这波兰婊子，再闹我宰了你」的威胁；闻言他把双手深深插进那几近透明的裤袋，狂怒地大步走开，嘴角真真切切地挂着白沫。——
「六百零六！（Six Cent Six!）」
人人喊。白袍跺着狂怒与羞辱。「真可惜（C'est dommage），」奥古斯特先生在我身旁温柔地说，「是个好人，可怜人，不该去烦他（il ne faut pas l'em-merd-er）。」
「看你背后！」
有人喊。白袍霍地转身，活像一只想咬自己尾巴的小猫，激起雷鸣般的大笑。再没有什么能同时更可怜又更可笑、更滑稽又更可怖的了。
「在你上衣上！看你上衣！」
白袍向后仰，从左肩、再从右肩望过去，把上衣先朝这边拽、再朝那边拽——从而让他那即兴的尾巴摆动起来，把巨室送进狂欢的痉挛——终于瞥见那败诉的附件，把上衣拽向左边，揪住纸，撕下来，狠狠摔在地上，对着揉皱的606疯狂乱跺；唾沫四溅，咆哮挥臂；像疯狗一样流涎。

===Original===

Then he faced the most prominently vociferous corner and muttered thickly and crazily:
“*Wuhwuhwuhwuhwuh.﻿ ﻿…*”
Then he strode rapidly to his paillasse and lay down; in which position I caught him, a few minutes later, smiling and even chuckling﻿ ﻿… very happy﻿ ﻿… as only an actor is happy whose efforts have been greeted with universal applause.﻿ ﻿…
In addition to being called “Syph’lis” he was popularly known as “Chaude Pisse, the Pole.” If there is anything particularly terrifying about prisons, or at least imitations of prisons such as La Ferté, it is possibly the utter obviousness with which (quite unknown to themselves) the prisoners demonstrate willy-nilly certain fundamental psychological laws. The case of Surplice is a very exquisite example: everyone, of course, is afraid of *les maladies vénériennes*﻿—accordingly all pick an individual (of whose inner life they know and desire to know nothing, whose external appearance satisfies the requirements of the mind apropos what is foul and disgusting) and, having tacitly agreed upon this individual as a Symbol of all that is evil, proceed to heap insults upon him and enjoy his very natural discomfiture﻿ ﻿… but I shall remember Surplice on his both knees sweeping sacredly together the spilled sawdust from a spittoon-box knocked over by the heel of the omnipotent *planton*; and smiling as he smiled at *la messe* when Monsieur le Curé told him that there was always Hell.﻿ ﻿…
He told us one day a great and huge story of an important incident in his life, as follows:
“*Monsieur*, disabled me﻿—yes, *monsieur*﻿—disabled﻿—I work, many people, house, very high, third floor, everybody, planks up there﻿—planks no good﻿—all shake﻿ ﻿…” (here he began to stagger and rotate before us) “begins to fall﻿ ﻿… falls, falls, all, all twenty-seven men﻿—bricks﻿—planks﻿—wheelbarrows﻿—all﻿—ten metres﻿ ﻿… *zuhzuhzuhzuhzuh***poom**!﻿ ﻿… everybody hurt, everybody killed, not me, injured﻿ ﻿… *oui monsieur*”﻿—and he smiled, rubbing his head foolishly. Twenty-seven men, bricks, planks and wheelbarrows. Ten metres. Bricks and planks. Men and wheelbarrows.﻿ ﻿…
Also he told us, one night, in his gentle, crazy, shrugging voice, that once upon a time he played the fiddle with a big woman in Alsace-Lorraine for fifty francs a night; “*c’est la misère*”﻿—adding quietly, “I can play well, I can play anything, I can play *n’importe quoi*.”

===Chinese===

然后他面向最响亮的那一角，含混而疯癫地咕哝：
「呃呃呃呃呃呃呃。……（Wuhwuhwuhwuhwuh.…）」
然后他快步走向草垫躺下；几分钟后我在这个姿势里逮住他，他正微笑、甚至轻笑……很幸福……像一个努力赢得满堂彩的演员那样的幸福。……
除了「梅毒」之外，他还有个通用的诨名：「尿痛 Pole（Chaude Pisse, the Pole）」。如果说监狱——或至少拉费尔泰这类监狱的仿制品——有什么特别可怕之处，那就是囚徒们（自己全然不知）不由自主地演示某些基本心理学法则的那副彻底的 obviousness。白袍的案子是个极精致的例子：当然，人人都怕性病（les maladies vénériennes）——于是全体挑出一个个体（对其内心生活一无所知也一无所求，其外表恰好满足头脑关于污秽可憎的全部要求），默认此人为一切邪恶之象征，继而对他堆砌辱骂，并享受他全然自然的窘态……但我将记住的白袍，是双膝跪地、神圣地把全能卫兵的鞋跟踢翻的痰盂箱里洒出的锯末扫拢的白袍；他微笑着，像他在弥撒（la messe）上、本堂神父先生告诉他地狱永远存在时那样的微笑。……
有一天他给我们讲了一件他人生大事的宏大故事，如下：
「先生，残废了我——是的，先生——残废了——我干活，好多人，房子，很高，三层，人人都在上头——木板不行——全晃……」（说着他在我们面前摇晃旋转起来）「开始塌了……塌了，塌了，全完了，二十七个人全——砖——木板——独轮车——全——十米……呃呃呃呃——**砰**！……人人伤，人人死，没我，残废了……是的先生（oui monsieur）」——他微笑着，傻傻地揉脑袋。二十七个人，砖，木板和独轮车。十米。砖和木板。人和独轮车。……
还有一天夜里，他用他那温柔、疯癫、耸肩的嗓音告诉我们，从前他在阿尔萨斯-洛林同一个大个子女人拉琴，一夜五十法郎；「这就是苦难（c'est la misère）」——又轻轻补一句：「我拉得好，我什么都会拉，我随便什么都能拉（n'importe quoi）。」

===Original===

Which I suppose and guess I scarcely believed﻿—until one afternoon a man brought up a harmonica which he had purchased *en ville*; and the man tried it; and everyone tried it; and it was perhaps the cheapest instrument and the poorest that money can buy, even in the fair country of France; and everyone was disgusted﻿—but, about six o’clock in the evening, a voice came from behind the last experimenter; a timid hasty voice:
“*monsieur, monsieur, permettez?*”
the last experimenter turned and to his amazement saw Chaude Pisse the Pole, whom everyone had (of course) forgotten.
The man tossed the harmonica on the table with a scornful look (a menacingly scornful look) at the object of universal execration; and turned his back. Surplice, trembling from the summit of his filthy and beautiful head to the naked soles of his filthy and beautiful feet, covered the harmonica delicately and surely with one shaking paw; seated himself with a surprisingly deliberate and graceful gesture; closed his eyes, upon whose lashes there were big filthy tears﻿ ﻿… and played.﻿ ﻿…
… and suddenly:
He put the harmonica softly upon the table. He rose. He went quickly to his paillasse. He neither moved nor spoke nor responded to the calls for more music, to the cries of “*Bis!*”﻿—“*Bien joué!*”﻿—“*Allez!*”﻿—“*Va-z-y!*” He was crying, quietly and carefully, to himself﻿ ﻿… quietly and carefully crying, not wishing to annoy anyone﻿ ﻿… hoping that people could not see that Their Fool had temporarily failed in his part.
The following day he was up as usual before anyone else, hunting for chewed cigarette ends on the spitty slippery floor of The Enormous Room; ready for insult, ready for ridicule, for buffets, for curses.
*Alors*﻿—
One evening, some days after everyone who was fit for *la commission* had enjoyed the privilege of examination by that inexorable and delightful body﻿—one evening very late, in fact, just before *lumières éteintes*, a strange *planton* arrived in The Enormous Room and hurriedly read a list of five names, adding:
“*demain partir, à bonne heure*,”
and shut the door behind him. Surplice was, as usual, very interested, enormously interested. So were we: for the names respectively belonged to Monsieur Auguste, Monsieur Pet-airs, The Wanderer, Surplice and The Spoonman. These men had been judged. These men were going to Précigne.

===Chinese===

这话我想、我料想我几乎不信——直到某天下午，有人把一支在镇上（en ville）买的口琴带了上来；那人试了试；人人都试了试；那大概是钱能买到的最贱最糟的乐器，哪怕在法兰西这个美丽的国度；人人都倒了胃口——可是，傍晚六点来钟，最后一个试验者背后响起一个声音；一个羞怯而急切的声音：
「先生，先生，可以吗？（monsieur, monsieur, permettez?）」
最后的试验者转过身，惊讶地看见了尿痛 Pole，那个人人（当然）都忘掉了的家伙。
那人把口琴朝桌上一丢，朝这个众矢之的投去轻蔑的一瞥（威胁性的轻蔑的一瞥）；背过身去。白袍，从他那脏而美的头顶到他那脏而美的赤脚脚底一路发着抖，用一只颤抖的爪子轻而稳地罩住口琴；以一个出奇从容而优雅的手势落座；阖上双眼，睫毛上挂着大颗脏浊的泪……然后演奏。……
……而后忽然：
他把口琴轻轻放在桌上。他起身。他快步走向自己的草垫。他不动，不言，不应答众人对更多音乐的呼唤，不应答那声声「再来！（Bis!）」——「奏得好！（Bien joué!）」——「来啊！（Allez!）」——「上！（Va-z-y!）」他在哭，安静而小心地，独自……安静而小心地哭，不愿打搅任何人……但愿人们看不出，他们的傻子暂时没演好自己的角色。
第二天他照旧比谁都起得早，在巨室唾汪汪滑溜溜的地板上搜寻嚼过的烟头；备好了领受侮辱，领受嘲弄，领受拳脚，领受咒骂。
于是（Alors）——
某晚，凡是够格上委员会（la commission）的人都已享受过那个铁面而可爱的机构的审问之后几天——一个很晚的晚上，实际上就在熄灯（lumières éteintes）之前，一名陌生的卫兵来到巨室，匆匆宣读了五个名字，附加一句：
「明早出发，趁早（demain partir, à bonne heure），」
随手带上了门。白袍照例极感兴趣，兴趣极大。我们也一样：因为这五个名字分别属于奥古斯特先生、佩泰尔先生、漫游者、白袍和勺子人。这些人已被判决。这些人要去普雷西涅了。

===Original===

These men would be *prisonniers pour la durée de la guerre*.
I have already told how Monsieur Pet-airs sat with the frantically weeping Wanderer writing letters, and sniffing with his big red nose, and saying from time to time: “Be a man, Demestre, don’t cry, crying does no good.”﻿—Monsieur Auguste was brokenhearted. We did our best to cheer him; we gave him a sort of Last Supper at our bedside, we heated some red wine in the tin cup and he drank with us. We presented him with certain tokens of our love and friendship, including﻿—I remember﻿—a huge cheese﻿ ﻿… and then, before us, trembling with excitement, stood Surplice﻿—
We asked him to sit down. The onlookers (there were always onlookers at every function, however personal, which involved Food or Drink) scowled and laughed. *Le con*, Surplice, *chaude pisse*﻿—how could he sit with men and gentlemen? Surplice sat down gracefully and lightly on one of our beds, taking extreme care not to strain the somewhat capricious mechanism thereof; sat very proudly; erect; modest but unfearful. We offered him a cup of wine. A kind of huge convulsion gripped, for an instant, fiercely his entire face: then he said in a whisper of sheer and unspeakable wonderment, leaning a little toward us without in any way suggesting that the question might have an affirmative answer,
“*pour moi, monsieur?*”
We smiled at him and said “*Prenez, monsieur.*” His eyes opened. I have never seen eyes since. He remarked quietly, extending one hand with majestic delicacy:
“*Merci, monsieur.*”
… Before he left, B. gave him some socks and I presented him with a flannel shirt, which he took softly and slowly and simply and otherwise not as an American would take a million dollars.
“I will not forget you,” he said to us, as if in his own country he were a more than very great king﻿ ﻿… and I think I know where that country is, I think I know this; I, who never knew Surplice, know.
---
For he has the territory of harmonicas, the acres of flutes, the meadows of clarinets, the domain of violins. And God says: Why did they put you in prison? What did you do to the people? “I made them dance and they put me in prison. The soot-people hopped; and to twinkle like sparks on a chimney-back and I made eighty francs every *dimanche*, and beer and wine, and to eat well. *Maintenant﻿ ﻿… c’est fini﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这些人将成为战争期间的囚犯（prisonniers pour la durée de la guerre）。
我已经讲过佩泰尔先生如何陪着发狂痛哭的漫游者写信，用他的大红鼻子抽气，不时说：「做个男子汉，德梅斯特，别哭了，哭没有用。」——奥古斯特先生心都碎了。我们尽力宽慰他；我们在床头为他办了一场最后的晚餐，用锡杯温了红酒，他同我们共饮。我们赠他以爱与友谊的信物，包括——我记得——一大块奶酪……然后，在我们面前，激动得发抖地，站着白袍——
我们请他坐下。围观者（凡涉及吃喝的场合，无论多么私密，永远有围观者）皱眉冷笑。那傻子（le con），白袍，尿痛——他怎么配同男人和绅士平起平坐？白袍轻巧而优雅地在一张我们床上坐下，格外小心不压坏那套多少任性的机构；坐得极骄傲；挺直；谦逊而无惧。我们敬他一杯酒。一阵巨大的痉挛，刹那间，猛烈攫住他整张脸：然后他以一种纯粹而不可言喻的惊奇的耳语说，微微朝我们倾身，丝毫不暗示这问题可能得到肯定的回答：
「给我的，先生？（pour moi, monsieur?）」
我们朝他微笑，说：「请用，先生（Prenez, monsieur.）。」他的眼睛睁开了。此后我再没见过那样的眼睛。他安静地说，以君王般的 delicacy 伸出一只手：
「谢谢，先生（Merci, monsieur.）。」
……临走前，B.送他几双袜子，我赠他一件法兰绒衬衫，他轻轻地、缓缓地、简单地接过，那样子同美国人接过一百万美元迥然不同。
「我不会忘记你们的。」他对我们说，仿佛在他的祖国，他是一位大过伟大君王的君王……而我想我知道那个祖国在哪儿，我想我知道这一点；我，这个从未认识过白袍的人，知道。
---
因为他拥有口琴的疆土，长笛的亩丘，单簧管的草甸，小提琴的领地。上帝说：他们为什么把你关进监狱？你对人们做了什么？「我让他们跳舞，他们把我关进监狱。煤烟的人们蹦跳；在烟囱背上像火星一样闪耀，我每个礼拜天（dimanche）挣八十法郎，还有啤酒和葡萄酒，吃得也好。如今（Maintenant……完了（c'est fini）……

===Original===

Et tout de suite* (gesture of cutting himself in two) *la tête*.” And He says: “O you who put the jerk into joys, come up hither. There’s a man up here called Christ who likes the violin.”

===Chinese===

马上就（Et tout de suite）」（一个把自己切成两段的手势）「脑袋（la tête）」。于是祂说：「哦你，把颠动放进欢乐里的人，上这儿来。这儿有个人叫基督，他喜欢小提琴。」

## XI Jean le Nègre / 十一 让·勒内格尔
===Original===

On a certain day the ringing of the bell and accompanying rush of men to the window facing the entrance gate was supplemented by an unparalleled volley of enthusiastic exclamations in all the languages of La Ferté Macé﻿—provoking in me a certainty that the queen of fair women had arrived. This certainty thrillingly withered when I heard the cry: “*Il y a un noir!*” Fritz was at the best peephole, resisting successfully the onslaught of a dozen fellow prisoners, and of him I demanded in English, “Who’s come?”﻿—“Oh, a lot of girls,” he yelled, “and there’s a **nigger** too”﻿—hereupon writhing with laughter.
I attempted to get a look, but in vain; for by this at least two dozen men were at the peephole, fighting and gesticulating and slapping each other’s back with joy. However, my curiosity was not long in being answered. I heard on the stairs the sound of mounting feet, and knew that a couple of *plantons* would before many minutes arrive at the door with their new prey. So did everyone else﻿—and from the farthest beds uncouth figures sprang and rushed to the door, eager for the first glimpse of the nouveau; which was very significant, as the ordinary procedure on arrival of prisoners was for everybody to rush to his own bed and stand guard over it.
Even as the *plantons* fumbled with the locks I heard the inimitable, unmistakable divine laugh of a negro. The door opened at last. Entered a beautiful pillar of black strutting muscle topped with a tremendous display of the whitest teeth on earth. The muscle bowed politely in our direction, the grin remarked musically: “*Bo’jour, tou’l’monde*”; then came a cascade of laughter. Its effect on the spectators was instantaneous: they roared and danced with joy. “*Comment vous appelez-vous?*” was fired from the hubbub.﻿—“*J’m’appelle Jean, moi*,” the muscle rapidly answered with sudden solemnity, proudly gazing to left and right as if expecting a challenge to this statement: but when none appeared, it relapsed as suddenly into laughter﻿—as if hugely amused at itself and everyone else including a little and tough boy, whom I had not previously noted, although his entrance had coincided with the muscle’s.
Thus into the *misère* of La Ferté Macé stepped lightly and proudly Jean le Nègre.
Of all the fine people in La Ferté, Monsieur Jean (“*le noir*” as he was entitled by his enemies) swaggers in my memory as the finest.

===Chinese===

某一天，铃声一响、众人照例涌向正对大门的那扇窗，这熟悉的场面之外，又添了一阵空前的、用拉费尔泰马塞（La Ferté Macé）所有语言发出的热情欢呼——这让我确信：绝色中的女王驾到了。这份确信令人心惊地枯萎了——当我听见那声喊：「来了个黑人（Il y a un noir）！」弗里茨占着最好的窥孔，成功顶住了十几个难友的冲击；我用英语问他：「谁来了？」——「哦，一大帮娘们儿，」他嚷道，「还有个黑鬼」——说着笑得直不起腰。
我试着挤进去看一眼，白费劲；窥孔前这时少说有二十来个人，推搡着、比划着，高兴得互相拍背。不过我的好奇心没等多久就有了答案。楼梯上传来上行的脚步声，我知道不出几分钟，两个 planton 就会押着新猎物到门口。别人也都知道——最远处的床铺上也蹦起一个个粗笨的身影，直扑房门，争看新来的（nouveau）第一眼；这很说明问题，因为按老规矩，囚犯一到，人人该冲向自己的床、严防死守才是。
planton 们还在摸锁，我就听见了那种黑人的无可模仿、错不了的、神一样的笑声。门终于开了。进来一根漂亮的黑柱子，满是用足了劲的肌肉，顶着一口地上最白的牙，灿烂夺目。那身肌肉朝我们彬彬有礼地一躬，那口白牙乐呵呵地招呼：「你好，大伙儿（Bo’jour, tou’l’monde）」；跟着是一串瀑布般的笑声。这笑声对观众的效果立竿见影：他们吼着、欢喜得手舞足蹈。「您叫什么名字（Comment vous appelez-vous）？」有人从喧闹中开炮。「我叫让，我（J’m’appelle Jean, moi）」那身肌肉突然一本正经地飞快答道，骄傲地左顾右盼，仿佛等着有人来挑战这句话；无人应战，它便又突然陷入大笑——仿佛被自己、被旁人、还被一个又结实又小的男孩逗得不亦乐乎——那男孩我先前竟没留意，尽管他是跟那身肌肉一同进来的。
就这样，让·勒内格尔（Jean le Nègre）轻快而骄傲地踏进了拉费尔泰马塞的苦难（misère）。
在拉费尔泰（La Ferté）所有出色的人物里，让先生（他的仇人封他「黑子」（le noir））在我记忆中大摇大摆地走着，数他最出色。

===Original===

Jean’s first act was to complete the distribution (begun, he announced, among the *plantons* who had escorted him upstairs) of two pockets full of Cubebs. Right and left he gave them up to the last, remarking carelessly, “*J’ne veux, moi.*”
*Après la soupe* (which occurred a few minutes after *le noir’s* entry) B. and I and the greater number of prisoners descended to the *cour* for our afternoon promenade. The cook spotted us immediately and desired us to “catch water”; which we did, three cartfuls of it, earning our usual *café sucré*. On quitting the kitchen after this delicious repast (which as usual mitigated somewhat the effects of the swill that was our official nutriment) we entered the *cour*. And we noticed at once a well-made figure standing conspicuously by itself, and poring with extraordinary intentness over the pages of a *London Daily Mail* which it was holding upside-down. The reader was culling choice bits of news of a highly sensational nature, and exclaiming from time to time: “You don’t say! Look, the King of England is sick. Some news!﻿ ﻿… What? The queen too? Good God! What’s this? Shit! My father is dead! Oh, well. The war is over. Good.”﻿—It was Jean le Nègre, playing a little game with himself to beguile the time.
When we had mounted *à la chambre*, two or three tried to talk with this extraordinary personage in French; at which he became very superior and announced: “*J’suis anglais, moi. Parlez anglais. Comprends pas français, moi.*” At this a crowd escorted him over to B. and me﻿—anticipating great deeds in the English language. Jean looked at us critically and said: “*Vous parlez anglais? Moi parlez anglais.*”﻿—“We are Americans, and speak English,” I answered.﻿—“*Moi anglais*,” Jean said. “*Mon père, capitaine de gendarmes, Londres. Comprends pas français, moi.* **Spee**-Kingliss”﻿—he laughed all over himself.
At this display of English on Jean’s part the English-speaking Hollanders began laughing. “The son of a bitch is crazy,” one said.
And from that moment B. and I got on famously with Jean.
His mind was a child’s. His use of language was sometimes exalted fibbing, sometimes the purely picturesque. He courted above all the sound of words, more or less disdaining their meaning.

===Chinese===

让的头一桩事，是把满满两口袋荜澄茄分完（他宣布，这项分发早在押他上楼的 planton 们中间就开始了）。他左右开弓地散，一颗不剩，漫不经心地说：「我不要，我（J’ne veux, moi）。」
汤后（après la soupe，就在黑子进门几分钟后），B. 和我，还有大半囚犯，下楼到 cour 里做午后散步。厨子一眼瞧见我们，要我们「打水」；我们打水三车，挣来惯常的加糖咖啡（café sucré）。在这顿美餐之后（它照例多少冲淡了一点官方营养品——泔水——的作用），我们离开厨房进了 cour。我们一眼就注意到一个身段匀称的人，孤零零地站在显眼处，正以非凡的专注研读一份《伦敦每日邮报》（London Daily Mail）——拿倒了。这位读者正在采摘高度耸人听闻的新闻精粹，不时惊呼：「不会吧！瞧，英格兰国王病了。好新闻！……什么？王后也病了？我的天！这是什么？他妈的！我爹死了！哦，好吧。仗打完了。好。」——正是让·勒内格尔，自己在跟自己玩个解闷的小游戏。
上了楼回到房间（à la chambre），有两三个人试着用法语同这位非凡人物攀谈；他顿时不屑起来，宣布：「我是英国人，我。说英语。不懂法语，我（J’suis anglais, moi. Parlez anglais. Comprends pas français, moi.）。」一听这话，一群人把他护送到 B. 和我这里——巴望着英语世界将有一番大作为。让挑剔地打量我们，说：「你们讲英语（Vous parlez anglais）？我讲英语（Moi parlez anglais）。」——「我们是美国人，说英语，」我答。——「我的英语（Moi anglais），」让说。「我爹，宪兵队长，伦敦。不懂法语，我。说-英-语（Spee-Kingliss）」——他浑身上下笑开了。
见让这番英语表演，会说英语的荷兰人笑开了。「这狗娘养的疯了，」一个说。从那一刻起，B. 和我同让打得火热。
他的头脑是个孩子的。他对语言的运用，有时是升华了的撒谎，有时是纯粹的妙趣横生。他首要追求的是词语的声音，对其意义多少不屑一顾。

===Original===

He told us immediately (in pidgeon French) that he was born without a mother because his mother died when he was born, that his father was (first) sixteen (then) sixty years old, that his father *gagnait cinq cent franc par jour* (later, *par année*), that he was born in London and not in England, that he was in the French army and had never been in any army.
He did not, however, contradict himself in one statement: “*Les français sont des cochons*”﻿—to which we heartily agreed, and which won him the approval of the Hollanders.
The next day I had my hands full acting as interpreter for “*le noir qui comprends pas français*.” I was summoned from the *cour* to elucidate a great grief which Jean had been unable to explain to the Gestionnaire. I mounted with a *planton* to find Jean in hysterics, speechless, his eyes starting out of his head. As nearly as I could make out, Jean had had sixty francs when he arrived, which money he had given to a *planton* upon his arrival, the *planton* having told Jean that he would deposit the money with the Gestionnaire in Jean’s name (Jean could not write). The *planton* in question who looked particularly innocent denied this charge upon my explaining Jean’s version; while the Gestionnaire puffed and grumbled, disclaiming any connection with the alleged theft and protesting sonorously that he was hearing about Jean’s sixty francs for the first time. The Gestionnaire shook his thick piggish finger at the book wherein all financial transactions were to be found﻿—from the year one to the present year, month, day, hour and minute (or words to that effect). “*Mais c’est pas là*” he kept repeating stupidly. The Surveillant was uh-ahing at a great rate and attempting to pacify Jean in French. I myself was somewhat fearful for Jean’s sanity and highly indignant at the *planton*. The matter ended with the *planton’s* being sent about his business; simultaneously with Jean’s dismissal to the *cour*, whither I accompanied him. My best efforts to comfort Jean in this matter were quite futile. Like a child who has been unjustly punished he was inconsolable. Great tears welled in his eyes. He kept repeating “*sees-tee franc﻿—planton voleur*,” and﻿—absolutely like a child who in anguish calls itself by the name which has been given itself by grownups﻿—“steel Jean munee.”

===Chinese===

他立刻（用洋泾浜法语）告诉我们：他生下来就没有娘，因为他娘生他时就死了；他父亲先是十六岁，后来是六十岁；他父亲挣五百法郎一天（gagnait cinq cent franc par jour）——后来改口，一年；他生在伦敦而不是生在英国；他在法国军队里当过兵，又从没进过任何军队。但有一句话他从不自相矛盾：「法国人都是猪（Les français sont des cochons）」——这话我们衷心赞同，也为他赢得了荷兰人的好感。
第二天，给这位「不懂法语的黑子（le noir qui comprends pas français）」当翻译，我忙得不可开交。我从 cour 里被叫上来，去解说一桩让没法向管理员（Gestionnaire）说清的巨大悲伤。我跟着一个 planton 上楼，只见让正歇斯底里，说不出话，眼珠子都要瞪出来了。我勉强弄明白：让到的时候有六十法郎，一到就把钱交给了一个 planton，那 planton 对让说，他会以让的名字把钱存到管理员处（让不会写字）。我转述了让的说法，那个模样格外无辜的 planton 一口否认；管理员则喘着气咕哝，竭力撇清自己与这桩所谓的盗窃案的干系，响亮地抗议说他今儿头一回听说让的六十法郎。管理员朝那本账簿摇着他粗大的猪指头——一切银钱往来都记录在案，从开天辟地那年直到今天这一年、这一月、这一天、这一时、这一分（大意如此）。「可账上没有（Mais c’est pas là）」他翻来覆去蠢笨地念叨。监督在一旁嗯嗯啊啊个不停，试着用法语安抚让。我呢，多少有点为让的神志担心，又对那 planton 憋了一肚子火。事情最后以那 planton 被打发走差事告终；让同时也被打发回 cour，我陪他下去。我百般安慰，全然无用。他像个受了不白之冤的孩子，伤心得没法劝。大颗大颗的泪涌上他的眼睛。他翻来覆去念叨：「六十法郎（sees-tee franc）——planton 是贼（voleur）」，还有——完全像痛苦中的孩子，用大人给他起的名字喊自己——「偷（steel）让的钱（munee）。」

===Original===

To no avail I called the *planton* a *menteur*, a *voleur*, a *fils d’un chien*, and various other names. Jean felt the wrong itself too keenly to be interested in my denunciation of the mere agent through whom injustice had (as it happened) been consummated.
But﻿—again like an inconsolable child who weeps his heart out when no human comfort avails and wakes the next day without an apparent trace of the recent grief﻿—Jean le Nègre, in the course of the next twenty-four hours, had completely recovered his normal buoyancy of spirit. The *sees-tee franc* were gone. A wrong had been done. But that was yesterday. Today﻿—
And he wandered up and down, joking, laughing, singing:
“*après la guerre finit.*”﻿ ﻿…
In the *cour* Jean was the target of all female eyes. Handkerchiefs were waved to him; phrases of the most amorous nature greeted his every appearance. To all these demonstrations he by no means turned a deaf ear; on the contrary. Jean was irrevocably vain. He boasted of having been enormously popular with the girls wherever he went and of having never disdained their admiration. In Paris one day﻿—(and thus it happened that we discovered why *le gouvernement français* had arrested Jean)﻿—
One afternoon, having *rien à faire*, and being flush (owing to his success as a thief, of which vocation he made a great deal, adding as many ciphers to the amounts as fancy dictated) Jean happened to cast his eyes in a store window where were displayed all possible appurtenances for the *militaire*. Vanity was rooted deeply in Jean’s soul. The uniform of an English captain met his eyes. Without a moment’s hesitation he entered the store, bought the entire uniform, including leather puttees and belt (of the latter purchase he was especially proud), and departed. The next store contained a display of medals of all descriptions. It struck Jean at once that a uniform would be incomplete without medals. He entered this store, bought one of every decoration﻿—not forgetting the Colonial, nor yet the Belgian Cross (which on account of its size and colour particularly appealed to him)﻿—and went to his room. There he adjusted the decorations on the chest of his blouse, donned the uniform, and sallied importantly forth to capture Paris.
Everywhere he met with success. He was frantically pursued by women of all stations from *les putains* to *les princesses.

===Chinese===

我骂那 planton 是骗子（menteur）、贼（voleur）、狗崽子（fils d’un chien），外加种种别的名号，统统白搭。让对冤屈本身感得太痛，顾不上关心我对这小小的执行人——不巧正是经由他手，不义才得以完成——的声讨。
可是——又像个劝不住的孩子，人间安慰无效时哭得死去活来，第二天醒来，昨日的悲伤竟不见一丝痕迹——让·勒内格尔不出二十四个钟头，就完全恢复了他素常的快活劲儿。那六十法郎（sees-tee franc）没了。冤已经受了。可那是昨天。今天嘛——
于是他走来串去，开玩笑，笑，唱：「打完仗就完啦（après la guerre finit）。」……
在 cour 里，让是所有女性目光的靶子。手绢朝他挥；最痴情的话儿迎着他每一次露面。对这一切表示他绝不充耳不闻；恰恰相反。让的虚荣心不可救药。他夸口说，无论走到哪儿他都极得姑娘们欢心，也从不屑于她们的倾慕。有一天在巴黎——（正因如此，我们才弄明白法兰西政府（le gouvernement français）为什么逮捕了让）——
一天下午，他闲得没事（rien à faire），手头宽裕（全靠他偷窃事业的成功——这门营生他吹得天花乱坠，数目后面添几个零，全凭兴致），让碰巧把眼睛落在一家商店橱窗上，那儿陈列着军用物品的一切可能行头。虚荣在让的灵魂里扎着深根。一套英国上尉军服映入他的眼帘。他片刻也不犹豫，进店买下整套军服，连皮质绑腿和腰带都算上（后一样买得他尤其得意），扬长而去。下一家店陈列着五花八门的勋章。让立刻醒悟：没有勋章的军服是不完整的。他进店，每种勋章各买一枚——没忘殖民地勋章，也没忘比利时十字章（那大小和颜色特别合他心意）——然后回自己的房间。在房间里，他把勋章别上军服前胸，穿好军装，威风凛凛地出马征服巴黎。
他处处旗开得胜。各阶层的女人疯了似的追他，从婊子（putains）到公主（princesses）。

===Original===

* The police salaamed to him. His arm was wearied with the returning of innumerable salutes. So far did his medals carry him that, although on one occasion a gendarme dared to arrest him for beating-in the head of a fellow English officer (who being a mere lieutenant, should not have objected to Captain Jean’s stealing the affections of his lady), the sergeant of police before whom Jean was arraigned on a charge of attempting to kill refused to even hear the evidence, and dismissed the case with profuse apologies to the heroic Captain. “*‘Le gouvernement français, Monsieur*, extends to you, through me, its profound apology for the insult which your honour has received.’ *Ils sont des cochons, les français*,” said Jean, and laughed throughout his entire body.
Having had the most blue-blooded ladies of the capital cooing upon his heroic chest, having completely beaten up, with the full support of the law, whosoever of lesser rank attempted to cross his path or refused him the salute﻿—having had “great fun” saluting generals on *les grands boulevards* and being in turn saluted (“*tous les générals, tous*, salute me, Jean have more medals”), and this state of affairs having lasted for about three months﻿—Jean began to be very bored (me *très ennuyé*). A fit of temper (“me *très faché*”) arising from this ennui led to a *rixe* with the police, in consequence of which (Jean, though outnumbered three to one, having almost killed one of his assailants), our hero was a second time arrested. This time the authorities went so far as to ask the heroic captain to what branch of the English army he was at present attached; to which Jean first replied “*parle pas français, moi*,” and immediately after announced that he was a Lord of the Admiralty, that he had committed robberies in Paris to the tune of *sees meel-i-own franc*, that he was a son of the Lord Mayor of London by the Queen, that he had lost a leg in Algeria, and that the French were *cochons*. All of which assertions being duly disproved, Jean was remanded to La Ferté for psychopathic observation and safe keeping on the technical charge of wearing an English officer’s uniform.
Jean’s particular girl at La Ferté was “**Loo**-Loo.” With Lulu it was the same as with *les princesses* in Paris﻿—“me no *travaille, jam-**mais**. Les femmes travaillent*, geev Jean mun-ee, *sees, sees-tee, see-cent francs.

===Chinese===

警察向他行额手礼。回敬数不清的敬礼，敬得他胳膊发酸。他的勋章如此管用：有一次，一个宪兵竟敢因为他砸破一位英军同僚军官的脑袋而逮捕他（那人不过区区中尉，本不该对让上尉偷走他情人的芳心有什么意见），可在警长面前，让被控杀人未遂受审，警长连证词都拒绝听，连声道歉，把这案子撤销了事，对这位英雄的上尉礼敬有加。「『法兰西政府，先生（Le gouvernement français, Monsieur），』通过我，就阁下所受的侮辱，向您致以深切的歉意。」「法国人都是猪（Ils sont des cochons, les français）」让说，浑身上下笑了个遍。
首都最高贵的夫人们在他的英雄胸膛上咕咕哝哝过了，凡有军衔比他低的人挡他的路或忘了向他敬礼，都在法律全力支持下被他痛揍过了——在大马路（les grands boulevards）上向将军们敬礼、又被将军们回敬，「其乐无穷」（「所有的将军，所有的（tous les générals, tous），都敬我，让的勋章多」）——这样的日子过了约莫三个月——让开始腻了（me très ennuyé）。这股腻味生出一场脾气（me très faché），跟警察动了手（rixe）；结果（让以一敌三，差点要了其中一个动手者的命），我们的英雄第二次被捕。这回当局竟问这位英雄上尉，他目前隶属英军哪个兵种；让先答「不说法语，我（parle pas français, moi）」，紧接着宣布自己是海军部勋爵，说自己在巴黎抢劫的数额高达六百万元（sees meel-i-own franc），说自己是伦敦市长跟王后生的儿子，说自己在阿尔及利亚丢了一条腿，还说法国人是猪（cochons）。以上种种断言被一一戳穿，让被押往拉费尔泰，接受精神病观察并「妥为看管」，技术上讲的罪名是：身穿英国军官制服。
让在拉费尔泰相好的姑娘是「露-露（Loo-Loo）」。对露露，跟对巴黎的公主们一样——「我不干活，从来-不（me no travaille, jam-mais）。女人干活（Les femmes travaillent），给让钱（geev Jean mun-ee），六块、六十、六百法郎（sees, sees-tee, see-cent francs）。从来不干活，我。」

===Original===

Jamais travaille, moi.*” Lulu smuggled Jean money; and not for some time did the woman who slept next Lulu miss it. Lulu also sent Jean a lace embroidered handkerchief, which Jean would squeeze and press to his lips with a beatific smile of perfect contentment. The affair with Lulu kept Mexique and Pete The Hollander busy writing letters; which Jean dictated, rolling his eyes and scratching his head for words.
At this time Jean was immensely happy. He was continually playing practical jokes on one of the Hollanders, or Mexique, or the Wanderer, or, in fact, anyone of whom he was particularly fond. At intervals between these demonstrations of irrepressibility (which kept everyone in a state of laughter) he would stride up and down the filth-sprinkled floor with his hands in the pockets of his stylish jacket, singing at the top of his lungs his own version of the famous song of songs:

> *après la guerre finit,*

*soldat anglais parti,*

*mademoiselle que je laissais en France*

*avec des pickaninee.*
 **Plenty!**

===Chinese===

露露偷偷给让塞钱；挨着露露睡的那个女人过了好一阵子才发现钱没了。露露还送了让一块绣花蕾丝手绢，让会把它攥在手里、贴到唇上，带着心满意的至福笑容。跟露露的情事忙坏了墨西哥人（Mexique）和荷兰人皮特（Pete The Hollander），替让写信；让口述，眼睛乱转，抓耳挠腮地找词。
这时候让快活极了。他不停地恶作剧，对象或是哪个荷兰人，或是墨西哥人，或是流浪者（The Wanderer），实际上，凡是他特别喜欢的人都跑不了。在这些收不住的表演间隙（大家笑个不停），他会双手插在时髦外衣口袋里，在那撒满污秽的地板上来回迈大步，扯开嗓子唱他那版的歌中之歌：

> 打完仗就完啦（après la guerre finit），

> 英国大兵回家啦（soldat anglais parti），

> 我留在法兰西的小姑娘呀（mademoiselle que je laissais en France）

> 搂着个小黑娃（avec des pickaninee）。

> 普伦提（Plenty）！

===Original===

and laughing till he shook and had to lean against a wall.
B. and Mexique made some dominoes. Jean had not the least idea of how to play, but when we three had gathered for a game he was always to be found leaning over our shoulders, completely absorbed, once in a while offered us sage advice, laughing utterly when someone made a *cinque* or a multiple thereof.
One afternoon, in the interval between *la soupe* and *promenade*, Jean was in especially high spirits. I was lying down on my collapsible bed when he came up to my end of the room and began showing off exactly like a child. This time it was the game of *l’armée française* which Jean was playing.﻿—“*Jamais soldat, moi. Connais tous l’armée française.*” John The Bathman, stretched comfortably in his bunk near me, grunted. “*Tous*,” Jean repeated.﻿—And he stood in front of us; stiff as a stick in imitation of a French lieutenant with an imaginary company in front of him. First he would be the lieutenant giving commands, then he would be the Army executing them. He began with the manual of arms. “*Com-pag-nie﻿ ﻿…*” then, as he went through the manual, holding his imaginary gun﻿—“*htt, htt, htt.*”﻿—Then as the officer commending his troops: “*Bon. Très bon. Très bien fait*”﻿—laughing with head thrown back and teeth aglitter at his own success. John le Baigneur was so tremendously amused that he gave up sleeping to watch. *L’armée* drew a crowd of admirers from every side. For at least three-quarters of an hour this game went on.﻿ ﻿…
Another day Jean, being angry at the weather and having eaten a huge amount of *soupe*, began yelling at the top of his voice: “***Merde** à la France*,” and laughing heartily. No one paying especial attention to him, he continued (happy in this new game with himself) for about fifteen minutes. Then The Trick Raincoat (that undersized specimen, clad in feminine-fitting raiment with flashy shoes, who was by trade a pimp, being about half Jean’s height and a tenth of his physique,) strolled up to Jean﻿—who had by this time got as far as my bed﻿—and, sticking his sallow face as near Jean’s as the neck could reach, said in a solemn voice: “*Il ne faut pas dire ça.*” Jean astounded, gazed at the intruder for a moment; then demanded: “*Qui dit ça? Moi? Jean? Jamais, ja-**mais**. **Merde** à la France!

===Chinese===

笑得他直打颤，只好靠在墙上。
B. 和墨西哥人做了副多米诺骨牌。让一点不会玩，可我们仨聚起来开一局时，总看得见他俯在我们肩膀后头，全神贯注，偶尔给我们出个高招，谁凑了个五（cinque）或五的倍数，他笑得底朝天。
一天下午，汤（la soupe）和散步（promenade）之间的空当，让兴致格外高。我正躺在折叠床上，他走到我这一头的屋子，开始露一手，活脱脱像个孩子。这回让玩的是法兰西军队（l’armée française）的游戏。——「从没当过兵，我。认得全（tous）法兰西军队。」洗澡工约翰（John The Bathman）在我旁边的铺上舒舒服服躺着，哼了一声。「全（Tous）」让重复道。——他在我们跟前站定；僵得像根棍子，扮演一个法国中尉，面前一支看不见的连队。他一会儿是发令的中尉，一会儿又是执行命令的大军。他从持枪操练开始。「全-连-队（Com-pag-nie）……」接着做操练动作，端着那杆看不见的枪——「嘿、嘿、嘿（htt, htt, htt）。」——然后又变回夸奖部队的军官：「好。很好。做得好（Bon. Très bon. Très bien fait）」——仰头大笑，一口牙闪闪发光，为自己的成功。洗澡工约翰（John le Baigneur）乐坏了，觉也不睡了，光看。这支大军从四面八方招来一群仰慕者。这游戏足足玩了三刻钟。……
又有一天，让跟天气怄气，又灌了一大肚皮汤，扯开嗓门吼：「法兰西，去他妈的（Merde à la France）」，一面开心大笑。没人特别理他，他就这么自得其乐地玩了约莫一刻钟。这时花哨雨衣（The Trick Raincoat）——那是个没长开的货色，穿一身女里女气的紧身衣裤，蹬双花哨的鞋，营生是拉皮条，个头只有让的一半，体格只有让的十分之一——踱到让跟前（这时让已经逛到我床边了），把那张菜色的脸尽量往前伸，凑到离让的脸最近处，一本正经地说：「这个可不能说（Il ne faut pas dire ça）。」让吓了一跳，盯着这闯入者看了一会儿；然后质问：「谁说的（Qui dit ça）？我？让？从来没有，从-来-没有（jamais, ja-mais）。法兰西，去他妈的（Merde à la France）！

===Original===

*” nor would he yield a point, backed up as he was by the moral support of everyone present except the Raincoat﻿—who found discretion the better part of valour and retired with a few dark threats; leaving Jean master of the situation and yelling for the Raincoat’s particular delectation: “***May-rrr-de** à la France!*” more loudly than ever.
A little after the epic battle with stovepipes between The Young Pole and Bill The Hollander, the wrecked *poêle* (which was patiently waiting to be repaired) furnished Jean with perhaps his most brilliant inspiration. The final section of pipe (which conducted the smoke through a hole in the wall to the outer air) remained in place all by itself, projecting about six feet into the room at a height of seven or eight feet from the floor. Jean noticed this; got a chair; mounted on it, and by applying alternately his ear and his mouth to the end of the pipe created for himself a telephone, with the aid of which he carried on a conversation with The Wanderer (at that moment visiting his family on the floor below) to this effect:
—Jean, grasping the pipe and speaking angrily into it, being evidently nettled at the poor connection﻿—“Heh-loh, hello, hello, hello”﻿—surveying the pipe in consternation﻿—“*Merde. Ça marche pas*”﻿—trying again with a deep frown﻿—“heh-**loh**!”﻿—tremendously agitated﻿—“**Hehloh!**”﻿—a beautiful smile supplanting the frown﻿—“hello Barbu. Are you there? *Oui? Bon!*”﻿—evincing tremendous pleasure at having succeeded in establishing the connection satisfactorily﻿—“Barbu? Are you listening to me? *Oui?* What’s the matter Barbu? *Comment? Moi? Oui, **moi**? **Jean**? ja**mais**! jamais, ja**mais***, Barbu. I have never said you have fleas. *C’était pas moi, tu sais. Ja**mais**, c’était un autre. Peut-être c’était Mexique*”﻿—turning his head in Mexique’s direction and roaring with laughter﻿—“Hello, **heh-loh**. Barbu? *Tu sais*, Barbu, *j’ai jamais dit ça. Au contraire*, Barbu. *J’ai dit que vous avez des totos*”﻿—another roar of laughter﻿—“What? It isn’t true? Good. Then. What have you got, Barbu? Barbu? Lice﻿—**Ohhhh**. I understand. It’s better”﻿—shaking with laughter, then suddenly tremendously serious﻿—“hellohellohellohello **hehloh**!”﻿—addressing the stovepipe﻿—“*C’est une mauvaise machine, ça*”﻿—speaking into it with the greatest distinctness﻿—“**Hel‑l‑loh.** Barbu? *Liberté*, Barbu. *Oui. Comment?

===Chinese===

」他寸步不让，在场人人道义上撑他的腰，除了雨衣——后者识时务者为俊杰，撂下几句阴恻恻的威胁撤了；留下让掌控全场，冲着雨衣的听觉享受专门高叫：「法-兰-西，去-他-妈-的（May-rrr-de à la France）！」比先前更响。
小波兰人（The Young Pole）和荷兰人比尔那场史诗级的炉筒大战过后没几天，那口被砸烂的炉子（poêle，正耐心等着修理）给了让一个兴许是他最光辉的灵感。最末一节炉筒（把烟导出墙上一个洞送到外头）孤零零地悬在原处，伸进屋里约六英尺长，离地七八英尺高。让注意到了；搬来一把椅子；爬上去，把耳朵和嘴轮流贴在筒口上，给自己造了一部电话，借助它同流浪者（此刻正在楼下探亲）进行了一场大致如下的通话：
——让攥着炉筒，气冲冲地对着筒口说话，显然为线路不佳而恼火——「喂-喽，喂，喂，喂（Heh-loh, hello, hello, hello）」——惊惶地端详炉筒——「他妈的（Merde）。打不通（Ça marche pas）」——皱紧眉头再试——「喂-喽（heh-loh）！」——激动万分——「喂喽（Hehloh）！」——愁眉换作粲然一笑——「喂，大胡子（Barbu）。在吗？是（Oui）？好（Bon）！」——为圆满接通线路显示出巨大的快乐——「大胡子？听着呢吗？是？你怎么了大胡子？什么（Comment）？我？对，我？让？从来-没有（jamais）！从来没有，从来没有，大胡子。我从没说过你有虱子。不是我说的（C’était pas moi），你懂的（tu sais）。从来不是，是别人说的。兴许是墨西哥人（Peut-être c’était Mexique）」——把头转向墨西哥人的方向，放声大笑——「喂，喂-喽。大胡子？你懂的，大胡子，我从没说过那话（j’ai jamais dit ça）。恰恰相反（Au contraire），大胡子。我说的是你们有跳蚤（J’ai dit que vous avez des totos）」——又一阵大笑——「什么？不是真的？好。那么。你身上有什么，大胡子？大胡子？虱子——噢噢噢噢（Ohhhh）。我懂了。那就好」——笑得发抖，随即突然无比严肃——「喂喂喂喂喂喽（hellohellohellohello hehloh）！」——对着炉筒训话——「这机器真差劲（C’est une mauvaise machine, ça）」——一字一顿地对着它说——「喂-喽（Hel-l-loh）。大胡子？自由（Liberté），大胡子。是。什么？」

===Original===

C’est ça. Liberté pour tou’l’monde. Quand? Après la soupe. Oui. Liberté pour tou’l’monde après la soupe!*”﻿—to which jest astonishingly reacted a certain old man known as the West Indian Negro (a stocky credulous creature with whom Jean would have nothing to do, and whose tales of Brooklyn were indeed outclassed by Jean’s *histoires d’amour*) who leaped rheumatically from his paillasse at the word “*Liberté*” and rushed limpingly hither and thither inquiring Was it true? to the enormous and excruciating amusement of The Enormous Room in general.
After which Jean, exhausted with laughter, descended from the chair and lay down on his bed to read a letter from Lulu (not knowing a syllable of it). A little later he came rushing up to my bed in the most terrific state of excitement, the whites of his eyes gleaming, his teeth bared, his kinky hair fairly standing on end, and cried:
“You﻿—me, me﻿—you? *Pas bon.* You﻿—you, me﻿—me: *bon*. Me﻿—me, you﻿—you!” and went away capering and shouting with laughter, dancing with great grace and as great agility and with an imaginary partner the entire length of the room.
There was another game﻿—a pure child’s game﻿—which Jean played. It was the name game. He amused himself for hours together by lying on his paillasse tilting his head back, rolling up his eyes, and crying in a high quavering voice﻿—“**Jaw**-neeeeee.” After a repetition or two of his own name in English, he would demand sharply “Who is calling me? Mexique? *Es-ce que tu m’appelle*, Mexique?” and if Mexique happened to be asleep, Jean would rush over and cry in his ear, shaking him thoroughly﻿—“*Es-ce tu m’appelle, toi?*” Or it might be Barbu, or Pete The Hollander, or B. or myself, of whom he sternly asked the question﻿—which was always followed by quantities of laughter on Jean’s part. He was never perfectly happy unless exercising his inexhaustible imagination.﻿ ﻿…
Of all Jean’s extraordinary selves, the moral one was at once the most rare and most unreasonable. In the matter of *les femmes* he could hardly have been accused by his bitterest enemy of being a Puritan. Yet the Puritan streak came out one day, in a discussion which lasted for several hours. Jean as in the case of France, spoke in dogma. His contention was very simple: “The woman who smokes is not a woman.”

===Chinese===

「就是它。人人有自由（Liberté pour tou’l’monde）。什么时候？汤后。对。汤后人人有自由！」——这句玩笑竟惊人地惊动了一位老人，人称西印度黑人（the West Indian Negro，一个矮胖的轻信的家伙，让从来不肯跟他来往，他那些布鲁克林的见闻实在也比不过让的情史（histoires d’amour））——一听「自由」二字，他犯了风湿病似的从草垫（paillasse）上蹦起来，一瘸一拐地东奔西跑，逢人便问：是真的吗？逗得整个巨大的房间（The Enormous Room）笑痛了肚皮。
之后，让笑瘫了，从椅子上下来，往床上一躺看露露的信（一个音节也不认得）。过了一会儿，他杀气腾腾地冲到我的床前，兴奋到了极点，眼白放光，龇着牙，一头卷发根根倒竖，喊道：
「你——我，我——你？不好（Pas bon）。你——你，我——我：好（bon）。我——我，你——你！」喊罢蹦蹦跳跳地笑着嚷着走开了，搂着一个想象中的舞伴，以极大的优雅和同样大的敏捷，舞过整间屋子。
还有一种游戏——纯粹的孩子游戏——让爱玩。是名字游戏。他一连几个钟头自娱自乐：躺在草垫上，头往后仰，眼珠往上翻，用尖细发颤的嗓音喊——「让-内-欸欸欸欸（Jaw-neeeeee）。」把自己的名字用英语喊上一两遍之后，他会厉声质问：「谁在叫我？墨西哥人？是你叫我吗（Es-ce que tu m’appelle），墨西哥人？」要是墨西哥人碰巧睡着，让会冲过去，对着他耳朵喊，把他狠狠摇醒——「是你叫我吗，你（Es-ce tu m’appelle, toi）？」或者挨个大胡子、荷兰人皮特、B. 或者我，一个一个厉声质问——问完照例是他自己大笑一通。他那份不知疲倦的想象力不使出来，他就从来不曾真正快活过。……
在让种种非同寻常的自我里，道德的那一个既最罕见又最不讲理。在女人的事上（les femmes），就连他最恨的仇人也很难骂他一句清教徒。可有一天，清教徒那根筋冒了出来，在一场持续了好几个钟头的讨论里。让跟谈法国时的做派一样，开口便是教条。他的主张非常简单：「抽烟的女人不是女人。」

===Original===

He defended it hotly against the attacks of all the nations represented; in vain did Belgian and Hollander, Russian and Pole, Spaniard and Alsatian, charge and countercharge﻿—Jean remained unshaken. A woman could do anything but smoke﻿—if she smoked she ceased automatically to be a woman and became something unspeakable. As Jean was at this time sitting alternately on B.’s bed and mine, and as the alternations became increasingly frequent as the discussion waxed hotter, we were not sorry when the *planton’s* shout “*A la promenade les hommes!*” scattered the opposing warriors. Then up leaped Jean (who had almost come to blows innumerable times) and rushed laughing to the door, having already forgotten the whole thing.
Now we come to the story of Jean’s undoing, and may the gods which made Jean le Nègre give me grace to tell it as it was.
The trouble started with Lulu. One afternoon, shortly after the telephoning, Jean was sick at heart and couldn’t be induced either to leave his couch or to utter a word. Everyone guessed the reason﻿—Lulu had left for another camp that morning. The *planton* told Jean to come down with the rest and get *soupe*. No answer. Was Jean sick? “*Oui*, me seek.” And steadfastly he refused to eat, till the disgusted *planton* gave it up and locked Jean in alone. When we ascended after *la soupe* we found Jean as we had left him, stretched on his couch, big tears on his cheeks. I asked him if I could do anything for him; he shook his head. We offered him cigarettes﻿—no, he did not wish to smoke. As B. and I went away we heard him moaning to himself “Jawnee no see LooLoo no more.” With the exception of ourselves, the inhabitants of La Ferté Macé took Jean’s desolation as a great joke. Shouts of Lulu! rent the welkin on all sides. Jean stood it for an hour; then he leaped up, furious; and demanded (confronting the man from whose lips the cry had last issued)﻿—“Feeneesh LooLoo?” The latter coolly referred him to the man next to him; he in turn to someone else; and round and round the room Jean stalked, seeking the offender, followed by louder and louder shouts of Lulu! and Jawnee! the authors of which (so soon as he challenged them) denied with innocent faces their guilt and recommended that Jean look closer next time. At last Jean took to his couch in utter misery and disgust.

===Chinese===

他激情澎湃地捍卫这条主张，抵挡在场所有国民的进攻；比利时人荷兰人、俄国人波兰人、西班牙人阿尔萨斯人，冲锋与反冲锋，全属徒劳——让岿然不动。女人干什么都行，就是不能抽烟——她一抽烟就自动不是女人了，变成了某种说不得的东西。这时让轮流坐在 B. 的床和我床上，讨论越吵越热，他换床换得也越来越勤，所以 planton 一声吼「囚犯们，散步去了（À la promenade les hommes）！」驱散了交战双方，我们并不遗憾。让（无数次差点动起手来的那位）一跃而起，笑着冲向门口，把这一整档子事全忘了。
现在我们讲到让栽跟头的故事了，愿造出让·勒内格尔的众神给我恩典，让我把它讲成本来的样子。
祸端起于露露。打完电话（那场炉筒通话）不久的一天下午，让心里害了病，怎么劝也不肯下床，也不肯说一个字。人人都猜到了缘故——露露那天早上被转去另一个营了。planton 叫让跟大伙儿一起下去喝汤。没有回音。让病了吗？「对（Oui），我病（me seek）了。」他坚决不肯吃饭，直到那个 planton 烦了，撂下不管，把让一个人锁在屋里。
汤后（la soupe）我们上楼，见让还是我们走时的样子，直挺挺躺在铺上，腮帮子上挂着大泪珠。我问他要不要我做什么；他摇头。我们递烟给他——不，他不想抽。B. 和我走开时，听见他自言自语地哼哼：「让尼再见不着露露了（Jawnee no see LooLoo no more）。」除了我们几个，拉费尔泰马塞的居民全把让的伤心当成天大的笑话。「露露！」的喊声四起，撕破了天。
让忍了一个钟头；然后腾地跳起来，怒气冲天；（堵住最后一个喊出口的人）质问：「完了吗，露露（Feeneesh LooLoo）？」那人不动声色地指向旁边的人；旁边的人又指向别人；让绕着屋子一路追凶，身后「露露！」「让尼！」的喊声越来越响，可喊的人（他一质问过去）个个一脸无辜地否认，还劝让下回看仔细点儿。最后让满心悲苦与厌恶，倒回自己的铺上。

===Original===

The rest of *les hommes* descended as usual for the promenade﻿—not so Jean. He ate nothing for supper. That evening not a sound issued from his bed.
Next morning he awoke with a broad grin, and to the salutations of Lulu! replied, laughing heartily at himself “**Feeneesh** Loo Loo.” Upon which the tormentors (finding in him no longer a victim) desisted; and things resumed their normal course. If an occasional Lulu! upraised itself, Jean merely laughed, and repeated (with a wave of his arm) “**Feeneesh.**” Finished Lulu seemed to be.
But *un jour* I had remained upstairs during the promenade, both because I wanted to write and because the weather was worse than usual. Ordinarily, no matter how deep the mud in the *cour*, Jean and I would trot back and forth, resting from time to time under the little shelter out of the drizzle, talking of all things under the sun. I remember on one occasion we were the only ones to brave the rain and slough﻿—Jean in paper-thin soled slippers (which he had recently succeeded in drawing from the Gestionnaire) and I in my huge sabots﻿—hurrying back and forth with the rain pouring on us, and he very proud. On this day, however, I refused the challenge of the mud.
The promenaders had been singularly noisy, I thought. Now they were mounting to the room making a truly tremendous racket. No sooner were the doors opened than in rushed half a dozen frenzied friends, who began telling me all at once about a terrific thing which my friend the *noir* had just done. It seems that The Trick Raincoat had pulled at Jean’s handkerchief (Lulu’s gift in other days) which Jean wore always conspicuously in his outside breast pocket; that Jean had taken the Raincoat’s head in his two hands, held it steady, abased his own head, and rammed the helpless T. R. as a bull would do﻿—the impact of Jean’s head upon the other’s nose causing that well-known feature to occupy a new position in the neighbourhood of the right ear. B. corroborated this description, adding the Raincoat’s nose was broken and that everyone was down on Jean for fighting in an unsportsmanlike way. I found Jean still very angry, and moreover very hurt because everyone was now shunning him. I told him that I personally was glad of what he’d done; but nothing would cheer him up. The T. R.

===Chinese===

男人们（les hommes）照常下楼散步——让不去。晚饭他一口没吃。那天晚上他的床上没发出一点声息。
第二天早上，他咧着大嘴醒了，对「露露！」的问候自己先笑个痛快，答一句「完了，露露（Feeneesh Loo Loo）」。于是折磨人的诸位（发现他身上再没有猎物可欺）收了手；诸事恢复正常。偶或有谁一声「露露！」扬起，让只管笑，扬一扬胳膊重复道：「完了（Feeneesh）。」露露似乎真是完了。
可是有一天（un jour），散步时我留在楼上没下去，一来想写点东西，二来天气比平时更糟。平常，不管 cour 里的泥多深，让和我总要来回小跑，不时在那座小棚子底下歇脚避着毛毛雨，天南地北地聊。记得有一回，冒雨蹚泥的只有我们俩——让趿着纸底薄拖鞋（他新近才从管理员那儿磨来的），我蹬着我的大木鞋（sabots）——大雨浇头，来回奔忙，他还得意得很。可这一天，泥泞的挑战我谢绝了。
散步的诸位一直吵得反常，我记得我当时这么想。这会儿他们正上楼回房，闹出天大的动静。门一开，六七个发疯的朋友冲进来，七嘴八舌地告诉我：我那位黑人（noir）朋友刚刚干下的一件惊人的事。事情似乎是：花哨雨衣扯了让的外胸袋里那块手绢（当年露露的赠物，让总把它显眼地别在外胸袋里）；让双手捧住雨衣的脑袋，定住，自己一低头，像公牛一样照那动弹不得的雨衣撞将过去——让的头撞上对方的鼻子，致使那个著名的器官挪了个新位置，迁往右耳附近居住。
B. 证实了这个描述，还补充说雨衣的鼻子断了，人人都怪让赢得不光彩。我见让还在气头上，而且非常伤心，因为现在人人都躲着他。我告诉他，我个人为他干的事高兴；可什么都宽慰不了他。那位雨衣——

===Original===

now entered, very terrible to see, having been patched up by Monsieur Richard with copious plasters. His nose was not broken, he said thickly, but only bent. He hinted darkly of trouble in store for *le noir*; and received the commiserations of everyone present except Mexique, The Zulu, B. and me.
The Zulu, I remember, pointed to his own nose (which was not unimportant), then to Jean, and made a moue of excruciating anguish, and winked audibly.
Jean’s spirit was broken. The well-nigh unanimous verdict against him had convinced his minutely sensitive soul that it had done wrong. He lay quietly, and would say nothing to anyone.
Some time after the soup, about eight o’clock, the Fighting Sheeney and The Trick Raincoat suddenly set upon Jean le Nègre apropos of nothing; and began pommelling him cruelly. The conscience-stricken pillar of beautiful muscle﻿—who could have easily killed both his assailants at one blow﻿—not only offered no reciprocatory violence but refused even to defend himself. Unresistingly, wincing with pain, his arms mechanically raised and his head bent, he was battered frightfully to the window by his bed, thence into the corner (upsetting the stool in the *pissoir*), thence along the wall to the door. As the punishment increased he cried out like a child: “*Laissez-moi tranquille!*”﻿—again and again; and in his voice the insane element gained rapidly. Finally, shrieking in agony, he rushed to the nearest window; and while the Sheeneys together pommelled him yelled for help to the *planton* beneath.﻿—
The unparalleled consternation and applause produced by this one-sided battle had long since alarmed the authorities. I was still trying to break through the five-deep ring of spectators (among whom was The Messenger Boy, who advised me to desist and got a piece of advice in return)﻿—when with a tremendous crash open burst the door; and in stepped four *plantons* with drawn revolvers, looking frightened to death, followed by the Surveillant who carried a sort of baton and was crying faintly: “*Qu’est-ce que c’est!*”
At the first sound of the door the two Sheeneys had fled, and were now playing the part of innocent spectators. Jean alone occupied the stage. His lips were parted. His eyes were enormous. He was panting as if his heart would break. He still kept his arms raised as if seeing everywhere before him fresh enemies.

===Chinese===

——进来了，样子惨不忍睹，理查先生（Monsieur Richard）拿大块大块的膏药把他糊好了。鼻子没断，他含混地说，只是弯了。他阴沉沉地暗示黑子（le noir）往后有苦头吃；在场人人都向他表示慰问，除了墨西哥人、祖鲁人、B. 和我。
祖鲁人呢，我记得，指了指自己的鼻子（那鼻子绝非无足轻重），又指指让，做出一副痛彻心扉的鬼脸，还响亮地使了个眼色。
让的精神垮了。几乎全场一致不利于他的裁决，说服了他那细如毫发的敏感灵魂：它错了。他静静躺着，对谁也不开口。
汤后过了一阵，约莫八点钟，好斗的希尼（The Fighting Sheeney）和花哨雨衣无缘无故突然扑向让·勒内格尔；开始狠命揍他。这根被良心击中的美丽肌肉之柱——本可以一拳轻松结果两个动手的——不仅没有以暴还暴，连自卫都拒绝了。他不抵抗，疼得直抽缩，双手机械地举着，头垂着，被人可怖地一路揍到他床边的窗户跟前，又从那儿揍进墙角（踢翻了尿池边的小凳），再沿着墙一路揍到门口。揍得越狠，他越像孩子似的喊：「别碰我（Laissez-moi tranquille）！」——一遍又一遍；他嗓音里的疯癫成分迅速上涨。最后，他痛苦地尖叫着冲向最近的窗户；两个希尼一起揍他，他朝楼下的 planton 呼救。——
这场一边倒的战斗所引起的空前骚动与喝彩，早就惊动了当局。我还在往五层厚的人圈里挤（其中有送信小子，他劝我别掺和，收回了我的一句忠告）——这时轰隆一声，门撞开了；跨进来四个拔出手枪的 planton，个个吓得半死，后面跟着监督（The Surveillant），手里攥着一根短棍之类，有气无力地喊着：「这是怎么回事（Qu’est-ce que c’est）！」
门一响，两个希尼早溜了，此刻正扮演无辜观众。台上只剩让一个人。他嘴唇张着。眼睛大得吓人。他喘得仿佛心都要碎了。胳膊还举着，仿佛眼前处处是新冒出来的敌人。

===Original===

Blood spotted here and there the wonderful chocolate carpet of his skin, and his whole body glistened with sweat. His shirt was in ribbons over his beautiful muscles.
Seven or eight persons at once began explaining the fight to the Surveillant, who could make nothing out of their accounts and therefore called aside a trusted older man in order to get his version. The two retired from the room. The *plantons*, finding the expected wolf a lamb, flourished their revolvers about Jean and threatened him in the insignificant and vile language which *plantons* use to anyone whom they can bully. Jean kept repeating dully “*laissez-moi tranquille. Ils voulaient me tuer.*” His chest shook terribly with vast sobs.
Now the Surveillant returned and made a speech, to the effect that he had received independently of each other the stories of four men, that by all counts *le nègre* was absolutely to blame, that *le nègre* had caused an inexcusable trouble to the authorities and to his fellow-prisoners by this wholly unjustified conflict, and that as a punishment the *nègre* would now suffer the consequences of his guilt in the *cabinot*.﻿—Jean had dropped his arms to his sides. His face was twisted with anguish. He made a child’s gesture, a pitiful hopeless movement with his slender hands. Sobbing he protested: “It isn’t my fault, *monsieur le Surveillant!* They attacked me! I didn’t do a thing! They wanted to kill me! Ask him”﻿—he pointed to me desperately. Before I could utter a syllable the Surveillant raised his hand for silence: *le nègre* had done wrong. He should be placed in the *cabinot*.
—Like a flash, with a horrible tearing sob, Jean leaped from the surrounding *plantons* and rushed for the coat which lay on his bed screaming﻿—“***Ahhhhh**﻿—mon couteau!*”﻿—“Look out or he’ll get his knife and kill himself!” someone yelled; and the four *plantons* seized Jean by both arms just as he made a grab for his jacket. Thwarted in his hope and burning with the ignominy of his situation, Jean cast his enormous eyes up at the nearest pillar, crying hysterically: “Everybody is putting me in *cabinot* because I am black.”

===Chinese===

血斑斑点点洒在他那块奇妙的巧克力色地毯般的皮肤上，浑身汗光闪闪。衬衫在他漂亮的肌肉上成了碎条条。
七八个人立刻抢着向监督解释这场架，监督听得一头雾水，便把一个信得过的年长的人拉到一旁，要听他的说法。两人退出房间去了。planton 们发现料想中的恶狼是只羔羊，便拿手枪在让面前挥舞，用 planton 对一切可以欺凌之人的那套下作而委琐的语言威胁他。让木然地反复念叨：「别碰我。他们要杀我（Ils voulaient me tuer）。」他的胸膛被巨大的呜咽抖得厉害。
这时监督回来了，发表了一篇讲话，大意是：他分别听取了四个人的说法，怎么算账都是黑鬼（le nègre）全错，黑鬼挑起这场毫无来由的冲突，给当局、给难友添了不可原谅的麻烦，作为惩罚，黑鬼现在就到禁闭室（cabinot）里去自食恶果。——让的胳膊垂到了身侧。他的脸痛苦地扭着。他做了个孩子的手势，细长的双手可怜而无望地动了一动。他呜咽着抗议：「不是我的错，监督先生（monsieur le Surveillant）！是他们打我！我什么也没干！他们要杀我！问他」——他孤注一掷地指着我。我一个音节还没出，监督就抬手示意肃静：黑鬼错了。该关禁闭室。
——电光石火间，让带着一声撕裂人心的呜咽，从围着他的 planton 中间蹦出来，直扑床上那件外衣，尖声喊叫——「啊——我的刀（mon couteau）！」——「小心他拿刀自杀！」有人大喊；四个 planton 赶在他抓到上衣的一瞬攥住了他的双臂。希望落空，又为眼下的奇耻大辱烧灼着，让把他巨大的眼睛投向最近的柱子，歇斯底里地哭喊：「人人都把我关禁闭室，就因为我是黑人。」

===Original===

﻿—In a second, by a single movement of his arms, he sent the four *plantons* reeling to a distance of ten feet: leaped at the pillar: seized it in both hands like a Samson, and (gazing for another second with a smile of absolute beatitude at its length) dashed his head against it. Once, twice, thrice he smote himself, before the *plantons* seized him﻿—and suddenly his whole strength wilted; he allowed himself to be overpowered by them and stood with bowed head, tears streaming from his eyes﻿—while the smallest pointed a revolver at his heart.
This was a little more than the Surveillant had counted on. Now that Jean’s might was no more, the bearer of the croix de guerre stepped forward and in a mild placating voice endeavoured to soothe the victim of his injustice. It was also slightly more than I could stand, and slamming aside the spectators I shoved myself under his honour’s nose. “Do you know,” I asked, “whom you are dealing with in this man? A child. There are a lot of Jeans where I come from. You heard what he said? He is black, is he not, and gets no justice from you. You heard that. I saw the whole affair. He was attacked, he put up no resistance whatever, he was beaten by two cowards. He is no more to blame than I am.”﻿—The Surveillant was waving his wand and cooing “*Je comprends, je comprends, c’est malheureux.*”﻿—“You’re god damn right its *malheureux*” I said, forgetting my French. “*Quand même*, he has resisted authority” The Surveillant gently continued: “Now Jean, be quiet, you will be taken to the *cabinot*. You may as well go quietly and behave yourself like a good boy.”
At this I am sure my eyes started out of my head. All I could think of to say was: “*Attends, un petit moment.*” To reach my own bed took but a second. In another second I was back, bearing my great and sacred pelisse. I marched up to Jean. “Jean” I remarked with a smile, “you are going to the *cabinot* but you’re coming back right away. I know that you are perfectly right. Put that on”﻿—and I pushed him gently into my coat. “Here are my cigarettes, Jean; you can smoke just as much as you like”﻿—I pulled out all I had, one full *paquet* of Maryland, and a half dozen loose ones, and deposited them carefully in the right hand pocket of the pelisse. Then I patted him on the shoulder and gave him the immortal salutation﻿—“*Bonne chance, mon ami!*”
He straightened proudly.

===Chinese===

——转瞬间，他双臂一振，把四个 planton 甩得踉跄后退十英尺：扑向柱子：像参孙一样双手抱住它，（又对它的长度凝望了一秒，脸上是全然的至福微笑）一头撞了上去。一下，两下，三下——planton 们这才抓住他——突然他全身的力气蔫了；任凭他们制住，垂头站着，眼泪从眼里涌流——而个子最小的那个，用手枪指着他的心口。
这可有点超出监督的预想。如今让的威力不复存在，这位挂战争十字勋章（croix de guerre）的先生走上前来，用温和抚慰的嗓音，努力去安抚被他冤枉的受害者。这也稍微超出了我受得了的限度，我把看客们往两旁一推，直插到大人阁下的鼻子底下。「你知道吗，」我问，「你对付的这个人是谁？一个孩子。我来的地方有一大堆让。你听见他说的话了吧？他是黑人，对不对，从你们这儿得不到公道。你听见了。我目睹了全部经过。他先被袭击，他丝毫没有还手，他被两个懦夫打。要说有错，他不比我多。」——监督摇着他的小棍，咕咕哝哝：「我明白，我明白，真不幸（Je comprends, je comprends, c’est malheureux）。」——「你他妈说得太对了，是真不幸（malheureux）」我说，法语都忘了。「话虽如此（Quand même），他抗拒了当局」监督温和地接着说：「好了，让，安静，要把你送禁闭室了。你不如乖乖地去，做个好孩子。」
我敢肯定，听到这话我的眼珠子都瞪出来了。我唯一想得起来说的是：「等等，一小会儿（Attends, un petit moment）。」到我自己床边不过一秒钟。再一秒钟我就回来了，捧着我那件巨大而神圣的皮大衣。我大步走向让。「让，」我笑着说，「你要去禁闭室，可你马上就回来。我知道你完全没错。把这个穿上」——我把他轻轻裹进我的大衣。「这是我的烟，让；想抽多少抽多少」——我掏出全部存货，一整包马里兰（Maryland），还有六支散烟，仔细存进皮大衣右手口袋。然后我拍拍他的肩膀，给了他一句不朽的祝辞——「祝你好运，我的朋友（Bonne chance, mon ami）！」
他骄傲地挺直了身子。

===Original===

He stalked like a king through the doorway. The astounded *plantons* and the embarrassed Surveillant followed, the latter closing the doors behind him. I was left with a cloud of angry witnesses.
An hour later the doors opened, Jean entered quietly, and the doors shut. As I lay on my bed I could see him perfectly. He was almost naked. He laid my pelisse on his mattress, then walked calmly up to a neighbouring bed and skillfully and unerringly extracted a brush from under it. Back to his own bed he tiptoed, sat down on it, and began brushing my coat. He brushed it for a half hour, speaking to no one, spoken to by no one. Finally he put the brush back, disposed the pelisse carefully on his arm, came to my bed, and as carefully laid it down. Then he took from the right hand outside pocket a full *paquet jaune* and six loose cigarettes, showed them for my approval, and returned them to their place. “*Merci*” was his sole remark. B. got Jean to sit down beside him on his bed and we talked for a few minutes, avoiding the subject of the recent struggle. Then Jean went back to his own bed and lay down.
It was not till later that we learned the climax﻿—not till *le petit belge avec le bras cassé, le petit balayeur*, came hurrying to our end of the room and sat down with us. He was bursting with excitement; his well arm jerked and his sick one stumped about and he seemed incapable of speech. At length words came.
“*Monsieur Jean*” (now that I think of it, I believe someone had told him that all male children in America are named Jean at their birth) “I saw **some sight**! *le nègre, vous savez?*﻿—he is **strong**: *Monsieur Jean*, he’s *a* **giant**, *croyez moi! C’est pas un homme, tu sais? Je l’ai vu, moi*”﻿—and he indicated his eyes.
We pricked up our ears.
The *balayeur*, stuffing a pipe nervously with his tiny thumb said: “You saw the fight here? So did I. The whole of it. *Le noir avait raison.* Well, when they took him downstairs, I slipped out too﻿—*Je suis le balayeur, savez vous?* and the *balayeur* can go where other people can’t.”
I gave him a match, and he thanked me. He struck it on his trousers with a quick pompous gesture, drew heavily on his squeaky pipe, and at last shot a minute puff of smoke into the air: then another, and another.

===Chinese===

他君王一般昂首阔步穿过门洞。惊呆的 planton 们和窘迫的监督跟在后面，后者随手带上了门。剩我一人面对一群怒气冲冲的见证人。
一个钟头后，门开了，让悄悄走进来，门又关上。我躺在自己床上，把他看得清清楚楚。他几乎是赤裸的。他把我的皮大衣放在他的草垫（paillasse）上，然后若无其事地走到邻床边，熟练而毫发不爽地从床底下抽出一把衣刷。他踮着脚回到自己床边，坐下，开始刷我的大衣。他刷了半个钟头，不跟人说话，也没人跟他说话。最后他把刷子放回去，把皮大衣仔仔细细搭在臂上，走到我床前，又仔仔细细放下。然后他从右手外袋里取出一整包黄烟（paquet jaune）和六支散烟，出示给我过目，再放回原处。「谢谢（Merci）」是他唯一的话。B. 拉让在他床边坐下，我们聊了几分钟，避开方才那场搏斗的话题。然后让回到自己床上躺下了。
直到后来我们才知道高潮——直到断臂小比利时人、小扫地的（le petit belge avec le bras cassé, le petit balayeur）急匆匆赶到我们这头的屋子，挨着我们坐下。他兴奋得要炸了；那条好胳膊直比划，那条病胳膊干戳着，话都说不出来。好半天才憋出词来。
「让先生（Monsieur Jean）」（如今想来，我相信是有人告诉他，美国所有的男孩生下来都叫让）「我可见着大场面了（some sight）！那黑鬼，您知道吧（vous savez）？——他有力气：让先生，他是巨人，信我（croyez moi）！那不是人，你懂吗？我亲眼见的，我（Je l’ai vu, moi）」——他指了指自己的眼睛。
我们竖起了耳朵。
小扫地的用小拇指紧张地往烟斗里按烟丝，说：「楼上这场架你看见了？我也看见了。从头到尾。黑子有理（Le noir avait raison）。嘿，他们押他下楼的时候，我也溜出去了——我是扫地的（Je suis le balayeur），您知道吧？扫地的能去别人去不得的地方。」
我给他一根火柴，他谢过我。他神气而利落地在裤腿上划着火柴，滋滋咂响地猛抽他那烟斗，终于朝空中射出一小股烟：又一股，又一股。

===Original===

Satisfied, he went on; his good hand grasping the pipe between its index and second fingers and resting on one little knee, his legs crossed, his small body hunched forward, wee unshaven face close to mine﻿—went on in the confidential tone of one who relates an unbelievable miracle to a couple of intimate friends:
“Monsieur Jean, I followed. They got him to the *cabinot*. The door stood open. At this moment *les femmes descendaient*, it was their *corvée d’eau, vous savez.* He saw them, *le noir*. One of them cried from the stairs, Is a Frenchman stronger than you, Jean? The *plantons* were standing around him, the Surveillant was behind. He took the nearest *planton*, and tossed him down the corridor so that he struck against the door at the end of it. He picked up two more, one in each arm, and threw them away. They fell on top of the first. The last tried to take hold of Jean, and so Jean took him by the neck”﻿—(the *balayeur* strangled himself for our benefit)﻿—“and that *planton* knocked down the other three, who had got on their feet by this time. You should have seen the Surveillant. He had run away and was saying, ‘Capture him, capture him.’ The *plantons* rushed Jean, all four of them. He caught them as they came and threw them about. One knocked down the Surveillant. The women cried ‘*Vive Jean*,’ and clapped their hands. The Surveillant called to the *plantons* to take Jean, but they wouldn’t go near Jean, they said he was a black devil. The women kidded them. They were so sore. And they could do nothing. Jean was laughing. His shirt was almost off him. He asked the planton to come and take him, please. He asked the Surveillant, too. The women had set down their pails and were dancing up and down and yelling. The Directeur came down and sent them flying. The Surveillant and his *plantons* were as helpless as if they had been children. Monsieur Jean﻿—*quelque chose*.”
I gave him another match. “*Merci, Monsieur Jean.*” He struck it, drew on his pipe, lowered it, and went on:
“They were helpless, and men. I am little. I have only one arm, *tu sais*. I walked up to Jean and said, Jean, you know me, I am your friend. He said, Yes. I said to the *plantons*, Give me that rope. They gave me the rope that they would have bound him with. He put out his wrists for me. I tied his hands behind his back. He was like a lamb.

===Chinese===

心满意足了，他接着讲；好手用食指和中指夹着烟斗，搁在小膝盖上，两腿交叉，小小的身子前倾，没刮胡子的小脸凑近我的脸——用的是那种向两三位密友讲述难以置信的奇迹的机密口吻：
「让先生，我跟去了。他们把他押到禁闭室。门开着。这时候娘儿们正下楼（les femmes descendaient），是她们的水役（corvée d’eau），您知道吧。他瞧见她们了，那黑子。有一个在楼梯上喊：法国人比你壮吗，让？planton 们围着他站，监督在后面。他抄起最近的一个 planton，顺着走廊扔出去，撞在走廊尽头那扇门上。又拎起两个，一边一个，扔了出去。砸在头一个身上。最后一个想抓让，让就掐住他脖子」——（小扫地的为我们表演了掐自己脖子）——「那个 planton 撞倒了另外三个，那三个这时候刚爬起来。您真该看看监督。他早跑了，一边喊：『抓住他，抓住他。』四个 planton 一齐扑向让。他们来一个他接一个，扔得东一个西一个。有一个撞倒了监督。娘儿们喊『让万岁（Vive Jean）』，拍巴掌。监督叫 planton 们上去抓让，可他们死也不肯靠近让，说他是黑魔鬼。娘儿们取笑他们。他们气得要命。可他们毫无办法。让在大笑。衬衫都快掉下来了。他请 planton 过来抓他，行个好。也请了监督。娘儿们撂下水桶，又蹦又跳又叫。所长（The Directeur）下来了，把她们轰跑了。监督和他的 planton 们束手无策，活像一帮孩子。让先生——好家伙（quelque chose）。」
我又给他一根火柴。「谢谢，让先生（Merci, Monsieur Jean）。」他划着，咂了口烟斗，放低，接着讲：
「他们没办法，还是大男人呢。我个子小。我只有一条胳膊，你懂（tu sais）。我走到让跟前说，让，你认识我，我是你朋友。他说，是。我对 planton 们说，把绳子给我。他们把本来要捆他的绳子给了我。他朝我伸出手腕。我把他的双手反绑在背后。他像只羔羊。

===Original===

The *plantons* rushed up and tied his feet together. Then they tied his hands and feet together. They took the lacings out of his shoes for fear he would use them to strangle himself. They stood him up in an angle between two walls in the *cabinot*. They left him there for an hour. He was supposed to have been in there all night; but the Surveillant knew that he would have died, for he was almost naked, and *vous savez*, Monsieur Jean, it was cold in there. And damp. A fully clothed man would have been dead in the morning. And he was naked.﻿ ﻿… *Monsieur Jean﻿—un géant!*”
—This same *petit belge* had frequently protested to me that *Il est fou, le noir*. He is always playing when sensible men try to sleep. The last few hours (which had made of the *fou* a *géant*) made of the scoffer a worshipper. Nor did “*le bras cassé*” ever from that time forth desert his divinity. If as *balayeur* he could lay hands on a *morceau de pain* or *de viande*, he bore it as before to our beds; but Jean was always called over to partake of the forbidden pleasure.
As for Jean, one would hardly have recognised him. It was as if the child had fled into the deeps of his soul, never to reappear. Day after day went by, and Jean (instead of courting excitement as before) cloistered himself in solitude; or at most sought the company of B. and me and Le Petit Belge for a quiet chat or a cigarette. The morning after the three fights he did not appear in the *cour* for early promenade along with the rest of us (including The Sheeneys). In vain did *les femmes* strain their necks and eyes to find the black man who was stronger than six Frenchmen. And B. and I noticed our bed-clothing airing upon the windowsills. When we mounted, Jean was patting and straightening our blankets, and looking for the first time in his life guilty of some enormous crime. Nothing however had disappeared. Jean said, “Me feeks *lits tous les jours*.” And every morning he aired and made our beds for us, and we mounted to find him smoothing affectionately some final ruffle, obliterating with enormous solemnity some microscopic crease. We gave him cigarettes when he asked for them (which was almost never) and offered them when we knew he had none or when we saw him borrowing from someone else whom his spirit held in less esteem. Of us he asked no favours. He liked us too well.
When B.

===Chinese===

planton 们冲上来把他的双脚捆上。然后把手和脚捆在一起。他们抽走他鞋里的鞋带，怕他拿去勒死自己。他们让他站在禁闭室两墙夹角的旮旯里。把他撂在那儿一个钟头。本来要关他一整夜的；可监督知道他会死掉，因为他几乎光着身子，您知道的，让先生，那儿冷。还潮。穿齐整衣裳的人到早上也得死。何况他光着。……让先生——巨人（un géant）！」
——就是这位小比利时人（petit belge），从前常跟我抗议：他疯了，那黑子（Il est fou, le noir）。正经人要睡觉他老捣乱。这几个钟头（把疯子（fou）变成了巨人（géant））把讥诮者变成了崇拜者。从那时起，「断胳膊」（le bras cassé）再没离开过他的神。要是当扫地工的他能弄到一块面包或肉（morceau de pain ou de viande），他还是照旧送到我们床前来；但总要把让叫过来，共享这违禁的口福。
至于让，简直认不出了。仿佛那个孩子逃进了他灵魂的深处，再也不出来。一天天过去，让（不再像从前那样招摇生事）把自己关进孤独里；至多找 B. 和我、小比利时人说说话，抽支烟。三场架之后的那个早晨，他没有跟大伙儿（包括那两个希尼）一起下 cour 早散步。娘儿们伸长脖子瞪大眼，白找那个比六个法国人还壮的黑人。而 B. 和我注意到，我们的铺盖晾在窗台上。我们上楼时，让正在拍打、抚平我们的毯子，平生第一次露出犯了什么弥天大罪的样儿。不过什么都没少。让说：「我天天整床（Me feeks lits tous les jours）。」于是每天早晨他替我们晾铺盖、铺床，我们上楼总见他情意绵绵地抚平最后一点褶皱，以无比的庄重抹平一道显微镜级的折痕。他开口要烟的时候我们给（他几乎从不开口），知道他没烟了、或是见他向他的精神较不看重的别人借烟时，我们主动递。他从不求我们什么恩惠。他太喜欢我们了，喜欢到不肯求。
当 B.——

===Original===

went away, Jean was almost as desolate as I.
About a fortnight later, when the grey dirty snow-slush hid the black filthy world which we saw from our windows, and when people lived in their ill-smelling beds, it came to pass that my particular *amis*﻿—The Zulu, Jean, Mexique﻿—and I and all the remaining *miserables* of La Ferté descended at the decree of Caesar Augustus to endure our biweekly bath. I remember gazing stupidly at Jean’s chocolate-coloured nakedness as it strode to the tub, a rippling texture of muscular miracle. Tout le monde had *baigné* (including The Zulu, who tried to escape at the last minute and was nabbed by the *planton* whose business it was to count heads and see that none escaped the ordeal) and now tout le monde was shivering all together in the anteroom, begging to be allowed to go upstairs and get into bed﻿—when Le Baigneur, Monsieur Richard’s strenuous successor that is, set up a hue and cry that one towel was lacking. The Fencer was sent for. He entered; heard the case; and made a speech. If the guilty party would immediately return the stolen towel, he, The Fencer, would guarantee that party pardon; if not, everyone present should be searched, and the man on whose person the serviette was found *va attraper quinze jours de cabinot*. This eloquence yielding no results, The Fencer exorted the culprit to act like a man and render to Caesar what is Caesar’s. Nothing happened. Everyone was told to get in single file and make ready to pass out the door, one after one we were searched; but so general was the curiosity that as fast as they were inspected the erstwhile bed-enthusiasts, myself included, gathered on the sidelines to watch their fellows instead of availing themselves of the opportunity to go upstairs. One after one we came opposite The Fencer, held up our arms, had our pockets run through and our clothing felt over from head to heel, and were exonerated. When Caesar came to Jean Caesar’s eyes lighted, and Caesar’s hitherto perfunctory proddings and pokings became inspired and methodical. Twice he went over Jean’s entire body, while Jean, his arms raised in a bored gesture, his face completely expressionless, suffered loftily the examination of his person. A third time the desperate Fencer tried; his hands, starting at Jean’s neck, reached the calf of his leg﻿—and stopped.

===Chinese===

——走的时候，让几乎跟我一样凄惶。
约莫两星期后，灰蒙蒙的脏雪泥盖住了窗外那个黑乎乎的腌臜世界，人们住进了自己气味不良的被窝，这时节，奉凯撒·奥古斯都的敕令，我那几位特别的朋友（amis）——祖鲁人、让、墨西哥人——和我，以及拉费尔泰其余全部可怜虫（misérables），下楼去受我们双周一次的洗澡。我记得自己呆呆地盯着让那巧克力色的裸体大步走向浴桶，一身波纹起伏的肌肉奇迹。全体人等（tout le monde）都洗完了（包括祖鲁人，他最后一分钟想溜，被那个专管数脑袋、确保无人逃过此劫的 planton 一把揪住），现在全体人等在前厅里集体发抖，恳求放他们上楼钻被窝——就在这时，洗澡工（Le Baigneur），也就是理查先生那位干劲十足的继任者，嚷嚷起来：少了一条毛巾。
击剑手（The Fencer）被请来了。他进来；听取案情；发表演讲。肇事者若立刻交还偷去的毛巾，他击剑手保证赦免其罪；不然，在场人人搜身，毛巾（serviette）在谁身上搜出来，谁就得蹲十五天禁闭室（va attraper quinze jours de cabinot）。雄辩无效，击剑手又敦促那犯人拿出点男子气概，把凯撒的归给凯撒。毫无动静。于是命令全体排成单行，准备依次出门；一个接一个地搜身；可好奇心实在太普遍，那些方才还嚷着要上床的人——包括我——一搜完就聚到场边看别人受检，全不利用机会先上楼去。
一个接一个，我们站到击剑手面前，举起胳膊，口袋被翻遍，衣裳从头到脚被摸了个透，然后宣判无罪。轮到让的时候，凯撒的眼睛亮了，凯撒此前敷衍了事的戳戳点点忽然来了灵感，井然有序。他把让全身上下搜了两遍，让呢，胳膊不耐烦地举着，脸上一无表情，高傲地忍受着对他人身的大检。走投无路的击剑手搜了第三遍；双手从让的脖颈出发，一路摸到小腿肚——停住了。

===Original===

The hands rolled up Jean’s right trouser-leg to the knee. They rolled up the underwear on his leg﻿—and there, placed perfectly flat to the skin, appeared the missing serviette. As The Fencer seized it, Jean laughed﻿—the utter laughter of old days﻿—and the onlookers cackled uproariously, while, with a broad smile, the Fencer proclaimed: “I thought I knew where I should find it.” And he added, more pleased with himself than anyone had ever seen him: “*Maintenant, vous pouvez tous monter à la chambre.*” We mounted, happy to get back to bed; but none so happy as Jean le Nègre. It was not that the *cabinot* threat had failed to materialize﻿—at any minute a *planton* might call Jean to his punishment: indeed this was what everyone expected. It was that the incident had absolutely removed that inhibition which (from the day when Jean *le noir* became Jean *le géant*) had held the child, which was Jean’s soul and destiny, prisoner. From that instant till the day I left him he was the old Jean﻿—joking, fibbing, laughing, and always playing﻿—Jean L’Enfant.
And I think of Jean le Nègre﻿ ﻿… you are something to dream over, Jean; summer and winter (birds and darkness) you go walking into my head; you are a sudden and chocolate-coloured thing, in your hands you have a habit of holding six or eight *plantons* (which you are about to throw away) and the flesh of your body is like the flesh of a very deep cigar. Which I am still and always quietly smoking: always and still I am inhaling its very fragrant and remarkable muscles. But I doubt if ever I am quite through with you, if ever I will toss you out of my heart into the sawdust of forgetfulness. Kid, Boy, I’d like to tell you: *la guerre est finie*.
O yes, Jean: I do not forget, I remember Plenty; the snow’s coming, the snow will throw again a very big and gentle shadow into The Enormous Room and into the eyes of you and me walking always and wonderfully up and down.﻿ ﻿…
—Boy, Kid, Nigger, with the strutting muscles﻿—take me up into your mind once or twice before I die (you know why: just because the eyes of me and you will be full of dirt some day). Quickly take me up into the bright child of your mind, before we both go suddenly all loose and silly (you know how it will feel). Take me up (carefully, as if I were a toy) and play carefully with me, once or twice, before I and you go suddenly all limp and foolish.

===Chinese===

那双手把让的右裤腿卷到膝盖。把腿上的内裤也卷上去——就在那儿，平平整整贴着皮肤，赫然现出那条失踪的毛巾。击剑手一把抓过毛巾，让笑了——往昔那种彻头彻尾的笑——观众哄堂大笑，而击剑手满面春风，宣布：「我早知道该上哪儿找着它。」他又补了一句，那份自得谁都没见他有过：「现在（Maintenant），你们可以都上楼回房了。」我们上楼，回被窝去，人人快活；可谁也不如让·勒内格尔快活。这倒不是因为禁闭室的威胁没兑现——随时都可能有 planton 来叫让去领罚：大家等的其实就是这个。而是因为这件事彻底解除了那道禁制——（自从让·黑子变成让·巨人（géant）那天起）它一直囚禁着那个孩子，那孩子就是让的灵魂与命运。从那一刻起，直到我离开他的那天，他又是原来的让了——玩笑、撒谎、大笑，永远玩个不停——孩子让（Jean L’Enfant）。
我想起让·勒内格尔……你是让人梦萦的东西，让；夏天冬天（鸟儿和黑暗）你都在我脑袋里散步；你是一件突兀而巧克力色的东西，你手里习惯性地攥着六到八个 planton（正要扔出去），你躯体的肉像一支深色雪茄的肉。那支雪茄我至今始终静静抽着：始终至今我吸着它那非常芬芳、非同寻常的肌肉。可我怀疑我究竟抽不抽得完你，怀疑我到底舍不舍得把你从心里扔出去、扔进遗忘的锯末堆。小家伙，孩子，我想告诉你：仗打完了（la guerre est finie）。
哦对了，让：我没忘，我记得普伦提（Plenty）；雪要来了，雪还会把一片很大很温柔的影子投进巨大的房间（The Enormous Room），投进你我那总是奇妙地走来走去的眼睛里。……
——孩子，小家伙，黑鬼，昂首挺胸的一身肌肉——在我死之前，把我拾进你的头脑里一两次吧（你知道为什么：只因为你我这两双眼睛总有一天会塞满泥土）。快把我拾进你头脑里那个明亮的孩子那儿去，趁你我还没忽然统统松掉、变傻（你知道那会是什么滋味）。把我拾起来（轻轻地，当我是件玩具），轻轻地跟我玩，一两次，趁我跟你还没忽然全都瘫软、犯糊涂。

===Original===

Once or twice before you go into great Jack roses and ivory﻿—(once or twice, Boy, before we together go wonderfully down into the Big Dirt laughing, bumped with the last darkness).

===Chinese===

趁你还没走进巨大的杰克玫瑰和象牙——（一两次吧，孩子，趁你我还没一起、笑着、被最后的黑暗撞了一下，奇妙地走进大泥土）。

## XII Three Wise Men / XII 三博士
===Original===

It must have been late in November when *la commission* arrived. *La commission*, as I have said, visited La Ferté every three months. That is to say, B. and I (by arriving when we did) had just escaped its clutches. I consider this one of the luckiest things in my life.
*La commission* arrived one morning, and began work immediately.
A list was made of *les hommes* who were to pass *la commission*, another of *les femmes*. These lists were given to the *planton* with the Wooden Hand. In order to avert any delay, those of the men whose names fell in the first half of the list were not allowed to enjoy the usual stimulating activities afforded by La Ferté’s supreme environment: they were, in fact, confined to The Enormous Room, subject to instant call﻿—moreover they were not called one by one, or as their respective turns came, but in groups of three or four; the idea being that *la commission* should suffer no smallest annoyance which might be occasioned by loss of time. There were always, in other words, eight or ten men waiting in the upper corridor opposite a disagreeably crisp door, which door belonged to that mysterious room wherein *la commission* transacted its inestimable affairs. Not more than a couple of yards away ten or eight women waited their turns. Conversation between the men and the women had been forbidden in the fiercest terms by Monsieur le Directeur: nevertheless conversation spasmodically occurred, thanks to the indulgent nature of the Wooden Hand. The Wooden Hand must have been cuckoo﻿—he looked it. If he wasn’t I am totally at a loss to account for his indulgence.
B. and I spent a morning in The Enormous Room without results, an astonishing acquisition of nervousness excepted. *Après la soupe* (noon) we were conducted *en haut*, told to leave our spoons and bread (which we did) and﻿—in company with several others whose names were within a furlong of the last man called﻿—were descended to the corridor. All that afternoon we waited. Also we waited all next morning. We spent our time talking quietly with a buxom pink-cheeked Belgian girl who was in attendance as translator for one of *les femmes*.

===Chinese===

la commission 抵达时，想必已是十一月下旬。如我所说，la commission 每三个月来拉费尔泰一次。也就是说，B. 和我（来得正是时候）刚好躲过了它的魔爪。我认为这是我此生最走运的事情之一。
la commission 是一天早晨到的，随即立刻开工。
先造了一份要过 la commission 的男人们（les hommes）名单，另造了一份女人们的。名单交给了木手（The Wooden Hand）那个 planton。为了避免任何耽搁，名字落在名单前半部分的男人们，不得享受拉费尔泰至高环境所提供的惯常提神活动：事实上他们被关在巨大的房间里，随叫随到——而且不是一个个叫，也不按各自顺位，而是三四个一组地叫；用意在于，不能让 la commission 因一点时间上的损失而遭受哪怕最小的烦扰。换句话说，总有八到十个男人在上层走廊里候着，正对着那扇令人不快的、脆生生的门，门属于那间神秘的房间，la commission 就在里面办理它那些无价的公务。不出几码远，十来个或八个女人也在等候轮到自己。男人与女人之间的交谈，已被所长先生以最凶狠的字眼严令禁止：尽管如此，交谈还是不时地发生，多亏木手生性宽厚。木手准是脑子不灵光——他那长相就像。他要不是，我全然无法解释他的宽厚。
B. 和我在巨大的房间里耗了一个上午，一无所获，除了惊人地攒下一身紧张。喝过汤（après la soupe）（中午），我们被带上去（en haut），被吩咐留下勺子和面包（我们照办了），然后——同另外几个名字排在最后被叫那人不出一弗隆远的家伙一道——被带到了走廊上。那个下午我们一直在等。第二天上午我们还是在等。我们把时间用来同一个面色红润、体态丰腴的比利时姑娘轻声交谈，她是来给一位女囚当翻译的。

===Original===

This Belgian told us that she was a permanent inhabitant of La Ferté, that she and another *femme honnête* occupied a room by themselves, that her brothers were at the front in Belgium, that her ability to speak fluently several languages (including English and German) made her invaluable to *Messieurs la commission*, that she had committed no crime, that she was held as a *suspecte*, that she was not entirely unhappy. She struck me immediately as being not only intelligent but alive. She questioned us in excellent English as to our offenses, and seemed much pleased to discover that we were﻿—to all appearances﻿—innocent of wrongdoing.
From time to time our subdued conversation was interrupted by admonitions from the amiable Wooden Hand. Twice the door **slammed** open, and Monsieur le Directeur bounced out, frothing at the mouth and threatening everyone with infinite *cabinot*, on the ground that everyone’s deportment or lack of it was menacing the aplomb of the commissioners. Each time, the Black Holster appeared in the background and carried on his master’s bullying until everyone was completely terrified﻿—after which we were left to ourselves and the Wooden Hand once again.
B. and I were allowed by the latter individual﻿—he was that day, at least, an individual not merely a *planton*﻿—to peek over his shoulder at the men’s list.
